--- Page 11 ---
<meta>This page follows the printer's colophon and serves as the opening of the Preface, detailing the scope of the translation and the existing scholarly landscape regarding Photius's "Bibliotheca."</meta>

# PREFACE

It is proposed to publish the current translation of the *Bibliotheca* <note>original: *Bibliotheca*, meaning "Library"</note> of Photius in five volumes; a sixth will contain an account of his life and works, a bibliography, and a general index to the entire collection.

A translator of the *Bibliotheca* has, it seems, an open field. As far as can be determined, no complete version exists in English or any other modern language. There are, however, English translations or editions of some of the longer extracts (for example, Ctesias' *Indica* <note>original: *Indica*, a work concerning India</note>), and some of the literary criticisms.¹ There are also selected codices <note>manuscripts or book entries</note> (chiefly on secular authors <note>original: "profane authors"</note>) in Italian by G. Compagnoni,² a well-known Milanese man of letters and statesman (1754–1834). Finally, there is a Latin translation of the whole work by the learned Jesuit Andreas Schott³ in Migne's *Greek Patrology* <note>original: *Patrologia Graeca*</note> (volume 103).

The text⁴ is unfortunately unsatisfactory in many places, and no critical edition has been attempted for nearly a hundred years—not since 1824, the date of Immanuel Bekker's edition. The nature of the work—a sort of enlarged table of contents—often leaves the meaning and connection obscure when there are no complete texts⁵ or other means of supplementing it.

---

¹ G. Saintsbury, *History of Criticism*, volume 1, page 176; La Rue van Hook in *Transactions of the American Philological Association* (volume 38, 1907) and *Classical Philology* (volume 4, Chicago, 1909), and here and there in books such as Hodgkin's *Italy and her Invaders*, and Bury's *Later Roman Empire*.
² Volumes 45 and 46 of the *Selected Library of Greek and Latin Works* <note>original: *Biblioteca Scelta di opere greche e latine*</note>.
³ Born and died at Antwerp (1552–1629). After a wandering life, he finally settled down as a professor of Greek at the Jesuit college in his native city. He was a tireless translator and editor. The translation of Photius is of varying quality, and it is supposed that parts of it are the work of a younger and less competent colleague.
⁴ See E. Martini, *History of the Text of the Library of the Patriarch Photius* <note>original: *Textgeschichte der Bibliothek des Patriarchen Photios*</note> (1911).
⁵ It would be difficult, for instance, to get a clear idea of the story of Heliodorus's *Theagenes and Chariclea* from Photius's summary alone.

<summary>This preface introduces a multi-volume translation of Photius’s 9th-century "Bibliotheca," noting the lack of previous complete modern translations and the difficulties posed by the century-old Greek text.</summary>
<keywords>Photius, Bibliotheca, translation, Andreas Schott, Immanuel Bekker, G. Compagnoni, Greek Patrology, Byzantine literature</keywords>

--- Page 12 ---
<meta>This page continues the Preface, moving from a discussion of the existing Latin and Italian versions of Photius to the current translator's methodology and the scope of the accompanying notes and bibliography.</meta>

# PREFACE

The current translator, in the effort to produce the most satisfactory rendering possible, has constantly consulted the most important literary, theological, and historical works and encyclopedias related to the wide range of subjects covered by Photius’s extensive reading. Naturally, there is little room for stylistic elegance in this translation; in the sections containing literary criticism, it is particularly difficult to find accurate and suitable English equivalents for the terms the author used.

Regarding the notes, the number of personal and geographical names and historical references is so vast that any attempt to explain them in detail would have overwhelmed the text. It would have turned the work into a mere framework upon which to hang a miniature encyclopedia. For well-known figures, I have provided only a brief note—just enough to identify them. This is often necessary when several historical figures share the same name, and it ensures that readers who want more information will know exactly which person to look for in standard biographical dictionaries. For less familiar names and specific points arising directly from the text, the details provided are somewhat more thorough. 

The Bibliography will include a list of the most useful general reference works, as well as specific editions or accounts of the authors reviewed in the *Bibliotheca* <note>original: *Bibliotheca*, the "Library" or collection of book summaries by Photius</note>. This current work does not claim to be a complete explanatory or critical commentary. Instead, it is a somewhat flexible translation intended to give the general reader an idea of the literary activity of the primary representative of the so-called Byzantine Renaissance <note>A period of increased interest in classical Greek literature and learning in the Byzantine Empire, roughly during the 9th and 10th centuries</note>.

<summary>The translator explains his methodology, noting that he prioritized clarity over elegance and kept historical notes brief to avoid overwhelming the text. The work is intended as an accessible introduction to Photius's literary reviews for a general audience.</summary>
<keywords>Photius, Bibliotheca, translation methodology, Byzantine Renaissance, bibliography, literary criticism</keywords>

*AGAINST the Jews and Quartodecimans* <note>Christian groups who insisted on celebrating Easter on the 14th day of the Jewish month of Nisan, the same day as Passover</note>. Anonymous (CXV)
AMYNTIANUS. *On Alexander the Great* (CXXXI)
ANDRONICIANUS. *Against the Eunomians* <note>Followers of Eunomius, who held extreme Arian views regarding the nature of the Trinity</note> (XLV)
APHTHONIUS. *Declamations* (CXXXIII)
APOLINARIUS of Hierapolis. *Against the Heathen, On Piety and Truth* (XIV)
*APOLOGY for Origen and his Doctrines* <note>A formal defense of the controversial early Christian theologian Origen</note>. Anonymous (CXVII)
APPIAN. *Roman History* (LVII)
ARRIAN. *History of Parthia* <note>original: *Parthica*</note>, *History of Bithynia* <note>original: *Bithynica*</note>, *Discourses of Epictetus* (LVIII); *History of India* <note>original: *Indica*</note>, *Campaigns of Alexander the Great* (XCI); *Events after the Death of Alexander* (XCII); *History of Bithynia* <note>original: *Bithynica*</note>, *The Acts of Dion and Timoleon* (XCIII)
ATHANASIUS. *Letters* (XXXII); *Commentary on Ecclesiastes and the Song of Songs* (CXXXIX); *Against Arius* (CXL)

<column-break/>

BASIL of Cilicia. *Ecclesiastical History* (XLII); *Against John of Scythopolis* <note>original: *Scythopolita*</note> (CVII)
BASIL the Great. *Refutation of Eunomius* (CXXXVIII); *Ascetic Works* <note>original: *Ascetica*</note> (CXLIV); *The Six Days of Creation* <note>original: *Hexaëmeron*</note> (CXLI); *Moral Discourses* (CXLII); *Letters* (CXLIII)
BOETHUS. *List of Platonic Words* (CLIV); *Doubtful Words in Plato* (CLV)

GAIUS (CAIUS) Presbyter. *On the Universe* (XLVIII)
CANDIDUS. *Histories* (LXXIX)
CEPHALION. *Historical Epitome* (LXVIII)
LUCIUS CHARINUS. *Travels of the Apostles* (CXIV)
CHORICIUS. *Declamations* (CLX)
CLEMENT (Titus Flavius Clemens), presbyter of Alexandria. *Outlines* (CIX); *The Tutor* (CX); *Miscellanies* <note>original: *Stromateis*</note> (CXI)
CLEMENT (Clemens Romanus), bishop of Rome. *Apostolic Constitutions, Recognitions* (CXII, CXIII); *Epistles to the Corinthians* (CXXVI)
CONON. *Against Philoponus* (XXIII)

# PHOTIUS

## BIBLIOTHECA OR MYRIOBIBLON <note>original: "Library" or "Ten Thousand Books"</note> ^1^

### *A list and numbering of the 279 books read by us, for which our beloved brother Tarasius requested a summary.*^2^

->Photius sends greetings in the name of the Lord to his beloved brother, Tarasius.<-

**My dearest brother Tarasius**,
After my appointment as ambassador to Assyria <note>original: "Assyria"; this likely refers to the Caliph of Baghdad in modern-day Iraq, or possibly Persia</note> ^3^ had been confirmed by the agreement of the diplomatic mission ^4^ and approved by the emperor, you requested to be provided with summaries of the works that were read and discussed while you were away. Your intention was to have something to comfort you during our painful separation, and at the same time to gain some knowledge—even if general and incomplete—of the books you had not yet read with us. We believe the number of these works is exactly 279. Therefore, viewing the fulfillment of your request as a sacred duty, we hired a secretary and recorded all the summaries we could remember. No doubt we have not been fast enough to satisfy your intense eagerness and strong desire, but we have still been quicker than might have been expected. The summaries will be arranged in the order that they come to mind. Certainly, it would 

***

^1^ For the life and works of Photius, refer to the Introduction in Volume 6.
^2^ The authenticity of this title is debated.
^3^ To the Caliph of Baghdad; according to others, to Persia, or one of the Eastern military commanders (Bury).
^4^ *Embassy* <note>original Greek: πρεσβεία/presbeia</note> cannot mean “senate,” as the scholar Schott translates it (using the Latin *senatus*), but likely refers to “the body of people making up the embassy”—a group of others nominated for the mission who found Photius to be a welcome person <note>original Latin: "persona grata"</note> and expressed a wish that he should accompany them. This explanation is provided through the kindness of Professor J. B. Bury.

<summary>In this preface, Photius introduces his massive collection of 279 book summaries, written for his brother Tarasius before Photius departs on a diplomatic mission to the East.</summary>
<keywords>Photius, Tarasius, Bibliotheca, Myriobiblon, Byzantine literature, embassy, Baghdad, book summaries</keywords>

--- Page 22 ---
<meta>This page concludes the author's preface to his brother Tarasius and begins the first entry of the "Bibliotheca," a review of a treatise by Theodore the Presbyter defending the works of Dionysius the Areopagite.</meta>

It would not be difficult, if someone preferred it, to describe historical events and other various subjects under separate headings. However, since nothing would be gained by doing so, we have recorded them randomly as they occurred to us. <note>original: "The text is corrupt." This means the surviving Greek manuscript is damaged or unclear at this point.</note> If, during your study of these volumes, any of the summaries seem incomplete or inaccurate, do not be surprised. It is no easy task to read every single work, understand the argument, and then remember and record it; when the number of works is vast and a long time has passed since they were read, remembering them with accuracy becomes extremely difficult. 

As for the general topics met within the course of our reading—which are so simple they likely didn't escape your notice—we have paid less attention to them and purposefully avoided examining them closely. You are better able than I to decide if these summaries will do more than just fulfill your original hopes for their usefulness. Certainly, these records will help you refresh your memory of what you have already read, find specific information more easily, and gain knowledge of books you haven't yet had the chance to study carefully.

### 1. THEODORE THE PRESBYTER <note>These sections are usually referred to as *Codices* (manuscripts). A *Codex* (original Latin: *caudex*, meaning a block of wood) was originally a set of wax-coated wooden tablets used for writing. These eventually evolved into the modern book format, as opposed to the older scroll format (original Latin: *volumen*).</note>

Read the treatise of Theodore the Presbyter <note>Nothing else is known about this author's life or works</note>, in which he attempts to prove that the works attributed to Saint Dionysius are genuine. He refutes the following arguments against their authenticity: 

(1) If they are genuine, why is it that none of the later Church Fathers cite them or quote passages from them? 

(2) Why is it that Eusebius Pamphili <note>The famous church historian Eusebius (around 260–340 AD), the Bishop of Caesarea. He took the name "Pamphili" to honor his close friend and mentor, Pamphilus the martyr</note>, in his list of the writings of the Holy Fathers, does not mention them? 

(3) Why is it that these treatises describe in detail church rituals and customs that only became established...

<summary>Photius concludes his introduction by explaining his disorganized method of categorization and begins his first entry: a defense of the authenticity of the writings of Saint Dionysius by an author named Theodore.</summary>
<keywords>Photius, Theodore the Presbyter, Saint Dionysius, Eusebius of Caesarea, Codex, ecclesiastical history, literary criticism</keywords>

--- Page 23 ---
<meta>This page continues the review of Theodore the Presbyter’s defense of the writings of Dionysius the Areopagite, then moves to brief summaries of works by Adrian and the historian Nonnosus.</meta>

...established in the Church gradually and after a long time? The great Dionysius, as is clear from the *Book of Acts* <note>original: Acts 17:34</note>, was a contemporary of the Apostles. However, most of the religious institutions described in these books only became established gradually in later times. It is therefore improbable (says the objector), or rather a clumsy fiction, to assert that Dionysius could have described institutions which were not fully developed until long after his death. (4) How is it that a letter of the divinely inspired Ignatius <note>Ignatius was the Bishop of Antioch, active around 70–107 AD</note> is referenced? For Dionysius flourished in the time of the Apostles, whereas Ignatius suffered martyrdom during the reign of Trajan and wrote the letter in question shortly before his death. Theodore endeavors to solve these difficulties and does his best to prove the authenticity of the treatises <note>These mystical works, once attributed to the Dionysius mentioned in the Bible, are now believed by scholars to have been written in the late 5th century by an anonymous author often called "Pseudo-Dionysius."</note>.

### II

Read Adrian’s (Hadrian’s) *Introduction to the Scriptures* <note>This work, likely written by a Syrian monk around 425 AD, focuses on explaining the metaphors and style of the Bible</note>, a useful book for catechumens <term>catechumen: a person receiving basic instruction in Christianity before being baptized</term>.

### III

Read the *History of Nonnosus* <note>Nonnosus was a 6th-century historian and diplomat</note>, containing a description of his embassy to the Ethiopians, the Homerites <note>original: "Amerites"; these were the Himyarites of ancient Yemen</note>, and the Saracens—who were then a most powerful nation—as well as to other Eastern peoples. At this time, Justinian was Emperor of the Romans, and Caïsus was the chief of the Saracens. This Caïsus was the grandson of...

<summary>This page concludes Theodore the Presbyter's defense of the authenticity of the Dionysian treatises and introduces Adrian's biblical guide and Nonnosus's diplomatic history of the East.</summary>
<keywords>Dionysius the Areopagite, Ignatius of Antioch, Theodore the Presbyter, Adrian, Nonnosus, Justinian, Ethiopia, Yemen, Saracens, church history, diplomacy</keywords>

--- Page 24 ---
<meta>This page continues the summary of the "History of Nonnosus," detailing his diplomatic missions to the Saracens and the Kingdom of Aksum during the 6th century.</meta>

# NONNOSUS

Arethas was a tribal leader to whom Nonnosus’s grandfather had been sent as an ambassador during the reign of Emperor Anastasius to conclude a peace treaty. Nonnosus’s father, Abrames¹ <note>The name Abrames suggests the family was of Semitic origin, although he was likely a convert to Christianity.</note>, had similarly been sent as an envoy to Al-Mundhir² <note>original: Alamundarus; he was the leader of the Saracens of Hira.</note>, the chief of the Saracens, during the reign of Justin I. He was successful in securing the release of Timostratus and John, two Roman generals who were being held as prisoners of war. 

Caïsus <note>likely Qays</note>, the man to whom Nonnosus himself was sent, was the leader of two of the most prominent Saracen tribes, the Kindah <note>original: Chindeni</note> and the Ma'ad <note>original: Maadeni</note>. Before Nonnosus was appointed as ambassador, his father had been sent to this same Caïsus by Emperor Justinian and had successfully negotiated a peace treaty, on the condition that Caïsus’s son, Mavias, be taken to Byzantium <note>Constantinople</note> as a hostage. After this, Nonnosus was entrusted with a three-part mission: 
1. To visit Caïsus and persuade him, if possible, to visit the Emperor in person.
2. To visit Elesbaas <note>Kaleb</note>, King of the Axumites <note>the Kingdom of Aksum in modern-day Ethiopia and Eritrea</note>.
3. To visit the Himyarites <note>original: Amerites; a kingdom in ancient Yemen</note>.

Aksum³ <note>Modern Axum, the former capital of the Ethiopian province of Tigray. It contains many ancient ruins and inscriptions and is still considered a holy city. Christianity was introduced to Ethiopia as early as the fourth century.</note> is a very large city and can be considered the capital of Ethiopia; it lies further south and east than the Roman Empire. Despite treacherous attacks by various tribes, the danger of wild animals, and many other difficulties and hazards on the road, Nonnosus successfully completed his mission and returned safely to his homeland.⁴ <note>Further details of this mission are found in the *Chronicle* of John Malalas and in Edward Gibbon's *The Decline and Fall of the Roman Empire*, chapter 41.</note>

Nonnosus records that after Abrames was sent to him a second time, Caïsus set out for Byzantium, having first divided his leadership between his brothers, Ambrus and Yezid. He brought a large number of his subjects with him and was appointed by the Emperor as the administrator of Palestine.

He also notes that the ancient word for what are now called *sandalia* (sandals) <note>original: σανδάλια</note> was *arbylai* <note>original: ἀρβύλαι; typically referring to a strong hunter's boot</note>, and that the word for a *phakiolion* (turban) <note>original: φακιόλιον</note> was once *phasolis*.⁵ <note>The specific form of this original word is uncertain.</note>

He informs us that most of the Saracens who live in—

<summary>This page describes the diplomatic career of Nonnosus and his family, detailing their embassies to Saracen leaders and the King of Aksum to negotiate peace and prisoner releases. It also includes brief notes on the geography of Ethiopia and historical shifts in Greek vocabulary for clothing.</summary>

<keywords>Nonnosus, Justinian, Aksum, Ethiopia, Himyarites, Saracens, diplomacy, Byzantine Empire, Caïsus, Al-Mundhir</keywords>

--- Page 25 ---
<meta>This page continues Nonnosus's account of his travels through the Red Sea region and Ethiopia, focusing on local customs, wildlife, and the unique climate of the Aksumite Empire.</meta>

# NONNOSUS

The people of Phoenicon <note>likely Wadi Feiran in the Sinai Peninsula</note>, as well as those in the regions beyond it and the Taurenian mountains <margin>Mountainous regions in Western Asia and the land of the Saracens.</margin>, have a sacred meeting place dedicated to one of their gods where they gather twice a year. One of these meetings lasts for an entire month, occurring nearly in the middle of spring when the sun enters the constellation of Taurus. The other lasts for two months and is held after the summer solstice. During these gatherings, a state of complete peace prevails—not only among the people themselves but also with all the local inhabitants. Even the animals are at peace, both with each other and with human beings. Other strange and somewhat mythical stories are also told about this place.

Nonnosus tells us that Adulis <note>A seaport town, generally identified with modern Zula in Annesley Bay on the western shore of the Red Sea.</note> is a fifteen-day journey from Aksum <note>original: Axumis</note>. On his way there, he and his companions witnessed a remarkable sight near the town of Avē <note>original: Auē</note>, which lies midway between Aksum and Adulis. They saw a massive number of elephants—nearly 5,000 of them. These animals were feeding in a vast plain, and the local people found it difficult to get near them or drive them away from their pasture. This was one of the notable sights they saw on their journey.

We must also describe the contradictory climates of summer and winter between Avē and Aksum. When the sun enters the signs of Cancer, Leo, and Virgo <note>the summer months of June, July, and August</note>, it is summer as far as Avē, just as it is for us, and the air is extremely dry. However, from Avē to Aksum and throughout the rest of Ethiopia <note>original: Aethiopia</note>, it is a severe winter. This does not last throughout the entire day but begins at midday, when the sky becomes covered with clouds and the countryside is flooded with violent rains. It is during this time that the Nile spreads over Egypt, overflowing its banks and irrigating the land. Conversely, when the sun enters Capricorn, Aquarius, and Pisces <note>the winter months of December, January, and February</note>, the atmosphere floods the territory of the Adulites as far as Avē. Meanwhile, it is summer from Avē to Aksum and the rest of Ethiopia, and the fruits of the earth reach their ripeness.

During his voyage from Pharsan <note>A town or island region in Ethiopia.</note>, upon reaching the last of the islands, Nonnosus had a remarkable experience. He saw certain creatures <note>The author likely refers here to Pygmies.</note> with human shapes and forms, though they were very short, black-skinned, and had bodies entirely covered with hair. The men were accompanied by women of the same appearance and by boys who were even shorter. All of them were naked—both the women and the men—except for a short apron made of skin worn around their loins. There...

<summary>Nonnosus describes a sacred Saracen sanctuary, the massive elephant herds near Aksum, and the unique seasonal monsoons that drive the flooding of the Nile. He also records an encounter with "Pygmies" on a Red Sea island.</summary>

<meta>This page concludes Nonnosus's travel account regarding a timid coastal tribe and transitions into a series of theological reviews by Photius. It covers works by Theodore of Mopsuestia, Sophronius, and Gregory of Nyssa, all of whom wrote in defense of Saint Basil the Great against the Arian teachings of Eunomius.</meta>

->**GREGORY OF NYSSA**<-

...there was nothing wild or savage about them. Their speech was human, but their language was unintelligible even to their neighbors, and even more so to Nonnosus and his companions. They live on shellfish and fish washed up on the shore. According to Nonnosus, they were very timid; when they saw him and his companions, they shrank away from them as we would from monstrous wild beasts.

### IV

I read the twenty-five books written by Theodore of Antioch <note>Theodore (c. 350–428 AD) was the Bishop of Mopsuestia in Cilicia and a leading scholar of the Antiochene school of Bible interpretation.</note> against Eunomius <note>Eunomius (died c. 393 AD) was a bishop and leader of an extreme branch of Arianism which argued that the Son was "unlike" the Father.</note> in defense of Saint Basil <note>Saint Basil the Great (330–379 AD) was the Bishop of Caesarea and a primary defender of the Nicene Creed.</note>. His style is somewhat obscure, but the work is full of ideas and sound reasoning, and it contains a wealth of evidence taken from the Scriptures. He refutes the arguments of Eunomius almost word for word and amply proves that Eunomius is very ignorant of secular knowledge <note>original: "outside knowledge," referring to classical Greek philosophy and science</note> and even more ignorant of our religion. I believe he is the same Theodore who was the Bishop of Mopsuestia.

### V

I also read the attack by Sophronius <note>Likely the friend of Saint Jerome who translated Jerome's works into Greek.</note> on Eunomius in defense of Saint Basil. His style is clearer and more concise than that of Theodore. He does not address every argument made by Eunomius, but instead chiefly attacks and refutes those which appear to contain the most essential points of his heresy. The style is aphoristic <note>consisting of short, pithy statements</note>, and the language is generally free and simple—literally "without conjunctions" <note>original Greek: Ἀσύνδετος (asyndetos). This is a rhetorical style that omits connecting words like "and" or "but" for speed and emphasis.</note>—and not disagreeable, although it is occasionally embellished with overly labored arguments.

### VI

I also read the attack of Gregory of Nyssa <note>Gregory of Nyssa (c. 335–395 AD) was the younger brother of Basil the Great and a highly influential theologian known for his philosophical depth.</note> on Eunomius in...

<summary>Photius completes the account of Nonnosus’s travels and begins reviewing several major theological works written to defend Saint Basil the Great against the Arian heretic Eunomius.</summary>

<meta>This page continues Photius’s review of theological works written in defense of Saint Basil against Eunomius. <page-num>21</page-num>
->**ORIGEN**<-

...defense of Saint Basil. His style is as brilliant as that of any skilled orator <note>original: "rhetorician"</note> and is a pleasure to listen to. He does not refute Eunomius in exhaustive detail, and is therefore more concise than Theodore, though more thorough than Sophronius. He is fond of using <term>enthymemes</term>¹ and arguments based on examples. I can say without bias that the abundance and richness of his arguments prove his superiority to Theodore just as much as the charm, brilliance, and pleasantness of his style does.

## VII

I also read another treatise by the same Gregory of Nyssa on the same subject. In this work, he challenges Eunomius with more logical arguments and attacks the crumbling defenses of his ungodly teachings <note>original: "impiety"</note>. The beauty of his style, with its blend of brilliance and charm, is equally evident in this work.

## VIII

I read the four books of Origen’s² *On First Principles*. The first book deals with the Father, the Son, and the Holy Spirit. In this, his statements are often sacrilegious <note>original: "blasphemous"</note>; for instance, he claims that the Son was created by the Father, and the Holy Spirit was created by the Son. He also asserts that the Father’s influence pervades all existing things, while the Son’s influence reaches only rational beings, and the Holy Spirit’s reaches only those who are saved. He makes other strange and ungodly statements, indulging in foolish talk about the reincarnation <note>original: "migration"</note> of souls, the idea that stars are living beings, and similar notions. This first book is full of myths about the Father, Christ (as he calls the Son), the Holy Spirit, and rational creatures. In the second book, he treats the world and created things. He maintains that the God of the Law and the prophets—the God of both the Old and the New Testaments—is one and the same; he also claims that the same Holy Spirit inspired Moses, the rest of the prophets, and—

---

¹ The specific meaning of an <term>enthymeme</term> is a rhetorical or incomplete logical argument <note>original: "syllogism"</note> drawn from probable premises. However, here and elsewhere in Photius’s writing, it seems to mean simply "arguments."

² Known by the surname <term>Adamantius</term> (184–253 AD), Origen was born in Alexandria and died in Tyre. This treatise provided the main evidence for the charge of <term>heresy</term> (teachings contrary to established church doctrine) that was brought against him. He was also called <term>Chalcenterus</term> <note>original: "brazen-bowelled," meaning he had a stomach of bronze</note> because of his incredible passion for work. His many writings include sermons <note>original: "Homilies"</note> and the famous treatise *Against Celsus*. Fragments of his *Hexapla* <note>original: Greek for "six-fold"; a massive comparative edition of the Old Testament</note> have been preserved.

# EUSEBIUS

...the Holy Apostles. He further discusses the <term>Incarnation</term> <note>The doctrine of God becoming human in the person of Jesus.</note> of the Savior, the soul, the resurrection, punishment, and the divine promises. The third book deals with free will; how the devil and hostile powers, according to the Scriptures, wage war against mankind; and that the world was created and is perishable, having had a beginning in time. The fourth book treats the final end, the divine inspiration of the Scriptures, and the proper manner of reading and understanding them.

->IX<-

I read fifteen books of the *Preparation for the Gospel* <note>original: "Praeparatio Evangelica"</note> by Eusebius, in which he refutes the foolish doctrines of the <term>gentiles</term> <note>original: "gentiles"; here referring to non-Christian pagans.</note> and shows that their beliefs were always contradictory. At the beginning and end of the fifteenth book, he mentions another treatise, the *Proof of the Gospel* <note>original: "Demonstratio Evangelica"</note>, which follows the *Preparation*. His goal in these works is to refute the errors of the non-Christians and to confirm the preaching of the Gospel.

->X<-

I read the twenty books of Eusebius’s *Proof of the Gospel* <note>original: "Demonstratio Evangelica"</note>. <sup>1</sup>

->XI<-

I read Eusebius’s *Ecclesiastical Preparation* <note>original: "Praeparatio Ecclesiastica"</note>, <sup>2</sup> in . . . books, which consists of various extracts. . . .

->XII<-

I read Eusebius’s *Ecclesiastical Proof* <note>original: "Demonstratio Ecclesiastica"</note>, <sup>2</sup> in . . . books.

->XIII<-

I read two books of Eusebius’s *Refutation and Defense*, and a second edition of the same work. While the second edition differs in certain passages, it agrees in other respects with the first in both style and sentiment. In this work, he mentions certain difficulties brought forward by the <term>heathen</term> <note>Non-Christians, specifically those practicing polytheism.</note> against our blameless religion and provides solutions that are satisfactory, though not entirely complete. His style is neither agreeable nor brilliant; however, he is a man of great learning, although he lacks shrewdness and firmness of character.

<sup>2</sup> Both of these works have been lost to history.

### XIV

I read Apolinarius’s <note>original: "Apolinarius"; likely Claudius Apollinaris, Bishop of Hierapolis, who lived around 175 AD.</note> *Against the Pagans* <note>original: "Against the Heathen"</note>, *On Piety*, and *On Truth*. The writer was the bishop of Hierapolis in Asia <note>Specifically in the region of Lesser Phrygia.</note> and flourished during the reign of Marcus Antoninus Verus <note>Better known as the Roman Emperor and philosopher Marcus Aurelius.</note>. He deserves to be mentioned, and his writing style is excellent. Other writings of his are said to be equally worthy of record, but they have not come into my possession.

### XV

I read the *Acts* of the <term>first council</term> <note>The First Council of Nicaea, held in 325 AD.</note> in three volumes. It carries the name of Gelasius <note>Gelasius of Cyzicus, a historian who lived around 475 AD.</note>, but it is more of a history than a record of the <term>Acts</term> <note>Official minutes or proceedings of a church council.</note>. The author's style is poor and lowly, but he provides a detailed account of the proceedings of the council.

# SYNODS

There were fifteen sessions <note>These sessions belong to the Fourth Ecumenical Council, the Council of Chalcedon, held in 451 AD.</note>, at which Dioscorus ^1 and Eutyches ^2 were condemned, and Nestorius was excommunicated. St. Flavian ^3 was declared innocent after his death, along with Eusebius of Dorylaeum,^4 Theodoret,^5 and Ibas.^6 Other special matters were discussed, and the spirit of piety was strengthened.

### XVIII

I read the *Acts* of the <term>Fifth Council</term> <note>The Second Council of Constantinople, held in 553 AD.</note>, during which the so-called <term>Three Chapters</term> ^8 <note>original: "three chapters"; a series of writings by Theodore of Mopsuestia, Theodoret of Cyrrhus, and Ibas of Edessa that were condemned in an attempt to reconcile with Monophysite Christians.</note> were addressed. At this council, Origen and his writings were excommunicated, as were Diodorus of Tarsus ^9 and Theodore of Mopsuestia. Theodoret’s response to the twelve <term>anathematisms</term> <note>Formal denunciations or curses issued by a church council against specific doctrines or people.</note> of Cyril was also excommunicated. Prior to this, the council discussed the cases of Zooras ^10 and Anthimus,^11 who had <term>intruded into</term> <note>original: "wormed his way into"; Anthimus was seen as an illegitimate patriarch who gained his position through political maneuvering.</note> the patriarchate of Constantinople, as well as several other matters.

### XIX

I read the *Acts* of the <term>Sixth Council</term> <note>The Third Council of Constantinople, held in 680–681 AD, which addressed the Monothelite heresy.</note>. During its sessions, Sergius,^13 Cyrus,^14 and Pyrrhus ^15 of Constantinople were excommunicated, along with Pope Honorius ^16 of Rome, Polychronius,^17 and others. They had dared to assert that there... <note>The text breaks off here; the Council went on to condemn the doctrine that Christ had only one "will" (Monothelitism).</note>

^2 Founder of the <term>Monophysite</term> sect (380–456) <note>A group that believed Christ had only one single nature, rather than being both fully human and fully divine.</note>.

^4 Bishop of Dorylaeum (modern-day *Eskişehir*) in Phrygia (400–451).
^5 Bishop of Cyrrhus (Cyrus) near the Euphrates, two days' journey from Antioch; he was a theologian and church historian (*c.* 393–458).
^6 Bishop of Edessa (*c.* 435–457).
^7 The Fifth Council was held at Constantinople in 553 AD.

^10 A Monophysite Syrian monk.
^11 Anthimus, Patriarch of Constantinople (served in 535, deposed in 536); he was transferred from the <term>episcopate</term> <note>The office or rank of a bishop.</note> of Trebizond through the influence of the Empress Theodora.

# JOHN PHILOPONUS

...that there was only one will and one energy in Christ. The dogma of truth was confirmed.

->XX<-

Read the *Acts* of the <term>Seventh Council</term> <note>The Second Council of Nicaea, held in 787 AD.</note>, at the sessions of which the <term>iconoclasts</term> <note>Those who opposed the use of religious images (icons) in worship.</note> were defeated and the orthodox faith shone with increased brightness.

->XXI<-

Read the treatise by <term>John Philoponus</term> ^2 titled *On the Resurrection*, which consists of several volumes. In this work, while rejecting the doctrine of the physical Resurrection of the body, he makes many ill-considered claims. He also ridicules our blessed and holy Church Fathers.

->XXII<-

Read the detailed *Refutation* by Theodosius the Monk ^3 of the passages John Philoponus cited as arguments against the Resurrection. This work includes quotations from Holy Scripture and the Fathers to disprove John’s pointless efforts.

->XXIII<-

Read the harsh criticisms written by Conon ^4, Eugenius ^5, and Themistius ^6 against the treatise of John Philoponus, in which they publicly expose his worthless labors. They also launch fierce personal attacks against him, describing him as a man entirely cut off from the Christian faith. However, they do agree with him in refusing to accept the decisions of the <term>Council of Chalcedon</term> <note>A major church council held in 451 AD that defined Christ as having two natures, divine and human.</note>.

---

^1 The Second Council of Nicaea (787 AD).

^2 John Philoponus of Alexandria was a theologian, grammarian, and philosopher who lived during the first half of the sixth century. His main work (of which significant fragments remain) was called *The Arbitrator*. It was an attempt to find a middle ground between <term>Monophysitism</term> <note>The belief that Christ had only one nature, rather than two.</note> and <term>Tritheism</term> <note>The heretical belief that the Trinity consists of three separate gods rather than one.</note>. Photius (Codes 55 and 75) mentions two other theological treatises by him. *On the Resurrection* is now lost, though some fragments of his notes on Aristotle are preserved. He was nicknamed "Philoponus" (lover of labor) due to his great industriousness, though his opponents mockingly called him "Mataioponus" <note>original: "Mataioponus"; meaning "one who works in vain."</note>.

^3 A monk from the fifth century, about whom nothing else is known.

^4 Bishop of Tarsus (around 600 AD).

^5 A bishop from Cilicia in the second half of the sixth century.

^6 A deacon of Alexandria (sixth century) and leader of the <term>Agnoetae</term> <note>A sect that believed Christ’s human soul was limited in knowledge and "ignorant" of certain things.</note>. They held that the human soul of Christ was like that of any other man in all things, including his "ignorance" or limited knowledge.

<meta>This page continues the catalog of theological works and ecclesiastical debates, specifically discussing the aftermath of the John Philoponus controversy, the homilies of John Chrysostom, and the writings of Synesius of Cyrene.</meta>

<page-num>26</page-num>

### XXIV

Read a volume containing the *Acts* <note>original: "Acts," referring to the official records or proceedings of a legal or ecclesiastical debate.</note> of a debate held before John III Scholasticus <note>John III (served 565–577) was the bishop of Constantinople, often called the "queen city." He was a lawyer by training and compiled a *Nomocanon*, or a digest of church laws.</note>, the bishop of the imperial city, during the reign of Emperor Justin II. In this debate, Conon and Eugenius the Tritheists <note>Tritheists were a sect who argued that the three persons of the Trinity were three distinct gods or substances.</note> argued on one side, while Paul the Black <note>The "Jacobite" patriarch of Antioch from about 550–578.</note> and Stephen <note>The "Jacobite" bishop of Cyprus.</note>, representing the Hesitators <note>original: "Hesitators" refers to the *Diakrinomenoi*, those who hesitated to either fully accept or fully reject the decisions of the Council of Chalcedon.</note>, argued on the other. 

Conon and Eugenius appear to have supported John Philoponus; when Paul and Stephen demanded that they should denounce <note>original: "anathematize"</note> Philoponus, they refused to do so. On the other hand, they presented arguments to prove that Philoponus's views were in agreement with those of Severus <note>The Monophysite patriarch of Antioch from 512–519.</note> and Theodosius, who were their own teachers. In many respects, they actually uphold orthodox views—specifically that the Trinity is of the same substance <note>original: "consubstantial"</note> and the same nature, and that there is only one God and one Godhead. However, they are guilty of blasphemy because they assert that the Father, the Son, and the Holy Spirit are "partial substances" <note>Meaning they viewed the Trinity as being composed of individual parts or separate divinities rather than one unified essence.</note> and have their own unique divinities and natures. In this, they contradict both themselves and the truth. Many of their other opinions are equally foolish and nonsensical.

### XXV

Read the work by John Chrysostom <note>John "the Golden-mouthed" (347–407) was the famous Bishop of Constantinople known for his powerful preaching.</note> entitled *Notes on Death*, which consists of twenty-two short homilies. In the same volume, there are also twenty-two homilies on the *Ascension of the Lord* and seventeen on *Pentecost*.

### XXVI

Read the orations of Synesius <note>Synesius (c. 373–414) was a Neo-Platonist philosopher and the bishop of Ptolemais in Cyrenaica, located in modern-day Libya.</note>, the bishop of Cyrene, titled *On Providence* <note>Also known as the *Egyptian Discourses*, this is a historical romance using the myths of Osiris and Typhon to comment on the politics of his own time.</note>, *On the Kingdom* <note>This speech was originally delivered at Constantinople before Emperor Arcadius.</note>, and some other subjects. His

<meta>This page continues the assessment of late antique Christian writers, specifically concluding the discussion of Synesius of Cyrene and moving on to the foundational ecclesiastical historians Eusebius of Caesarea and Socrates Scholasticus.</meta>

<page-num>27</page-num>

<header>SOCRATES</header>

His style is lofty and dignified, <note>original Greek: *ogkos*, which can also mean "massive" or "weighty."</note> but somewhat inclined to be overly poetical.

I also read various letters of his, which radiate grace and charm while remaining vigorous and full of shrewd <note>original: "closely-packed"</note> ideas.

He originally belonged to a pagan <note>original: "heathen"</note> school of philosophy but was favorably disposed to the inspired truths of Christianity. He was ready to accept all its doctrines except for that of the Resurrection. Despite this stance, he was admitted into our Church and even raised to the office of bishop <note>original: "episcopate"</note> because of his goodness and purity, and in the conviction that a man of such holiness could not fail to be illuminated by the light of the Resurrection. These hopes were not disappointed; as soon as he became a bishop, he readily accepted that doctrine. Synesius was a distinguished figure in Cyrene <note>An ancient Greek and Roman city in modern-day Libya.</note> during the time when Theophilus was the patriarch of Alexandria <note>Theophilus served from 385–412 AD.</note>.

## XXVII

Read the ten books of Eusebius's *Ecclesiastical History*. Beginning from the birth of Christ, our true God, it carefully describes the era of the tyrants <note>Refers to the Roman emperors who persecuted Christians before the Edict of Milan.</note> and ends with the reign of Constantine the Great. It provides a more detailed account of the Church institutions established by Constantine during his reign.

## XXVIII

Read Socrates’s <note>Socrates of Constantinople (c. 380–439 AD), also known as Socrates Scholasticus, was originally a lawyer.</note> *Ecclesiastical History*, which is a continuation of the work by Eusebius. Beginning with the reign of Constantine, it continues down to the time of Theodosius the Younger <note>Emperor Theodosius II, who reigned from 408–450 AD.</note>. 

The writer had been instructed in "grammar" <note>original: *grammatikē*. In the ancient world, this term referred to a general literary education, including the study of poetry, rather than just sentence structure.</note> even as a boy by pagan tutors. He had attended the lectures of Ammonius and Helladius, grammarians from Alexandria who had been banished from their native country for rebellion <note>original: "sedition"</note> and carried on their profession at Constantinople. This work covers the events of 140 years, and the entire history is included...

<keywords>Synesius of Cyrene, Eusebius of Caesarea, Socrates Scholasticus, Ecclesiastical History, Resurrection, Theophilus of Alexandria, Constantine the Great, Theodosius II, Late Antiquity, Church history</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the assessment of Socrates Scholasticus’s history and moves through other major ecclesiastical historians of Late Antiquity, including Evagrius, Sozomen, and Theodoret.</meta>

<page-num>28</page-num>

<header>THEODORET</header>

divided into seven books. There is nothing remarkable about the author's style, and he is not very accurate in matters of doctrine.

### XXIX

Read the *Ecclesiastical History* of Evagrius Scholasticus <note>Evagrius lived approximately 536–600 AD. He was a "Scholasticus," a title for a professional lawyer or advocate.</note>, a former prefect born at Epiphania in Coele-Syria, in six books. The work begins where the histories of Socrates and Theodoret end, and continues down to the twelfth year of the reign of the emperor Maurice <note>This covers the period from 431 to 593 AD.</note>. The style is not without charm, although it is somewhat diffuse <note>original: "diffuse," meaning wordy or verbose.</note>; regarding matters of doctrine, he is certainly more trustworthy than other historians. The work also contains some passages concerning sacred images <note>This likely refers to early debates over icons, which became a major controversy in the later Byzantine Empire.</note>.

### XXX

Read the *Ecclesiastical History* of Salamanus Hermeias Sozomen <note>He was born near Gaza in Palestine and later settled in Constantinople (c. 400–450 AD).</note>, in nine books. Dedicated to the emperor Theodosius the Younger, it begins with the consulship of Crispus and his father Constantine, and goes down to the reign of Theodosius the Younger <note>The work originally covered the period from 324 to 439 AD, but the history of 425–439 is missing.</note>. Sozomen was at one time a lawyer in Constantinople. His style is better than that of Socrates, though he differs from him in certain details.

### XXXI

Read the *Ecclesiastical History* of Theodoret <note>Refer to Codex XVII for earlier notes on this author.</note>. Of all the writers mentioned, his style is best suited for history. It is generally clear, dignified, and free from redundancies, although he sometimes employs metaphors that are too bold, almost insipid <note>In this context, "insipid" suggests metaphors that are flat or lack proper taste.</note>. He gives a fuller account of the proceedings of the second council <note>The First Council of Constantinople, held in 381 AD.</note> than other historians, who give it only a brief mention, as if they were unwilling to say much about it. However, even he does not give all the details. He begins his *History* with the heresy of Arius and goes down to the reign of Theodosius the Younger, and the

### XXXII

Read various letters by Athanasius <note>Athanasius (c. 296–373 AD) was the Patriarch of Alexandria and the primary defender of Trinitarian orthodoxy against Arianism.</note>, some of which contain a type of *Apology* <note>In this context, an "apology" is a formal written defense of one's conduct or beliefs.</note> for his flight <note>Athanasius was accused of cowardice by his Arian opponents for fleeing into the desert to avoid arrest.</note>. The style is elegant, brilliant, and clear, full of grace and persuasiveness. It is a pleasure to listen to the *Apology*.

### XXXIII

Read the *Chronicle* of Justus of Tiberias <note>A Jewish historian and contemporary of Josephus. His works are now lost, making this summary by Photius historically significant.</note>, titled *A Chronicle of the Kings of the Jews in the form of a genealogy, by Justus of Tiberias.* <note>original Greek: βασιλέων τῶν ἐν τοῖς στέμμασι (*basileōn tōn en tois stemmasi*). While often translated as "crowned kings," the word *stemma* here likely refers to a genealogical family tree.</note> He was from Tiberias in Galilee, the city from which he took his name. He begins his history with Moses and continues it down to the death of the seventh Agrippa <note>This refers to Herod Agrippa II, the king mentioned in the New Testament (Acts 25–26) before whom the Apostle Paul made his defense.</note> from the family of Herod, who was the last of the Jewish kings. His kingdom, which was granted to him by the Emperor Claudius, was expanded by Nero, and even more so by Vespasian. He died in the third year of the reign of Trajan <note>This date (approximately 100 AD) is debated by historians because Josephus’s autobiography, which mentions Justus, was likely published earlier.</note>, which is where the history ends. 

Justus’s style is very brief, and he omits a great deal of information that is of the utmost importance. Sharing in what Photius considers a common bias of the Jewish people to whom he belonged, Justus does not even mention the coming of Christ, the events of His life, or the miracles He performed. 

His father was a Jew named Pistus. Justus himself, according to Josephus <note>Flavius Josephus (c. 37–100 AD), the famous Jewish-Roman historian who was a bitter rival of Justus.</note>, was one of the most corrupt of men, a slave to vice and greed. He was a political opponent of Josephus, against whom he is said to have organized several plots; but Josephus, although on...

<header>JULIUS AFRICANUS</header>

...on several occasions he had his enemy in his power, yet he only scolded him with words and let him go free. It is said that the history he wrote is largely fictional, especially where he describes the Jewish-Roman War and the fall of Jerusalem.

### XXXIV

Read the *History* of Africanus <note>Julius Africanus (c. 160 – c. 240 AD) was a Christian traveler and historian often called the "father of Christian chronography."</note>, who was also the author of the *Cesti* in fourteen books <note>The *Cesti*—meaning "embroidered girdles"—was a collection of miscellaneous notes on subjects ranging from agriculture to magic. While Photius mentions fourteen books, other historical sources suggest there were twenty-four.</note>. Although his style is brief, he omits nothing worth recording. He begins with the Mosaic account of creation <note>This refers to the "Mosaic cosmogony," the creation of the world as described by Moses in the Book of Genesis.</note> and continues down to the coming of Christ. He also gives a brief summary of events from that time until the reign of Macrinus <note>Roman Emperor from 217 to 218 AD.</note>. At that date, as he tells us, the *Chronicle* was finished—specifically, in the 5,723rd year of the world. The work consists of five volumes.

Africanus also wrote a letter to Origen <note>Origen of Alexandria (c. 184 – c. 253 AD) was one of the most influential early Christian theologians.</note> arguing against the authenticity of the history of Susannah <note>Susannah is a story found in the Book of Daniel in Catholic and Orthodox Bibles, but categorized as Apocrypha by Protestants and not included in the Hebrew Bible.</note>. He argued (among other reasons) that it is not included in the Jewish scriptures and that the play on words—from "mastic tree" to "cleave" and from "holm-oak" to "saw" <note>original: *apo tou prinou prisai . . . apo tou schinou schisai*. Africanus noted that these puns only work in Greek, which suggested the story was originally written in Greek rather than Hebrew.</note>—is inconsistent with the genuine Hebrew style. Origen replied and rejected these objections.

Africanus also wrote a letter to Aristides <note>Very little is known about this Aristides.</note>, in which he demonstrated that there was actually no real contradiction between the genealogies of our Savior in the Gospels of Matthew and Luke, despite what was commonly believed.

---

¹ Julius Africanus (c. 170–240 AD) was a Christian historical writer. He was born in Jerusalem (not in Africa) and later lived in Emmaus-Nicopolis in Palestine. He was the author of a *Chronicle*, a history of the world from the Creation to 221 AD; the *Cesti*, a collection of notes on various subjects; a letter to Aristides regarding the genealogies of Jesus in Matthew and Luke; and a letter to Origen to show that the *History of Susannah* in the Apocrypha is a later addition from a Greek original. This last letter has been preserved in full, while only fragments remain of the first three. According to his system of chronology, known as the Alexandrian era, there were 5,499 years between the Creation and the birth of Christ, which he dated three years earlier than our modern calendar.

² The correct number of books is twenty-four.

³ Roman Emperor from 217–218 AD. However, an excerpt found in the writings of George Syncellus <note>A Byzantine chronicler and church official who died after 810 AD.</note> shows that the *Chronicle* actually extended a little further in time.

<header>PHILIP OF SIDE</header>

### XXXV

Read the work of Philip of Side <note>Philip of Side in Pamphylia (fifth century) was a priest in Constantinople and a friend of the famous preacher John Chrysostom.</note>, titled *Christian History*, which begins with the words, "In the beginning God created the heavens and the earth." He provides an account of the history of the time of Moses <note>original: Mosaic history</note>, which is sometimes brief and sometimes detailed, though excessively verbose throughout. This first book contains twenty-four volumes, similar to the twenty-three other books we have examined so far <note>The work originally contained thirty-six books and nearly one thousand volumes.</note>. His language is repetitive, lacking refinement or elegance; it quickly becomes tiresome or even outright repulsive. His goal seems to be to show off his own knowledge rather than to benefit the reader. Most of the content has nothing to do with history, and the work might be better called a treatise on all kinds of subjects rather than a history—it is a tasteless outpouring. Philip was a contemporary of Sisinnius and Proclus, archbishops <note>original: patriarchs</note> of Constantinople. In his history, he frequently attacks Sisinnius because, although both held the same rank <note>Both were priests (presbyters).</note> and Philip was considered the more eloquent speaker, Sisinnius was chosen for the archbishopric instead of him.

He traveled to Arabia and East Africa, though it is debated if he actually reached India.</note>, who lived during the reign of Emperor Justin, dedicated the work to a man named Pamphilus. The book begins by defending certain church doctrines <note>original: ecclesiastical dogmas</note> using evidence drawn from the Scriptures. The style is poor, and...

<page-num>32</page-num>

<header>COSMAS INDICOPLEUSTES</header>

The arrangement of the work is hardly up to the usual standard. He relates many stories that are unbelievable from a historical perspective, so he may be fairly regarded as a teller of fables rather than a reliable authority. The views he emphasizes most are: that neither the sky nor the earth is a sphere, but rather the sky is a kind of vault and the earth is a rectangular plane (twice as long as it is wide), with the edges of the sky joined to the edges of the earth. He also claims that all the stars are kept in motion by the help of angels, among other similar ideas. 

He also mentions the books of *Genesis* and *Exodus* as if by way of a side note, and enters into a lengthy discussion and theories about the Tabernacle <note>The portable dwelling place of God's presence in the Hebrew Bible.</note>. The prophets and apostles are only briefly mentioned. He claims that the sun is only twice as large as two "climates" <margin>¹ Before the earth was understood to be a sphere, people thought its surface "sloped" <note>original: κλίνω (*klino*)</note> from south to north, and this slope was called a "climate" <note>original: κλίμα (*klima*)</note>. Later, the word was used for belts or zones of the earth's surface, and then for the temperature of those zones. According to Cosmas, the two "climates" were the regions between Alexandria and Rhodes, and between Rhodes and Constantinople (a distance of about 635 miles).</margin>; that the angels do not live in heaven but above the firmament <term>The "heavens" or the sky, traditionally viewed as a solid dome.</term> and interact with us; that Christ, during his Ascension, entered the space between the sky and the firmament, and that only this space is the kingdom of heaven; along with similar absurdities. 

He dedicates the first six books to a man named Pamphilus. Of the remaining six (there are twelve in total), he dedicates the seventh to Anastasius, in which he argues that the heavens are indestructible. The eighth book, which concerns the song of Hezekiah <margin>² Isaiah 38.</margin> and the sun moving backward <note>A biblical event where the sun's shadow moved backward on a sundial as a sign to King Hezekiah.</note>, is dedicated to a certain Peter. In this book, he also mentions that he has written a commentary on the *Song of Songs*. The four remaining books have no dedication.

### XXXVII

Read an essay *On Government* <margin>³ There seems no reason to identify this with the treatise *On the Civil Constitution* <note>original: Περὶ πολιτικῆς καταστάσεως</note> by Peter the Patrician, a sixth-century Byzantine historian. The treatise consists of six books which discuss a form of government called just rule <note>original: δικαιαρχικόν (*dikaiarchikon*)</note>, which differs from those

...proposed by ancient writers. The *Republic* of Plato is rightly criticized. The participants in the dialogue argue that the constitution they propose should be a combination of the three forms of government—monarchy, aristocracy, and democracy. Each of these is meant to contribute its best and most authentic qualities to the formation of the ideal state.

### XXXVIII

Read the work of Theodore of Antioch <note>See Codex IV; Photius refers to his entries as "Codices."</note> titled *A Commentary on Genesis* (the history of the Creation). The first book of this work consists of seven volumes. The style is neither polished nor very clear. The author avoids the use of allegory as much as possible, focusing instead on literal historical interpretation. He repeats himself frequently, which leaves a negative impression on the reader. Even though he lived before the heretic Nestorius, he expresses <note>original: "vomits up"</note> Nestorian doctrines in advance. This is the same Theodore of Mopsuestia <note>A influential but controversial theologian (c. 350–428 AD).</note> from whom John Philoponus (as Philoponus himself mentions) demanded a serious explanation regarding his method of interpretation in his own work on the Creation.

### XXXIX

Read the short refutation by Eusebius Pamphili <note>Eusebius of Caesarea (c. 260–339 AD), the "Father of Church History." The name "Pamphili" means "son of [his mentor] Pamphilus."</note> of the discourse by Hierocles <note>Hierocles of Caria was a Roman proconsul and a fierce opponent of Christianity under Emperor Diocletian. His work, *Truth-loving Words to the Christians*, argued that Apollonius of Tyana was superior to Christ.</note> written in support of Apollonius of Tyana <note>Apollonius (c. 4 BC – 97 AD) was a Greek philosopher and reputed miracle-worker.</note>.

### XL

Read the so-called *Ecclesiastical History* by Philostorgius the Arian <note>Born c. 368 AD. He was a follower of Eunomius, who held the radical Arian view that the Son was "unlike" the Father.</note>. The spirit of this work is different from nearly all other church historians. He praises all Arians but mocks and insults all the orthodox believers, so that his work is not so...

<meta>This page continues the "Bibliotheca" or "Myriobiblos" by Photius, a 9th-century Patriarch of Constantinople. Having finished discussing Eusebius's critique of Hierocles, Photius now begins Codex 40, an evaluation of the "Church History" written by Philostorgius.</meta>

### XL

<note>original title: Philostorgius's Church History</note>

...as much a history as a speech of praise <note>original: "panegyric"</note> for heretics, and nothing but an open attack on the orthodox faith. His style is elegant, and his choice of words is often poetic, though not so much that it becomes boring or unpleasant. His use of metaphors <note>original: "figurative use of words"</note> is very expressive and makes the work both pleasant and enjoyable to read. Sometimes, however, these metaphors are too bold and forced, giving the impression of being cold and poorly timed. The language is decorated in various ways—even excessively—so that the reader eventually finds themselves lost in an unpleasant confusion <note>original: "disagreeable obscurity"</note>. In many places, the author includes his own relevant moral insights.

He begins with the devotion of Arius <note>Arius (c. 256–336 AD) was the priest whose teachings led to Arianism, the belief that Jesus Christ was a created being rather than co-eternal with God the Father.</note> to the heresy and its origins, and ends with the recall of the unholy Aëtius. <margin>Aëtius of Antioch, founder of an extreme Arian sect, died at Constantinople in 367 AD. He was exiled by Emperor Constantius but recalled by Julian the Apostate.</margin> This Aëtius was dismissed from his position by his fellow heretics because he surpassed even them in wickedness, as Philostorgius himself reluctantly admits. He was later recalled and welcomed by the unholy Julian <note>Emperor Julian "the Apostate" (reigned 361–363 AD), who attempted to revive Roman paganism.</note>. The history, contained in one book and six volumes, continues down to this period. 

The author is a liar, and his narrative is often fictional. He primarily praises Aëtius and Eunomius for their learning, claiming they alone restored the doctrines of faith that had been buried by time; in this, he shows himself to be a shameless liar. He also praises Eusebius of Nicomedia <margin>Eusebius of Nicomedia (died 342 AD) was the Patriarch of Constantinople, a major supporter of Arianism, and a fierce rival of Saint Athanasius.</margin> (whom he calls "the Great"), Theophilus the Indian, <margin>Theophilus was a bishop of the Church in Ethiopia around 358 AD. Born on the island of Diu (India), he was taken to Constantinople as a hostage where he became an Arian Christian.</margin> and several others for their lives and miraculous works. 

He severely attacks Acacius, the Bishop of Caesarea <margin>Acacius, nicknamed "the One-Eyed," succeeded his mentor Eusebius of Caesarea as bishop in 340 AD and died in 365 AD.</margin> in Palestine, for his extreme harshness and unbeatable cunning. In this, Philostorgius claims Acacius surpassed all his fellow heretics—despite their mutual hatred—as well as those who held different religious beliefs.

This was as far as we had read. Soon after, six other books were discovered in another volume, making it clear that the entire work consists of twelve books. The initial letters of each...

<keywords>Philostorgius, Arianism, Church History, Photius, Aëtius, Julian the Apostate, Eunomius, Eusebius of Nicomedia, Acacius of Caesarea, Byzantine literature</keywords>

<meta>This page continues Photius’s review of ecclesiastical (church) historians. Having just critiqued Philostorgius's writing style, he now concludes his review of Philostorgius and moves on to the histories written by John of Aegae and Basil of Cilicia.</meta>

The books are arranged so that they form an acrostic of the author’s name. The work continues down to the time of Theodosius the Younger <note>Theodosius II, reigned 402–450 AD</note>. After the death of Honorius, Theodosius transferred the throne of the Western Empire to his cousin Valentinian the Younger <note>Valentinian III, reigned 425–455 AD</note>, the son of Constantius and Placidia.

Despite his hostility toward the orthodox, Philostorgius does not dare to attack Gregory the Theologian <note>original: Gregory of Nazianzus (329–389 AD), a prominent defender of the Trinity</note>, but instead reluctantly accepts his doctrines. His attempt to slander Basil the Great <note>Basil of Caesarea (330–379 AD), a leading figure in early Christianity</note> only had the effect of increasing Basil's reputation. Philostorgius was forced to admit the power and beauty of Basil's sermons based on his actual knowledge of them, although he hesitantly calls Basil overconfident and unskilled in debate <note>original: "overbold and inexperienced in controversy"</note> because he dared to challenge the writings of Eunomius <note>An Arian leader who argued that Jesus was not of the same substance as God the Father</note>.

### XLI

Read the *Church History* by a certain John <note>John was a priest (presbyter) of Aegae in Cilicia who lived during the late 5th century</note>. It begins with the reign of Theodosius the Younger, the heresy of Nestorius <note>Nestorius taught that Christ's human and divine natures were distinct persons</note> and his removal from office <note>original: "deposition"</note>. It continues down to the time of Emperor Zeno and the removal of Peter the heretic <note>Peter the Fuller, the Patriarch of Antioch who was deposed for his non-orthodox views</note>, who had seized the bishopric <note>original: "see"</note> of Antioch.

The style is clear but quite ornate <note>original: "florid"</note>. The author describes in detail the Third Ecumenical Council held at Ephesus <note>431 AD</note>, as well as another council held in the same place—the "Robber Council" <note>449 AD; originally called the "Latrocinium" because it was marked by violence and procedural corruption</note>. He glorifies this council along with its president, Dioscorus, and his companions. He also gives a malicious <note>original: "slanderous"</note> account of the Council of Chalcedon <note>The 451 AD council which rejected the "Robber Council" and defined the two natures of Christ</note>. This leads to the conclusion that the author is John, a priest from Aegae and a heretic who wrote a specific attack on the Council of Chalcedon. According to his own statement, the history is in ten books. I have only read five, which contain—as previously mentioned—a record of events from the emergence of the Nestorian heresy to the removal of Peter the heretic.

### XLII

Read the *Church History* of Basil of Cilicia <note>Basil was a priest of Antioch and later became the Bishop of Irenopolis in Cilicia around 500 AD</note>. It

<summary>Photius concludes his review of Philostorgius, noting his reluctant respect for Gregory and Basil, then critiques John of Aegae’s history for its heretical bias and support of the "Robber Council."</summary>
<keywords>Theodosius II, Philostorgius, Gregory of Nazianzus, Basil the Great, John of Aegae, Nestorius, Peter the Fuller, Council of Ephesus, Robber Council, Council of Chalcedon, Basil of Cilicia</keywords>

--- Page 42 ---
<meta>This page continues Photius’s review of John of Aegae's church history (Codex 41), evaluating its content and style before moving on to works by John Philoponus and Philostratus.</meta>

The history begins with the death of Simplicius, the bishop of Rome <note>reigned 468–483 AD</note>, who wrote to Acacius of Constantinople <note>Patriarch of Constantinople, 471–489 AD</note> warning him to have no dealings with Peter, nicknamed "the Stammerer" <note>original: Mongus; Peter was the Monophysite patriarch of Alexandria in 482 AD</note>. At that time, Peter was corrupting Alexandria by publicly and formally condemning <note>original: anathematizing</note> the holy Council of Chalcedon in church. It was because of his association with Peter that Acacius was eventually removed from his position as bishop <note>original: see</note>. Although Acacius was at first rightly angry with Peter, he later showed no opposition to Peter's doctrines and was therefore suspected of being a heretic.

This matter resurfaced during the reign of Emperor Zeno. The history begins at this point and continues until the death of Emperor Anastasius, after he had reigned for twenty-seven years and three months, when Justin the Thracian was announced as his successor <note>The work therefore covers the period from 450–527 AD</note>. This current book ends around this time, covering the era from the reign of Zeno to the death of Anastasius and the proclamation of Justin as emperor. The author also states that he wrote two other books, the first and the third. The first book begins with the reign of Marcian and ends with Zeno (where the second book starts), while the third continues the narrative of the second, beginning with the reign of Justin.

The author's style is quite careless <note>original: slovenly</note> and inconsistent. He also includes a large amount of correspondence between bishops, which he claims is to provide evidence for what he writes. However, these letters greatly increase the size of the book while containing very little actual history, which is often buried under a mass of wordiness <note>original: verbiage</note>. The clarity of the story is also ruined by the frequent use of interruptions <note>original: parentheses</note>.

### XLIII

Read the treatise of John Philoponus on *The Six Days of Creation* <note>original: Hexaëmeron</note>. In this work, his style is pure and clear, and better than his usual writing. He generally agrees with Basil the Great, but he constantly opposes Theodore of Mopsuestia, who wrote his own *Interpretation of Genesis* on the same subject. Philoponus attempts to disprove Theodore's work in this treatise.

### XLIV

Read the eight books of the *Life of Apollonius of Tyana* by <note>The text likely refers to Philostratus, the 3rd-century author of this famous biography of the Neo-Pythagorean philosopher</note>

# PHILOSTRATUS OF TYRE

Philostratus ¹ of Tyre.² His style is clear, pleasant, concise, and full of charm, due to his love for both old-fashioned words <term>archaisms</term> and innovative sentence structures.

He tells us that Apollonius visited the Indians, whom he calls Brahmins, from whom he learned much of their divine wisdom. He also visited the wise men of Ethiopia, whom he calls the *Gymni* ³ <note>original: *Gymni*, "the naked ones"; a reference to the Gymnosophists</note>, because they spend their entire lives naked and never wear clothes, even in the most difficult weather. However, he declares that the wise men of India are far superior to those of Ethiopia, since their traditions are older and their minds are clearer and sharper, because they live closer to the sun's rays.

He does not, however, claim that Apollonius performed any miracles <note>original: wonders</note> such as legend attributes to him; he merely praises him for leading a philosophical and disciplined life, in which he demonstrates the teachings of Pythagoras in both his behavior and his beliefs. Various accounts are given of his death, the details of which are unclear, just as he himself intended. During his lifetime, he was in the habit of saying that a wise man should keep his life a secret from others, or, if he could not, should at least keep his death a secret.⁴ <note>original Greek: *Lathe biōsas, ei de mē, Lathe apothanōn*. This is an Epicurean maxim meaning, "Live in secret; or if not, die in secret."</note> The location of his grave is unknown.

Philostratus states that Apollonius had a great contempt for wealth; he gave away everything he owned to his brother and others, and could never be persuaded to accept money from rulers,⁵ even though they urged it upon him, believing he deserved it. He claims that Apollonius foresaw the famine at Ephesus long before it happened and stopped it once it broke out. He once saw a certain lion, which he declared contained the soul of Amasis, king of the Egyptians,⁶ which had entered the body of the animal as a

...punishment for the crimes Amasis had committed during his lifetime. He also exposed an Empusa <note>original: *Empusa*; a shape-shifting hobgoblin or ghoul in Greek mythology, said to devour human beings</note>, who, disguised as a prostitute <note>original: courtesan</note>, pretended to be in love with Menippus <note>original: *Menippus*; a student of Apollonius from Lycia</note>. He brought back to life a Roman girl who had apparently just died, and he freed his own limbs from his shackles <term>fetters</term> while he was tied up in prison. Before Emperor Domitian, he defended himself and praised <term>extolled</term> Nerva (Domitian’s successor). After this, he vanished from the court and joined Demetrius <note>original: *Demetrius*; a Cynic philosopher who taught in Rome and was eventually exiled for his outspoken criticism of the emperors</note> and Damis <note>original: *Damis*; a pupil from Nineveh who allegedly wrote the journals that served as Philostratus's primary source</note> as they had planned. He arrived in just a few moments, even though they were several days’ journey apart. 

Such are the fictional tales of Philostratus concerning Apollonius. Philostratus denies, however, that Apollonius was a sorcerer or miracle-worker, even if he did perform some of the wonders usually attributed to him. Instead, he claims these acts were the result of his philosophy and the purity of his life. On the contrary, he argues that Apollonius was an enemy of magicians and sorcerers and certainly not a practitioner of magic.

Everything he says about the people of India is a collection <term>tissue</term> of absurd and unbelievable statements. He claims they have certain jars full of rain and wind; in times of drought, they can use these to water the land, and they can remove the moisture once the rain has fallen, because these casks allow them to control the alternating supply of wind and rain. He tells similar stories that are equally foolish and ridiculous <term>preposterous</term>, and reading these eight books is a waste of study and effort.

# THEODORET

He was himself a devoted student of philosophy in his character, his outlook, and his writing style. In terms of religion, he was a Christian.

## XLVI

I read twenty-seven books written by Theodoret, the Bishop of Cyrrhus <note>Cyrrhus was an ancient city in Syria; Theodoret was a key figure in 5th-century Christian theology</note>, which argue against various heretical <term>heretical: opinions that go against established church doctrine</term> claims. <note>original: These books have been variously attributed to Theodoret, Athanasius, or Eutherius, the bishop of Tyana.</note>

The **first book** is directed against those who claim that "God the Word" had only one nature and that He only began to exist through the lineage <note>original: "seed"</note> of David; it also argues against those who believe the divine nature of God can suffer pain or change <note>original: "attribute passions to the Godhead"</note>. 

In the **second book**, he supports his arguments more heavily by using evidence from Holy Scripture. <note>original: *graphikōteron* (γραφικώτερον); this Greek term could mean "more scripturally," or it might mean "more vividly" or "more descriptively."</note> 

The **third book** deals with the same subject. 

The **fourth book** contains the teachings of the holy Church Fathers regarding the glorious Incarnation <note>original: *Oikonomia* (Οἰκονομία), referring to the "divine arrangement" or the plan of God becoming human</note> of our Lord Jesus Christ, the Son of God. 

The **fifth book** contains a collection of the opinions held by heretics; these are compared with the views of those who refuse to acknowledge two natures in Christ (the divine and the human) and are shown to be closely related. 

The **sixth book** clearly states that there is only one Son, our Lord Jesus Christ. 

The **seventh book** is written in the form of a letter that completes the first book. 

The **eighth book** is written against those who judge what is true only by following the opinion of the crowd. 

The **ninth book** is against those who claim we should neither look for logical arguments nor quote from the Scriptures, but should instead be satisfied with blind faith. 

The **tenth book** is against those who spitefully use the argument that "the Word was made flesh" to support their errors. 

The **eleventh book** is against those who forbid us from recognizing two natures in the Incarnation. 

The **twelfth book** is against those who claim that anyone who says the Word is one thing and the flesh is another is implying there are two separate Sons of God. 

The **thirteenth book** is against those who say that to view Christ as a man is to put one's hope in a mere human. 

The **fourteenth book** is against those who use the contradictory phrase, "He suffered without suffering." 

The **fifteenth book** is against those who say, "He suffered only because he willed to," <note>This refers to the debate over whether Christ's suffering was real or merely a choice that didn't affect his divine nature</note>. 

The **sixteenth book** is against those who argue that we should simply accept religious words without considering what they actually mean, claiming the meaning is beyond all human understanding. 

The **seventeenth book** is against those who say, "The Word suffered..."

<summary>Photius summarizes the first seventeen books of a polemical work by Theodoret of Cyrrhus, focusing on the defense of the dual nature of Christ (divine and human) against various heretical groups.</summary>
