...in the flesh.” The **eighteenth book** is directed against those who ask what punishment the Jews would have suffered if they had not crucified God. The **nineteenth** is against those who declare that anyone who does not believe that God was crucified is essentially a Jew. The **twentieth** is against those who claim that the angels who ate with Abraham did not truly take on a physical human nature. The **twenty-first** is against those who undermine <term>depreciate: to lower the value or importance of</term> each of the miracles by denying Christ's physical body. The **twenty-second** is against those who insult our human race by denying that the Savior began his life with our same nature. The **twenty-third** is against those who command us to simply believe whatever is said, without considering whether it is appropriate or inappropriate <note>original: "seemly or unseemly"</note>. The **twenty-fourth** is against those who reject the distinction between Christ's two natures (divine and human) after his suffering <term>Passion: the suffering and death of Jesus</term> and his Ascent into heaven. The **twenty-fifth** is a summary of everything that has already been stated in detail. The **twenty-sixth** deals with the later explained formation or shared substance <term>consubstantiation: the theological concept of different substances—like the divine and human—existing together as one</term>; the **twenty-seventh** deals with the example of an ordinary man as it is applied to Christ. The subject of each book is enough to show which of them support the orthodox faith and which contradict it.

In the same volume, I also read three larger works than those previously mentioned, entitled *Eranistes* (The Beggar) or *Polymorphos* (The Many-Shaped). <gloss>1. This work is written as a dialogue between a representative of the established church (Theodoret) and a representative of Monophysitism—the belief that Christ had only one nature. This opponent is compared to a "beggar" because he gathers scraps of various old ideas from earlier heretical writings.</gloss> The first part teaches that the Word of God is unchangeable; the second, that the union of divine and human is unmixed; the third, that the "God-Word" is incapable of suffering <term>impassible: not subject to pain, change, or physical suffering</term>. In a fourth book, these statements are supported by logical arguments. These first three books were composed by him as a dialogue, but the rest are written in continuous prose. The style is clear, distinct, and pure; it is not lacking in charm, and the works are full of appropriate insights.

# JOSEPHUS 41

The capture of other Jewish fortresses is described, and in the last book, the destruction of Jerusalem and the fortress of Masada <note>original: "Masada" — a Judaean stronghold, modern-day Sebbeh</note>. The work consists of seven books. The author has a pure style and is skillful at expressing his meaning with dignity, clarity, and charm. In the speeches he includes, he is persuasive and pleasant, even when the situation requires him to present opposing views. He is clever and productive in using arguments for either side and is extremely fond of aphorisms <term>aphorisms: short, pithy statements expressing a general truth</term>. He is also very skilled at introducing emotion, as well as stirring and calming the passions of his readers.

He relates that many signs and omens <note>original: "portents"</note> preceded the capture of Jerusalem. A heifer being led to sacrifice gave birth to a lamb; a light shone in the temple, and a voice was heard saying, "Let us depart from here" <note>original: "Metabainomen enteuthen"</note>; the temple gates, which twenty men could hardly open, swung open on their own; and in the evening, troops appeared in the sky clad in armor. A man named Jesus, son of Ananias, for six years and three months incessantly repeated—as if he were inspired—the words, "Woe, woe to Jerusalem!" When he was whipped for this, he made no reply but simply repeated the same words. He was present during the capture of the city, and while crying out, "Woe, woe to the city!" he was hit by a stone from one of the enemy's siege engines <term>engines: mechanical weapons like catapults</term> and died <note>original: "gave up the ghost"</note>.

Such were the signs that predicted the city’s capture, but it was internal rebellion <note>original: "sedition"</note>, along with the enemy army, that overthrew it. Divided into the factions of the Zealots and the Sicarii <note>original: "Zelotae and Sicarii"</note>, the inhabitants destroyed one another. Thus, the body of the state was cruelly and mercilessly torn apart by the common people. The city suffered so severely from famine that the inhabitants were driven to all kinds of desperate acts; one woman even ate the flesh of her own son. Famine was followed by plague <note>original: "pestilence"</note>, which was a clear sign of divine wrath, fulfilling the Lord's prophecy and warning that the city would be taken and utterly destroyed.

---

¹ A Judaean stronghold (modern *Sebbeh*).
² The Zealots and Sicarii (assassins) made up the fanatical anti-Roman Jewish war party, whose goal was to drive out the Romans and all who supported them. They did not hesitate to commit murder and carried small daggers (*sicae* <note>Latin: daggers</note>) to stab those they considered enemies of their country.

<header>JOSEPHUS</header>

## XLVIII

I have read the treatise by Josephus <note>original: "Josephus" — The author is most probably Hippolytus of Rome, an important Christian theologian who lived from roughly 170 to 235 AD</note> titled *On the Universe*, which is called elsewhere *On the Cause of the Universe* and *On the Nature of the Universe*. It consists of two short essays in which the author shows that Plato contradicts himself. He also argues against Alcinous <note>original: "Alcinous" — a Platonic philosopher of the 1st or 2nd century AD</note>, whose views on the soul, matter, and the Resurrection he finds false and absurd, while presenting his own opinions on these subjects. 

He proves that the Jewish nation is far older than the Greek. He believes that man is a compound of fire, earth, and water, and also of spirit, which he calls the soul. Of the spirit, he writes the following: "Taking the most important part of this spirit, he shaped it together with the body and opened a passage for it through every joint and limb." The spirit, shaped in this way with the body and spreading throughout it, takes on the likeness of the visible body, though its nature is "colder" compared to the three other substances that make up the body. These views are not in harmony with traditional Jewish ideas of human physiology <term>physiology: the study of how the body and its parts function</term> and are below the usual quality of his other writings. 

He also gives a brief summary of the creation of the world. Regarding Christ, the true God, he speaks as we do, openly giving Him the name of God and describing His indescribable generation <note>original: "generation" — a theological term referring to the eternal relationship between God the Father and God the Son</note> from the Father in language that is perfectly acceptable. This might perhaps cause people to doubt whether the treatise is truly by Josephus, even though the writing style does not differ from the rest of his works.

I found a marginal note stating that the work is not actually by Josephus, but by a man named Gaius <note>Gaius was a Christian priest in Rome at the beginning of the 3rd century AD</note>, a priest in Rome. This Gaius is also the author of *The Labyrinth* <note>original: "The Labyrinth" — another work often attributed to Hippolytus of Rome</note> and of a dialogue against Proclus, the leader of the Montanists <note>original: "Montanists" — a Christian sect founded by Montanus of Phrygia, who claimed to receive direct revelations from the Holy Spirit</note>. This latter work had no...

# HESYCHIUS OF CONSTANTINOPLE

### LI

Read the four books by Hesychius <note>original: "presbyter"; a priest. Little is known of this specific Hesychius, though some suggest he was the one who accused the heretic Eunomius.</note>, a priest of Constantinople, titled *On the Brazen Serpent* <note>Referring to the story in Numbers 21, where Moses created a bronze snake on a pole to heal the Israelites.</note>. The work is written for rhetorical display and is a deliberate attempt to appeal to the reader's emotions. It contains speeches addressed to the people that the author puts into the mouth of Moses, along with fictional replies from the people. There are also elaborate speeches from God addressed to Moses and the people, as well as their replies in the form of prayers and excuses. A large portion of this substantial volume is dedicated to these speeches. Based on this treatise, the author appears to be orthodox in his religious views.

### LII

Read the account of the church council <note>original: "synod"</note> held at Side <note>A city in Pamphylia; this council took place around 383 AD.</note> against the sect of the Messalians <note>A Syriac name meaning "those who pray."</note>, also known as the Euchites <note>A Greek name also meaning "those who pray." This group believed that only constant prayer, rather than the sacraments, could drive out the demons they believed everyone was born with.</note> or Adelphians <note>Named after Adelphius, one of the sect's early leaders.</note>. Amphilochius, the bishop of Iconium, presided over the council, supported by twenty-five other bishops. In the same record, read a letter from the council to Flavian, the bishop of Antioch, giving him an account of the proceedings.

As a result of this letter, Flavian summoned another council against these same heretics. He was assisted by three other bishops: Bizus of Seleucia, Maruthas (the bishop of the Sufareni <note>A region in Mesopotamia.</note>), and Samus. There were also thirty priests and deacons present. The council refused to accept Adelphius's profession of repentance or to readmit him when he offered to renounce his heresy, as it was shown that his renunciation and his repentance were not sincere. 

The founders of this sect were Adelphius—who was a layman rather than a monk or priest—Sabas, nicknamed *Apokopos* <note>original: "castrated"</note>, who wore the clothing of a monk, another man named Sabas, Eustathius of Edessa, Dadoes, and Simeon. These men, along with others who rose up with them, were described as the weeds <note>original: "tares"; a biblical metaphor for false believers planted by the devil.</note> of the evil one. Adelphius and his followers were condemned, even though they sought an opportunity to repent.

# SYNOD AT SIDE

...which was refused to them, since they were caught communicating in writing with people they had previously condemned <note>original: "anathematized"; to formally curse or excommunicate</note> as Messalians, as if they actually shared their views.

Flavian <note>Flavian I, Bishop of Antioch (reigned 381–404 AD)</note> wrote a letter to the people of Osroene <note>A region in Upper Mesopotamia</note>, informing them of what had been done and giving an account of the punishment and excommunication of the heretics. The bishops who received the letter wrote back to Flavian, thanking him and expressing their approval. Letoius ¹, the bishop of Armenia, also wrote to inquire about the Messalians, and a copy of the council’s decree and sentence was sent to him. The great Flavian also wrote to another Armenian bishop on the same subject; in this second letter, he accuses that bishop of having sympathy for the Messalians. Atticus, the bishop of Constantinople, also wrote to the bishops of Pamphylia, ordering them to expel the Messalians from every location as accursed and an abomination. He wrote in similar terms to Amphilochius, the bishop of Side.

Sisinnius of Constantinople and Theodotus of Antioch sent a joint letter to Verinianus ², Amphilochius, and the rest of the bishops in Pamphylia. It was addressed: "To our colleagues, beloved of God—Verinianus, Amphilochius, and the rest of the bishops in Pamphylia: Sisinnius, Theodotus, and all the holy council <note>original: "synod"</note> which by the grace of God was assembled in the mighty city of Constantinople to consecrate the most holy Sisinnius, beloved of God, and our emperor Theodosius, beloved of Christ, greet you in the Lord." In this letter from the council, Bishop Neon declared that if anyone, after the excommunication of the Messalians, should at any time be found saying or doing anything that made him suspected of favoring this heresy, he should not be allowed to keep his position. This would hold true even if he offered to pay ten thousand times the penalty imposed upon those who repent; furthermore, anyone who supported him—whether a bishop or anyone else—should be liable to the same penalty. 

John of Antioch also wrote a letter to Nestorius <note>The Bishop of Constantinople whose later views led to the Nestorian Schism</note> regarding the Messalians. The holy ecumenical council, the third, held at Ephesus ³, also issued a decree exposing the blasphemies and heresies of the Messalian book titled *Asceticus* and formally condemning it. Archelaus, the bishop of Caesarea in Cappadocia, also wrote twenty-four condemnations <note>original: "anathematisms"</note> against these articles. Heraclidas, the bishop of Nyssa, also wrote two letters against them; in the second of these, he provides evidence of the ancient tradition of worshipping holy images <note>This is a significant early historical reference to the use and veneration of icons in the Church</note>.

<meta>This page continues the historical account of the ecclesiastical actions taken against the Messalian sect, focusing on the downfall of the leader Lampetius and his associates.</meta>

<page-num>46</page-num>

-> # SYNOD AT SIDE <-

Some time later, Gerontius—a priest <note>original: "presbyter"</note> and the superior of the monks at Glitis—wrote to Alypius, the Archbishop of Caesarea in Cappadocia. In his letter, he brought various charges against Lampetius, <note>Lampetius was a major Messalian leader, second only to Adelphius. His followers were known as Lampetians.</note> a profane impostor who was the first member of the Messalian sect to successfully infiltrate the priesthood. Upon receiving the letter, Alypius ordered Hormisdas, the Bishop of Comana, to investigate the allegations against Lampetius.

The main points of the indictment were as follows:
* That he had been guilty of sexual misconduct with women.
* That he had used obscene language when discussing such matters.
* That he mocked and sneered at those who performed the daily prayers <note>original: "chanted the hours"; the set times of prayer in the early church</note>, viewing them as still being bound by the old Law <note>The Messalians believed that spiritual "perfection" exempted them from standard religious observance and moral law</note>.

He and the Messalians were accused of many other wicked words and deeds. We ourselves, while doing everything in our power to turn them away from this error (which has recently begun to resurface), have witnessed much festering passion and vice consuming their souls. 

Ultimately, Lampetius was convicted—with Gerontius as his accuser and Bishop Hormisdas as his judge—based partly on the testimony of witnesses and partly on his own confession. He was unanimously stripped of his priestly rank. Alypius of Caesarea, who had previously been deceived into promoting this villain to the dignity of a priest, joined in the vote against him.

This notoriously sinful Lampetius wrote a book titled the *Testament*, which included several of his heretical doctrines. Severus—who later seized the bishop's seat in Antioch—wrote a refutation of the book while he was still only a priest. 

A certain Alpheus, the Bishop of Rhinocorura <note>A city on the border between Egypt and Palestine.</note>, defended Lampetius, claiming he was innocent of the charges in both word and deed. Although Alpheus’s own published work reportedly contains no blasphemies, he was removed from his office for being a supporter of Lampetius. Another man named Alpheus, who had been ordained as a priest by Timothy of Alexandria, was also dismissed for the same heresy. This is known from a report made by Ptolemy, also a Bishop of Rhinocorura, to the same Timothy.

<page-num>47</page-num>

# SYNOD AT CARTHAGE

The synod was held in the great church in Carthage <note>original footnote 1: likely around 411 or 412 AD</note> during the reign of Emperor Honorius, to oppose the teachings of Pelagius <note>original footnote 3: Pelagius (c. 370–440 AD) was a monk, possibly from Wales or Ireland, who argued that humans have the free will to achieve perfection without divine grace; he rejected the doctrine of original sin</note> and Coelestius <note>original footnote 4: Coelestius was a student of Pelagius, possibly of Irish or Italian origin; he was a more aggressive promoter of Pelagian ideas than his teacher</note>. The meeting was held in the Church of Faustus <note>original footnote 2: "Faustus" likely refers to the name of the church building rather than an honorary title for Emperor Honorius</note>. The assembly was presided over by Aurelius, the Bishop of Carthage, and Dotianus of Telepte, the primary prelate of the province of Byzacena <note>an ancient Roman province in what is now Tunisia</note>. They were supported by 224 distinguished church dignitaries from various provinces.

This synod excommunicated those who claimed that Adam was created naturally mortal and that he did not suffer death as a specific punishment for his sin. They also condemned those who declared that newborn infants have no need for baptism because they are not burdened by original sin inherited from Adam. Additionally, they rejected the claim that there was a middle ground between hell and paradise where unbaptized infants went to live in a state of happiness. Six other similar points, which were central to the heresies of Pelagius and Coelestius, were also formally condemned <note>original: "anathematized"</note>.

The Emperors Theodosius and Honorius also wrote to Bishop Aurelius to condemn these same heretics. Following this, Constantius—the husband of Placidia and the father of Valentinian the Younger—issued a decree to Volusianus, the city prefect, ordering that Coelestius be banished. 

<insert>This Volusianus was the uncle of Saint Melania the Younger <note>original footnote 5: Melania the Younger (383–439 AD) was a Roman noblewoman who became a famous ascetic and nun in Africa and Jerusalem</note>. At the time, Volusianus was a pagan <note>original: "heathen"</note>, but when he faced the threat of death, he converted to the true faith and was baptized by Proclus <note>original footnote 6: Patriarch of Constantinople from 434 to 447 AD</note> in Constantinople, where he had been sent on an official embassy. It is possible that while there, he met that holy woman (Melania), who had traveled from Jerusalem to the imperial city.</insert> <note>original footnote 7: The scholar Bekker notes that this paragraph about Volusianus is an addition by a later author.</note> Leo of Rome <note>original footnote 8: Known as Leo the Great, Pope from 440 to 461 AD</note> also wrote...

<summary>This page records the decrees of the Synod of Carthage (c. 411-412 AD) against Pelagianism, specifically upholding the doctrines of original sin and the necessity of infant baptism. It also describes the imperial legal actions taken to banish the movement's leaders.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the historical account of the Church's efforts to suppress the Pelagian heresy, moving from the Synod of Carthage to the involvement of the Bishops of Rome and the theological connection between Pelagianism and Nestorianism.</meta>

<page-num>48</page-num>

<header>THE HERESY OF</header>

Regarding the converted Pelagians <note>original: Pelagians; followers of Pelagius who believed humans could achieve salvation through free will without divine grace</note>: if they wished to be readmitted into the Church, they were required to formally condemn <note>original: "anathematize"</note> their heresy in writing. In a letter from Coelestine, the Bishop of Rome <note>Coelestine I, Pope from 422–432 AD</note>, to Nestorius, these same heretics are condemned. Coelestine also wrote to the bishops of Gaul <note>modern-day France</note> to defend the teachings of St. Augustine and to oppose those who had become bold enough to speak recklessly due to the freedom allowed to this heresy. The priest Jerome <note>St. Jerome (c. 347–420 AD), the scholar who translated the Bible into the Latin Vulgate</note> also wrote to Ctesiphon <note>likely a Roman living in Palestine who sought Jerome’s advice</note> to refute those who held the idea of "impassibility"—the belief that a human can reach a state of being completely free from sin and emotion (in other words, he wrote against Pelagius). This Pelagius was a monk, and Coelestius was his student.

### LIV

I read a work attacking the heresy of Pelagius and Coelestius, titled *A Copy of the Proceedings taken against the Doctrines of Nestorius by the Bishops of the West*. It claims that the Nestorian and Coelestian heresies were undoubtedly identical, citing as its authority a letter from Cyril of Alexandria <note>Archbishop of Alexandria from 412–444 AD and a fierce opponent of Nestorianism</note> to the Emperor Theodosius. 

The Coelestians, when speaking of the "body" or the "members" of Christ—meaning the Church—boldly deny that it is God (specifically the Holy Spirit) who individually distributes faith and everything necessary for life, devotion, and salvation to each person as He sees fit. According to them, human nature—which fell from a state of blessedness, was separated from God, and was handed over to death because of sin—invites or rejects the Holy Spirit based entirely on its own free will. 

The Nestorians hold and dare to assert the same opinion regarding Christ, the "head" of that body. They argue that because Christ shares our human nature and God wants everyone to be saved, any person can correct their own errors and make themselves worthy of God through their own free will. Therefore, they claim that the one born of Mary was not the Word <note>original: "the Word"; the divine Logos or the second person of the Trinity</note> Himself. Instead, they say that because of the nobility of His human will, He was simply accompanied by the Word, sharing the status of "Son" only through His noble character and the use of the same name.

This Pelagian or Coelestian heresy thrived not only in the East but also spread throughout the West. In Carthage, Africa, it

<page-num>49</page-num>

## PELAGIUS AND COELESTIUS

The heresy was detected and refuted by Aurelius and Augustine, and publicly condemned at various synods <note>synods: assemblies of church delegates or bishops to decide on matters of doctrine or administration</note>. Those who held these opinions were expelled from the Church as heretics—during the time when Theophilus was bishop of Alexandria <note>Theophilus served from 385–412 AD</note> and Innocent was bishop of Rome <note>Innocent I served from 402–417 AD</note>—by Roman, African, and other Western bishops. 

At a synod held in Palestine <note>this occurred at Diospolis, the ancient city of Lydda, in 415 AD</note>, which fourteen bishops attended, Pelagius was acquitted. He utterly denied some of the charges brought against him as foolish and anathematized <note>anathematized: formally cursed or denounced</note> them. While he admitted to making certain other statements, he argued they were not meant in the sense his accusers suggested, but were instead in agreement with the doctrines of the Catholic Church. His accusers were Neporus <note>The text likely means Heros, the bishop of Arles</note> and Lazarus, the bishop of Aix. These two bishops of Gaul <note>modern-day France</note> were not present at the inquiry, having obtained permission to be absent because one of them was ill. Augustine states this in his letters to Aurelius, the bishop of Carthage.

After the death of the holy Augustine, certain members of the clergy began to reassert these impious <note>impious: wicked or lacking reverence for God</note> doctrines. They began to speak evil of Augustine and falsely accused him of denying free will. However, Bishop Coelestine <note>Pope Celestine I</note> checked the renewal of this slander by writing to the bishops of that region in defense of that godlike man and against those who had started this heresy again. 

As time went on, and after these heretics had renounced <note>original: "abjured"</note> their own doctrines and were received back into the Church, the scandal was revived by them once more. It had to be put down before it spread further by Bishop Septimus <note>Bishop of Altinum, near modern Venice</note>, who wrote to Leo, the Pope at that time and a fervent opponent of these impious doctrines. Not long afterward, when the shameless heresy again sprang up from an "evil root," certain persons in Rome openly expressed themselves in favor of it. But Prosper <note>Prosper of Aquitaine (c. 403–463 AD), a disciple of Augustine and author of several chronicles</note>, truly a man of God, soon crushed them with his pamphlets while Leo still occupied the papal throne. The heresy was also condemned at the holy Synod of Ephesus <note>The Council of Ephesus, held in 431 AD</note>. John, the patriarch of Alexandria <note>John Talaia, who was later the bishop of Nola</note>, in his *Defense* <note>original: "Apologia"; a formal justification or explanation of one's positions</note> to...

Gelasius, the Bishop of Rome <note>Gelasius I served as Pope from 492 to 496 AD</note>, excommunicated and cursed not only the Pelagian heresy but also Pelagius and Coelestius themselves. He also condemned Julian <note>Julian was the Bishop of Eclana, a prominent leader of the Pelagians in the 5th century</note>, who was known to have succeeded them as the leader of this sect.

### LV

I read the treatise of John Philoponus—whom some mockingly call "Mataeoponus" <note>original: "Mataeoponus," a pun on his name meaning "laborer in vain" or "pointless worker"</note>—titled *Against the Holy Fourth Ecumenical Council* <note>The Fourth Ecumenical Council refers to the Council of Chalcedon, held in 451 AD to define the nature of Christ</note>. The writing style is typical of him. In this work, he shamelessly attempts to prove that the council actually favored the heresy of Nestorius <note>Nestorianism: the controversial belief that Christ existed as two separate persons, one human and one divine</note>. He claims that the council only agreed to Nestorius's excommunication because the members imagined they were doing no harm to the man <note>The original Greek text suggests the council believed a person is not truly harmed if his doctrines are upheld even while he is personally condemned</note> by ratifying his teachings—teachings which Nestorius himself, despite his condemnation, dearly loved and considered the most important of all. 

In these arguments, Philoponus indulges in lies and outrageous statements that reflect his poor judgment and his inconsistent opinions. The bold and empty claims he makes against the council—a "comedy" organized into four parts—are completely unworthy of belief and lack any common sense.

In the same volume, I read a treatise by another man named John, a Nestorian, also written *Against the same Holy Fourth Council*. The author is John of Aegae, a wicked person, yet his style of writing is beautiful, charming, brilliant, and clear. <note>There is some historical confusion here; though Photius calls him a Nestorian, John of Aegae is often identified as a Eutychian, which is the opposing heresy. Photius notes this potential error in the records.</note>

### LVI

I read the treatise by Theodoret of Cyrrhus <note>Theodoret (c. 393–457 AD) was a famous theologian and historian</note> titled *Against Heresies*. This work covers the history of false teachings from the time of Simon Magus <note>Simon Magus: a figure from the Book of Acts who was considered the first heretic</note> down to the heresies that appeared in his own time. The book is dedicated to a man named Sporacius <note>Sporacius was a high-ranking official, a commander of the imperial guards and a consul in 453 AD</note>, who was fond of studying these matters. 

Theodoret's account reaches as far as Nestorius and his heresy, which he condemns without mercy, and goes even further to the heresy of Eutyches <note>Eutychianism: the belief that Christ's human nature was absorbed by his divine nature</note>. In the last of the five books contained in this treatise, he provides a summary...

APPIAN

It consists of divine and orthodox doctrine compared with idle heretical talk, showing that it is not to be confused with the latter, but is pure and beyond reproach. The style is clear and free from unnecessary repetition.

### LVII

I read Appian’s <note>original: Appian of Alexandria, who lived in Rome during the reigns of Trajan, Hadrian, and Antoninus Pius (between 98–161 AD). Of the twenty-four books of his *Roman History* that Photius possessed, only eleven and a preface are completely preserved today; the others are lost or exist only in fragments.</note> *Roman History*, which consists of three parts and twenty-four books. The first part treats the seven kings: Romulus, Numa Pompilius, Ancus Hostilius <note>Usually known as Tullus Hostilius</note>, Ancus Marcius (grandson of Numa), Tarquinius Priscus, Servius Tullius, and Lucius Tarquinius <note>The son of Tarquinius Priscus, usually known as Tarquinius Superbus</note>. It contains an account of their acts and deeds. 

The first of these, the founder and <term>oekist</term> <note>oekist: the official founder of a Greek or Roman colony or city</note> of the city, was assassinated—or, according to others, simply disappeared from view—even though his rule was more like that of a father than a tyrant. The second king, who was in no way inferior as a ruler to his predecessor (and perhaps even his superior), died at the age of... <note>The original text is missing the specific age</note>. The third king was struck by lightning. The fourth died of illness. The fifth was murdered by shepherds. The sixth was also murdered. The seventh was deposed and driven out of the city because of his tyranny. After this, the monarchy was abolished, and its powers were transferred to consuls. 

Such is the content of the first book, which is titled *The Book of the Kings*. The second book, titled *Italica*, provides an account of the history of Italy, with the exception of that part situated on the Ionian Sea. The following book, *Samnitica*, relates the wars of the Romans with the Samnites <note>The most important Samnite wars were: 343–341 BC; 326–304 BC (which included the Roman disaster at the Caudine Forks); and 298–290 BC (ending with the decisive Roman victory at Sentinum in 295 BC)</note>. The Samnites were a powerful nation and an enemy difficult to conquer; it took the Romans eighty years to subdue them and the other nations who fought on their side. The fourth book, *Celtica*, relates the wars of the Romans with the Celts (Gauls). The remaining books are similarly named. The fifth contains the history of Sicily and the other islands, and the sixth gives an account of...

<summary>This page contains Photius's summary of the first four books of Appian's "Roman History," detailing the reigns of the seven Roman kings and the early wars against the Samnites and Gauls.</summary>
<keywords>Appian, Roman History, Photius, Roman Kings, Romulus, Samnites, Samnite Wars, Gauls, Italica, Celtica</keywords>

--- Page 58 ---
<meta>This page continues the detailed breakdown of the books within Appian’s "Roman History" and begins the narrative of Rome’s legendary origins.</meta>

APPIAN

The seventh book covers the Hannibalic Wars, the eighth deals with Libyan affairs (specifically Carthage and Numidia), the ninth covers Macedonian affairs, the tenth deals with Greek and Ionian affairs, the eleventh with Syrian and Parthian affairs, and the twelfth describes the Mithridatic War <note>original: "Mithradatic war"; the wars between Rome and Mithridates VI of Pontus (88–63 BC)</note>. Up to this point, Rome's relations and wars with foreign nations are presented in this order. The books that follow describe the civil wars and internal disturbances among the Romans themselves. These are titled the first and second books of the *Civil Wars*, continuing through the ninth book, which is the twenty-first book of the entire collection. The twenty-second book is called the *History of One Hundred Years* <note>original: *Hekatontaëtia*</note>, the twenty-third is the *Dacian Wars* <note>original: *Dacica*</note>, and the twenty-fourth is the *Arabian Wars* <note>original: *Arabica*</note>.

These are the divisions of the entire work. The account of the civil wars first contains the war between Marius and Sulla, then the conflict between Pompey and Julius Caesar—occurring after their rivalry turned into violent hostilities—until fortune favored Caesar and Pompey was defeated and fled. Next, it describes the actions of Mark Antony and Octavian Caesar (also known as Augustus) against the murderers of Julius Caesar, a time when many distinguished Romans were executed without a trial. Lastly, it recounts the desperate conflict between Antony and Augustus, which involved terrible slaughter and ended with Augustus's victory. Antony, abandoned by his allies, fled to Egypt, where he died by his own hand. The final book of the *Civil Wars* describes how Egypt fell under Roman power and how Augustus became the sole ruler of Rome.

The history begins with Aeneas, the son of Anchises and grandson of Capys, who lived during the Trojan War. After the fall of Troy, Aeneas fled and, after much wandering, landed on the coast of Italy at a place called Laurentum. His camp is still shown there, and the coast is named Troy after him. Faunus, the son of Mars, who was the ruler of the native Italian inhabitants at the time, gave his daughter Lavinia in marriage to Aeneas. He also granted him a piece of land 400 stades <note>A stade was an ancient unit of distance; 400 stades is approximately 46 miles or 74 kilometers.</note> in circumference, on which Aeneas built a city and named it Lavinium after his wife, Lavinia. Three years later, Faunus died, and Aeneas, who inherited the throne through his marriage, gave the native inhabitants <note>original: "aborigines"</note> the name "Latins" in honor of his father-in-law, Latinus <note>Photius/Appian implies Faunus was also known as Latinus, or that Latinus was the relevant ancestor for the name.</note>.

---

<note>Attempts to identify these specific "Aborigines" (the legendary first inhabitants of Italy) have generally been inconclusive for historians.</note>

# APPIAN 53

Aeneas succeeded his father-in-law, Latinus Faunus. After another three years, Aeneas was killed in battle against the Rutulians of Tyrrhenia, to whose king Lavinia had previously been promised in marriage. He was succeeded by Euryleon, also known as Ascanius. This Ascanius was the son of Aeneas and Creusa (the daughter of Priam), who had been his wife in Troy. According to other accounts, however, the Ascanius who succeeded him was his son by his second wife, Lavinia. Ascanius died four years after he had founded the city of Alba with a group of settlers from Lavinium, and Silvius became king. 

The son of this Silvius is said to have been Aeneas Silvius, and his son was Latinus Silvius. His descendants included Capys, Capetus, Tiberinus, and Agrippa. Agrippa was said to be the father of a man named Romulus <note>This is Romulus Silvius, a legendary king of Alba Longa, not the Romulus who founded Rome.</note>, who was killed by lightning. He left behind a son named Aventinus, who in turn had a son named Procas. All of these men are said to have taken the surname Silvius. Procas had two children: the elder was named Numitor, and the younger was named Amulius. 

When the elder brother, Numitor, inherited the throne upon their father's death, the younger brother, Amulius, seized power through force and crime. Amulius killed his brother’s son, Egestus, and made Numitor's daughter, Rhea, a priestess <note>Specifically, a Vestal Virgin, who was required to remain celibate.</note> so that she would not have children. However, Numitor’s own mild and gentle nature saved him from the plot against his life. Silvia broke her religious vows and became pregnant <note>The legend states she was impregnated by the god Mars.</note>. She was arrested by Amulius for punishment, and her two sons were given to some shepherds to be thrown into the nearby Tiber River. These infants, Romulus and Romus <note>original: "Romus"; more commonly known as Remus</note>, were descended from Aeneas through their mother; the identity of their father was unknown <note>One manuscript contains an additional passage: "for, detesting their unknown father, they rather prided themselves on descent from Aeneas."</note>.

As previously stated, this history begins with a brief account of Aeneas and his descendants. However, from the time of Romulus—the founder <note>original: "oekist" (οἰκιστής), a Greek term for the leader of a new colony</note> of the city—it provides full details of events through the reign of Augustus, and occasionally as late as the reign of Trajan.

Appian was a native of Alexandria and began his career as a lawyer.

---

The name "Aborigines" is derived from various sources: from "from the beginning" <note>original: *ab origine*</note>, meaning the original inhabitants or "children of the soil" <note>original: *αὐτόχθονες* (autochthones)</note>; from "to wander" <note>original: *aberrare*</note>, referring to nomads; or from "mountain-dwellers" <note>original: *βορείγενοι* (boreigenoi)</note>.

<meta>This page transitions from the works of Appian to a detailed review of the historian Arrian, focusing on his career, writing style, and his histories of Parthia and Alexander the Great.</meta>

->ARRIAN<-

in Rome, and was later elevated to the rank of procurator <note>1</note> under the emperors. His writing style is straightforward and lacks unnecessary flourishes. As a historian, he is as reliable as possible and is a top authority on military affairs. The speeches he includes are perfectly designed to inspire discouraged soldiers, hold back those who are too impulsive, and accurately portray human emotions and sentiments. He was at the height of his career during the reigns of Trajan and Hadrian.

### 58

I read Arrian's <note>2</note> *Parthica* (*History of Parthia*) in seventeen books. He also wrote the most definitive account of the military campaigns of Alexander of Macedon. Another of his works is the *Bithynica* (*History of Bithynia*), which covers the history of his own homeland. He also wrote the *Alanica* (*History of the Alani*). <note>3</note> 

In the *Parthica*, he describes the wars between the Parthian Empire and Rome during the reign of Trajan. He believes the Parthians were originally a Scythian people who had been ruled by Macedonia for a long time. They revolted around the time of the Persian uprising <note>4</note> for the following reason: Arsaces and Tiridates were two brothers and descendants of Arsaces, the son of Phriapetes. These two brothers, along with five conspirators, killed Pherecles, who had been appointed as the satrap <note>term: A provincial governor in the ancient Persian or Parthian empires.</note> of Parthia by—

---

<note>1</note> Probably in Egypt. Other translators suggest: "He was considered worthy of managing the emperors' affairs."

<note>2</note> Flavius Arrianus lived during the late second century AD and died before 180 AD. Born in Nicomedia in Bithynia, he studied philosophy under Epictetus and became a distinguished soldier. He was appointed governor of Cappadocia in 136 AD and consul in 146 AD. He lived out his remaining years in his home city, serving as the lifelong priest of Demeter and Kore. Besides the works mentioned here, he wrote: *A Voyage Round the Euxine* (the Black Sea), a manual on *Tactics*, the *Order of Battle Against the Alani* (whom he defeated while governor), a book *On Hunting*, and an account of *India*. This last work was likely a sequel to his *Anabasis* (the history of Alexander's campaigns), which he named after the *Anabasis* written by his role model, Xenophon.

<note>3</note> The *Order of Battle Against the Alani*, mentioned above, is a part of this work.

<note>4</note> The Latin word *rebellantibus* (rebelling) appears in the versions by Schott and Müller (*Fragments of the Greek Historians*, volume 3, page 586). However, it is doubtful that the original Greek word *katastraphenton* means this. A more natural translation would be: "who had long been under the Macedonian yoke, the Persians having been conquered at the same time"—that is, by the Seleucids.

<meta>This page concludes the overview of Arrian’s history of the Parthians and his personal biography before moving to a review of the Acts of the Synod of the Oak, which led to the deposition of St. John Chrysostom.</meta>

->SYNOD OF "THE OAK"<-

Antiochus Theos <note>original: Antiochus II (king 261–246 B.C.)</note>, to avenge an insult offered to one of them. They drove out the Macedonians, set up a government of their own, and became so powerful that they were a match for the Romans in war, sometimes even emerging victorious over them. Arrian further relates that during the reigns of Sesostris, King of Egypt, and Iandysus, King of Scythia, the Parthians moved from their own country, Scythia, to the land which they now inhabit. The Emperor Trajan reduced them to submission but left them free under a treaty and appointed a king over them.

This Arrian—called the "young Xenophon"—was a philosopher and one of the pupils of Epictetus <note>original: Epictetus of Hierapolis in Phrygia (c. A.D. 60–140), a Stoic philosopher</note>. He flourished during the reigns of Hadrian, Antoninus Pius, and Marcus Antoninus. Owing to his remarkable learning, he was entrusted with various offices of state and was finally promoted to the consulship. He was also the author of other works: the *Lectures of Epictetus* (his master), of which we know eight books, and the *Conversations of Epictetus* in twelve books. His style is dry, and he is a genuine imitator of Xenophon. It is said that he was also the author of other works, but they have not come into my hands. Certainly, he does not lack rhetorical skill and power.

### 59 <note>original: LIX</note>

I read the proceedings of the synod <note>The Synod of the Oak (403) was held at an estate near Chalcedon, across the Bosporus from Constantinople, belonging to the imperial official Rufinus.</note> that was unlawfully summoned against St. John Chrysostom. The presiding bishops were Theophilus of Alexandria, Acacius of Beroea, Antiochus of Ptolemaïs, Severian of Gabala, and Cyrinus of Chalcedon. These men were bitterly hostile to Chrysostom and appointed themselves as judges, accusers, and witnesses. There were thirteen sessions: twelve against Chrysostom and the thirteenth against Heraclides, whom Chrysostom had ordained as Bishop of Ephesus. 

However, because of the pressure of other business, the deposition of Heraclides could not be officially ratified. His accuser was Macarius, the Bishop of Magnesia. The open enemy and chief accuser of Chrysostom was his own deacon, John. He first...

--- Page 62 ---
<meta>This page continues the list of charges brought against St. John Chrysostom during the Synod of the Oak in 403 AD. The previous page introduced the illegal nature of the assembly and the leading bishops involved in the prosecution.</meta>

->SYNOD OF "THE OAK"<-

[The witnesses] charged Chrysostom with having wronged a man by expelling him because he had beaten his own servant, Eulalius. The second charge was that a certain monk named John had been flogged by order of Chrysostom, dragged along, and put in chains like those possessed by demons. The third, that he had sold much valuable Church property. The fourth, that he had sold the marble which Nectarius <note>Nectarius was Chrysostom's predecessor as Archbishop of Constantinople (381–397 AD).</note> had set aside for decorating the church of St. Anastasia. The fifth, that he had insulted the clergy as dishonorable, corrupt, useless in themselves <note>original Greek: *autoparachretous*; another reading is *autoparakletous*, meaning "self-invited"</note>, and worthless. The sixth, that he had called St. Epiphanius <note>Epiphanius of Salamis (c. 310–403), a staunch defender of orthodoxy who initially opposed Chrysostom.</note> a fool and a demon. The seventh, that he had plotted against Severian and set the *decani* <note>original: *decani*; these were monastic officials who each oversaw ten monks. The name was also given to the *Copiatae* or *Fossarii*—the grave-diggers and undertakers who buried the poor for free.</note> against him. The eighth, that he had written a book slandering the clergy. The ninth, that, having called all the clergy together, he had summoned three deacons—Acacius, Edaphius, and John—on a charge of having stolen his hood <note>original Greek: *maphorion*, a head covering or cowl, perhaps a monk’s scapular. Another reading is *omophorion*, a shoulder-cape, known in Latin as a *pallium*.</note>, and had asked whether they had taken it for any other purpose. The tenth, that he had consecrated Antonius as bishop, although he had been convicted of robbing graves. The eleventh, that he had denounced Count John at a rebellious gathering of the soldiers. The twelfth, that he did not pray either when walking to the church or entering it. The thirteenth, that he ordained deacons and priests without standing by the altar. The fourteenth, that he consecrated four bishops at once. The fifteenth, that he received visits from women by themselves, after he had sent everyone else out of the room. The sixteenth, that he had sold, through the agency of Theodulus, the inheritance left by Thecla. The seventeenth, that no one knew how the revenues of the Church were spent. The eighteenth, that he had ordained Serapion as a priest at a time when he was under accusation. The nineteenth, that he paid no attention to those who belonged to the wider Christian community <note>original: "communion of the world"</note> who had been imprisoned by his orders, and when they died in prison, he did not even bother to make arrangements for the burial of their bodies. The twentieth, that he

-># SYNOD OF "THE OAK"<-

[The witnesses further charged that Chrysostom] had insulted the most holy Acacius and refused to grant him an interview; the twenty-first, that he had handed over the priest <note>original: presbyter</note> Porphyry to Eutropius to be sent into exile <note>original: banished</note>; the twenty-second, that he had also handed over the priest Venerius and severely insulted him; the twenty-third, that a bath was heated for him alone, and that after he had bathed, Serapion emptied the bath so that no one else might use it; the twenty-fourth, that he had ordained many people to the clergy without witnesses; the twenty-fifth, that he ate greedily alone, living like a Cyclops <note>In Greek mythology, the Cyclopes were giants who lived solitary, lawless lives; here, the term implies Chrysostom was unsociable and gluttonous.</note>; the twenty-sixth, that he acted as accuser, witness, and judge all at once, as was evident from the cases of Martyrius the chief deacon <note>original: proto-deacon</note> and Proaeresius, the bishop of Lycia; the twenty-seventh, that he struck Memnon with his fist in the Church of the Apostles, and while Memnon was still bleeding from the mouth, Chrysostom celebrated the communion service; the twenty-eighth, that he dressed and undressed while sitting on his episcopal throne and ate a lozenge; <note>Chrysostom reportedly advised those receiving communion to eat a small piece of bread or a lozenge to prevent accidentally spitting out any of the sacred elements. His enemies used this to accuse him of violating the ritual fast.</note> the twenty-ninth, that he bribed the bishops who were consecrated by him to oppress the lower clergy.

Such were the charges brought against this holy man. He was summoned four times, but he refused to appear. He declared that if the synod would remove his open enemies from the list of judges, he was ready to appear and defend himself against any charges brought against him; if they refused to do so, he said that no matter how many times they summoned him, it would be of no use.

The first and second counts were then investigated, after which the synod proceeded to deal with the case of the bishops Heraclides and Palladius of Helenopolis. The monk John (mentioned by the deacon John in the second charge against Chrysostom) presented a written statement <note>original: memorial</note> accusing Heraclides of being a follower of Origen <note>Origen of Alexandria (c. 185–253 AD) was a highly influential but controversial theologian whose works were the subject of intense debate regarding their orthodoxy during this period.</note> and of having been arrested at Caesarea in Palestine for stealing the clothes of Aquilinus the deacon. Despite this, the monk declared, Chrysostom had consecrated him as the bishop of Ephesus. He further accused Chrysostom himself, whom he blamed for all the suffering he had endured at the hands of Serapion and Chrysostom because of the Origenists <note>Followers of Origen’s teachings.</note>. After this, the ninth and twenty-seventh charges were investigated.

Then Bishop Isaac again charged Heraclides with being a follower of Origen, a man with whom the most holy Epiphanius would hold no fellowship, either in prayer or at meals. He also presented a written statement <note>original: memorial</note> containing the following charges against...

[The charges brought by Isaac against] Chrysostom: (1) That the monk John, whom we have already mentioned, had been flogged and put in chains because of the Origenists; <note>Origenists were followers of the controversial 3rd-century theologian Origen. By 403 AD, being labeled an Origenist was a serious heresy charge.</note> (2) that Epiphanius refused to share communion with him because of his connection with the Origenists Ammonius, Euthymius, Eusebius, Heraclides, and Palladius; (3) that he neglected his duties of hospitality and always ate alone; (4) that in church he used such language as "the [altar] table is full of furies"; (5) that he loudly shouted, "I am in love, I am mad"; (6) that he should explain what "furies" he was referring to, and what he meant by "I am in love, I am mad"—phrases that are unheard of in the Church; (7) that he gave people permission to sin, since he taught, "If you sin again, repent again," and, "As often as you sin, come to me and I will heal you"; (8) that he uttered blasphemy while in the church, claiming that Christ’s prayer was not heard because He did not pray in a proper way; (9) that he stirred up the people to reject the authority of the synod; (10) that he had welcomed several pagans <note>original: heathens</note> who had oppressed Christians, keeping them in the church and providing them with protection; (11) that he had interfered in the jurisdictions <note>original: provinces</note> of others and consecrated bishops there; (12) that he had insulted the bishops, and ordered both the bishops and the . . .¹ to be thrown out of his house; (13) that he had subjected the clergy to unprecedented insults; (14) that he had violently seized sums of money left to other people; (15) that he performed ordinations without a meeting of the clergy and against their wishes; (16) that he had welcomed the Origenists, yet allowed those who were in full communion with the Church and who had come to him with letters of recommendation to be thrown into prison without trying to release them; even if they died there, he ignored them; (17) that he had consecrated foreign slaves as bishops, even though they had not yet been freed and, in some cases, were under legal accusations; (18) and that Isaac himself had often been mistreated by him.

Of these charges, the first had already been discussed and did not seem to require further examination. However, the second and seventh charges, followed by the third of the charges brought by Deacon John, were investigated. In this last matter, the chief priest <note>original: archpresbyter</note>—

<meta>This page concludes the records of the Synod of the Oak (403 AD), detailing the final vote to depose John Chrysostom. It then transitions to a new entry in Photius's "Bibliotheca," providing a literary review of the historian Herodotus.</meta>

Arsacius—the man who would succeed Chrysostom—and the priests <note>original: presbyters</note> Atticus and Elpidius came forward in some manner as witnesses against that holy man. They, along with the priest Acacius, also testified against him regarding the fourth charge. After these matters were investigated, the aforementioned priests, together with Eudaemon and Onesimus, demanded that the synod hurry its decision. Accordingly, Paul, the bishop of Heraclea, called upon everyone to cast their votes.

The members present, forty-five in total, then recorded their opinions, starting with Bishop Gymnasius and ending with Theophilus of Alexandria. It was unanimously decided that Chrysostom should be stripped of his bishopric <note>original: episcopate</note>. A letter regarding his removal was sent by the synod to the clergy of Constantinople, and a report was made to the emperors. Gerontius, Faustinus, and Eugnomonius also presented three petitions, complaining that they had been unfairly deprived of their own bishoprics by Chrysostom. The emperors replied by sending an imperial decree <note>original: rescript; an official written reply to a legal query or petition</note> to the synod. These were the proceedings of the twelfth session; the thirteenth, as previously mentioned, dealt with the case of Heraclides, the bishop of Ephesus.

### LX

Read the nine books of the *History* of Herodotus,¹ which are identical in name and number to the nine Muses. He may be considered the finest representative of the Ionic dialect, just as Thucydides represents the Attic dialect. He is fond of folktales <note>original: old wives' tales</note> and side stories <note>original: digressions</note>, filled with charming sentiments; however, these sometimes make it difficult to fully appreciate the history and its accurate, proper character. Truth does not allow its accuracy to be spoiled by myths or excessive departures from the main subject.

He begins his history with Cyrus, the first king of Persia, describing his birth, education, adulthood, and reign. The narrative continues down to the reign of Xerxes and his military expedition against the—

---

¹ Herodotus of Halicarnassus (lived approximately 484–424 BC) is often called the "father of history." His work, in nine books each named after one of the Muses, provides an account of the Persian Wars from the reign of Cyrus the Great to the Battle of Mycale during the reign of Xerxes, covering a period of 126 years. It also contains lengthy descriptions of the early history, manners, and customs of various peoples. It is curious that Photius did not devote more attention to him in this collection.

<meta>This page continues the review of Herodotus's *Histories* from entry LX, focusing on the lineage of Persian kings and an anecdote about the young Thucydides. It then transitions to entry LXI, which reviews the works and career of the orator Aeschines.</meta>

Athenians, and their subsequent retreat. Xerxes was the third who succeeded Cyrus, with the first being Cambyses and the second Darius. Smerdis the Magian is not counted among these, as he was a tyrant who craftily seized a throne that did not belong to him. Darius was succeeded by his son Xerxes, with whom the history concludes, although it does not continue until the very end of his reign. Herodotus himself, according to the evidence of Diodorus Siculus <note>original: See LXX; refers to entry 70 in this collection, where Photius discusses Diodorus</note>, was at the peak of his career during these times. It is said that when he read his work to an assembly of the Greeks at Olympia, Thucydides—then very young and present with his father at the reading—burst into tears. Whereupon Herodotus exclaimed, “Oh, Olorus! How passionate your son is for learning!”

### LXI

Read the three orations of Aeschines <note>An Athenian orator (c. 390–314 B.C.) and the primary rival of Demosthenes. He had a varied career as a secretary, a third-rate actor, and a statesman. Though he first opposed Philip of Macedon, he was eventually bribed to favor him.</note>: *Against Timarchus* (the first of his speeches), *On the False Embassy*, and *Against Ctesiphon* (the third and last). These three speeches and nine letters are said to be his only genuine works; for this reason, the orations were sometimes called the "Three Graces" due to their number and the charm of their style, and the letters were called the "Nine Muses." Another oration, the *Delian Law*, was known under his name, but Caecilius <note>Caecilius Calactinus, a Greek rhetorician who worked in Rome during the reign of Augustus; none of his works survive today.</note> denies its authenticity and attributes it to another Aeschines, a contemporary Athenian.

Aeschines was one of the "ten" Attic orators <note>A traditional group of the ten greatest orators of the golden age of Athens.</note>. He was accused by Demosthenes of having failed in his duties during a diplomatic mission <note>original: embassy; specifically a mission to Philip of Macedon.</note>, but he was not convicted. This was because the popular leader Eubulus <note>A distinguished financial official and bitter opponent of Demosthenes.</note>, in whose service Aeschines had formerly been a secretary, sided with him against Demosthenes.

<header>AESCHINES</header>

...Demosthenes, and caused the jury to rise before Demosthenes had finished his speech. Subsequently, when he attacked the proposal of Ctesiphon <note>Ctesiphon had proposed that Demosthenes be honored with a golden crown for his service to Athens. Aeschines sued, arguing the proposal was illegal, which led to the famous "On the Crown" speech by Demosthenes.</note> on behalf of Demosthenes as illegal,¹ having himself settled the amount of the fine he was prepared to pay if he did not make good the charge, he failed to do so and left his country. 

He first set out for Asia, intending to seek refuge with Alexander <note>Alexander the Great (356–323 B.C.), King of Macedon.</note>, the son of Philip, who was then on his Asiatic expedition. However, when he heard of Alexander's death and that his successors were quarreling among themselves, he sailed to Rhodes, where he remained for some time, giving young men lessons in rhetoric. When his admirers were at a loss to understand how so great an orator could have been defeated by Demosthenes, he replied, "If you had heard that beast (meaning Demosthenes), you would not be surprised." He is said to have been the first to compose imaginary speeches and what are called "declamations" in his leisure hours. In his old age, he removed to Samos, where he died. 

He was of humble origin;² his father was Atrometus and his mother was Glaucothea, a priestess. He had two brothers, Aphobetus and Philochares. At first, being possessed of a loud voice, he became a third-rate actor; then he was a copying-clerk to the Council <note>The Council of Five Hundred (Boule), which managed the daily affairs of the Athenian city-state.</note>; and soon afterwards, he came forward as a public speaker. He belonged to the pro-Macedonian party <note>original: "philippizing party"; those who favored King Philip II of Macedon.</note> at Athens and was consequently a political opponent of Demosthenes. He is said to have attended Plato’s lectures and to have been the pupil of Antalcidas.³ These statements are supported by the grandeur of his language and the dignity of his style.⁴ 

The scholar Dionysius,⁵ when he came across the oration *Against Timarchus*, after he had read the opening—"I have never yet publicly indicted a citizen nor harassed him when he was rendering an account of his office"—is reported to have said, "Would that you had indicted or harassed many, so that—"

<meta>This page concludes the entry on the Athenian orator Aeschines, comparing his style to other great orators like Demosthenes and Lysias. It then moves on to Aeschines "Socraticus," a student of Socrates, before beginning a summary of a lost history of Constantine the Great by Praxagoras of Athens.</meta>

<header>PRAXAGORAS</header>

"...you might have left us more speeches of that kind," so delighted was he with this orator's style.

His language appears natural and spontaneous, and does not create as much admiration for the writer's art as it does for his natural gifts. Abundant proofs of his cleverness and ability are to be found in his orations. In his choice of words, he aims for simplicity and clarity, and in the structure of his sentences <note>original: "periods"</note> he is neither as weak as Isocrates <note>Isocrates (436–338 B.C.) was known for a very polished, rhythmic, and sometimes overly elaborate style.</note>, nor as dense and brief as Lysias <note>Lysias (c. 445–380 B.C.) was famous for a "plain style" that used everyday language with great precision.</note>, while in spirit and energy he is not inferior to Demosthenes. He employs figures of thought and speech not to create the impression of using artistic language, but because they are necessary for the subject. Consequently, his style appears direct and straightforward, well-suited for both public speaking and private conversation; he does not rely constantly on proofs and arguments, and he is not overly elaborate.

Aeschines <note>This is Aeschines "Socraticus" (the Socratic), to be distinguished from the orator Aeschines mentioned previously.</note>, the son of Lysanias, is considered by Phrynichus <note>A 2nd-century grammarian and rhetorician.</note> and others to be one of the greatest orators. His speeches are seen as models of the Attic style <note>The "Attic style" refers to the refined, clear, and elegant Greek dialect of Athens, prized as the highest literary standard.</note>, second only to those of its very best representatives. <note>Aeschines Socraticus was a pupil of Socrates. He spent some time at the court of Dionysius the Younger of Syracuse, then settled in Athens and wrote speeches for the law courts. He also composed a number of Socratic dialogues, of which seven were supposed to be genuine; the three that still exist under his name, along with some letters, are certainly not by him.</note>

### LXII

I read the *History of Constantine the Great* by Praxagoras of Athens <note>Praxagoras flourished in the 4th century B.C. Both works mentioned by Photius are now entirely lost.</note>, in two books. In this work, he tells us that Constantine’s father, Constantius, was governor of Britain and Spain. Maximian <note>original: "Maximin." Historically, this refers to Marcus Aurelius Valerius Maximianus.</note> governed Rome, the rest of Italy, and Sicily. The other Maximian <note>original: "Maximin." Historically, this refers to Galerius Valerius Maximianus.</note> governed Greece, Asia Minor, and Thrace. Diocletian, as the eldest, governed Bithynia, Arabia, Libya, and that part of Egypt watered by the Nile. Constantine was sent by his father to Diocletian in Nicomedia <note>An ancient city in what is now Turkey, which served as the eastern capital of the Roman Empire under Diocletian.</note> to be educated. At that time Galerius <note>original: "Maximin," meaning the emperor Galerius.</note>, the governor of Asia Minor, who happened to be there, determined to plot against the youth and...

<meta>This page continues the summary of Praxagoras of Athens' history of Constantine the Great. # PRAXAGORAS

...set him to fight with a savage lion. But Constantine overcame and killed the beast, and having discovered the plot against him, took refuge with his father. After his father's death, he succeeded to the throne.

Soon after his rise to power, he conquered the Celts and Germans—neighboring and uncivilized <note>original: "barbarous"</note> nations. Having learned that Maxentius (who had seized control of Rome after Maximin¹) treated his subjects with cruelty and brutality, he marched against him to punish him for his conduct. He was quickly victorious and put his enemy to flight. Maxentius fell into the trap <note>original: "pit"; this refers to the collapse of the Milvian Bridge, which Maxentius had reportedly tampered with to trap Constantine</note> which he had prepared for others and met the very death he had planned for his enemies. The Romans cut off his head, displayed it on a spear, and carried it through the city. This part of the empire eagerly and joyfully submitted to Constantine.

In the meantime, Maximin (who had also plotted against Constantine) died and was succeeded in his government by Licinius. Constantine, hearing that Licinius also treated his subjects with cruelty and inhumanity, and being unable to tolerate such brutality toward people of the same race <note>likely meaning fellow citizens of the Roman Empire</note>, marched against him to end his tyranny and replace it with a constitutional government. 

When Licinius was informed of the expedition, he became alarmed. He attempted to hide his cruelty under a pretense <note>original: "cloak"</note> of humanity and took an oath that he would treat his subjects kindly and strictly keep his promise. Constantine, therefore, abandoned his expedition for a time. Soon afterward, however—since the wicked cannot remain at peace—Licinius broke his oath and gave himself over to every kind of villainy. 

Consequently, Constantine attacked and defeated him in several major battles. He eventually cornered and besieged him in Nicomedia, from which Licinius approached Constantine dressed as a humble petitioner <note>original: "suppliant"</note>. His kingdom was taken from him and given to Constantine, who thus secured and became the sole ruler of the various parts of the great empire, which had long desired an emperor worthy of it. He inherited his father's kingdom, gained the kingdom of Rome after the overthrow of Maximin² <note>The original text says Maximin, but historically this refers to the defeat of Maxentius</note>, and obtained possession of Greece, Macedonia, and Asia Minor by the removal of Licinius. He further assumed control of the territories that had belonged to Diocletian and had been held by Licinius, who had—

---

¹ Valerius <note>This likely refers to Galerius, whose full name was Gaius Galerius Valerius Maximianus.</note>
² Should be Maxentius.

<summary>This page describes Constantine the Great's military victories over his rivals Maxentius and Licinius, leading to his emergence as the sole ruler of the unified Roman Empire.</summary>
<keywords>Constantine the Great, Maxentius, Licinius, Maximin, Nicomedia, Roman Empire, unification, civil war</keywords>

<meta>This page concludes the summary of Praxagoras of Athens' history of the Emperor Constantine the Great and introduces the major historical work of Procopius of Caesarea, which covers the reign of Justinian I.</meta>

...Constantine took the territory by right of war from Maximin,¹ the successor to Diocletian.

Having become the sole ruler of a unified empire, Constantine founded the city of Byzantium and renamed it after himself <note>Constantinople</note>. Praxagoras states that even though Constantine was a pagan <note>original: "heathen"</note>, his virtue, goodness, and success far surpassed all of the emperors who came before him. With these remarks, the history concludes.

Praxagoras mentions that he was twenty-two years old when he wrote this history. He was also the author of two books titled *The Kings of Athens*, written when he was nineteen, and six books on *Alexander, King of Macedon*, written when he was thirty-one. His writing style is clear and pleasant, though it lacks a certain power <note>original: "vigour"</note>. He writes in the Ionic dialect <note>an ancient Greek dialect used by famous historians like Herodotus</note>.

### LXIII

I read the *History* written by Procopius the rhetorician <term>rhetorician</term> (an orator or teacher of rhetoric) in eight books. He describes the wars fought by the Romans during the reign of Justinian against the Vandals, Persians, and Goths. These campaigns were primarily led by the general Belisarius; the author was a close friend of Belisarius and accompanied him on his military campaigns, recording events as an eyewitness.

The following is the content of the first book. Arcadius, the Emperor of the Romans, stated in his will that Yezdegerd, the King of Persia, should be the guardian of his young son, Theodosius. Yezdegerd accepted this responsibility, carried out his duties faithfully, and kept his ward’s throne secure. Upon the death of Yezdegerd, his successor Vararanes <note>Bahram V</note> declared war on the Romans. However, after Anatolius—the Master-General of the East—was sent by Theodosius on a diplomatic mission to Persia, Vararanes concluded a treaty and returned home. After this, King Perozes <note>Peroz I</note> of Persia, who succeeded another Yezdegerd (the son of Vararanes), went to war against the—

---

¹ Valerius Maximinus, known as Daza or Daia, who was emperor from 311 to 314.
² Procopius of Caesarea in Palestine died sometime after 562. In addition to his eight books on *The Wars*, he wrote a description of *The Buildings* constructed by Justinian and the *Anecdota* <note>original: "*Anecdota* or *Chronique Scandaleuse*"; translated as "Unpublished Works" or "Scandalous Chronicle," often called the *Secret History*</note>, which attacks the private lives of Justinian and his infamous wife, Empress Theodora. The authenticity of this last work has been debated. In his style, Procopius imitates the ancient Greek historians Herodotus and Thucydides. The title "rhetorician" given to him by Photius is likely more appropriate for Procopius of Gaza, a Christian teacher of rhetoric (465–528). For the history of this period, see Edward Gibbon’s *The Decline and Fall of the Roman Empire* (chapter 40), J. B. Bury’s *Later Roman Empire*, T. Hodgkin’s *Italy and her Invaders*, and G. Rawlinson’s *The Seventh Oriental Monarchy*.

<meta>This page continues the summary of Procopius of Caesarea’s *History*, specifically focusing on the Roman-Persian wars during the reign of Justinian I and the background of the Sassanid King Cabades.</meta>

# PROCOPIUS OF CAESAREA

->VOL. I. E<-

There are Huns known as the Ephthalites, or "White" Huns, so-named because of their fair complexions. They are not unattractive and do not resemble other Hunnic tribes. Unlike typical Huns, they do not lead a wild or nomadic life, but instead live under the protection of established laws and the rule of kings. They were the northern neighbors of Persia, which led King Perozes <note>also known as Peroz I, r. 459–484</note> to invade their territory to settle a boundary dispute. The Ephthalites cunningly lured him into difficult terrain, from which he barely escaped after concluding a humiliating peace treaty. He was forced to submit to the king of the Ephthalites and was only allowed to depart after swearing an oath that he would never attack them again. 

Subsequently, however, he broke his word. Having declared war on them a second time, he was shamefully destroyed along with his entire army, which fell into pits and ditches that the enemy had cleverly hidden. He died in the twenty-fourth year of his reign. On this occasion, the famous pearl that he wore in his right ear was lost.

Perozes was succeeded by his youngest son Cabades,¹ who was accused of violating Persian laws and imprisoned in the fortress of Lethe <note>original: "Lethe" (Greek for "Oblivion"); this refers to the "Prison of Oblivion," where it was a capital offense to even mention the names of the prisoners</note>. Having escaped with the assistance of his wife, he took refuge with the Ephthalites. Their ruler betrothed Cabades to one of his daughters and provided him with a large army. With these forces, he marched against the Persians and recovered his throne without a fight. His brother Blases,² who was ruling in his place, was abandoned by his soldiers, captured, and blinded by having boiling oil poured into his open eyes—a long-established Persian custom for dealing with deposed rulers. An account follows concerning a dispute between Pacurius, King of Persia, and Arsaces, King of Armenia, including the hostile advice given by the <term>magi</term> (Persian priests and advisors) to Pacurius. It seems probable, however, that this story is legendary or fictitious.

The aforementioned Cabades, who was heavily in debt to the Ephthalites, attempted to obtain a loan from the Roman Emperor Anastasius but was refused. Consequently, Cabades, without any further justification, suddenly overran Armenia and besieged the city of Amida. Just as he was on the point of abandoning the siege in despair,

¹ Also known as Kobad or Kavadh I.
² Also known as Balash. According to some sources, he was the brother of Cabades; according to others, he was his uncle. Historians differ as to whether he died a natural death; if he did, Procopius and other writers have likely confused him with Cabades’s other brother, Zamasp.

<meta>This page continues the history of the Roman-Persian wars by Procopius of Caesarea, focusing on the reign of the Byzantine Emperor Anastasius I and the Persian King Cabades (Kavad I). It details the fall of the city of Amida and provides geographical context for the region.</meta>

# PROCOPIUS OF CAESAREA

A crude insult from some of the women among the besieged people provoked King Cabades to return and resume his military operations. He attacked with frantic energy, captured the city by storm, and took the inhabitants as slaves. Later, a large number of them were released without having to pay a ransom and were treated with great kindness by the Emperor Anastasius.

When Anastasius heard that Amida was under siege, he sent a massive force against the Persians. This army was led by four commanders: Areobindus, the Master of Soldiers <note>original: "master-general"; a high-ranking military official</note> for the East (who was the son-in-law of Olybrius, a former Emperor of the West); Celer, the Master of Offices <note>original: "captain of the imperial household"; a senior administrative and military position</note>; Patricius the Phrygian; and the Emperor’s own nephew, Hypatius. They were joined by Justin—who would later succeed Anastasius as Emperor—and many other battle-hardened soldiers. It is said that such a large army had never before been sent into the field against the Persians. However, because they arrived too late, the city had already been taken. Furthermore, the generals failed to work together; the different units acted independently, which led to a shameful defeat with heavy losses. Eventually, they reached Amida and laid siege to it themselves. But as they stalled, the Persians inside (who were in a desperate situation) negotiated a seven-year peace treaty. This agreement was brokered by Celer and Asperedes, representing Rome and Persia.

Mount Taurus in Cilicia stretches through Cappadocia, Armenia, Persarmenia <note>Armenian territory under Persian control</note>, Albania <note>referring to Caucasian Albania, an ancient kingdom in the eastern Caucasus</note>, Iberia <note>referring to Caucasian Iberia, an ancient kingdom in modern-day Georgia</note>, and all the other independent countries that had fallen under Persian rule. Just past the borders of Iberia, there is a narrow path about six miles <note>original: "fifty stades"; a Greek unit of distance</note> long, ending at a steep and unreachable peak. There appears to be no passage through, except for a natural opening that looks as if it were man-made; in ancient times, this was called the Caspian Gate. Beyond this gate lie plains suitable for riding, filled with natural springs and vast stretches of sloping land that provide excellent grazing for horses. This entire region, extending as far as the Sea of Azov <note>original: "Palus Maeotis"</note>, is inhabited by Huns. Whenever they decide to invade Persian or Roman territory through the Caspian Gate, they ride at full speed on strong horses. Those wishing to enter Iberia only have to deal with the difficult terrain for the six miles mentioned; however, if they choose any other route, it is only with great trouble that they reach their destination. Alexander, the son of Philip,
