--- Page 73 ---
<meta>This page continues the history of the Roman-Persian conflicts during the early 6th century, transitioning from the reign of Emperor Anastasius I to Emperor Justin I. It discusses strategic fortifications, diplomatic failures regarding the adoption of Chosroës I, and the geopolitical shifts among the Huns and Iberians that led to renewed war.</meta>

# PROCOPIUS OF CAESAREA

Realizing the strategic importance of the pass, he built gates there and erected a fortress. During the reign of Anastasius, this fortress was occupied by a Hun named Ambazuces. He was a friend to both the Romans and Anastasius, and he offered to hand over control of the gates to the Emperor on the condition that he be paid a sum of money. Anastasius thanked him for his goodwill but declined to take on the responsibility. After Ambazuces died, Cabades <note>Kavad I, King of Persia</note> forcibly drove out the man's sons and took possession of the gates himself. 

Consequently, after a treaty had been concluded with Cabades, Anastasius built a stronghold near Daras, despite the objections of the Persians. He also established another city in Armenia, on the frontier of Persarmenia <note>the portion of Armenia under Persian rule</note>. This city was formerly called Theodosiupolis, named after Emperor Theodosius, who had raised its status from a village to a city.

Upon the death of Anastasius, although many of his relatives were worthy to succeed him, they were all passed over, and Justin was elected Emperor. Soon after he took the throne, Cabades—wishing to secure the succession for his youngest son, Chosroës—wrote a letter to Justin. He proposed that Justin should formally adopt Chosroës. 

Justin and his nephew Justinian, the heir-apparent, welcomed the proposal. However, they changed their minds after receiving advice from Proclus the quaestor <note>a senior legal official</note>. Proclus argued that because adopted sons were the legal heirs and successors of their fathers, an adopted Chosroës might later claim the Roman throne. Because of this fear, the adoption was never ratified. 

Later, Seoses (who had once saved the life of Cabades) and Beodes (also known as Mebodes) were sent by the Persians to discuss peace terms and the adoption. The Romans sent Rufinus and Hypatius. During these negotiations, Beodes accused Seoses of various crimes; Seoses was subsequently tried by his own countrymen and condemned to death. Meanwhile, Rufinus accused Hypatius to the Emperor, who then stripped Hypatius of his office.

The region between the Bosporus and Cherson <note>located in the modern-day Crimea</note>, a journey of twenty days, is inhabited by Hunnish tribes. These tribes were formerly independent but had recently submitted to Justin’s authority; Cherson remained the final city within Roman territory. Additionally, the Iberians <note>a kingdom in modern-day Georgia</note> were being mistreated by the Persians. They and their king, Gurgenes, declared themselves subjects of Justin. These territorial shifts became the primary cause of the war between the Romans and the Persians.

<summary>This page describes the transition of power from Emperor Anastasius to Justin I and the diplomatic breakdown between Rome and Persia regarding the adoption of the Persian prince Chosroës. It also identifies the rebellion of the Iberians and Hunnish tribes as the catalyst for renewed war.</summary>

--- Page 74 ---
<meta>This page continues the transition of power from Emperor Justin I to Justinian I and details the early military campaigns of the Roman-Persian War, specifically the Battle of Daras and maneuvers in Armenia.</meta>

# PROCOPIUS OF CAESAREA

During his lifetime, Justin had made Justinian his partner in the empire; after his uncle's death, Justinian became the sole ruler. Belisarius and Sittas were the two army commanders under Justinian. Belisarius had been appointed to the command of the troops in Daras when Procopius, the writer of this history, became his secretary. When Justinian became the sole emperor, Belisarius was made General of the East and ordered to undertake an expedition against the Persians. Perozes, the *mirran* <term>mirran</term> <note>original: "mirran"; likely the Persian title "mihran," denoting a high-ranking general or the head of one of the seven great houses of Persia.</note>, had been appointed to the command of the Persian army by Cabades <note>Kavad I, King of Persia</note>. 

While both armies were encamped near Daras, Perozes sent a message to Belisarius, ordering him to prepare a bath in the city, since he intended to bathe there on the following day. The Romans, in response, prepared vigorously for battle. During the engagement, a man named Andrew—a Byzantine <note>a citizen of Constantinople</note> who was a wrestling instructor, a master of a wrestling school, and one of the bath-attendants of Buzes (who was associated with Belisarius in the command)—was challenged to a duel. Andrew made his way through the ranks unnoticed, and he defeated and killed his challenger. After this, the battle was discontinued for the day. In a subsequent engagement, the Persians were completely defeated with heavy losses. They decided not to risk any more pitched battles <note>large-scale, pre-arranged battles</note> with the Romans, and both sides confined themselves to minor skirmishes.

Cabades then sent another army into Roman Armenia, consisting of Persarmenians <note>Armenians living in the Persian-controlled sector of the country</note>, Sunites, and Sabirites, under the command of Mermeroës. Dorotheus, the general of Armenia, and Sittas, who was in command of the whole army, joined battle. Although they were greatly inferior in numbers, the Romans defeated the Persians, who then returned home. The Romans then took possession of some Persian territory, including the district of Pharangium, where the gold mines provided a significant revenue for the Persian king. The Tzani (formerly called Sani), an independent people who lived by plundering their neighbors, were defeated by Sittas and submitted to Rome. They embraced Christianity and were drafted into the ranks of the Roman army.

After the defeat of both his armies, Cabades was at a loss for what to do. Then Alamundarus <note>Al-Mundhir III ibn al-Nu'man, king of the Lakhmids</note>, the chief of the Persian Saracens <note>Arab allies of the Persian Empire</note>, suggested a new plan. Alamundarus was an experienced and vigorous soldier who had harassed the Romans for fifty years. He suggested to the king that he should attack...

# PROCOPIUS OF CAESAREA

[...to attack] Antioch, which was unprotected, and ravage the neighboring country. But Belisarius, hearing of his intention, set out with all speed against him with a force of Isaurians <note>mountainous people from southern Asia Minor known for being fierce soldiers</note> and Saracens <term>Saracens</term> <note>original: "Saracens"; a term used by the Romans to describe the nomadic Arab tribes of the desert.</note>. The latter were under the command of Arethas, a Saracen chief who was allied with Rome.

Alamundarus <note>Al-Mundhir III, king of the Lakhmid Arabs and a Persian ally</note> and Azarethes retired in alarm, closely followed by Belisarius. He did not intend to force a battle, but only pretended to pursue them to drive them away. However, the soldiers criticized him, at first secretly and then openly, so that he reluctantly consented to give battle. At first, after both sides had suffered heavy losses, the outcome remained in doubt; but after the forces of Arethas and the Isaurians gave way, the Persians gained a decisive victory. Had Belisarius not dismounted and gone to the assistance of those who remained, they would all have been destroyed. Azarethes, the Persian commander, received no thanks from Cabades <note>Kavad I, King of Persia</note> for his victory upon his return. Because he had lost a large number of men—even though the enemy’s losses had been greater—he was regarded as disgraced.

Belisarius was recalled to Byzantium <note>the capital city, Constantinople</note> by Justinian to command the expedition against the Vandals <note>a Germanic people who had established a kingdom in North Africa</note>, while the protection of the East was entrusted to Sittas. At this time, while the Persians were attacking the Romans, Cabades died and was succeeded by Chosroës <note>Khosrow I, one of the most famous Sassanid kings</note>. Hearing of this, the Romans sent Rufinus, Alexander, Thomas, and Hermogenes on an embassy to him, with offers to conclude an “endless peace” and also to pay a sum of 110 centenars <term>centenars</term> <note>a Roman unit of weight; 110 centenars equaled 11,000 pounds of gold, a massive sum intended to settle border disputes.</note>. At first the negotiations were unsuccessful, and it was not until later that the “endless peace” was concluded in the sixth year of the reign of Justinian [532 AD]. According to its terms, the Persians received the money agreed upon, and the district of Pharangium and the fortress of Bolon were restored to them. In exchange, they abandoned the fortresses captured in Lazica <note>a kingdom on the Black Sea, in modern-day Georgia</note>, and exchanged Dagaris, an excellent soldier, for a Persian of high rank.

Soon afterward, their subjects conspired against both Chosroës and Justinian. The Persians hated Chosroës, seeing him as turbulent and restless, and they intended to give the crown to Cabades, the son of Chosroës’ brother Zames. But the plot was discovered, and Chosroës put to death Zames, his other brothers, and all who had taken part in it. Thus, the conspiracy was suppressed. [...the grandson of Kavad I] was very young and escaped death through the wisdom and compassion of Khanaranges Adergadunbades <note>a Persian title</note>, who was later put to death for this by Chosroës <note>Khosrow I, the King of Persia</note>.

The people of Rome <note>referring here to Constantinople, the "New Rome"</note> also rose against Justinian and declared Hypatius, the nephew of the former Emperor Anastasius, as emperor against his will. The uprising originated from the circus factions <note>rival groups of chariot racing fans, the Blues and the Greens, who turned into political militias</note> during the Nika Riots of 532. By Justinian’s order, the generals Belisarius and Mundus put Hypatius to death along with a number of conspirators and 30,000 of the common people. Justinian also had the support of his nephews, Boraïdes and Justus. 

In the same book, Procopius provides an account of the greedy and cunning Tribonian, a Pamphylian by birth, who held the office of <term>quaestor</term> <note>the senior legal advisor to the emperor</note>. He also writes of John, the prefect of Cappadocia, who was notorious for his wickedness, greed, drunkenness, and every kind of vice. Procopius relates how Antonina, the wife of Belisarius, used John’s daughter Euphemia to deceive him and convict him of conspiring against the emperor. He also describes how John was suspected of the treacherous murder of Eusebius, the Bishop of Cyzicus, which led to John being whipped and shamefully banished.

The contents of the second book are as follows: Chosroës was anxious to break the treaty with the Romans because their conquest of Libya <note>North Africa</note> had made him jealous. He was further encouraged by Witigis, the King of the Goths, who sent an embassy to him consisting of certain Ligurians <note>people from Northern Italy</note> and Bassacus, an Armenian chief. The Armenians had revolted against Rome and joined the Persians after the Roman commander Sittas was killed while fighting them. 

Consequently, Chosroës decided to break the "endless" truce and wage war on the Romans. When Justinian heard of this, he sent an envoy named Anastasius to Chosroës to advise him against it. In the meantime, Witigis had been taken prisoner by Belisarius and sent to Constantinople. However, Chosroës crossed the Roman frontiers anyway, captured the town of Sura, and enslaved its inhabitants. Soon afterward, Candidus, the Bishop of Sergiopolis, offered to ransom the 1,200 captives. Chosroës let them go free after receiving a promissory note for two <term>centenars</term> <note>original: "centenars"; a Roman unit of weight, here referring to a massive sum of gold (roughly 200 pounds)</note>. But when Candidus failed to keep his promise, he was justly punished. Chosroës next marched against Hierapolis and was preparing to besiege it when the Bishop of Beroea offered him 2,000 pounds of silver to abandon the blockade. Chosroës agreed and even promised to leave...

<meta>This page continues the account of the Persian King Chosroës I’s invasion of the Eastern Roman (Byzantine) Empire in 540 AD. It describes the fall of major cities like Beroea and Antioch, the extraction of tribute, and the legendary history of Edessa.</meta>

# PROCOPIUS OF CAESAREA

Meanwhile, Buzes, the general of the East, did not believe himself strong enough to oppose Chosroës <note>Khosrow I, the King of Persia</note> and kept moving from place to place to avoid him. Chosroës then advanced to Beroea <note>modern Aleppo, Syria</note>, where he demanded a ransom of 2,000 pounds of silver from the inhabitants. He later increased this demand to twice that amount; when the citizens failed to pay, he launched a vigorous siege against the city. 

However, Megas, the bishop of Beroea, appealed to Chosroës and convinced him to let the inhabitants go free, allowing them to travel wherever they wished. Many of the Roman soldiers voluntarily defected to Chosroës because their wages were heavily in arrears. In the same way, Chosroës demanded a ransom from the people of Antioch, and when they refused, he besieged the city. While he was attacking fiercely, the commanders Theoctistus and Malatzes <note>original: Molatzes</note>, along with the soldiers from Lebanon, secretly escaped from the city and fled. Consequently, Chosroës easily forced Antioch to surrender and reduced its inhabitants to slavery. 

Roman ambassadors were then sent to him: John, the son of Rufinus, and Julian, his private secretary. After a long negotiation over terms, it was agreed that the Romans would pay Chosroës 5,000 pounds of gold immediately and 500 pounds every year in the future as tribute. In return, Chosroës promised to stop raiding Roman territory, and ambassadors were to be sent from Emperor Justinian to confirm the agreement. 

Despite this arrangement, Chosroës proceeded to Apamea and demanded a large sum of money from its bishop, Thomas. Ultimately, he seized all the sacred vessels and religious offerings and left the town. It was here that the miracle of the precious and life-giving cross took place <note>Apamea was believed to possess a fragment of the True Cross</note>. Chosroës is also said to have attended the circus games <note>chariot races</note> in that same city. He then moved on to Chalcis and, after extracting 200 pounds of gold from the inhabitants, he retired without besieging it. 

He next visited Edessa, from which he extracted the same amount of gold, but he left this city unbesieged as well, having been alarmed by certain divine warnings. Edessa is the city famously connected to the story of Augarus <note>usually known as Abgar(us), likely a title. This Abgar ruled from 4 B.C. to A.D. 50. Legend says he wrote to Christ asking for a cure for a disease. Christ promised to send a disciple after His ascension; Thaddeus was sent by the Apostle Thomas, and Abgar was cured.</note> and the miracle performed by Christ.

# PROCOPIUS OF CAESAREA

Augarus <note>Abgar V of Edessa</note> was a great friend of Augustus, at whose court he remained for some time, and only succeeded through a stratagem <note>a clever plan or trick</note> in obtaining permission to return to his own country. Chosroës <note>Khosrow I, the King of Persia</note> then left Edessa. At the same time, Justinian wrote to his ambassadors, expressing his readiness to confirm the treaty. Nevertheless, Chosroës extracted a ransom from Constantina and, proceeding to Daras, began to besiege it. Its commander, Martin, prepared to defend it; seeing no hope of taking it by siege, Chosroës retired to his own country after being paid 1,000 pounds of silver. The inhabitants of Antioch were all transferred to a city called "Antioch of Chosroës," which he had built in Assyria, a day’s journey from Ctesiphon. He treated them with great kindness and favor, ordered that they should be exempt from all legal jurisdiction except his own, built a hippodrome <note>a stadium for horse and chariot racing</note>, and provided them with other amusements.

Belisarius, who had been recalled from Italy to take command, set out against Chosroës at the beginning of spring. On his arrival in Mesopotamia, he armed and encouraged the soldiers, who were almost without equipment and dreaded the reputation of the Persians. Chosroës, at the invitation of the Lazians—who, along with their ruler Gubazes <gloss>1</gloss>, had joined the Persians because of the extortions and corruption <note>original: "jobbery"</note> of the Roman commandant John <gloss>2</gloss>—rapidly advanced against Petra, a city of Colchis on the shore of the Euxine <note>the Black Sea</note>. As long as John was in command of the fortress, the siege was unsuccessful; however, after he was killed by a shot to the neck, the city surrendered. The inhabitants were allowed to depart unharmed according to an agreement. Only the large amount of money John had accumulated through his monopoly was seized by Chosroës. 

In the meantime, Belisarius, after a failed attack on Nisibis, laid siege to the fortress of Sisauranum and compelled it to surrender. Its commander, Blischames <gloss>3</gloss>, and the most distinguished Persians were taken prisoner and sent to Byzantium <note>modern Istanbul</note>. Arethas <note>al-Harith ibn Jabalah, leader of the Ghassanid Arabs</note> also, who had been sent with an army against the Assyrians, ravaged their country; however, his companions, who had secured large sums of loot, refused to return to Belisarius. The latter, whose army had been struck by sickness, was unaware of what Arethas had done. Recithangus and Theoctistus were eager to return home to defend Phoenicia, which was being

### LXIV

Read the *History of Theophanes of Byzantium*¹ in ten books. The first book begins with the war against the Persians, which broke out after the treaty concluded between Justinian and Khosrow <note>original: "Chosroës"</note> was renounced by Khosrow himself and by Justin II, the successor of Justinian, at the end of the second year of his reign. The history begins at this point and continues down to the tenth year of the war. In the first book, the author also mentions that he has written another work on the history of Justinian; indeed, it is evident that he added other books to these ten. In this book, he narrates how the treaty was annulled. Justin sent Comentiolus to demand the return of Suania <note>a mountainous region in modern-day Georgia</note> from Khosrow, who promised to give it back but did not keep his promise. At this time, there was also a severe earthquake throughout Mesopotamia, a prelude to the calamities that were to come.

The Turks—formerly called Massagetae, and known by the Persians as the Kirmikhiones—who live to the east of the Tanaïs <note>the River Don</note>, sent an embassy with gifts to the Emperor Justin at this time, beseeching him not to receive the Avars <note>a nomadic group migrating from the east</note>. Justin accepted the gifts, received the ambassadors kindly, and dismissed them. When the Avars subsequently approached Justin, requesting permission to live in Pannonia <note>a Roman province including parts of modern-day Hungary, Austria, and Croatia</note> and desiring to conclude a peace treaty, he refused, owing to the agreement he had made with the Turks.

During the reign of Justinian, a certain Persian visitor to...

¹ Nothing is known of the author. His history treated the events of 566–581, containing the reigns of Justin II and Tiberius II. The fragment here given is important for the account of the introduction of silkworm breeding into Constantinople during the reign of Justinian, and for the first mention of the Turks (see Gibbon, chapter 45; J. # THEOPHANES OF BYZANTIUM

Byzantium, he explained to the emperor the art of rearing silkworms, which had until then been unknown to the Romans. This Persian, who had come from the land of the Seres <note>original: "Seres"; this refers to China</note>, concealed the eggs of the silkworms in a hollow cane and brought them to Byzantium. At the beginning of spring, the eggs were placed on mulberry leaves. After hatching, the worms fed on these leaves and later turned into moths, which spun the silk. When King Justin later showed the Turks how the worms were bred and how silk was produced, he greatly surprised them; at that time, the Turks controlled the markets and harbors of the Chinese, which had previously been held by the Persians. 

Ephthalanus, king of the Hephthalites <note>original: "Ephthalites"; a nomadic confederation also known as the White Huns</note> (from whom the tribe derived its name), defeated Peroz <note>original: "Perozes"</note> and the Persians, drove them out of their territory, and took possession of it. However, the Hephthalites themselves were shortly afterward defeated and dispossessed by the Turks. Zemarchus, an ambassador sent by Justin to the Turks, entertained them at a magnificent banquet and, having been received with every kindness, returned home. 

Khosrow <note>original: "Chosroës"</note> then marched against the Ethiopians—formerly called the Macrobii <note>original: "Macrobii," a Greek term meaning the "long-lived"</note> and at that time known as the Himyarites <note>original: "Homerites"; a kingdom in modern-day Yemen</note>—who were on friendly terms with the Romans. With the aid of Miranes <note>This appears to be used here as a proper name, though it was often a title for members of the Persian House of Mihran</note>, the Persian general, he captured Sanaturces, king of the Himyarites, sacked their city, and enslaved the inhabitants. 

The author also relates how the Armenians were ill-treated by Suren <note>original: "Surenas"</note>, especially regarding their religion. They entered into a conspiracy with Vardan <note>original: "Vardanes"</note> (whose brother Manuel had been put to death by Suren) and a man named Vardus. They killed Suren, revolted against the Persians, and defected to the Romans, leaving the town of Dvin <note>original: "Dubios"</note> where they lived and crossing into Roman territory. This was the primary reason the Persians broke their treaty with the Romans. Immediately afterward, the Iberians <note>A kingdom in the Caucasus, located in modern-day Georgia</note> also revolted and, along with their king Gurgenes, defected to the Romans. At that time, Tbilisi <note>original: "Tiphilis"</note> was the capital of Iberia.

Marcian, a cousin of the Emperor Justin who had been appointed commander in the East, was sent against Khosrow in the eighth year of Justin’s reign. John, the general of Armenia, and Miranes, the Persian leader (who was also called Baramaanes), gathered an army to oppose them.

The Armenians were joined by the Colchians <note>An ancient people living on the eastern coast of the Black Sea.</note>, the Abasgi, and Saroes, king of the Alani. Meanwhile, Miranes <note>A Persian general.</note> was supported by the Sabiri, the Daganes, and the tribe of the Dilmaini <margin>Probably the same as the Dilimnitae.</margin>. Marcian defeated Miranes at Nisibis <note>A strategic fortress city in Upper Mesopotamia, modern-day Nusaybin.</note> and forced him to flee. While 1,200 Persians were killed and seventy taken prisoner, the Roman losses were only seven men. Following this victory, Marcian laid siege to Nisibis. When Khosrow <note>original: "Chosroës"; specifically Khosrow I of the Sassanid Empire.</note> heard of this, he gathered 40,000 cavalry and more than 100,000 infantry and rushed to the city's aid to attack the Romans. In the meantime, Marcian was accused before the emperor of plotting to seize the throne. Emperor Justin, believing the charge was true, removed him from command and appointed Theodore—the son of Justinian, nicknamed "Tzirus"—in his place. This leadership change caused disturbances among the troops; the Romans abandoned the siege, and Khosrow subsequently besieged and captured Daras <note>A vital Roman border fortress.</note>.

### LXV

Read the *Histories* of Theophylact <note>Theophylactus Simocatta. His *Oecumenical History* covers the reign of Emperor Maurice (582–604) and is considered the best source for this period.</note>, a prefect and imperial secretary, consisting of eight books. He was Egyptian by birth. His writing style is not without elegance, but it becomes stilted <note>original: "frigid"</note> and shows a childish <note>original: "puerile"</note> lack of taste because of his excessive use of metaphors and allegorical ideas. Furthermore, his habit of inserting moralizing sentiments at inappropriate times reveals a desire for excessive and unnecessary display. In other respects, he is less deserving of criticism. The history begins with the reign of Emperor Maurice and continues until the rise of Phocas.

In the first book, he provides an account of the proclamation of Maurice as successor by Emperor Tiberius. At that time, John was the Patriarch of Constantinople. Tiberius offered sound advice to Maurice through John the Quaestor <note>A high-ranking legal official in the Byzantine court.</note>, who was appointed to speak to Maurice and the people on the emperor’s behalf. Tiberius betrothed his daughter to Maurice and died the day after the proclamation. Just before

Before his death, he saw a vision and heard a voice saying:

> “Thus says the Trinity to you, O Tiberius; the tyrannical times of impiety shall not arrive during your reign.”

These words were a prediction of the tragedy of the impious and tyrannical reign of the cursed Phocas <note>Phocas was a Byzantine Emperor who usurped the throne from Maurice in 602 AD; his reign was noted for its brutality.</note>. Maurice made peace with the Avars, who a short time earlier had laid siege to Sirmium <note>A major Roman city in Pannonia, modern-day Sremska Mitrovica, Serbia.</note>, and agreed to pay the barbarians 80,000 pieces of gold yearly in shipments of garments and money. The treaty was kept for two years, but it was broken due to the greed of the barbarians, who demanded 20,000 more pieces of gold. This led to the collapse of the truce: Singidunum <note>original: "Singidum"; modern Belgrade.</note>, Augusta, and Viminacium were captured by the barbarians, and Anchialus was besieged. Elpidius and Comentiolus, who were sent as ambassadors from Rome <note>The Eastern Roman or Byzantine Empire.</note> to the *chagan* <gloss>the title given to the princes of the Avars and other Turkic tribes</gloss> of the Avars, were treated with insults because Comentiolus had spoken too bluntly to the barbarian leader. In the following year, Elpidius was sent again to the *chagan* with an offer to pay the additional 20,000 pieces of gold; he returned to Byzantium <note>Constantinople.</note> with Targitius, the representative of the Avars, to ratify the agreement. After the barbarians had plundered much Roman territory, Targitius was banished to the island of Chalcis for six months. Comentiolus was later appointed to lead the command against the Slavs and greatly distinguished himself. The *chagan* violated the truce again because of the incident involving Boukolobras <gloss>also spelled Bucolabra</gloss> the magus <note>original: "magian"; a term used for a practitioner of magic or a Zoroastrian priest.</note>, and many Roman towns were devastated.

The battle between the Romans and Persians at the river Nymphius <note>The Batman River in modern Turkey.</note> and the marriage of Maurice and Constantina, the daughter of Tiberius, are described next. Then the text mentions the fire that broke out in the forum at the beginning of Maurice’s reign; the execution of Paulinus and the miracle of the basin of Glyceria the martyr <gloss>The holy ointment or oil that trickled from the bones of the martyr stopped flowing when the bishop of Heraclea replaced the basin it dripped into with a different one.</gloss>; how the patriarch John, when the emperor seemed inclined toward mercy, insisted that the magician should be handed over to the flames, appealing to the words of the Apostle <note>Likely referring to Saint Paul's biblical teachings on sorcery.</note>; and how Paulinus and his son, who had participated in the crime, were put to death <gloss>Paulinus was crucified and his son’s head was cut off.</gloss>. Finally, it describes the conflict over the fortresses of Aphumon and Acbas. The battle between the Romans and...

The Persians and how John was defeated by the cunning of the barbarians. The great earthquake which took place at the beginning of Maurice’s reign, and an account of his consulship <note>The Roman consulship was by this time a largely ceremonial title, usually held by the Emperor himself.</note>. The appointment of Philippicus, the husband of the emperor’s sister, to be commander in the East, and his valiant deeds. The withdrawal of the Romans from Media, during which the army suffered greatly from a lack of water. How Philippicus handed over the district of Arzanene <note>A region in Upper Mesopotamia, often a frontier between the Roman and Persian empires.</note> to his army to plunder, and the bravery of the Romans. The devastation of the country around Martyropolis by the Persians, and their first and second embassy to the Romans. Such are the contents of the first book.

The second book describes Mount Izala and the haughtiness of Cardarigan <gloss>1</gloss>, the Persian commander. It details the battle between the Romans under Philippicus and the Persians under Cardarigan at Arzamon. It describes how Philippicus, carrying the image "not made with hands" <note>original: *Acheiropoieta* — icons or images believed to have come into existence miraculously rather than being created by a human painter.</note>, marched through the camp and consecrated the army; how the Romans won a glorious victory; and how the image was sent with all due reverence to Simeon, Bishop of Amida. 

The territory of the barbarians was plundered by the Romans. Cardarigan fled for refuge to Daras, but the inhabitants refused to admit him because he had been defeated. The book records how a Roman soldier belonging to the "Fourth Parthian" legion (the name for those quartered at Beroea in Syria) was brought into camp dying of wounds. It describes the expedition of the Romans against Arzanene and the desertion of its commanders, Maruthas and Jovius, to Philippicus. 

The text covers the private forces raised by Cardarigan to deceive the Romans and the marvelous escape of Heraclius (the father of the Heraclius who was afterwards emperor), who was sent by Philippicus on a scouting <note>original: "reconnoitring"</note> expedition. It mentions Zabertas the Persian and the abandonment of the siege of Chlomaron by the Romans. It details the headlong and inexplicable flight of Philippicus and the subsequent confusion in the Roman army. 

Philippicus was stricken by disease, and the command was taken over by Heraclius. The Roman vice-commander attacked the Southern Persians, and at the beginning of spring, the Romans invaded Persian territory. Comentiolus set out against the Avars, having under him Martin and Castus, who distinguished themselves against the enemy. Castus was taken prisoner. Ansimuth, commander of the infantry in Thrace, was captured by the Avars, who سپس overrun Thrace.

He hesitates to attack the enemy; speeches are given for and against the plan at a council of war. A false alarm, which also spread to the ranks of the enemy, ruins Comentiolus's plan to attack the Chagan <note>original: "chagan" — A title for the ruler of the Avars, similar to "Khan".</note>. The text describes how a soldier named Busas, who had been captured by the enemy while hunting and left to his fate by his countrymen, taught the barbarians how to build siege engines. The unsuccessful sieges of Beroea and Diocletianopolis by the Chagan are recorded. The Emperor Maurice is insulted by the Byzantine mob because of the disasters brought upon Europe by the raids of the barbarians. John, nicknamed Mystacon <note>original: "Mystakon" — "The Bearded".</note>, is appointed by Maurice to the command in Thrace, with Drocton as second-in-command; when Adrianople was besieged by the Avars, Drocton attacked them and saved the city. Heraclius attacks a Persian stronghold. The fortress of Beiudaes is taken by the distinguished bravery of Sapir. Philippicus returns to the imperial city.

The third book begins with the appointment of Priscus to the command of the East in place of Philippicus. The latter, jealous of Priscus, persuades the emperor to announce a reduction in the army's rations. When Priscus approached the camp, he did not dismount from his horse to greet the army, as was the usual custom. This insult and the reduction of their rations caused a mutiny among the soldiers. Priscus then gave the image "made without hands" <note>original: "Acheiropoieta" — an icon believed to have been created miraculously rather than by human hands.</note> to Elifredas <gloss>1</gloss> <note>Others take the word to mean the image itself. Elifredas (or Ilifredas) is the name Wilfrid.</note> and endeavored to calm the soldiers by displaying it, but they pelted it with stones. Priscus takes refuge in Constantina, and Germanus is chosen as commander by the army against his will. 

While these disturbances were taking place, the Persians greatly harassed the Romans. The emperor accordingly stripped Priscus of his command and reappointed Philippicus. However, the army mutinied against him as well. Constantina is besieged by the Persians but is relieved by Germanus. At the Battle of Martyropolis, the Romans gain a brilliant victory over the Persians; their general Maruzas and 3,000 men were killed, and 1,000 were taken prisoner. The army is reconciled to the emperor through the efforts of Aristobulus. The Roman prisoners in the fortress of Giligerdon show great gallantry. Gregory, the Patriarch of Antioch, restores friendly relations between Philippicus and the army. Martyropolis is captured by the Persians due to the treachery of—

Sittas. Philippicus is replaced in the command against Persia by Comentiolus. The Getae (or Slavs) ravage the borders of Thrace. Rome takes up arms against the Lombards. Under the command of Comentiolus, the Romans engage the Persians at Sisarbanum near Nisibis; the Romans, fighting with great bravery, are victorious. Heraclius <note>Likely Heraclius the Elder, father of the future emperor.</note> distinguishes himself greatly in the engagement. The Persian commander Phraates is killed, and much booty is taken from the enemy. The defeat of the Turks by Baram <note>Bahram Chobin, a famous Persian general.</note>, who secured great and valuable spoils for King Hormisdas. Baram then takes up the sword against Suania. The Romans, under the command of Romanus, attack Baram and his army, inflicting a severe defeat upon him. Thereupon, Hormisdas insults Baram by sending him a woman's garment; Baram returns the insult by addressing a letter to Hormisdas as "daughter," not "son," of Chosroës. The Armenians, prompted by Symbatius, kill their commander John and prepare to defect to the Persians. Comentiolus, being sent by the emperor, puts down the mutiny and carries Symbatius off to Byzantium. He is condemned to be thrown to the wild beasts, but his life is spared by the clemency of the emperor. Hormisdas sends Sarames against Baram. Baram defeats Sarames, causes him to be trampled by an elephant, and openly revolts against the king. Before he meditated revolt, Baram had become so powerful that he was considered next in rank to the king and held what the Romans call the office of *palace master* <note>original: "curopalates" — A high-ranking Byzantine court title, literally the one in charge of the palace.</note>. The narrative returns to the events of earlier times, and a brief account is given of what took place in the reigns of Justin and Tiberius, of the cruelty of Hormisdas the Persian king, and the origin of his family. This concludes the contents of the third book.

The fourth book relates the spread of civil war among the Persians, as well as their victories and successes during the rule of Baram. The murder of Pherochanes. Zadespras <note>Also known as Zadesprates.</note> goes over to the enemy. Hormisdas, deprived of the throne by Bindoës, is allowed to plead his case while in chains. After Bindoës has replied, the son of Hormisdas and the queen are put to death and cut to pieces before his eyes; Hormisdas himself is blinded and subsequently beaten to death by order of his son Chosroës, who was chosen to succeed him. The vigorous rule of Baram.

The seventh book relates the disturbances among the soldiers and their bravery against the Slavs (or <term>Getae</term> <note>An ancient tribal name formerly used to describe the people in this region</note>, as they were formerly called). The death of John the Faster, Patriarch of Constantinople <note>He served from 582–595. Of humble origin, he was known for his piety and asceticism, but was also described as having a harsh disposition. His use of the title "Ecumenical Patriarch" led to conflict with Pelagius II and Gregory I, the bishops of Rome. Four of his works survive today.</note>. Concerning the money lent to him by Emperor Maurice, for which he gave a formal bond. The great respect shown by the truly pious emperor for the ragged garments left behind by the patriarch.

The Turkish civil war. An account of their constitution, manners, and customs. An oration regarding Maurice. The soldiers were punished by divine providence for their crime against Maurice; not one of those who participated in the rebellion survived for long afterward. They all died, some from disease, others by fire from heaven <note>Likely lightning or a perceived supernatural event.</note>, and others by the sword. When Heraclius <note>The future Emperor Heraclius (reigned 610–641), who eventually overthrew Phocas.</note> decided to wage war against Razates, king of the Persians, and gathered his army, he found only two men left alive who had supported the usurper Phocas. After those men were gone, the Romans began to prove themselves superior to the Persians; however, as long as any of the original mutineers remained alive, the enemy always claimed victory.

Theodosius, the son of Maurice, was killed by Alexander on the orders of Phocas, along with Peter, Comentiolus, and Constantine Lardys. There was a false report that Theodosius had not actually been executed. 

The text describes how the statues in Alexandria, in the district known as the Tychaeum, moved on their own accord. This event announced what had happened in Byzantium <note>Constantinople</note> to a scribe as he was returning home after supper. 

Maurice is said to have cancelled one-third of the taxes <term>tribute</term> for his subjects and to have given thirty talents to the citizens of Constantinople for the repair of the aqueducts. He was also noted for his generous treatment of scholars and students. 

The author describes the miraculous events regarding the "bloody flow" <note>original: "bloody flux." This refers to a miracle where blood or fragrant oil was said to flow from the relics of a saint.</note> of the martyr Euphemia. Maurice, who initially doubted the miracle and wished to test it, eventually found it to be authentic. 

The history details how Phocas imprisoned Maurice's wife and daughter in a private house. Phocas sent an unsuccessful embassy to Chosroës II, the King of Persia. Chosroës broke the treaty with the Romans, pretending that it was his sacred duty to avenge the murdered Maurice. Thus, the ambassador Lilius returned without having succeeded in his mission. 

Finally, the text records the murder of Alexander, who had originally conspired with Phocas against Maurice. Phocas killed him on the suspicion that he had saved the life of Maurice's son, Theodosius, even though Alexander had actually carried out the murder. This concludes the history.

## LXVI

Read the *Historical Brief* <note>original: "Historical Epitome"</note> of Nicephorus, Patriarch of Constantinople.^1 It begins with the death of Maurice and—

<meta>This page concludes the review of the *Short History* by Nicephorus, Patriarch of Constantinople (covering 602–769), and begins the summary of the *History* by Sergius the Confessor and the *Historical Epitome* by Cephalion.</meta>

<page-num>87</page-num>
<center>SERGIUS CONFESSOR</center>

[The history of Nicephorus] continues down to the marriage of Leo and Irene <note>Leo IV (reigned 775–780) and Irene of Athens. Their marriage took place in 768.</note>. His style is clear and free from unnecessary repetition; his choice of words is excellent, and the composition is neither too loose nor too dense, but exactly what a true and perfect orator would employ. He avoids using new or strange terms, yet at the same time, he is perhaps too fond of using old-fashioned words and an overly elaborate, polished style. His language is pleasant and not without charm. Generally speaking, he outshines all the historians who came before him; his only flaw is an excessive brevity, which some might feel prevents his work from being entirely enjoyable.

## LXVII

Read the *History* of Sergius the Confessor <note>Sergius's work is now lost. The title "Confessor" suggests he suffered for his faith during the Iconoclast controversies. He was likely the Sergius exiled by Emperor Leo III.</note>. It begins with the reign of the emperor Michael <note>Michael II, known as "the Stammerer" (reigned 820–829).</note>, and then looks back at the lawless and disgusting acts of Copronymus <note>Constantine V (reigned 741–775). His nickname "Copronymus" literally means "named from dung," a slur given to him by his religious enemies.</note>. Political and church events down to the eighth year of Michael's reign are narrated in chronological order; his military successes and his views on religious matters are described in detail.

The style is exceptionally clear and simple, both in the meaning of the words and the overall arrangement, which gives an impression of natural flow. The language, full of natural charm, is not marked by the forced changes in form that come from over-editing. In keeping with his plan, he has maintained the style best suited for a history of the Church.

--- Page 94 ---
<meta>This page continues the review of the "Historical Epitome" by Cephalion and begins the entry for Hesychius Illustrius’s "History of Roman and General History."</meta>

<page-num>88</page-num>
<header>HESYCHIUS ILLUSTRIUS</header>

[The history begins with] the reign of Ninus and Semiramis <note>original: "Ninus and Semiramis." In legend, the founders of the city of Nineveh.</note> and goes down to the times of Alexander the Great. The entire history comprises nine books, named after the nine Muses—Clio, Thalia, Polyhymnia, Melpomene, Terpsichore, Euterpe, Calliope, Erato, and Urania—in which the deeds of Alexander, King of Macedon, are also recorded.

He writes in the Ionic dialect. The extreme brevity with which he describes the bare facts of history leaves no room for admiration or imitation. He says nothing about his ancestry or family, passing over them in silence, just as he claims Homer did. However, it appears that he wrote the work during his exile in Sicily. While saying nothing about his family, as he ought to have done, he mentions his exile—an indication of a petty spirit. His boasting about the number of authors he consulted while compiling his history shows a mind unable to shake off a childish love of display. He claims that the first book of his history was compiled from 570 books, and he provides the names of 31 of those authors; the second book was from 208 books, with 25 authors named; the third from 600, with 26 authors named; the fourth from 850, with 36 authors named; the fifth from 200, with 26 authors named, and so on. Such is the *History* of Cephalion.

### LXIX

<margin>></margin>
Read the *History* of Hesychius Illustrius <note>Lived during the reign of Justinian I (reigned 527–565). He wrote a world history, a work on the early years of Justinian's reign, and the "Onomatologos," a biographical dictionary of famous authors. "Illustrius" (Illustris) was a high-ranking title in the Roman imperial service.</note>, son of Hesychius and Sophia, a Milesian by birth. It is a type of summary of world history, as is shown by the title—*History of Roman and General History*. It begins with the reign of Belus, King of Assyria, and continues down to the death of Anastasius <note>Anastasius I, Emperor of the Eastern Roman Empire, who died in 518.</note>, Emperor of Rome.

His style is concise and elegant; his language is clear though ornate, and the composition is appropriately sophisticated. He is especially careful in his choice of words. His mode of expression is distinct and emphatic, and he charms <note>The original text is damaged or unclear at this point.</note> the reader...

--- Page 95 ---
<meta>This page concludes the review of Hesychius Illustrius’s world history and his shorter work on the emperors Justin and Justinian, then begins the entry for the "Library of History" by Diodorus Siculus.</meta>

<page-num>89</page-num>
<header>DIODORUS SICULUS</header>

...by his figures of speech <note>original: "figures of speech," referring to rhetorical ornaments or metaphors</note>. These, however, do not prevent events from being described as clearly as if he had made no use of such devices—or perhaps even more clearly. He also declares his intention to adhere strictly to the truth.

The work is divided into six parts:
*   The first part contains the narrative of events preceding the Trojan War.
*   The second covers events from the capture of Troy to the foundation of Rome.
*   The third describes events from the foundation of Rome to the time when the appointment of consuls ended the monarchy, in the 68th Olympiad <note>Approximately 508–505 BC.</note>.
*   The fourth covers events from the government of the consuls to the 182nd Olympiad <note>Approximately 52–49 BC.</note>, when Julius Caesar became sole emperor and the office of consul was effectively abolished.
*   The fifth records events that took place under the rule of Julius Caesar down to the time when the glory of Byzantium <note>Byzantium was renamed Constantinople in 330 AD by Constantine the Great, becoming the capital of the empire.</note> reached its height, at the beginning of the 277th Olympiad.
*   The sixth begins with the time when Byzantium had the good fortune of having Constantine for its emperor, and continues down to the death of Anastasius. The author (for reasons I do not understand) praises Anastasius as being superior to many of his predecessors in clemency and mildness. His death took place in the eleventh indiction <note>An "indiction" was a 15-year tax cycle used for dating; the "eleventh indiction" here corresponds to 518 AD.</note>, during the year Magnus was sole consul. The total period of time covered by this history is 1,190 years.

I also read another book by the same author, containing the events of the reign of the Emperor Justin <note>Justin I, reigned 518–527 AD.</note>. It relates how, upon the death of Anastasius, Justin was chosen to succeed him, and how Justin was then succeeded by Justinian <note>Justinian I, reigned 527–565 AD.</note>. It also describes various events that occurred during the early years of Justinian's reign. However, the author was prevented from writing more by the death of his son John, which so deeply affected him that he was unable to devote himself to further study or literary work.

### LXX (70)

Read the forty books of Diodorus Siculus <note>Diodorus Siculus (of Agyrium in Sicily) lived during the 1st century BC in the time of Julius Caesar and Augustus. He spent thirty years traveling and writing his "Library of History."</note>, containing a...

<summary>This page outlines the structure of Hesychius Illustrius’s six-part history, ending with the reign of Anastasius, and begins the review of Diodorus Siculus's massive historical collection.</summary>

<keywords>Hesychius Illustrius, Byzantine history, Olympiads, Constantine the Great, Emperor Anastasius, Justinian I, indiction, Diodorus Siculus, Roman history</keywords>

--- Page 96 ---
<meta>This page continues the review of Diodorus Siculus's "Library of History," specifically discussing his style, the scope of his 40-book work, and his sources, before beginning the entry for the historian Cassius Dio.</meta>

<page-num>90</page-num>
<header>DIO CASSIUS</header>

...a kind of world history. He provides more detail than Cephalion and Hesychius Illustrius in his description of the same periods. His style is clear, simple, and admirably suited for historical writing. He neither excessively uses Atticisms <note>original: "atticisms"; a style of Greek that imitates the classical vocabulary and grammar of ancient Athens</note> or outdated modes of expression, nor does he sink to the level of common, everyday language. Instead, he takes pleasure in a style halfway between the two, avoiding elaborate rhetorical flourishes. He only uses the language of myth, in the manner of poets, when he relates the legends of gods and heroes.

He begins his history with the mythical ages of the Greeks and the barbarians <note>original: "barbarians"; here meaning non-Greeks, as was standard in ancient terminology</note>, and continues down to the beginning of the war between the Romans and the Celts. This was the time when Gaius Julius Caesar (called "divine" by the Romans because of his mighty deeds) conquered most of the Celtic nations, including the most warlike among them. Diodorus spent thirty years on this history, as he tells us himself, visiting several different countries to obtain information and exposing himself to many dangers. He was a Sicilian from Agyrium; through his long interaction with the Romans, he had become familiar with those people and their language, and he diligently collected accounts of all their major successes and failures.

The entire history is comprised of forty books. In the first six, the events preceding the Trojan War and other legends are described. In the next eleven, he covers the history of the world from the fall of Troy to the death of Alexander the Great. In the remaining twenty-three, he covers events up to the time when war broke out between the Celts and Romans under the leadership of Julius Caesar. Caesar conquered most of their nations—even the most warlike—and extended the Roman Empire to the British Isles, which is where the history ends.

### LXXI

I read the *History* of Cassius Dio Cocceianus <note>original: "Cassianus Coccianus (or Coccius) Dio"</note> in eighty books. He begins with the arrival of Aeneas in Italy...

---

<margin>Diodorus Siculus certainly lived until after 21 BC. His *Historical Library* in forty books was a world history down to Caesar’s Gallic wars. Only books 1–5 and 11–20 still exist today. The chief value of the work lies in his use of sources whose own works are now lost, the preservation of ancient traditions, and especially his chronological data.</margin>

<margin>Cassius Dio Cocceianus (approximately 155–230 AD), born at Nicaea in Bithynia <note>An ancient region in northwest Asia Minor, modern-day Turkey</note>.</margin>

--- Page 97 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of ancient historians, moving from the conclusion of the entry on Diodorus Siculus to a detailed summary and stylistic critique of Cassius Dio and his "Roman History."</meta>

# DIO CASSIUS

The history begins with the journey to Italy from Troy, the foundation of Alba Longa and Rome, and continues without a break until the murder of the emperor Antoninus, known as Elagabalus. Because of his many vices, Elagabalus was also called Tiberinus, Sardanapalus, Pseudantoninus, and Assyrius. The author also provides details about the reign of Alexander <note>Alexander Severus, reigned 222–235 AD</note> who, after the death of Antoninus (who had adopted him as his junior partner in the empire), escaped the dangers that threatened him and succeeded to the throne. 

The writer tells us that this Alexander served as consul for the second time alongside the author himself. To honor his colleague, the emperor personally paid the necessary expenses associated with the author's office. The author was appointed governor of Pergamum and Smyrna by Macrinus, and was later the commander of the military forces in Africa. Soon after, he served as governor of Pannonia. After being elected consul for a second time, he was allowed to return home due to an ailment in his feet, spending the rest of his life in Bithynia. This retirement followed the prediction of his "personal spirit" <note>original: "genius"; in Roman religion, a protective spirit assigned to a person at birth</note> that he would remain "beyond the reach of weapons, the shedding of blood, and the roar of battle." <note>original: "beyond the reach of slaughter, bloodshed, and tumult"; a quote from Homer’s Iliad, 11.164</note>

He was born at Nicaea in Bithynia, a city that is partially surrounded by Lake Ascania. His writing style is grand and elevated, reflecting his awareness of the mighty events he records. His language is full of archaic constructions and vocabulary chosen to match the importance of his subject. His sentences are filled with long parenthetical statements and poorly timed reversals of word order. Because his writing is generally clear, the rhythmic flow and the sudden interruptions—which he uses carefully—might go unnoticed by a casual reader. The speeches he includes are excellent; they are modeled after the style of Thucydides <note>an Athenian historian famous for his history of the Peloponnesian War</note> but are easier to understand. In almost every other aspect, Thucydides is his primary model.

***

Cassius Dio was a senator who held various offices under the emperor Commodus and his successors, serving as consul twice. In the year 229, he retired into private life. His history of Rome consists of eighty books, starting from the mythical era and continuing down to the reign of Alexander Severus (ending in 229). Of these eighty books, volumes 37 through 60 have survived almost completely. Books 36 through 80 exist today in an eleventh-century summary <note>original: "abridgment"</note> by the monk John Xiphilinus. In the tenth century, selected passages were collected by order of the Emperor Constantine VII "the Purple-Born," and the twelfth-century writer Zonaras made a summary of books 1 through 20. Cassius Dio's point of view is that of a loyal imperial official who does not understand, and has little sympathy for, the political struggles of earlier Roman history. His belief in omens and the supernatural is very prominent.

1. *Iliad*, 11. 164.
2. The text is corrected to read "untimely" <note>original Greek: akairos</note> instead of "well-timed" <note>original Greek: eukairos</note>.

### 72 <note>original: LXXII</note>

I read the *Persica* <note>The History of Persia</note> by Ctesias of Cnidus <margin>Ctesias of Cnidus was a physician at the Persian court who wrote histories often critical of Herodotus.</margin> which consists of twenty-three books. In the first six books, he discusses Assyrian history and events that took place before the foundation of the Persian Empire; he only begins to address Persian affairs in the seventh book. In books 7 through 13, he provides an account of Cyrus, Cambyses, the Magian <note>a reference to Smerdis, the usurper</note>, Darius, and Xerxes. In these volumes, his account differs almost entirely from that of Herodotus. He even accuses Herodotus of lying in many passages, calling him a writer of myths. 

Ctesias lived later than Herodotus and claims that he was an eyewitness to most of the events he describes. He asserts that where he was not a witness, he obtained his information directly from Persian sources, which he used to compose his history. He disagrees not only with Herodotus but also, in some respects, with Xenophon, the son of Gryllus. Ctesias reached the height of his career during the time of Cyrus the Younger (the son of Darius and Parysatis) and his brother Artaxerxes, who succeeded to the throne.

He begins his narrative by stating that Astyages (whom he also calls Astyigas) was not actually related to Cyrus. He records that Astyages fled from Cyrus to Agbatana <note>original: Agbatana; modern: Ecbatana</note> and hid himself in the vaults of the royal palace with the help of his daughter Amytis and her husband Spitamas. According to Ctesias, when Cyrus came to the throne, he ordered that not only Spitamas and Amytis, but also their sons Spitaces and Megabernes, be tortured to reveal the location of Astyigas. To save his grandchildren from being...

^1 Ctesias was from Cnidus on the coast of Caria in Asia Minor. For seventeen years (from 401 to 384 BC), he served as the court physician to Artaxerxes Mnemon, whom he treated for a wound received at the Battle of Cunaxa. He accuses Herodotus of being a liar, though he himself is often accused of the same. It is worth noting that while writing his *Persica*, Ctesias had the opportunity to consult the Persian archives and local citizens, but he did not have these advantages when writing his *Indica* <note>History of India</note>. He also wrote an account of the Persian imperial revenues, as well as several books of travel and geography.
^2 More commonly known as *Artaxerxes*.

...tortured on his account, he gave himself up. He was taken and loaded with chains by Oebaras <note>Cyrus’s chief advisor and general</note>. Shortly afterward, however, he was set free by Cyrus and honored as if he were Cyrus's own father. Cyrus treated Astyages's daughter, Amytis, with the respect due to a mother, and she later became his wife. Her previous husband, Spitamas, was put to death because he had falsely claimed he did not know where Astyages <note>original: Astyigas</note> was hiding. In his account of these events, Ctesias differs from Herodotus <note>Herodotus claims Cyrus was the grandson of Astyages and does not mention this marriage to Amytis</note>. Ctesias adds that Cyrus made war upon the Bactrians without obtaining a decisive victory; but when they learned that Astyages had been adopted by Cyrus as a father, and Amytis as his mother and wife, they voluntarily submitted to them.

He also relates how Cyrus made war on the Sacae <note>a group of Scythian nomads from Central Asia</note> and took their king, Amorges, prisoner. Amorges was the husband of Sparethra, who, after her husband was captured, collected an army of 300,000 men and 200,000 women. She went to war against Cyrus and defeated him. Among the large number of prisoners taken by the Sacae were Parmises (the brother of Amytis) and his three sons, who were eventually released in exchange for the return of Amorges.

Cyrus, now assisted by Amorges, marched against Croesus and the city of Sardis <note>original: Sardes</note>. On the advice of Oebaras, Cyrus set up wooden figures dressed to look like Persians around the city walls. The sight of these figures so terrified the inhabitants that the city was easily taken. Before this, the son of Croesus had been handed over as a hostage because the king had been deceived by a divine vision. When it became clear that Croesus was meditating treachery, his son was executed before his eyes; the boy's mother, witnessing the execution, committed suicide by throwing herself from the walls. 

After the city was captured, Croesus fled for refuge to the temple of Apollo. He was chained three times, and three times his bonds were invisibly loosened, even though the temple was shut and sealed and Oebaras was standing guard. Other prisoners who had been held with Croesus were beheaded on suspicion that they had conspired to release him. He was subsequently taken to the palace and bound even more securely, but he was again loosened by thunder and lightning sent from heaven. Finally, Cyrus set him free against his own initial will. From then on, Cyrus treated him kindly and granted him a large city near Ecbatana <note>original: Agbatana</note> named Barene, in which...

...there were 5,000 horsemen and 10,000 peltasts <note>original: peltasts; light-armed infantrymen</note>, javelin-throwers, and archers.

Cyrus then sent Petisacas the eunuch <note>a high-ranking court official</note>, who had great influence with him, to Persia to fetch Astyages <note>original: Astyigas</note> from the Barcanians, as both Cyrus and his daughter Amytis were anxious to see him. Oebaras <note>Cyrus’s advisor</note> then advised Petisacas to leave Astyages in some lonely spot to perish of hunger and thirst, which he did. However, the crime was revealed in a dream, and Petisacas—at the urgent request of Amytis—was handed over to her by Cyrus for punishment. She ordered his eyes to be dug out, had him flayed alive, and then crucified. Oebaras, afraid of suffering the same punishment (even though Cyrus assured him that he would not allow it), starved himself to death by fasting for ten days. Astyages was granted a splendid funeral; his body had remained untouched by wild beasts in the wilderness, as some lions had guarded it until it was recovered by Petisacas.

Cyrus then marched against the Derbices, whose king was Amoraeus. The Derbices suddenly brought up several elephants which had been kept in ambush and put Cyrus’s cavalry to flight. Cyrus himself fell from his horse, and an Indian soldier wounded him mortally with a javelin under the thigh. The Indians were fighting on the side of the Derbices and had supplied them with the elephants. Cyrus’s friends rescued him while he was still alive and returned to their camp. Many Persians and Derbices were slain, numbering about 10,000 on each side.

Amorges <note>the king of the Sacae who had become Cyrus's ally</note>, when he heard of what had happened to Cyrus, went in great haste to assist the Persians with 20,000 Sacan cavalry. In a subsequent engagement, the Persians and Sacae gained a brilliant victory, and Amoraeus, the king of the Derbices, was killed along with his two sons. Thirty thousand Derbicans and 9,000 Persians fell in the battle. Following this, the country submitted to Cyrus.

Cyrus, as he neared his death, declared his elder son Cambyses as king. He appointed his younger son, Tanyoxarces <note>original: Tanyoxarkés</note>, as the governor of Bactria, Choramnia, Parthia, and Carmania, and decreed that these lands should be free from paying tribute. Of the children of Spitamas <note>Amytis’s first husband</note>, he appointed Spitaces as the satrap <note>a provincial governor</note> of the Derbices and Megabernes as the satrap of the Barcanians, ordering them to obey their mother in everything. Cyrus endeavored to reconcile his people with Amorges, giving his blessing to those who remained on friendly terms with one another and a curse upon those who were the first to do wrong. He died with these words, three days after being wounded, following a reign of thirty years. This marks the end of the eleventh book.

The twelfth book begins with the reign of Cambyses. Immediately after taking the throne, he sent his father's body to Persia for burial by the eunuch <note>a high-ranking, castrated court official often entrusted with sensitive tasks</note> Bagapates, and in all other respects, he carried out his father's wishes. The men who held the greatest influence over him were Artasyras the Hyrcanian and the eunuchs Izabates, Aspadates, and Bagapates, the latter of whom had become his father's favorite after the death of Petisacas. 

Bagapates was in command of the expedition against Egypt and its king, Amyrtaeus. He defeated the king through the treachery of his chief counselor, the eunuch Combaphis. Combaphis betrayed the locations of the bridges and other important secrets on the condition that Cambyses would make him the governor of Egypt. Cambyses first made this arrangement through Izabates, who was Combaphis's cousin, and later confirmed it with his own personal promise. 

After capturing Amyrtaeus alive, Cambyses did him no harm. Instead, he simply moved him to Susa with 6,000 Egyptians of his own choosing. Consequently, all of Egypt became subject to Cambyses. In the deciding battle, the Egyptians lost 50,000 men, while the Persians lost 7,000.

In the meantime, a certain Magus <note>original: Magian; a member of a hereditary priestly class in ancient Persia</note> called Sphendadates,¹ who had been flogged by Tanyoxarces <note>Cambyses’s brother</note> for some offense, went to Cambyses and informed him that his brother was plotting against him. As proof, he claimed that Tanyoxarces would refuse to come if he were summoned. Cambyses then summoned his brother, who was busy with another matter and delayed his arrival. Because of this, the Magus accused him even more boldly. 

Their mother, Amytis, suspected the Magus and advised Cambyses not to listen to him. Cambyses pretended not to believe the accusations, though in reality, he did. When summoned by Cambyses a third time, Tanyoxarces obeyed. His brother embraced him but nonetheless determined to put him to death. Without the knowledge of their mother Amytis, Cambyses took steps to carry out his plan. The Magus then made the following suggestion: since he himself looked very much like Tanyoxarces, he advised...

<meta>This page continues the narrative of Cambyses II's reign from the twelfth book of Ctesias's Persica, specifically detailing the conspiracy to replace his brother Tanyoxarces with an impostor and the eventual death of Cambyses.</meta>

<page-num>96</page-num>

The king publicly ordered that the Magian's head should be cut off for falsely accusing the king’s brother. However, the plan was that Tanyoxarces should be secretly put to death in the meantime, and the Magian <note>Sphendadates, a member of the Zoroastrian priestly caste</note> should be dressed in his clothes so that Tanyoxarces would be thought to still be alive. Cambyses agreed to this plan. Tanyoxarces was executed by being forced to drink bull’s blood <note>In antiquity, bull's blood was widely, though incorrectly, believed to be a fast-acting poison</note>. The Magian put on the prince's clothes and was mistaken for him by the public. The fraud remained unknown for a long time to everyone except Artasyras, Bagapates, and Izabates, the only men Cambyses had trusted with the secret.

Then Cambyses summoned Labyzus, the chief of Tanyoxarces’s eunuchs, along with the other servants. He showed them the Magian seated and dressed in the style of his brother and asked them if they thought he was Tanyoxarces. Labyzus, in total astonishment, replied, “Who else could we possibly think he is?” The likeness was so perfect that even he was deceived. The Magian was then sent to Bactria <note>an ancient region in Central Asia, modern-day Afghanistan and Tajikistan</note>, where he successfully played the part of Tanyoxarces. Five years later, Amytis <note>the mother of the princes</note> learned the truth from the eunuch Tibethis, whom the Magian had flogged. She demanded that Cambyses hand over the impostor Sphendadates to her for punishment, but he refused. Distraught, Amytis heaped curses upon her son, drank poison, and died.

On one occasion, while Cambyses was performing a religious sacrifice, no blood flowed from the slaughtered animals. This omen greatly alarmed him, and the birth of a son without a head to his wife Roxana increased his fear. The wise men interpreted this portent to mean that he would leave no heir to the throne. His mother also appeared to him in a dream, threatening him with retribution for the murder he had committed, which terrified him even more. Later, in Babylon, while carving a piece of wood with a knife for his own amusement, he accidentally wounded himself in the thigh and died eleven days later, in the eighteenth year of his reign.

Before Cambyses died, Bagapates and Artasyras conspired to raise the Magian to the throne, which they eventually did. Izabates, who had left to transport the body of Cambyses back to Persia, found on his return that the Magian was reigning under the name of Tanyoxarces. He revealed the truth to the army and publicly denounced <note>original: θριαμβεύσας (thriambeúsas) — to triumph over or publicly expose</note> the Magian as an impostor. After this, Izabates took refuge in a temple, but he was seized and put to death.

<meta>This page follows the death of Cambyses II and the rule of the Magian impostor, Sphendadates. It details the conspiracy of the seven Persian noblemen to reclaim the throne and the beginning of Darius the Great’s reign and his Scythian campaign.</meta>

Then seven distinguished Persians conspired against the Magian <note>Sphendadates, the Zoroastrian priest who had been impersonating the king’s brother</note>. Their names were Onophas, Idernes, Norondabates, Mardonius, Barisses, Ataphernes, and Darius Hystaspis. <note>Herodotus gives a different list of names: Intaphernes, Otanes, Gobryas, Hydarnes, Megabyzus, Aspathines, and Darius.</note> After they had exchanged the most solemn oaths of loyalty, they brought Artasyras and Bagapates—who held all the keys to the palace—into their plans. The seven, having been admitted into the palace by Bagapates, found the Magian asleep. At the sight of them he jumped up, but finding no weapon ready at hand (as Bagapates had secretly removed them all), he smashed a chair made of gold and defended himself with one of its legs. However, he was finally stabbed to death by the seven. He had reigned for seven months.

Darius was chosen as king from among the seven conspirators based on a pre-arranged test: his horse was the first to neigh after the sun had risen, a result of a clever trick. <note>According to Herodotus, Darius’s groom, Oebares, ensured this by rubbing his hand against a mare and then letting the horse smell it at the designated time.</note> The Persians celebrate the day on which the Magian was put to death with a festival called *Magophonia* <note>meaning "The Slaughter of the Magi"</note>. Darius ordered a tomb to be built for himself in a mountain with two peaks, but when he wished to go and see it, he was talked out of it by his soothsayers and his parents. His parents, however, were still anxious to make the climb to see it, but the priests who were pulling them up with ropes became terrified at the sight of some snakes; they let go of the ropes, and his parents fell and were dashed to pieces. Darius was deeply grieved and ordered the heads of the forty men responsible for the accident to be cut off.

Darius then ordered Ariaramnes, the <term>satrap</term> <note>a provincial governor in the ancient Persian Empire</note> of Cappadocia, to cross over into Scythia and capture a number of prisoners, both male and female. Ariaramnes crossed the sea in thirty <term>penteconters</term> <note>original: penteconters; ancient galleys with fifty oars</note> and, among others, took Marsagetes captive. Marsagetes was the brother of the Scythian king and had been imprisoned by his own brother for various offenses. The ruler of the Scythians (the *Scytharch*) was enraged and wrote an insulting letter to Darius, who replied in the same aggressive tone. Darius then gathered an army of 800,000 men and crossed the Bosporus and the <note>original: Ister</note> Danube River by a bridge of boats, entering Scythian territory in fifteen days. The two kings sent...

...gave each other a bow in turn. Darius, seeing that the Scythian bow was stronger, turned back and fled across the bridges, destroying some of them in his haste before his entire army had crossed. Eighty thousand of his men, who had been left behind in Europe, were put to death by the ruler of the Scythians. After Darius had crossed the bridge, he set fire to the houses and temples of the Chalcedonians because they had attempted to break down the bridges he had built near their city and had also destroyed the altar he had erected upon crossing in honor of **Zeus the God of Crossings.** <note>original: "Zeus Diabaterios." Zeus as the god of crossing. The "altar" was probably a column set up to commemorate his passage.</note>

Datis, the commander of the Persian fleet, ravaged Greece and the islands on his return from the Black Sea <note>original: "Pontus"</note>. At Marathon he was met by Miltiades; the Persians <note>original: "barbarians"</note> were defeated and Datis himself was killed. The Athenians afterward refused to give up his body at the request of the Persians.

Darius then returned to Persia, where, after having offered sacrifice, he died after an illness of thirty days. He was seventy-two years old and had reigned for thirty-one years. Artasyras and Bagapates also died; the latter had served for seven years as the keeper of Darius's tomb.

Darius was succeeded by his son Xerxes. Artapanus, the son of Artasyras, had as much influence over Xerxes as his father had held over Darius. His other confidential advisers were the elderly Mardonius and the eunuch Matacas. Xerxes married Amestris, the daughter of Onophas, who bore him a son, Dariaeus. Two years later, she bore Hystaspes and Artoxerxes, and two daughters: one named Rhodogune and another called Amytis, named after her grandmother.

Xerxes decided to make war upon Greece because the Chalcedonians had attempted to break down the bridge, as previously stated, and had destroyed the altar which Darius had set up. He also sought revenge because the Athenians had killed Datis and refused to return his body. However, he first visited Babylon, desiring to see the tomb of **Bel** <note>original: "Belitanes." Likely Belus or Marduk. For the story see Aelian, *Varia Historia*, xiii. 3.</note>, which Mardonius showed him. But he was unable to fill the vessel with oil, as had been written in prophecy.

From there he proceeded to Ecbatana <note>original: "Agbatana"</note>, where he heard of the revolt of the Babylonians and the murder of Zopyrus, their governor.

<meta>This page continues the history of the Persian Empire as told by Ctesias, focusing on the transition from the reign of Darius the Great to his son, Xerxes I, and the subsequent invasion of Greece.</meta>

...satrap. Ctesias's account differs from that of Herodotus. The events that Herodotus attributes to Zopyrus are instead credited by Ctesias to Megabyzus—with the exception of the story about his mule giving birth to a foal. Megabyzus was the son-in-law of Xerxes and the husband of his daughter, Amytis. Babylon was captured by Megabyzus; in recognition, Xerxes awarded him several gifts, including a golden hand-mill weighing six talents <note>A "talent" was a heavy unit of weight; six talents would be roughly 150 to 360 pounds depending on the local standard.</note>, which was considered the most prestigious of all royal honors. 

Then Xerxes, having assembled a Persian army of 800,000 men and 1,000 triremes <note>original: "triremes" — ancient warships with three banks of oars.</note> (not counting the chariots), set out against Greece after first building a bridge across the water at Abydos. Demaratus the Spartan, who had arrived there first and accompanied Xerxes across, tried to discourage him from invading Sparta. Xerxes's general, Artapanus, with 10,000 men, fought an engagement at Thermopylae against the Spartan general Leonidas. The Persian host was slaughtered, while only two or three of the Spartans were killed. The king then ordered an attack with 20,000 men, but these were also defeated; though they were whipped into the battle, they were routed once more. The next day, he ordered an attack with 50,000 men, but without success, and he eventually stopped the assaults. 

Thorax of Thessaly and the Trachinian leaders, Calliades and Timaphernes, who were present with their forces, were summoned by Xerxes along with Demaratus and Hegias of Ephesus. They informed him that the Spartans could never be defeated unless they were surrounded. A Persian army of 40,000 men was led by the two Trachinian leaders over a nearly impassable mountain path to the rear of the Lacedaemonians <note>Another name for the Spartans and their neighbors in the region of Laconia.</note>. The Spartans were surrounded and died bravely to the last man.

Xerxes sent another army of 120,000 men against Plataea under the command of Mardonius, at the encouragement of the Thebans. He was opposed by the Spartan leader Pausanias with only 300 Spartiates <note>The elite class of full Spartan citizens who functioned as a high nobility.</note>, 1,000 *perioeki* <note>Free inhabitants of the surrounding towns who enjoyed civil rights but had no political power.</note>, and 6,000 soldiers from other cities. The Persians suffered a massive defeat; Mardonius was wounded and forced to flee. Xerxes later sent him to plunder the temple of Apollo, where he is said to have died from injuries sustained during a violent hailstorm, much to Xerxes's great sorrow.

Xerxes then advanced against Athens itself, its inhabitants...
