<meta>This page continues Ctesias's account of the Persian Wars, detailing the Athenian abandonment of their city, the Greek victory at the Battle of Salamis, and the subsequent palace intrigues in Persia that led to the assassination of Xerxes I and the rise of Artaxerxes I.</meta>

<page-num>100</page-num>

# CTESIAS

...of which they manned 110 <term>triremes</term> <note>Ancient warships with three banks of oars.</note> and took refuge in Salamis. Xerxes took possession of the empty city and set fire to it, with the exception of the <term>Acropolis</term> <note>The "high city" or fortified citadel of Athens.</note>, which was defended by a small band of men who had remained behind. At last, they also made their escape by night, and the Acropolis was burned. After this, Xerxes proceeded to a narrow strip of land in Attica called Heracleum and began to construct an embankment toward Salamis, intending to cross over on foot. On the advice of the Athenians Themistocles and Aristides, archers were summoned from Crete <note>These archers were likely used to hinder the construction of the Persian embankment.</note>. 

Then, a naval battle took place between the Greeks with 700 ships and the Persians with more than 1,000 ships under the command of Onophas. The Athenians were victorious, thanks to the advice and clever strategy of Aristides and Themistocles. The Persians lost 500 ships, and Xerxes fled. In the remaining battles, 12,000 Persians were killed.

Xerxes, having crossed back into Asia and advanced toward Sardis, dispatched Megabyzus to plunder the temple at Delphi. When Megabyzus refused to go, the <term>eunuch</term> <note>A castrated man who served as a high-ranking official in the Persian court.</note> Matacas was sent in his place to insult Apollo and plunder the temple. Having carried out his orders, he returned to Xerxes, who had meanwhile arrived in Persia from Babylon. 

Here, Megabyzus accused his wife Amytis (the daughter of Xerxes) of having committed adultery. Xerxes severely reprimanded her, but she declared that she was not guilty. Artapanus and Aspamitres the eunuch, who were the confidential advisers of Xerxes, resolved to kill their master. After doing so, they persuaded Artaxerxes <note>original: Artoxerxes</note> that his brother Darius <note>original: Dariaeus</note> had murdered him. Darius was taken to the palace of Artaxerxes, and, although he vehemently denied the accusation, he was put to death.

Thus Artaxerxes became king, thanks to Artapanus, who entered into a conspiracy against him with Megabyzus. Megabyzus was bitterly aggrieved at the suspicion of adultery against his wife, and each man took an oath to remain loyal to the other. Nevertheless, Megabyzus revealed the plot. Artapanus's guilty conduct came to light, and he met the same death which he had intended for Artaxerxes. Aspamitres, who had taken part in the murders of Xerxes and Darius, was cruelly put to death by being exposed in "the trough" <note>This refers to scaphism, a Persian execution method where a prisoner was trapped between two boat-like structures and exposed to the sun and insects until they died.</note>. After the death of—

<meta>This page continues Ctesias’s history of the Persian Empire during the reign of Artaxerxes I (r. 465–424 BCE). It follows the bloody transition of power after the assassination of Xerxes I and details the suppression of major revolts in the provinces of Bactria and Egypt.</meta>

<page-num>101</page-num>

# CTESIAS 101

After the downfall of Artapanus <note>Artapanus was the official who assassinated Xerxes I; he was subsequently killed by Artaxerxes I in a counter-coup.</note>, a battle broke out between his fellow-conspirators and the other Persians. In this conflict, the three sons of Artapanus were killed, and Megabyzus <note>A high-ranking Persian general and son-in-law to the royal family.</note> was severely wounded. Artaxerxes, his sisters Amytis and Rhodogune, and their mother Amestris were deeply grieved by his injury. Megabyzus's life was only saved through the skill and care of Apollonides, a physician from Cos.

Bactra <note>The capital of the province of Bactria, located in modern-day Afghanistan.</note> and its <term>satrap</term> <note>A provincial governor in the Persian Empire.</note>—another man also named Artapanus—revolted against Artaxerxes. The first battle was indecisive, but in a second encounter, the Bactrians were defeated because the wind blew directly into their faces. Following this defeat, the entirety of Bactria submitted to the King.

Egypt also revolted under the leadership of Inarus, a Libyan, who was assisted by an Egyptian native. As preparations for war began, the Athenians—at the request of Inarus—sent forty ships to aid the rebellion. Artaxerxes initially wanted to lead the expedition himself, but his advisors dissuaded him. He instead sent his brother Achaemenides with 400,000 infantry and eighty ships. Inarus engaged Achaemenides in battle, and the Egyptians were victorious; Achaemenides was slain by Inarus, and his body was sent back to Artaxerxes. Inarus was equally successful at sea. Charitimides, the commander of the forty Athenian ships, won great glory in a naval engagement where twenty out of fifty Persian ships were captured along with their crews, while the remaining thirty were sunk.

The King then sent Megabyzus against Inarus with an additional army of 200,000 men and 300 ships commanded by Oriscus. Not counting the ships' crews, this combined army consisted of 500,000 men (of the original 400,000 led by Achaemenides, 100,000 had perished). A desperate battle followed with heavy losses on both sides, though the Egyptian casualties were higher. Megabyzus wounded Inarus in the thigh and forced him to flee, allowing the Persians to claim a complete victory. Inarus fled to Byblus <note>A fortified city in the Nile Delta, not to be confused with the city in Phoenicia.</note>, an Egyptian stronghold, accompanied by the Greeks who had survived the battle. Eventually, all of Egypt except for Byblus submitted to Megabyzus. Because this stronghold appeared to be impregnable, Megabyzus came to terms with Inarus and the Greeks (who numbered more than 6,000). He promised that they would suffer no harm from the King and that the Greeks would be allowed to return home whenever they pleased.

<meta>This page continues the history of the Persian Empire under Artaxerxes I. Following the suppression of the Egyptian revolt, the narrative focuses on the fallout between the general Megabyzus and the Persian royal court, leading to a domestic rebellion within the empire.</meta>

<page-num>102</page-num>

# CTESIAS

After appointing Sarsamas as the <term>satrap</term> <note>A provincial governor in the Persian Empire.</note> of Egypt, Megabyzus took Inarus and the Greeks to King Artaxerxes. The king was extremely angry with Inarus because he had killed the king's brother, Achaemenides. Megabyzus explained the situation, reminding the king that he had given his word to Inarus and the Greeks when he occupied Byblos, and he earnestly pleaded with the king to spare their lives. The king eventually agreed, and the news that Inarus and the Greeks would not be harmed was immediately sent to the army.

However, Amestris <note>The Queen Mother, known for her influence and ruthlessness.</note> was bitter that Inarus and the Greeks might escape punishment for the death of her son Achaemenides. She asked the king to turn them over to her for execution, but he refused. She then appealed to Megabyzus, but he also turned her away. At last, through her relentless demands, she obtained her wish from her son the king. After five years, Artaxerxes gave Inarus and the Greeks up to her. Inarus was executed by being impaled on three stakes; fifty of the Greeks—all those she could seize—were beheaded. Megabyzus was deeply grieved by this betrayal of his word. He asked for permission to return to his own province, Syria. Having secretly sent the remaining Greeks there ahead of him, he arrived, collected a massive army (150,000 men, not including cavalry), and raised the banner of rebellion.

Usiris was sent against him with 200,000 men. A battle took place in which Megabyzus and Usiris engaged in single combat and wounded each other. Usiris stabbed Megabyzus in the thigh with a spear to the depth of two fingers; Megabyzus, in turn, wounded Usiris first in the thigh and then in the shoulder, causing him to fall from his horse. As Usiris fell, Megabyzus protected him from further harm and ordered that his life be spared. Many Persians were killed in the battle, in which Zopyrus and Artyphius (the sons of Megabyzus) distinguished themselves, and Megabyzus gained a decisive victory. Usiris received the best medical care and was eventually sent back to Artaxerxes at the king’s request.

Another army was sent against the rebels under the command of Menostanes, the son of Artarius (the <term>satrap</term> of Babylon and brother of King Artaxerxes). Another battle followed in which the royal Persian forces were routed. Megabyzus shot Menostanes with arrows, first in the shoulder and then in the head, though the wounds were not fatal. Nevertheless, Menostanes fled with his army, and Megabyzus gained a brilliant victory. Artarius then sent a message to Megabyzus, advising him to come to terms with the king. Megabyzus replied that he was ready to make peace, but only on the condition that he should not be forced to appear at

...to return to court and should be allowed to remain in his <term>satrapy</term> <note>original: "satrapy"; a province governed by a satrap.</note>. When his answer was reported to the king, the Paphlagonian eunuch Artoxares and Amestris urged the king to make peace without delay. Accordingly, Artarius, his wife Amytis, Artoxares (who was then twenty years old), and Petisas, the son of Usiris and father of Spitamas, were sent to Megabyzus for that purpose. After many entreaties and solemn promises, they eventually succeeded with great difficulty in persuading Megabyzus to visit the king, who finally pardoned him for all his offenses.

Some time afterward, while the king was out hunting, he was attacked by a lion. Megabyzus killed the animal as it reared up and was preparing to rush at the king. The king, furious because Megabyzus had killed the animal before he could, ordered his head to be cut off. However, due to the pleading of Amestris, Amytis, and others, his life was spared, and he was instead banished to Curtae, a town on the <term>Red Sea</term> <note>In ancient texts, this often referred to the Persian Gulf or the Indian Ocean.</note>. Artoxares the eunuch was also banished to Armenia for having often spoken out to the king in favor of Megabyzus. After spending five years in exile, Megabyzus escaped by pretending to be a <term>leper</term> <note>Those with skin diseases were social outcasts whom no one was permitted to approach.</note>, and returned home to Amytis, who hardly recognized him. Through the intercession of Amestris and Amytis, the king reconciled with him and admitted him to his table as he had done before. Megabyzus died at the age of seventy-six, and the king deeply mourned his passing.

After his death, his wife Amytis, following the example of her mother Amestris before her, showed a great fondness for the company of men. When Amytis was suffering from a slight illness, the physician Apollonides of Cos was called in to treat her and fell in love with her. For some time they carried on a secret affair, but she eventually told her mother. She, in turn, informed the king, who left it to her to deal with the offender as she saw fit. As a punishment, Apollonides was kept in chains for two months and then buried alive on the same day that Amytis died.

Zopyrus, the son of Megabyzus and Amytis, rebelled against the king after the death of his father and mother. He visited Athens, where he was well received because of the services his mother had rendered to the Athenians.<sup>1</sup> From Athens, he sailed with some Athenian troops to Caunus and demanded that the city surrender. The inhabitants expressed them—

While Zopyrus was mounting the wall, a Caunian named Alcides hit him on the head with a stone and killed him. The Caunian was crucified by order of Zopyrus's grandmother, Amestris. Some time afterward, Amestris died at a great age, and Artaxerxes <note>original: "Artoxerxes"</note> also died after having reigned for forty-two years. Here the seventeenth book ends.

Artaxerxes was succeeded by his son Xerxes II, his only legitimate son by Damaspia, who died on the same day as her husband. <note>The original text notes a correction here: reading "Artaxerxes" [Ἀρτοξέρξης], not "Xerxes" [Ξέρξης], regarding who died on that day.</note> The bodies of the king and queen were conveyed by Bagorazus to Persia. Artaxerxes had seventeen illegitimate sons, among them Secydianus (by Alogune the Babylonian), Ochus—who later became king—and Arsites (by Cosmartidene, also a Babylonian). Besides these three, he also had a son, Bagapaeus, and a daughter, Parysatis, by Andria, also a Babylonian; Parysatis became the mother of Artaxerxes II and Cyrus. During his father’s lifetime, Ochus was made <term>satrap</term> <note>A provincial governor in the Persian Empire.</note> of Hyrcania and was given in marriage to Parysatis, the daughter of Artaxerxes and his own half-sister.

Secydianus, having won over the eunuch Pharnacyas—who had the greatest influence over Xerxes next to Bagorazus—along with Menostanes and several others, entered the palace after a festival. While Xerxes was lying in a drunken sleep, they put him to death, only forty-five days after the death of his father. The bodies of both father and son were conveyed together to Persia. It is said that the mules drawing the chariot with the father’s body refused to move, as if waiting for the son's body; and when it arrived <note>original: "κατέλαβε" (*katelabe*); some interpret this as "when death overtook him," referring to the son's death.</note>, they immediately moved on rapidly.

Secydianus thus became king and appointed Menostanes as his <term>azabarites</term> <note>A Persian court title, possibly identical to the *azarapates*, a high-ranking official or "gentleman-usher" of the court.</note>. After Bagorazus returned to court, Secydianus—who had a long-standing grudge against him—ordered him to be stoned to death on the pretext that he had left the father’s body in Persia without permission. The army was greatly grieved by this, and although Secydianus distributed large sums of money among the soldiers, they hated him for the murder of his brother Xerxes and now for the death of Bagorazus.

# CTESIAS

Secydianus then summoned Ochus to court. Ochus promised to present himself but failed to do so. After he had been summoned several times, he collected a large force with the obvious intention of seizing the throne. He was joined by Arbarius, the commander of the cavalry, and Arxanes, the <term>satrap</term> <note>A provincial governor in the Persian Empire.</note> of Egypt. The <term>eunuch</term> <note>Castrated men who often held high-ranking administrative or domestic positions in ancient royal courts.</note> Artoxares also arrived from Armenia and placed the crown <note>original Greek: "Κίταρις" (kitaris), also known as a *cidaris* or *tiara*, the formal royal headdress of Persia.</note> on the head of Ochus against his will.

Thus Ochus became king and changed his name to Darius <note>original: "Dariaeus".</note>. At the suggestion of his wife, Parysatis, he attempted to win over Secydianus through trickery and solemn promises. Menostanes did all he could to prevent Secydianus from trusting these promises or coming to terms with those who were trying to deceive him. In spite of this, Secydianus allowed himself to be persuaded. He was arrested and killed by being "thrown into the ashes." <note>The Roman historian Valerius Maximus describes this punishment: an enclosure surrounded by high walls was filled with ashes; a beam projected over it, on which the victims were placed after being given plenty to eat and drink; once they fell asleep, they would tumble from the beam into the treacherous heap below and suffocate.</note> He died after a reign of six months and fifteen days.

Darius <note>original: "Ochus (also called Dariaeus)"</note> thus became the sole ruler. Three eunuchs—Artoxares, Artibarzanes, and Athous—had the greatest influence with him, but his chief adviser was his wife. By her, he had had two children before he became king: a daughter, Amestris, and a son, Arsaces, who was afterwards called Artaxerxes <note>original: "Artoxerxes".</note>. After his accession, she bore him another son, named Cyrus after the sun. <note>In modern Persian the sun is *Khur*, and in the ancient Zend language it is *Hware*.</note> A third son was named Artostes, who was followed by several others, totaling thirteen in all. The writer says that he obtained these particulars from Parysatis herself. Most of the children died soon after birth; the only survivors were those just mentioned and a fourth named Oxendras.

Arsites, the king's own brother by the same father and mother, revolted against him together with Artyphius, the son of Megabyzus. Artasyras was sent against them. After being defeated in two battles, Artasyras gained the victory in a third after he had bribed the Greeks who were serving under Artyphius, so that only three Milesians <note>Greek soldiers from the city of Miletus.</note> remained faithful to him. At length, Artyphius, finding that Arsites did not appear, surrendered to the king after Artasyras had solemnly...

<meta>This page continues the history of the Persian king Darius II (Ochus), focusing on the numerous revolts and court intrigues that defined his reign, specifically involving his wife Parysatis and various satraps.</meta>

<page-num>106</page-num>

# CTESIAS

King Darius II <note>original: "The king"</note> had promised Artyphius that his life would be spared. The king was anxious to execute him immediately, but the queen Parysatis advised him not to do so yet. She wanted to deceive Arsites and induce him to surrender as well; she suggested that once both had surrendered, they could be executed together. The plan succeeded: Artyphius and Arsites both surrendered and were "thrown into the ashes." <note>As described on the previous page, this was a Persian method of execution by suffocation in a pit of ash.</note> The king wished to pardon Arsites, but Parysatis, through her persistent urging, persuaded him to put him to death. Pharnacyas, who had assisted Secydianus in the murder of Xerxes II, was stoned to death. Menostanes was also arrested and condemned, but he committed suicide before he could be executed.

Pissuthnes also revolted, and the king sent Tissaphernes, Spithradates, and Parmises against him. Pissuthnes set out to meet them with Lycon the Athenian and a force of Greek mercenaries. However, the king's generals bribed the Greeks to desert Pissuthnes. Left without support, Pissuthnes surrendered after receiving assurances that his life would be spared. He accompanied Tissaphernes to the royal court, but the king ordered him to be "thrown into the ashes" regardless. The king then gave Pissuthnes’s <term>satrapy</term> <note>A province of the Persian Empire governed by a satrap.</note> to Tissaphernes. Lycon also received several towns and districts as a reward for his treachery.

Artoxares the <term>eunuch</term> <note>Castrated men who held high-ranking positions; they typically lacked the facial hair that symbolized Persian masculinity.</note>, who held great influence over the king, plotted to seize the throne for himself. He ordered his wife to make him a false beard and mustache so that he might look like a "complete" man <note>original Greek: "ὡς ἀνήρ" (hos aner). Only non-eunuchs could traditionally serve as king; the beard was a necessary symbol of his claim.</note>. His wife, however, betrayed him. He was seized, handed over to Parysatis, and put to death. 

Arsaces, the king's son—who later changed his name to Artaxerxes II—married Statira, the daughter of Idernes. Idernes’s son, Teritukhmes, was appointed to his father's <term>satrapy</term> after his death and married the king's daughter, Amestris. Teritukhmes had a half-sister named Roxana, who was famous for her great beauty and her skill in archery and spear-throwing. Teritukhmes fell in love with Roxana and grew to hate his wife, Amestris. In order to get rid of Amestris, he resolved to sew her into a sack and have her stabbed to death by 300 accomplices with whom he had conspired to start a rebellion. However, a man named Udiastes, who had great influence with Teritukhmes, received letters from the king promising a generous reward if he could save the princess. Udiastes attacked and murdered Teritukhmes, who fought back courageous— <note>The text cuts off mid-word, continuing the story of Teritukhmes's death on the following page.</note>

He courageously defended himself and killed, it is said, thirty-seven of his attackers.

Mithradates <note>original: "Mitradates," an Ionic form of the name.</note>, the son of Udiastes and the armor-bearer of Teritukhmes, took no part in this affair. When he learned what had happened, he cursed his father and seized the city of Zaris to hand it over to the son of Teritukhmes. Queen Parysatis ordered the mother of Teritukhmes, his brothers Mitrostes and Helicus, and his sisters—except for Statira—to be put to death. Roxana was cut into pieces while still alive. The king told his wife Parysatis to inflict the same punishment upon the wife of his son Arsaces <note>Arsaces’s wife was Statira, the sister mentioned above.</note>. But Arsaces, through his tears and cries, calmed the anger of his father and mother. Parysatis having relented, Ochus spared Statira’s life, but at the same time, he told Parysatis that she would one day greatly regret it.

In the nineteenth book, the author relates how Ochus Darius <note>original: "Dariaeus"</note> fell sick and died at Babylon, having reigned for thirty-five years. Arsaces, who succeeded him, changed his name to Artaxerxes <note>original: "Artoxerxes"</note>. Udiastes had his tongue cut out and pulled out by the roots from behind, and so he died. His son Mithradates was appointed to his <term>satrapy</term> <note>A province of the Persian Empire governed by a satrap.</note>. This was done at the urging of Statira, which greatly offended Parysatis. 

Cyrus, being accused by Tissaphernes of plotting against the life of his brother Artaxerxes, took refuge with his mother; through her intervention, he was cleared of the charge. Disgraced by his brother, he retired to his province and began planning a revolt. Satibarzanes <note>original: "Satibarzenes"</note> accused Orontes of having an affair with Parysatis, although her conduct was beyond reproach; Orontes was put to death, and his mother was greatly enraged against the king, because Parysatis had poisoned the son of Teritukhmes. The author also mentions someone who cremated his father contrary to the law, claiming this proves the historians Hellanicus and Herodotus were lying. <note>The extreme brevity of this summary by Photius makes it unclear exactly what this refers to; it may be a critique of Herodotus’s claim that King Croesus was burned on a funeral pyre.</note>

Having revolted against his brother, Cyrus collected an army composed of both Greeks and <term>barbarians</term> <note>In Greek history, "barbarians" simply meant anyone who did not speak Greek; here it refers to the non-Greek Persian and local troops. This expedition is the famous subject of Xenophon’s *Anabasis*.</note>. Clearchus was in command of the Greeks; Syennesis, king of Cilicia, assisted both Cyrus and Artaxerxes. Clearchus the Spartan, who was in command of the Greeks, and Menon the Thessalian, who accompanied Cyrus, were always at odds <note>original: "at variance"</note> because Cyrus followed Clearchus's advice in everything, while Menon was ignored. Large numbers of men deserted from Artaxerxes <note>original: "Artoxerxes"</note> to Cyrus, but none deserted from Cyrus to Artaxerxes. For this reason Artabarius, who planned to desert, was accused and thrown into the ashes <note>A Persian method of execution for high-ranking criminals where the victim was thrown into a tower filled with hot ash and suffocated.</note>. Cyrus attacked the king’s army and was victorious, but he lost his life because he ignored Clearchus's advice. Artaxerxes mutilated his body, ordering his head and the hand that had struck the king to be cut off; he then paraded them around in triumph. Clearchus the Spartan withdrew during the night with his Greeks, and after he had seized one of the cities belonging to Queen Parysatis, the king made peace with him.

Parysatis set out for Babylon, mourning the death of Cyrus. After some difficulty, she recovered his head and hand and sent them to Susa for burial. It was Bagapates who had cut off Cyrus’s head by Artaxerxes’s order. Later, while playing dice with the king, Parysatis won the game and took Bagapates as her prize; she then had him flayed alive <note>A brutal Persian punishment where the skin was removed from the living body.</note> and crucified. At last, she was persuaded by Artaxerxes's pleas to end her mourning for her son. The king rewarded the soldier who brought him Cyrus’s cap, as well as the Carian who was believed to have wounded Cyrus—though Parysatis later tortured and killed that Carian. When Mithradates <note>original: "Mitradates"</note> boasted at a banquet that he was the one who had killed Cyrus, Parysatis demanded he be handed over to her. Once she had him in her power, she put him to death with great cruelty. This concludes the nineteenth and twentieth books.

The twenty-first, twenty-second, and twenty-third books conclude the history. Tissaphernes began to plot against the Greeks with the help of Menon the Thessalian, whom he had won over. Through cunning and solemn promises, Tissaphernes got Clearchus and the other generals into his power. Clearchus suspected treachery and tried to prevent it, but the soldiers, deceived by Menon’s words, forced the unwilling Clearchus to visit Tissaphernes. Proxenus the Boeotian, who had already been deceived, also advised him to go. Clearchus and the other generals were then sent in chains to...

<page-num>109</page-num>

# CTESIAS 109

Artaxerxes <note>original: "Artoxerxes"</note> was at Babylon, where crowds of people flocked to see Clearchus. Ctesias himself, who was Queen Parysatis's physician, gave every possible attention to Clearchus while he was in prison and did everything he could to improve his situation <note>original: "mitigate his lot"</note>. Parysatis would have granted him his freedom and let him go, if Stateira <note>original: "Statira"</note> had not persuaded the king to put him to death. After his execution, a miraculous thing happened: a strong wind sprang up and piled a great amount of earth over his body, which formed a natural tomb. The other Greek generals who had been sent with him were also executed, with the exception of Menon.

The author next tells us of the insults Parysatis heaped upon Stateira, and the poisoning of Stateira. This was carried out in the following manner, even though Stateira had long been on her guard against such an assassination. A carving knife was smeared with poison on only one side. Parysatis took a small bird about the size of an egg, called a <term>rhyndace</term> <note>A Persian bird, possibly a type of warbler, described by ancient writers as living on air and dew.</note>, and cut it in half. Parysatis herself took and ate the portion that had not touched the poisoned side of the blade, while offering Stateira the poisoned half. Seeing that Parysatis was eating her own portion, Stateira had no suspicions and ate the fatal dose. The king, enraged with his mother, ordered her servants <note>original: "eunuchs"</note> to be seized and tortured, including her chief confidant, Ginge. When Ginge was accused and brought to trial, she was acquitted by the judges; however, the king condemned her anyway and ordered her to be tortured and executed. This caused a lasting quarrel between the mother and her son.

Eight years later, the tomb of Clearchus was found covered with palm trees, which Parysatis had secretly ordered her servants to plant.

The author next explains the cause of the quarrel between Artaxerxes and Evagoras, the king of Salamis <margin>Located in Cyprus. The orator Isocrates composed a speech of praise <note>original: "panegyric"</note> for this Evagoras.</margin>. He mentions the messengers sent by Evagoras to Ctesias regarding the receipt of letters from Abuletes, and a letter from Ctesias to Evagoras concerning a reconciliation with Anaxagoras, the prince of the Cyprians. He also records the return of Evagoras's messengers to Cyprus and the delivery of Ctesias's letters. Finally, he describes a speech by Conon <note>A famous Athenian admiral who sought refuge in Cyprus and helped broker an alliance between the Persians and the Athenians against Sparta.</note> to Evagoras about visiting the king, a letter from Evagoras regarding the honors he had received from him, and a letter from Conon to Ctesias regarding Evagoras's agreement to pay tribute <note>A tax or payment made by a subordinate ruler to a superior power.</note>.

<header>CTESIAS</header>

...to the king, and the delivery of the letters to Ctesias. The author recounts Ctesias’s speech to the king regarding Conon <note>A prominent Athenian admiral who served the Persians against Sparta.</note> and the letter intended for him. The gifts sent by Evagoras <note>The King of Salamis in Cyprus.</note> were delivered to Satibarzanes, followed by the arrival of the messengers in Cyprus. Conon sent letters to both the king and Ctesias. Meanwhile, the Spartan ambassadors to the king were detained. A letter from the king addressed to Conon and the Spartans was delivered to them by Ctesias himself. Finally, Pharnabazus <note>A Persian satrap (governor).</note> appointed Conon as the commander of the fleet.

The text describes Ctesias’s visit to his native city of Cnidus and his journey to Sparta. It also mentions the legal proceedings against the Spartan ambassadors at Rhodes and their eventual acquittal. <note>The original Greek text is grammatically unclear regarding the details of this acquittal.</note> The author records the number of travel stations, the days required for the journey, and the distance in <term>parasangs</term> <note>original: "parasangs"; a Persian unit of distance equal to about 3.5 miles or 5.6 kilometers.</note> from Ephesus to Bactria and India. The work concludes with a chronological list of the Assyrian kings, starting from Ninus and Semiramis and continuing down to Artaxerxes <note>original: "Artoxerxes"</note>.

Ctesias's writing style is clear and very simple, which makes his work pleasant to read. He uses the <term>Ionic dialect</term> <note>The literary form of Greek used by historians like Herodotus, though Ctesias uses it less strictly.</note>, though not throughout the entire work as Herodotus does; he only employs it in specific expressions. Furthermore, he does not interrupt the flow of his narrative with poorly timed digressions as Herodotus often does. Although Ctesias criticizes Herodotus for telling "old wives' tales," he is not free from the same flaw himself, particularly in his account of India. The charm of his history lies primarily in his storytelling method, which is very effective at evoking emotion and using the unexpected. He also makes frequent use of mythical embellishments. However, his style is more careless than it ought to be, and his language often becomes common or ordinary. In contrast, the writing of Herodotus remains the superior model of the <term>Ionic dialect</term> in terms of both artistic skill and vigor.

One stade is approximately 600 feet or 185 meters, roughly an English furlong.</note> He asserts that the population of India is greater than that of almost the entire rest of the world. He also mentions a large worm found in...

# CTESIAS

...this river <note>Referring to the Indus River mentioned on the previous page.</note> is the only living creature that breeds there. Beyond India, there are no countries inhabited by humans. It never rains there; instead, the country is watered by the river. He describes the *pantarba* <note>original: "pantarba"; likely a hydrophane (also called *oculus mundi*, or "eye of the world"), a type of opal that absorbs water and changes color.</note>, a kind of seal-stone. He claims that 477 seal-stones and other precious gems belonging to a Bactrian merchant, which had been thrown into the river, were all drawn up from the bottom—clinging together—by this single stone.

He also speaks of elephants that knock down walls, of large <note>original: "little"; the Greek text likely should read *makron* (large) instead of *mikron* (small).</note> apes with tails four cubits <note>A cubit is approximately 18 inches or 45 centimeters.</note> long, and of very large roosters. He describes a parrot about the size of a hawk, which has a human-like tongue and voice. It has a dark-red beak, a black beard, and blue feathers up to its neck, which is as red as cinnabar <note>original: "cinnabar"; a bright red mineral used as a pigment. The text here is noted as being somewhat corrupted or unclear in the original Greek.</note>. It speaks the Indian language like a native, and if it is taught Greek, it speaks Greek as well.

He next mentions a fountain that is filled every year with liquid gold, from which a hundred pitchers are drawn. These pitchers must be made of clay, because the gold becomes solid once it is drawn off, and the vessel must be broken to get the gold out. The fountain is square, sixteen cubits in circumference, and one fathom <note>About 6 feet or 1.8 meters.</note> deep. The gold in each pitcher weighs one talent <note>An ancient unit of weight, roughly 60–75 pounds.</note>. At the bottom of the fountain, there is iron; the author says that he himself owned two swords made from it—one given to him by the King, and the other by the King’s mother, Parysatis. If this iron <note>This may refer to early observations of magnetism or perhaps a legendary belief in lightning rods.</note> is fixed into the ground, it wards off clouds, hail, and hurricanes. Ctesias declares that the King proved its effectiveness twice and that he himself was a witness to it.

The Indian dogs are very large and even attack lions. There are great mountains from which sardonyx, onyx, and other seal-stones <note>Gemstones used for carving signet rings.</note> are mined. It is intensely hot, and the sun appears ten times larger than in other countries; large numbers of people are suffocated by the heat. The sea is as large as the sea of Greece; it is so hot on the surface—to a depth of four fingers—that fish cannot live near the top, but must stay at the bottom.

The river Indus flows across plains and between mountains, where the so-called "Indian reed" <note>likely bamboo</note> grows. It is so thick that two...

<page-num>112</page-num>

<header>CTESIAS</header>

Men can barely wrap their arms around it, and it is as tall as the mast of the largest merchant ship. Some are larger and some smaller, which is natural considering the size of the mountain. Of these reeds, some are male and others are female. The male has no pith <note>The spongy or hollow center of a plant stem.</note> and is very strong, but the female does have pith.

The *martikhora* <note>original: *martikhora*; from the Persian *mardikhora*, meaning "man-eater." This creature is the origin of the legendary "manticore."</note> is an animal found in this country. It has a face like a man's, skin as red as cinnabar <note>A bright red mineral used as a pigment.</note>, and is as large as a lion. It has three rows of teeth, and ears and light-blue eyes like those of a human. Its tail is like that of a land scorpion, containing a sting more than a cubit <note>About 18 inches or 45 centimeters.</note> long at the end. It has other stings on each side of its tail and one on the top of its head, like a scorpion, with which it inflicts a wound that is always fatal. If it is attacked from a distance, it raises its tail in front and discharges its stings as if from a bow; if attacked from behind, it straightens its tail out and launches its stings in a direct line to a distance of a hundred feet. The wound it inflicts is fatal to all animals except the elephant. The stings are about a foot long and about as thick as a small rush. The *martikhora* is called "man-eater" in Greek <note>original: *anthropophagos*</note> because, although it preys upon other animals, it kills and devours a great number of human beings. It fights with both its claws and stings, which, according to Ctesias, grow back after they have been discharged. There are many of these animals in India, which are hunted and killed with spears or arrows by natives mounted on elephants.

Observing that the Indians are extremely just <note>Fair or righteous.</note>, Ctesias goes on to describe their manners and customs. He mentions a sacred spot in an uninhabited district, which they honor in the name of the Sun and the Moon. It is a fifteen days' journey from Mount Sardo <note>It is not clear whether Sardo is merely a proper name or refers to the "mountain of the *sardo*" (a precious stone).</note>. Here, the sun is always cool for thirty-five days in the year, so that his worshippers may attend his feast and return home after the celebration without being scorched. In India, there is neither thunder, lightning, nor rain; however, winds and hurricanes, which carry away everything in their path, are frequent. The sun is cool for half the day after rising, but for the rest of the day, it is excessively hot in most parts of the country. It is not the heat of the sun that makes the Indians swarthy <note>Dark-skinned.</note>; they are naturally so. Some of...

<page-num>113</page-num>

<header>CTESIAS</header>
VOL. I. H

Among them, both men and women are very fair-skinned, though they are few in number. Ctesias says that he himself saw five white men and two white women. To support his claim that the sun cools the air for thirty-five days, he mentions that the fire which flows from Mount Etna does no damage to the middle of the country through which it passes—because it is the home of righteous men—but it destroys everything else. <note>According to legend, during an eruption of Mount Etna, two brothers—Amphinomus and Anapus of Catana—carried their elderly parents to safety on their shoulders. The lava miraculously parted around them, leaving them untouched. The spot was later known as "the Field of the Pious."</note> On the island of Zakynthos, there are springs full of fish from which pitch <note>A thick, black, oily substance used in ancient times for waterproofing ships.</note> is harvested. On the island of Naxos, there is a spring from which wine of a very pleasant flavor sometimes flows. The water of the river Phasis, if allowed to sit for a day and a night in a container, turns into a most delicious wine. Near Phaselis in Lycia <note>An ancient Greek city in modern-day Turkey.</note>, there is a fire that never goes out but burns on a rock both day and night. It cannot be extinguished by water—which actually makes the flame grow—but only by throwing earth upon it.

In the middle of India, there are black-skinned men called Pygmies <note>original: *Pygmaioi*; the name comes from the Greek word for a unit of measure from the elbow to the knuckles, literally meaning "a fist long."</note>, who speak the same language as the other inhabitants of the country. They are very short; the tallest among them are only two cubits <note>About 3 feet or 90 centimeters.</note> in height, and most are only one and a half cubits tall. Their hair is very long, reaching down to their knees and even lower, and their beards are larger than those of any other men. When their beards are full-grown, they stop wearing clothes and let the hair on their heads fall down behind them far below their knees, while their beards trail down to their feet in front. Once their bodies are entirely covered with hair in this way, they fasten it around themselves with a belt, so that it serves as their clothing. They are snub-nosed and ugly. Their sheep are no bigger than lambs, and their oxen, donkeys, horses, mules, and other beasts of burden are about the size of rams. Being very skilled archers, 3,000 of them serve as part of the King of India's retinue. They are very just and follow the same laws as other Indians. They hunt hares and foxes, not with dogs, but with ravens, kites <note>A type of bird of prey.</note>, crows, and eagles.

There is a lake 800 stades <note>About 92 miles or 148 kilometers. A stade was an ancient Greek unit of distance.</note> in circumference, the surface of which—when not disturbed by the wind—is covered with floating oil. Sailing over it in small boats, the people ladle out the oil with small vessels and keep it for their use. They also use oil of...

--- Page 120 ---
<meta>This page continues the summary of Ctesias’s *Indica*, moving from a description of the Pygmies to other natural resources, legendary creatures, and the customs of the Indian people.</meta>

<page-num>114</page-num>

# CTESIAS

They use sesame and nut oil, but the oil taken from the lake is considered the best. This lake is also full of fish.

The country produces a great deal of silver, and there are many silver mines. These are not very deep, though the mines in Bactria <note>An ancient region in Central Asia, covering parts of modern-day Afghanistan, Uzbekistan, and Tajikistan.</note> are said to be deeper. There is also gold, but it is not found in rivers or washed out of the sand like the gold in the river Pactolus <note>original: *Paktōlos*; a river in Lydia, modern Turkey, famous in the ancient world for its gold deposits.</note>. Instead, it is found in many large mountains inhabited by griffins <note>original: *gryps*; legendary creatures usually depicted with the body of a lion and the head and wings of an eagle.</note>. These are four-footed birds as large as wolves, with legs and claws like those of a lion. The feathers on their breasts are red, while the feathers on the rest of their bodies are black. Although there is an abundance of gold in these mountains, it is difficult to extract because of these creatures.

Indian sheep and goats are larger than donkeys and usually give birth to four young at a time, sometimes even six. There are no pigs there, either domestic or wild. The palm trees and their dates are three times as large as those found in Babylon. There is even a river of honey that flows directly out of a rock.

The author describes at length the Indians' sense of justice, their loyalty to their kings, and their lack of fear toward death. He also mentions a fountain whose water thickens like cheese when it is drawn. If a small amount—about the weight of three obols <note>An ancient Greek unit of weight; three obols weighed approximately 2 grams.</note>—of this thickened substance is crushed, mixed with water, and given to someone to drink, they will confess everything they have ever done, remaining in a state of frenzy and delirium for the entire day. The king uses this test when he wants to discover the truth about an accused person. If the person confesses, they are ordered to starve themselves to death; if they reveal nothing, they are acquitted.

The Indians do not suffer from headaches, ophthalmia <note>An ancient term for severe inflammation of the eye.</note>, or even toothaches. They do not get mouth ulcers or sores on any other part of the body. They live for 120, 130, 150, and some even as long as 200 years.

There is a snake about a span <note>A unit of measure based on the width of an expanded human hand, roughly 9 inches.</note> in length, of a beautiful purple color with a very white head and no teeth. It is caught on the volcanic mountains where sardonyx <note>A gemstone with parallel layers of sard and onyx, highly valued for carved seals and jewelry.</note> is mined. It does not bite, but its vomit causes anything it touches to rot. If it is hung up by its tail, it secretes two types of poison: one is yellow like amber, produced while it is alive; the other is black, produced after it dies. If someone drinks even a tiny amount of the yellow poison—as small as a sesame seed dissolved in water—their brain leaks out through their nose, and they die instantly. If the other poison is administered, it causes consumption <note>original: *phthisis*; a wasting disease, historically referring to tuberculosis.</note>, which takes at least a year to become fatal.

<summary>Ctesias describes the natural wealth and legendary creatures of India, including gold-guarding griffins, a truth-inducing fountain used in trials, and the remarkable health and longevity of the Indian people.</summary>
<keywords>Ctesias, India, griffins, Bactria, Pactolus, gold mines, silver mines, truth serum, longevity, exotic animals, ancient medicine</keywords>

--- Page 121 ---
<meta>This page continues Ctesias’s descriptions of the wonders of India, moving from geological features to a catalog of exotic wildlife, miraculous plants, and the legendary dog-headed tribes.</meta>

<page-num>115</page-num>

# CTESIAS

There is a bird called the *dikaerum* (meaning "just" in Greek <note>original: *dikaion*</note>), which is about the size of a partridge’s egg. It buries its waste in the ground to hide it. If anyone finds this waste and consumes even a tiny portion of it—about the size of a sesame seed—in the morning, they are overcome by sleep, lose consciousness, and die by sunset.

There is also a tree called the *parebum*, roughly the size of an olive tree, which is found only in the royal gardens. It produces neither flowers nor fruit and has only fifteen very thick roots, the smallest of which is as thick as a man’s arm. If a piece of this root about a span <note>A unit of measurement roughly equal to 9 inches or 23 cm.</note> in length is placed near any material—such as gold, silver, brass, or stone (essentially anything except amber)—it attracts the object to itself. If a piece the length of a cubit <note>An ancient unit of length, roughly 18 inches or 45 cm.</note> is used, it can attract lambs and birds; the latter are commonly caught this way. If you wish to solidify a gallon of water, you only need to throw in a piece of the root weighing an obol <note>A small ancient Greek unit of weight.</note>. It has the same effect on wine, which can then be handled like wax, though it becomes liquid again the following day. The root is also used as a remedy for those suffering from digestive or bowel problems.

A river flows through India that is not very large, being about two stades <note>Roughly 400 yards or 370 meters.</note> wide. It is called the *Hyparchus* <note>original: *Hyparchos*; likely from the Persian *aver-khosh*, meaning "bringing good."</note> in the Indian language, which means "bestowing all blessings" in Greek. For thirty days out of the year, this river carries amber downstream. It is said that in the mountains where the river passes, trees grow along the banks that shed "tears" at a certain season, much like almond, fir, or other resinous trees—especially during those thirty days. These tears drop into the river and harden into amber. This tree is called *Siptakhora* <note>likely from the Persian *shiftehkhor*, meaning "agreeable to eat."</note> in Indian, which means "sweet" in Greek, and it is from these trees that the inhabitants gather amber. The tree also bears fruit in clusters like grapes, with pits as large as Pontic nuts <note>Hazelnuts from the Pontus region of the Black Sea.</note>.

In these mountains live the dog-headed people <note>Known in Greek as the *Cynocephali*.</note>, who wear the skins of wild beasts as clothing. They do not speak any human language but bark like dogs, and in this manner, they communicate with one another. Their teeth are larger than a dog's, and their nails are like those of a dog but longer and more rounded. They inhabit the mountains as far as the Indus River. Their complexion is dark. They are an extremely just people, like the rest of the Indians with whom they associate.

<summary>This page details several fantastical elements of ancient India, including a lethal bird, a magnetic tree root with chemical properties, a river that produces amber, and the "just" dog-headed tribe known as the Cynocephali.</summary>

<keywords>Ctesias, India, dikaerum, parebum, amber, Hyparchus, Siptakhora, Cynocephali, Indus River, ancient medicine, legendary creatures</keywords>

--- Page 122 ---
<meta>This page continues Ctesias’s account of the exotic peoples and natural resources of India, specifically describing the "dog-headed" tribes and the production of dyes and amber.</meta>

<page-num>116</page-num>

# CTESIAS

They understand the Indian language but are unable to speak it. Instead, they bark or make signs with their hands and fingers to reply, much like those who are deaf and mute. The Indians call them *Calystrii*, which in Greek means *Cynocephali* (“dog-headed” <note>original: *Kynokephaloi*</note>). They live on raw meat and number about 120,000.

Near the sources of the Hyparchus River¹ grows a purple flower from which a purple dye is obtained. This dye is as high in quality as Greek dye but has an even more brilliant hue. In the same district, there is a creature about the size of a beetle, red as cinnabar <note>A bright red mineral pigment.</note>, with very long feet and a body as soft as a worm's. It breeds on the trees that produce amber, eating their fruit and eventually killing the trees, much as the woodlouse destroys vines in Greece. The Indians crush these insects and use them to dye their robes, tunics, and anything else they wish.² This dye is considered superior to Persian dye.

The Dog-headed people living in the mountains do not practice any trade but live by hunting. When they have killed an animal, they roast it in the sun. They also raise many sheep, goats, and donkeys, drinking the sheep's milk and the whey made from it. They eat the fruit of the *Siptakhora* tree—the source of amber—because it is sweet. They also dry this fruit and keep it in baskets, just as the Greeks keep their dried grapes. They construct rafts which they load with this fruit, along with well-cleaned purple flowers and 260 talents <note>A talent was a heavy ancient unit of weight; 260 talents would be several tons.</note> of amber. They also include the same quantity of purple dye and an additional 1,000 talents of amber, all of which they send annually as tribute to the King of India. They trade the rest of their goods with the Indians for bread, flour, and cotton clothes. They also buy swords for hunting wild beasts, as well as bows and arrows, as they are very skilled at archery and hurling spears. They are considered invincible in war because they live on high, inaccessible mountains. Every five years, the King of India sends them a gift of 300,000 bows, the same number of spears, 120,000 shields, and 50,000 swords.

They do not live in houses but in caves. They go out to hunt with bows and spears, and because they are very fast runners, they quickly pursue and overtake their prey. The women bathe once a month, but the men do not bathe at all; they only wash their hands. Three times a month, they anoint themselves with oil made from milk and then wipe themselves dry with...

--- Page 123 ---
<meta>This page continues Ctesias’s description of the Dog-headed people and introduces a mysterious group of milk-drinkers, followed by a description of the famous "Indian wild ass"—often identified as the earliest Greek account of a rhinoceros or a unicorn.</meta>

<page-num>117</page-num>

# CTESIAS

They wear clothes made of skins. These garments, worn by both men and women, are not raw hides with the hair still on, but are finely tanned skins. The wealthiest among them wear linen clothing, though these individuals are few. They do not use beds, but instead sleep on beds of leaves or grass. A person's wealth is measured by the number of sheep they own, as well as their other livestock. Everyone, both men and women, has a tail above their hips like a dog, though theirs are longer and more hairy. They are a just people and live longer than any other humans, reaching 170 or sometimes even 200 years of age.

It is said that beyond their territory, near the sources of the river, there lives another group of people who are black-skinned like the rest of the Indians. They perform no manual labor, do not eat grain, and do not drink water. Instead, they raise large numbers of cattle, cows, goats, and sheep, and they live entirely on the milk of these animals. When they drink milk in the morning and again at noon, they also eat a sweet root. This root prevents the milk from curdling in the stomach; at night, it allows them to easily vomit everything they have consumed <note>This likely describes a ritual or medicinal cleansing practice.</note>.

In India, there are wild asses <note>¹ Perhaps the rhinoceros, although the description is not altogether suitable.</note> that are as large as horses, or even larger. Their bodies are white, their heads are dark red, and their eyes are bluish. They have a single horn in the middle of their forehead, about a <term>cubit</term> <note>An ancient unit of length based on the distance from the elbow to the tip of the middle finger, roughly 18 inches (45 cm).</note> in length. The base of the horn, about two palms' width from the forehead, is pure white; the middle section is black, and the upper part, which ends in a point, is a vivid flaming red. 

Those who drink from cups made from this horn are protected against convulsions, epilepsy, and even poison—provided that before or after consuming the poison, they drink wine, water, or another liquid from these cups. While the domestic and wild asses of other countries and all other solid-hoofed animals lack <term>huckle-bones</term> <note>The astragalus or ankle bone, often used by ancient peoples as dice for games.</note> and gall bladders, these Indian asses possess both. Their ankle bone is the most beautiful I have ever seen; it is similar to an ox’s in size and shape, but it is as heavy as lead and colored like <term>cinnabar</term> <note>A bright red mineral pigment.</note> all the way through. 

These animals are incredibly strong and swift; no horse or any other creature can overtake them. They begin running slowly, but as they continue, their pace increases wonderfully, becoming faster and faster. There is only one way to catch them: when they take their young out to graze, if they are surrounded by many horsemen, they refuse to abandon their foals. Instead, they stay to fight, butting with their horns and kicking...

# CTESIAS

They bite and kill many men and horses. They are eventually captured only after they have been pierced with arrows and spears, for it is impossible to capture them alive. Their flesh is too bitter to eat, and they are only hunted for the sake of their horns and ankle-bones <note>original: "huckle-bones"; these were often used as dice or gaming pieces in antiquity</note>.

In the Indus River, a worm is found that resembles the caterpillars usually found on fig trees. Its average length is seven <term>cubits</term> <note>about 10.5 feet or 3.2 meters</note>, though some are longer and others shorter. It is so thick that a ten-year-old child could hardly wrap his arms around it. It has two teeth, one in the upper jaw and one in the lower. It devours everything it seizes with these teeth. During the day, it remains in the mud of the river, but at night it comes out and seizes whatever it encounters—whether an ox or a camel—dragging its prey into the river and devouring everything except the intestines. It is caught with a large hook baited with a lamb or kid goat and attached to iron chains. After it has been caught, it is hung up for thirty days with vessels placed underneath to catch the oil that drips from its body. This produces enough oil to fill ten Attic *kotylae* <note>original: "kotylae"; an ancient Greek measure roughly equal to half a pint. Ten kotylae is about five pints.</note>. At the end of the thirty days, the worm is thrown away, and the vessels of oil are sealed and taken as a gift to the King of India, who alone is permitted to use it. This oil sets everything on fire—whether wood or animals—over which it is poured, and the flame can only be extinguished by throwing a large quantity of thick mud on it.

There are trees in India as high as cedars or cypresses, with leaves like those of the palm tree, except they are a little broader and have no shoots. They flower like the male laurel but produce no fruit. The tree is called *karpion* by the Indians and *myrorodon* <note>meaning "perfume-rose"</note> by the Greeks; it is not common. Drops of oil ooze out of it, which are wiped off with wool and then squeezed into stone alabaster boxes. The oil is reddish, rather thick, and so fragrant that it scents the air for a distance of five <term>stades</term> <note>roughly 3,000 feet or 925 meters</note>. Only the king and his family are allowed to use it. The King of India sent some to the King of Persia, and Ctesias, who saw it, says that he cannot compare the perfume to any other.

The Indians also have very excellent cheese and sweet wine, both of which Ctesias tested himself.

There is a square fountain in India, about five <term>ells</term> <note>an ancient unit of measurement; in England, an ell was usually 45 inches</note> in circumference. The water is contained in a rock, about three cubits deep, and the water itself is three <term>fathoms</term> <note>about 18 feet or 5.5 meters</note> deep. The Indians...

People of the highest rank—men, women, and children—bathe in this water, not only for cleanliness but as a way to prevent disease. They jump into the water feet-first, and as they plunge in, the water throws them back out onto dry land. This happens not only to humans but to every animal, whether living or dead. In fact, everything thrown into the water is cast out, except for iron, silver, gold, and copper, which sink to the bottom. The water is very cold and pleasant to drink; it makes a loud noise similar to water boiling in a cauldron. It cures leprosy and skin irritations <note>original: "scab"</note>. In the Indian language, this fountain is called *balladē*, and in Greek, it is called *ophelime* (meaning "useful").

In the mountains where the Indian reed <note>This likely refers to bamboo, which ancient writers described as growing to enormous, tree-like proportions in India</note> grows, there lives a population of about 30,000 people. The women there only give birth once in their lives. The children are born with beautiful teeth in both their upper and lower jaws. Both male and female children are born with white hair on their heads and eyebrows. Until the age of thirty, the men have white hair all over their bodies, but then it begins to turn black; by the age of sixty, it is completely black. Both the men and the women have eight fingers on each hand and eight toes on each foot. They are a very warlike people, and 5,000 of them—both archers and spearmen—accompany the King of India on his military campaigns. Their ears are so long that they cover their arms as far as the elbows, as well as their backs, and one ear can even touch the other <note>This refers to the "Panotii," a mythical race of people with giant ears described by several ancient Greek and Roman geographers</note>.

It is also said that beyond the gates of Mauretania <note>the region of modern-day Morocco and Algeria</note>, the rain is heavy in the summer, while the winter is scorching hot. In Metadrida, there is another spring a short distance from the sea; its flow is so powerful at midnight that it throws a huge number of fish onto the land. <header>HELIODORUS</header>

He himself had seen with his own eyes some of the things he describes, and had been informed of the rest by eye-witnesses. He says that he has omitted many even more marvelous things, for fear that those who had not seen them might think that his account was utterly untrustworthy.

### LXXIII

I read the *Aethiopica* <note>also known as "The Ethiopian Story"</note> of Heliodorus.¹ The work is dramatic, and the style used is suited to the subject, being full of simplicity and charm. The narrative is varied by actual, expected, or unexpected incidents that appeal to the emotions, by strange escapes from danger, and by clear and pure language. If, as is only natural, there is a tendency to use figures of speech, they are easy to understand and vividly illustrate the subject matter. The sentence structures <note>original: "periods"</note> are balanced and concisely arranged for the sake of brevity. The composition in other respects corresponds to the subject. The story is about the love of a man and a woman, and shows a desire for the strict observance of moral standards <note>original: "propriety"</note>.

The main characters are Theagenes and Chariclea, two virtuous lovers who keep their purity <note>original: "modesty"</note> intact throughout all their travels and frequent periods of being held captive. Their names and a brief account of their fortunes and sufferings are provided. The story describes the Athenian festival at which Chariclea is a priestess and Theagenes a competitor in the stadium race. It tells how they fall in love with each other at first sight; Chariclea's resulting illness; and how she is carried off, with her own consent, by Theagenes and Calasiris from the house of her supposed <note>original: "reputed"</note> father, Charicles. It then covers their voyage to Zacynthus, during which the captain of the ship is struck with love for Chariclea; Calasiris pretends to accept his offer of marriage. Chariclea and her party are hospitably...

---

¹ Heliodorus of Emesa in Syria, belonging to a family of the priests of the Sun, flourished in the third century A.D. This view is supported by the mention of the Blemmyes (a nomadic people who first became prominent around 250 A.D.), traces of neo-Pythagorean influence, and the emphasis placed on Sun-worship. The church historian Socrates <note>Socrates Scholasticus, a 5th-century historian</note> wrongly identifies him with a bishop of Tricca who lived during the reign of Emperor Theodosius; the latter was probably confused with the father of the author of the *Aethiopica*. The standard work on the Greek romance writers is E. Rohde’s *The Greek Romance* <note>original Latin/German: "Der griechische Roman"</note> (1914); see also *Blackwood's Magazine*, vol. xliv. (1892). The entire work has been preserved, and it would be difficult to understand the complicated plot just from Photius's summary <note>original: "abstract"</note>.

They are received on shore and informed by the fisherman who provides them lodging that Trachinus, the captain of a band of robbers, intends to kidnap her. Calasiris and Chariclea flee, but Trachinus pursues and captures their ship. He falls in love with Chariclea, who feigns a willingness to marry him. Calasiris and Theagenes, posing as her father and brother, request that they be allowed to remain on the vessel, and their request is granted. 

A storm breaks out at sea; they narrowly escape shipwreck and land on the coast of Egypt. Trachinus speaks of his intended marriage to Chariclea. Her supposed father, Calasiris, devises a scheme <note>original: "stratagem"</note> and helps with the arrangements for the wedding feast. Calasiris convinces Pelorus that Chariclea is actually in love with him, which leads to a dispute over her between Pelorus and Trachinus. 

A full-scale battle breaks out in which many of the pirates kill each other,¹ and several are killed by Chariclea. She is overcome with grief at the sight of Theagenes covered in wounds. Suddenly, they are attacked by Egyptian brigands. These brigands are astonished by the sight of Chariclea and carry her and Theagenes off to Thyamis, the captain of the *Bucoli*² <note>original: "the herdsmen"</note>, as the brigands were called. He also falls in love with Chariclea, who once again pretends that Theagenes is her brother. 

The *Bucoli* are in turn attacked and defeated. Thyamis and Hermuthis³ flee, followed by Cnemon⁴ and Theagenes. Chariclea remains in the cave⁵ where she had been hidden. At the entrance lies the dead body of Thisbe; the sight of the corpse throws Theagenes⁶ into unbearable grief, until Chariclea calls out to him from within the cave. 

The mystery of Thisbe’s death is explained, and Hermuthis mourns. Cnemon and Hermuthis depart, as do Chariclea and Theagenes. Cnemon, after leaving Hermuthis, meets Calasiris. They share their adventures with one another. Cnemon tells the story of Thisbe and his stepmother Demaenete, his banishment, and his other misfortunes. Calasiris tells the story of Charicles, Chariclea, and Theagenes. Both lament their misfortunes. Then Cnemon shares the welcome news that Theagenes and Chariclea are still alive, since he himself had been a fellow prisoner with them in the...

Hearing this name, Cnemon—knowing that Thisbe is dead—is puzzled; however, his confusion turns to joy when he discovers that Chariclea is actually alive. They first search for Theagenes, and then Cnemon and Nausiclea are married. 

Calasiris sets out with Chariclea to find Theagenes. Along the way, they find an old woman grieving over her son who died in battle. She addresses his body using magical arts, while Calasiris and Chariclea watch. The old woman begs the corpse to tell her if her other son will return. The dead son, cursing his mother for her violent and forbidden <note>original: "unlawful"</note> acts, tells her that her other son will be killed, but that she will die before him for having insulted the dead. She dies soon after by accidentally falling onto a fragment of a spear.

Thyamis, Theagenes, and the rest of the bandit group set out for Memphis. Thyamis wants to reclaim the priesthood that his younger brother, Petosiris, has stolen. This causes a major riot in the city. Arsace,¹ who is in charge of the city, tries to end the conflict by ordering the two brothers to fight in single combat; the priesthood will be awarded to the winner. The duel takes place against the wish of Petosiris, who is unskilled in weapons, while Thyamis is an experienced soldier. Thyamis immediately terrifies his brother, who drops his weapons and runs away; he is chased twice around the city walls by Thyamis. 

Theagenes—with whom Arsace (the wife of Oroöndates) has fallen in love—is serving as Thyamis's attendant. Calasiris and Chariclea arrive at the scene. Seeing his sons in a fight to the death, Calasiris runs toward them shouting and manages to stop the fight with great difficulty, as they do not recognize him at first. Chariclea throws herself into Theagenes's arms. The brothers put down their weapons, and Thyamis is restored to the priesthood by his father, who dies shortly afterward.

Arsace begins a plot against Theagenes and Chariclea, aided by her servant Cybele, who invites them to the palace. Arsace’s obsession with Theagenes grows out of control; she uses various schemes, mistreatment, and charms to win him over. Cybele, planning to give a cup of poisoned wine to—

Theagenes and Chariclea are treated cruelly and tortured because Theagenes rejects the romantic advances of Arsace. Chariclea is sentenced to be burned at the stake, but the flames are put out by the magical *pantarbe* stone. <note>Which she wore on her finger (see the work *Indica* by the Greek historian Ctesias, page 111).</note> In this way, Chariclea escapes for the moment, but Arsace, in her fury, prepares to have her executed the following day. This happened because Cybele’s son, frustrated in his own desire to marry Chariclea, had hurried to inform his master of his wife’s unfaithful behavior.

The Ethiopians attack, and Theagenes and Chariclea are captured and taken to Hydaspes, the King of Ethiopia. They are dedicated for sacrifice—Theagenes to the sun and Chariclea to the moon. Games and sacrifices take place in the presence of Sisimithres, the leader of the gymnosophists, <note>An Indian sect of philosophers who lived an extremely ascetic life. Their doctrine was a kind of Pantheism <term>the belief that God and the universe are one and the same</term>, and they believed in the transmigration of souls <term>reincarnation</term>. By disciplining the body, they hoped to purify their souls. They wore no clothing, which is the origin of their name (from the Greek words *gymnos*, meaning "naked," and *sophistes*, meaning "wise man"). Their influence in the Eastern world (and even among the Greeks) was significant; Alexander the Great even tried to convince them to join his traveling court during his military campaigns.</note> and Persine, the King’s wife. Chariclea demands to be allowed to argue her case before the King. Her request is granted, with Sisimithres appointed as the judge. It is proven by the testimony of witnesses that Chariclea is actually the daughter of Hydaspes and Persine.

Hydaspes is only persuaded of the truth with great difficulty. However, in obedience to the customs of the country, he is still determined that she must be sacrificed. The people oppose this, and Chariclea is released to everyone's joy. But Chariclea is soon threatened by another danger. Theagenes is still held for the sacrifice; despite her desperate pleas and various arguments, her father refuses to release him. Deeply distressed, Chariclea tells her mother everything that has happened to her and Theagenes. Meanwhile, Theagenes performs a feat of strength with a bull, much to the delight of the crowd. He also defeats the strongest wrestler among the Ethiopians amidst shouts of applause. Despite his victory, he is crowned and led toward the sacrifice. Charicles, who has arrived from Athens, begs the King to return the girl he believes is his daughter. The King promises to do so if Charicles can find her, but the old man is unable to locate her. Charicles then identifies and seizes Theagenes

<header>THEMISTIUS</header>

and drags him before the king, exclaiming, "This is the man who stole my daughter from Athens." An investigation is held, and Theagenes is found innocent with the approval of Sisimithres, who secures the abolition of human sacrifice for the future, amidst general celebration. Thus, after all their trials and perils, Theagenes and Chariclea become husband and wife. Chariclea is crowned with the mitre <term>a tall, pointed headdress worn by priests</term> of the priesthood by her mother, and Theagenes is crowned by his father-in-law. A sacrifice is offered, and preparations are made for the mystical wedding rituals.

This romance was written by Heliodorus, son of Theodosius, a Phoenician from the city of Emesa. It is said that he later became a bishop.

### LXXIV

I read the thirty-six political orations of Themistius.<sup>1</sup> Some are addressed to the Emperor Constantius, others to Valens, the younger Valentinian, and Theodosius; they contain tributes and formal speeches of praise <note>original: "encomiums and panegyrics"</note> for these emperors. The style is clear and free of repetitive phrases, though it is somewhat ornate <note>original: "florid"</note>. The language is formal, with a tendency toward solemnity. As is clear from his works, Themistius was at the height of his career during the reign of Valens. He was still a young man in the time of Constantius, who elected him as a member of the Senate, as evidenced by the letter the Emperor himself addressed to that body on behalf of Themistius. His father, who was also a philosopher, was named Eugenius. We have seen his commentaries on all the works of Aristotle, as well as his concise and useful paraphrases of the *Analytics*, *On the Soul*, the *Physics*, and similar works. He also contributed to the interpretation of Plato and was, in fact, a devoted student and lover of philosophy.

---

<sup>1</sup> Known as *Euphrades* ("the eloquent speaker" <note>original: "beautiful speaker"</note>), he was a Neoplatonist and a Sophist <term>a professional teacher of rhetoric and philosophy</term> (living roughly 317–390 AD). Born in Paphlagonia, he later resided in Constantinople, where he entered government service and eventually became the Prefect of the city. One of his special duties was to deliver official orations welcoming the emperors, as he lived under the reigns of six different rulers. In addition to the orations mentioned here by Photius, he was a prolific writer on philosophical subjects. When he was quite a young man, he gained a significant reputation as the author of a commentary on Aristotle. He was on friendly terms with many famous literary figures, orators, and philosophers—both Christian and Pagan—and Gregory of Nazianzus calls him "the king of arguments." Of the thirty-six speeches mentioned by Photius, we still have thirty-four (one survives only in a Latin translation), as well as some of his work on Aristotle.
<sup>2</sup> "Written in the style of official state documents" (noted by historian Thomas Hodgkin).

I also read the sixteen speeches of Lesbonax.¹ This Lesbonax . . .

### LXXV

I read the short treatise by John Philoponus² written against the divinely inspired doctrine of the holy and consubstantial <term>original: "consubstantial"; meaning sharing the same essence or substance</term> Trinity. This doctrine was set forth by John Scholasticus, the Archbishop of Constantinople, in his catechetical speech delivered during the first indiction³ of the reign of Emperor Justin.

The writing style is exactly what one would expect from this author: it is clear, but it completely lacks vitality and grandeur <note>original: "energy and dignity"</note>. His arguments are not only sacrilegious but are also entirely unsound and weak. He proves himself unable to give even a thin veneer of truth to his deceptive arguments against the true faith. By inventing various natures, substances, and deities—acting like the arrogant babbler he is—he pours out a flood of blasphemy against the Christian faith. He obsessively dissects minute points, such as asking exactly "how many" persons or natures one means. In what he considers clever reasoning—but what is actually just childish and tasteless <note>original: "puerile"</note>—he arrogantly boasts of his contempt for the sacred rituals of our Church.

It is not only in these specific arguments that he appears weak and foolish; this is true of all the works he has written, except for those where he has stolen ideas from others while falsely claiming they were his own. He is far removed from those authors who know how to distinguish truth from falsehood and who are capable of deep, subtle thought. Regarding the writings he falsely claims as his own, they generally retain the quality of their true authors, though in some cases his poor style and lack of skill have ruined the noble strength <note>original: "manliness"</note> of the original writers. These works are like natural substances that should be excellent, but because of the way they are handled and presented, they show signs of being fake and degraded. In this specific work, he includes quotations from the Holy Fathers, such as Gregory the Theologian, Basil the Great, the long-suffering Athanasius, and Saint Cyril, but these quotes do nothing to support his unholy goals.

---

¹ Likely Lesbonax of Mytilene, a teacher and author of three surviving practice speeches <note>original: "declamations"</note>. It is not certain when he lived, but it was probably during the first century AD.
² John Philoponus (c. 490–570 AD) was an Alexandrian philosopher and theologian. While initially respected, he was later condemned for "tritheism," the belief that the Father, Son, and Holy Spirit are three separate gods or natures.
³ <term>An indiction was a 15-year cycle used for dating and tax purposes in the Roman and Byzantine Empires.</term> See p. 89, note 4.

<summary>Photius critiques a treatise by John Philoponus, dismissing his arguments against the Trinity as weak, unoriginal, and blasphemous. He characterizes Philoponus as a plagiarist with a poor writing style who fails to understand the Church's mystical traditions.</summary>

<keywords>John Philoponus, Lesbonax, John Scholasticus, Holy Trinity, Tritheism, Byzantine Theology, Photius, Bibliotheca, Church Fathers, Athanasius, Basil the Great</keywords>
