<meta>This page transitions to Photius's review of the *Antiquities of the Jews* by Flavius Josephus, summarizing the history of the high priesthood and the transition from the Hasmonean dynasty to Roman oversight.</meta>

### LXXVI

I read the *Antiquities of the Jews* by Flavius Josephus <note>original: "Cod. XLVII"; Codex 47 of the Library of Photius</note> in twenty books. He begins with the account of the creation of the world as described by Moses <note>original: "Mosaic cosmogony"</note>. Although his account generally agrees with the version that is widely accepted, he differs in some details. The work ends with the war between the Jews and the Romans, concluding during the time when Agrippa <note>Agrippa II, who ruled from approximately 27–100 AD and is the king before whom St. Paul was brought to trial</note> was king of the Jews. This Agrippa was the son of the great Agrippa <note>the grandson of Herod the Great, famous for his persecution of early Christians and his death by a sudden, terrible disease mentioned in the Bible in Acts 12:23</note>. This younger Agrippa removed Jesus, the son of Gamaliel, from the office of high priest and gave the position to Matthias, the son of Theophilus.

Antiochus <note>Antiochus IV Epiphanes, the Syrian king from 175–164 BC who aggressively tried to erase Jewish customs and introduce Greek culture</note> and his general Lysias were the first to introduce a bold and forced change regarding how the high priesthood was held. They removed Onias (also known as Menelaus) from that office, executed him, and denied his son the right of succession. In his place, they appointed Alcimus (also called Iacimus), who was from the tribe of Aaron but belonged to a different family line. Before this time, the law since the days of Aaron required that the high priest hold office for life and that the son should succeed the father. 

However, when Alcimus died after serving as high priest for three years, the office remained vacant for seven years. After Matthias and his sons from the Hasmonean <note>original: "Asmonean"</note> family were given leadership over the Jewish people and fought against the Macedonians, Jonathan was appointed high priest. This family also included Judas (called Aristobulus), who was the first to wear a royal crown <term>original: "diadem"; a jeweled headband or crown worn by monarchs</term>, thus holding the offices of both high priest and king at once. 

A year later he died and left his brother Alexander as his successor in the kingdom and priesthood; Alexander held these honors for twenty-seven years. From this time on, the combined role of king and priest remained in the Hasmonean family until the time of Hyrcanus. After the Roman general Pompey conquered Jerusalem, he stripped Hyrcanus of his kingdom but allowed him to keep the office of high priest. Thirty-three years later, Hyrcanus was taken prisoner by the Parthian <note>an empire located in modern-day Iran</note> generals Barzapharnes and Pacorus, who instead appointed Antigonus—the son of Aristobulus’s brother—as king. After Anti- <note>The text breaks here; the catchword indicates the continuation of the name Antigonus on the following page.</note>

<summary>A summary of Flavius Josephus’s "Antiquities of the Jews," detailing the history of the Jewish high priesthood and the transition of power from the Hasmonean dynasty to the period of Roman and Parthian interference.</summary>
<keywords>Flavius Josephus, Antiquities of the Jews, High Priesthood, Hasmoneans, Agrippa II, Roman Empire, Jewish History, Antiochus Epiphanes, succession laws</keywords>

--- Page 133 ---
<meta>This page continues the summary of Flavius Josephus's "Antiquities of the Jews," moving from the history of the high priesthood to the biography of Josephus himself and the start of the Jewish-Roman War.</meta>

->### JOSEPHUS<-

After Antigonus <note>the last Hasmonean king of Judea</note> had reigned for three years and three months, the Roman general Sosius and Herod the First—the son of Antipater (a priest of Ashkelon) and Cypris (an Arabian)—overthrew him. They took him to Antioch, where he was put to death by Mark Antony. This marked the end of the Hasmonean <note>original: "Asmonean"; the dynasty that had ruled Judea since the Maccabean Revolt</note> dynasty, and Herod was appointed King of the Jews by the Romans. Herod began appointing the high priests without following the traditional rules of succession, setting an example for his successors to follow. 

As stated above, the author, in his twenty books, records history beginning from the creation of the world and continuing down to the start of the final war between the Jews and the Romans. This occurred at the time when Agrippa <note>Agrippa II, the last ruler from the house of Herod</note>, son of Agrippa, had been appointed King of the Jews by the Romans, and Gessius Florus <note>governor from 64–66 AD</note> had succeeded Albinus as governor of Judea. The Jews, unable to endure Florus’s wickedness and cruelty, revolted; they believed it was better to die all at once in freedom than to perish gradually in slavery. The war broke out in the second year of Florus’s governorship and the twelfth year of the reign of Nero <note>66 AD</note>, which is where the history of Josephus ends. We have already spoken about his writing style.

Josephus was a Jew and a priest by birth, belonging to a family descended from a long line of priests on his father’s side. On his mother’s side, he was of royal blood, for the descendants of Hasmoneus—from whom her family was derived—had for a long time held both the high priesthood and the rank of king. His father’s name was Matthias. Josephus was born in the first year of the reign of the Emperor Gaius <note>better known as Caligula, who reigned from 37–41 AD</note>, and from his boyhood, he was an earnest student. 

In his sixteenth year, he began to devote his attention to the three sects of the Jews, conscientiously examining them so that, after trying them all, he might be able to choose the best. These sects are the Pharisees, the Sadducees, and the Essenes. Having experienced them all, he retired into the wilderness, living there for three years with a man named Banus <note>original: "a man who led a solitary and ascetic life"</note>, who lived as an ascetic. This man’s clothes were made of the leaves of trees, his food consisted of whatever herbs and fruits grew naturally, and he bathed frequently in cold water both day and night to keep himself pure. When he was nineteen, Josephus returned to the city and joined the sect of the Pharisees, which is said to resemble the Greek philosophical sect called the Stoics...

<summary>This page concludes the historical overview of the Hasmonean dynasty's fall to Herod the Great and introduces the biography of the historian Josephus, detailing his priestly lineage and early education among the Jewish sects.</summary>
<keywords>Josephus, Herod the Great, Hasmonean Dynasty, Gessius Florus, Jewish-Roman War, Pharisees, Sadducees, Essenes, Banus the Ascetic, Caligula</keywords>

--- Page 134 ---
<meta>This page concludes the biographical sketch of the Jewish historian Flavius Josephus and begins a review of the historical "Chronicle" by Eunapius of Sardis.</meta>

<header>EUNAPIUS</header>

among the Greeks. In his thirtieth year, he was sent by the people of Jerusalem to investigate the condition of affairs in Galilee, where there was great confusion and things were in a very unsettled state. He was then appointed commander-in-chief of Galilee and proved to be an efficient administrator. He successfully escaped various plots organized against him by his political rivals, and by showing a spirit of moderation when dealing with his enemies, he often won them over to his side. Having taken up arms against the Romans against his will, he was taken prisoner by Vespasian after a brave resistance at Jotapata <note>a fortified Jewish town in Galilee</note>. Vespasian treated him kindly at the time, and even more so after he became emperor. Not only Vespasian, but also his sons and successors, Titus and Domitian, held him in the highest regard. Roman citizenship was bestowed upon him, and he became very wealthy. He finished his *History* in the fifty-sixth year of his age, during the thirteenth year of the reign of Domitian <note>around 93–94 AD</note>.

### LXXVII

Read the new edition of the continuation of the *Chronicle* of Dexippus <gloss>1</gloss> by Eunapius <gloss>2</gloss> in fourteen books. It begins with the reign of Claudius Caesar <note>specifically Claudius Gothicus, reigned 268–270 AD</note>, where the history of Dexippus ends, and continues down to the time of Honorius and Arcadius, the sons of Theodosius. The work actually ends at the time when Arsacius was raised to the archbishopric of Constantinople after the banishment of John Chrysostom <gloss>3</gloss>, and when the wife of Arcadius <note>Empress Eudoxia</note> died from a miscarriage. This Eunapius was a native of Sardis in Lydia and an impious pagan <note>original: "heathen"</note>. He slanders and insults in every way and without restraint all those who brought honor to the empire by their piety—especially Constantine the Great. On the other hand, he highly praises the wicked, above all Julian the Apostate <note>the last pagan emperor, who attempted to suppress Christianity</note>. Indeed, it almost seems as if the work was written as an elaborate tribute <note>original: "panegyric"</note> to him.

---

<gloss>1</gloss> *See Volume 82 <note>original: "Cod. LXXXII"</note>.*
<gloss>2</gloss> Eunapius of Sardis (living around 345–420 AD) was educated at Athens and spent the rest of his life in his native town as a physician and teacher of rhetoric <note>original: "rhetorician"</note>. His "historical memoirs" deal with events from 270 to 414 AD. He was also the author of some surviving *Lives of the Sophists*. He was a bitter enemy of Christianity and a devoted follower of Neoplatonism <note>a philosophical system based on the teachings of Plato that competed with early Christianity</note>. In the "second edition" of the *Chronicle*, much that was offensive to Christianity was removed.
<gloss>3</gloss> 404–405 AD.

<keywords>Josephus, Jotapata, Vespasian, Eunapius of Sardis, Dexippus, Julian the Apostate, Constantine the Great, Neoplatonism, John Chrysostom, Byzantine history</keywords>

--- Page 135 ---
<meta>This page continues the critique of the historian Eunapius of Sardis and introduces the work of the historian Malchus of Philadelphia.</meta>

<page-num>129</page-num>
<header>MALCHUS</header>

His style is elegant, provided one removes terms and expressions such as "bird-like," "more deer-like," "more pig-like," "hawk-like," "crow-like," "ape-like," "a tear like a river," and so on, which spoil <note>original: "vitiate"</note> and degrade the nobility of the rest of the writing. He also uses metaphors and rhetorical devices inconsistently <note>original: "figures of speech capriciously"</note>, a flaw that the rules of historical writing forbid; however, in general, his powerful style combined with his sophistication offsets <note>original: "palliates"</note> the offense. His method of composition, his clarity, and his use of structured sentences <note>original: "periods"</note> are exactly suited and appropriate for historical narrative. Sometimes, however, the style is overly wordy, with a tendency toward legalistic <note>original: "forensic"</note> rather than historical language. In his sentence structure, he introduces many innovations, but not so much as to cause unpleasantness or provide an excuse for attacking his methods <note>original: *methódois*. Another reading is *periódois*, meaning "complex, structured sentences."</note>.

He wrote two volumes covering the same period. In the first, he covers the pure faith of us Christians with insults, glorifies pagan <note>original: "heathen"</note> superstition, and attacks many holy <note>original: "pious"</note> emperors. In the second volume, which he calls a "new edition," he has removed the insults and brutal abuse he had showered upon Christian devotion. After joining the remaining parts of the work together, he calls it, as we have said, a "new edition," although it still shows many traces of his original fanatical anger <note>original: "frenzy"</note>. We have come across old copies of both editions, both in separate volumes and combined; having read both, we are able to judge the difference. The result is that in the new edition, many passages are fragmented <note>original: "mutilated"</note> and unclear because of the removals, even though the author generally cares a great deal about clarity. For some reason, in this second edition, he failed to connect the narrative properly after the deletions, and thus spoiled the meaning.

## LXXVIII

Read the *Byzantine History* of Malchus the sophist <term>sophist: a professional teacher of rhetoric or a scholar</term> in seven books. It begins with the final illness and death of the—

<sig>VOL. I. &nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp; I</sig>

<note>1. *Methods* (Greek: *methódois*). Another reading is *periods* (Greek: *periódois*), meaning "structured sentences."</note>
<note>2. Malchus was from Philadelphia in Syria. According to the encyclopedia *Suidas*, his *History* began with the reign of Constantine the Great; according to Photius, it began with the seventeenth year of Emperor Leo’s reign (473 AD), serving as a continuation of the history by Priscus. It ended with the murder of the Western Roman Emperor Nepos (480 AD). --- Page 136 ---
<meta>This page continues the summary of Malchus of Philadelphia's history, focusing on the reign of the Eastern Roman Emperor Zeno and the various internal conflicts of his era.</meta>

<page-num>130</page-num>
<header>CANDIDUS</header>

...Emperor Leo in the seventeenth year of his reign. The author provides an account of the proclamation and accession of Zeno, his expulsion from the throne and his life as a private individual, and the rise and fall <note>original: "accession and abdication"</note> of the usurper Basiliscus. He describes the restoration of Zeno to the throne and the murder of Basiliscus, whose wife and children were unjustly put to death at the same time. Harmatius, who had helped restore Zeno, met with a similar reward <note>used ironically; original: "recompense"</note>, being put to death by Onulphus.

The author also gives an account of the rebellion of Theodoric, the son of Triarius; the friendship of Theodoric, the son of Malamir <note>original: "Malamir," also known as Valamir</note>, and his war with Theodoric, the son of Triarius. He details the second revolt against Zeno, the rebellion of Marcian, the conspiracy <margin>against Zeno</margin> of Zeno’s mother-in-law <note>Verina</note>, and the permanent banishment of Marcian. He covers Verina’s plot against Illus, and the treacherous seizure of Epidamnus <note>a city on the Adriatic coast, in modern-day Albania</note> by Theodoric, the son of Malamir.

Having described these events, the author then touches upon Roman affairs. The seventh book ends with the death of Nepos, who, after driving out Glycerius and assuming imperial power, ordered Glycerius’s hair to be shorn like a clergyman's <note>referring to a forced tonsure, which disqualified him from secular rule</note> and made him a high priest instead of an emperor. Nepos himself was later killed at the instigation of Glycerius. These seven books show that the author had already written an account of earlier events, as is also clear from the beginning of the first of these seven books. The conclusion of the seventh book further shows that he had intended to continue the history, had he lived longer.

Malchus, a native of Philadelphia <note>modern-day Amman, Jordan</note>, is a most admirable historian. His style is pure, free from unnecessary wordiness <note>original: "redundancies"</note>, and easy to understand. His language is elegant and clear, if somewhat grand; he does not hesitate to use unusual expressions characterized by emphasis, pleasant sound, and dignity. Generally speaking, his language is a model for any historian. A scholar <note>original: "sophist"</note> by profession and one of the greatest of public speakers, he appears to have been a member of the Christian Church.

### LXXIX <note>Entry 79</note>

Read the *History* by Candidus <note>nothing more is known of his life than what Photius tells us here; he wrote during the late 5th century</note> in three books. It begins with the rise to power of Leo, a native of Dacia in Illyria <note>the Roman provinces in the Balkan region</note>...

--- Page 137 ---
<meta>This page continues the summary of historical writings, transitioning from the work of Malchus of Philadelphia to the history written by Candidus, focusing on the reign of Emperor Leo I and the rise of the Isaurians.</meta>

<page-num>131</page-num>
<header>CANDIDUS 131</header>

The author <note>referring to Emperor Leo I</note> was a military tribune and in command of the troops in Selymbria, and he obtained the throne with the assistance of Aspar. Aspar was an Alan <note>an Iranian nomadic group</note> and had been a soldier from his early years. He had been married three times and had three sons: Ardaburius, Patricius, and Ermenarichus.¹ The narrative continues until the proclamation of Anastasius as emperor. The author of this history was a native of Isauria Tracheia <note>original: "Tracheia," meaning "rugged" or "stony" Isauria</note>,² as he tells us himself, and by profession he was a secretary <note>original: "clerk"</note> to certain influential Isaurians. In religion, he was an orthodox Christian, which is evident from his praise of the fourth synod <note>The Council of Chalcedon in 451 AD</note> and his well-justified attack on those who sought to change its doctrines <note>original: "innovators"</note>. His writing style is not well-suited for history. He uses poetic expressions that are dull and childish; the composition is harsh and unharmonious, tending toward exaggerated, flowery language <note>original: "dithyrambic bombast"</note> or falling into carelessness and lack of elegance. He introduces new grammatical constructions which do not—as they might with other writers—add smoothness or charm to the work, but instead make it disagreeable to read and utterly unattractive. While his style improves in some places, his history is clearly a mixture of very different materials. He claims that the name "Isauria" is derived from Esau.

The first book describes the influence of Aspar and his sons, the election of Leo to the throne by Aspar, the great fire that broke out in Constantinople, and the measures Aspar took for the general welfare. It also discusses Tatian and Vivian, the dispute between Aspar and the emperor concerning them, and the words they exchanged with one another. It recounts how this led to an alliance between the emperor and the Isaurians through Tarasicodissas, the son of Rusumbladeotes. This man's name was changed to Zeno when he became Leo’s son-in-law after the death of his first wife. It describes how Ardaburius, seeking to oppose the emperor, also tried to win the Isaurians over to his side. It tells how a certain Martin, a friend of Ardaburius, informed Tarasicodissas <note>Zeno</note> of Ardaburius’s plot against the emperor. This mutual suspicion worsened until finally, Emperor Leo decided to execute Aspar and his sons, Ardaburius and Patricius the Caesar <note>a high-ranking title, often designating an heir</note>. Aspar was killed; however, Patricius unexpectedly recovered from his wounds, and Aspar’s other son, Ermenarichus, who happened not to be with his father at the time, also escaped. Finally, Leo gave Tarasicodissas the hand of his daughter Ariadne in marriage.

¹ Ermenaric.
² Tracheotis, a district of Asia Minor between Cilicia and Pisidia.

<header>CANDIDUS</header>

The author describes how he <note>Referring to Tarasicodissa, an Isaurian chieftain</note> changes his name to Zeno, and Emperor Leo appoints him general of the East. It covers the successes and failures of Basiliscus during his military campaigns in Africa. The text explains how Leo desired and plotted to ensure the election of his son-in-law Zeno as emperor, but he could not convince his subjects to agree to this. Shortly before his death, however, Leo proclaimed his grandson Leo (the son of Zeno and Ariadne) as emperor. After the grandfather’s death, the young Leo, with the approval of the senate, placed the imperial crown upon his father Zeno's head. 

Then follows a detailed family history <note>original: "genealogy"</note> of the Isaurians, in which the author does his best to prove that they were descendants of the biblical Esau <note>Esau was the twin brother of Jacob in the Book of Genesis; the author is attempting to give the Isaurians an ancient, though perhaps notorious, lineage</note>. The narrative tells how Zeno, tricked by the dowager empress Verina, fled with his wife and mother, abandoning both the city of Constantinople and the throne. Verina hoped that Patricius the *magister* <term>magister: a high-ranking administrative official, specifically the Master of Offices</term> would marry her and make himself emperor. To achieve this, she used treachery to drive out her son-in-law <note>Zeno</note>, but she was disappointed in her hopes; those in authority instead raised her brother Basiliscus to the throne. This period saw a terrible massacre of Isaurians within Constantinople. Meanwhile, in the West, Nepos, the emperor of Rome, was succeeded by Augustulus, the son of Orestes. This concludes the contents of the first book.

The second book describes how Patricius the *magister*, who had carried on an affair with Verina, was killed by her angry brother, the new Emperor Basiliscus. Verina came to hate her brother because of this and assisted Zeno with money to help him recover the throne. Consequently, she was persecuted by her brother, and she would likely have lost her life if Armatus <note>Also known as Harmatius, a high-ranking military officer and nephew of Basiliscus</note> had not secretly removed her from the protection of the church. 

Armatus, who had carried on an affair with the wife of Basiliscus, gained great influence and was entrusted with leading the war against Zeno. However, he later entered into a secret agreement with the general Illus and defected to Zeno's side. Armatus was initially held in great respect by Zeno, and his son was even raised to the rank of Caesar <note>Caesar: a high imperial title, often designating a formal successor to the Emperor</note>. Nevertheless, Armatus was later put to death, and his son was stripped of his high rank and became a reader <term>reader: a minor clerical rank in the church</term> at Blachernae <note>a famous district in Constantinople known for its imperial palace and church</note>. Before these events, Basiliscus had declared his own son Marcus as Caesar and later as emperor. Illus, having made peace with Zeno, prepared to help him reclaim the throne. Basiliscus, abandoned by his own supporters who had revolted against him, fled with his children and his wife Zenonis, and was treacherously led to...

...[was persuaded] by Armatus to leave the church in which he had taken refuge, and was then banished to Cappadocia, where he was put to death along with his entire family. When the impious Peter¹ was disturbing the Churches of the East, Zeno sent Calandion to be consecrated as the Patriarch of Antioch. As the Emperor was in need of money, he managed to acquire some through the methods recommended to him.² Many people who conspired against him were captured and executed. Illus provided great service to the empire through his bravery in war, his military victories, his determined³ political actions, and his fair dealings. 

After the death of the Roman Emperor Nepos and the removal of his son Augustulus, Odoacer⁴ seized control of Italy and the city of Rome itself. However, the Gauls in the west revolted against him; both the Gauls and Odoacer sent ambassadors to Zeno, who showed a preference for Odoacer. 

A certain man of the Alan people attempted to assassinate Illus; after wounding him, the attacker claimed that he had been bribed by Epinicius, a close associate of Empress Verina. Epinicius was surrendered to Illus, and after receiving a promise of forgiveness and a reward, he revealed Verina’s plots against Illus. Zeno delivered Verina into Illus’s custody, who then exiled her to a fortress in Cilicia to ensure his own safety. 

Illus, who had become very close with the ungodly Pamprepius,⁵ to whom he had been introduced by Marsus, was gradually led to ruin by the association. A civil war against Zeno was started by Marcian and Procopius, sons of the Roman Emperor Anthemius. After they were defeated, Marcian was forced to become a priest, and Procopius fled to seek refuge with Theodoric in Thrace. While in exile in Cappadocia, Marcian escaped and incited a rebellion in Ancyra, Galatia, until he was finally captured and banished to Isauria. This section also explains the origins of the Emperor’s growing hatred for Illus. This concludes the contents of the second book.

The third book, among other things, relates how Illus—

### LXXX

I read the *Histories* of Olympiodorus <note>Olympiodorus was a native of Egyptian Thebes and served as an ambassador to the Hun prince Donatus in 412. He was a pagan. His *History*, dedicated to Theodosius II, covered events from 407 to 425. It is an essential contemporary guide, and its loss—surviving only through this summary by Photius—is greatly regretted. For more on this era, see the historical works of Edward Gibbon, J.B. Bury, Thomas Hodgkin, and E.A. Freeman.</note>, which consist of twenty-two books. They begin with the seventh consulship of the Emperor Honorius and the second of Theodosius II, and continue down to the time when Valentinian—the son of Placidia and Constantius—was proclaimed Emperor of the Romans. The author, a pagan <note>original: heathen</note>, was a native of Thebes in Egypt and describes himself as a poet by profession. 

His writing style is clear but loose and lacking in energy; at times, it descends into a common vulgarity, so much so that the work hardly deserves to be considered a formal history. Perhaps this is why the author himself, being aware of these flaws, claims that his work is not a history, but rather a collection of materials for a history <note>original: "Silva," a Latin term meaning "forest" or "raw material," often used to describe a rough draft or collection of notes</note>. He clearly felt that his own style and phrasing lacked proper form. He does not show much skill in structure, except for the few moments when he achieves a certain simplicity. However, even in this, he is unsuccessful due to his extremely poor and petty choice of words, gradually sliding into a vulgar mannerism. He titles his work *The Forest* <note>original: Silva</note>, yet he still divides it into books and attempts to decorate them with prefaces. The work is dedicated to the Emperor Theodosius II, the son of Arcadius and the nephew of Honorius and Placidia.

The text details the rise of Stilicho to power; his appointment by Theodosius the Great as the guardian of his children, Arcadius and Honorius; and his marriage to Serena, who was betrothed to him by the Emperor himself (her uncle). It also covers the marriage of Stilicho's daughter, Thermantia, to Honorius, and his rise to the height of his...

<summary>This page concludes Candidus's account of the rebellion against Emperor Zeno and introduces the historical work of Olympiodorus, detailing the author's background, his unpolished writing style, and the early political career of the Roman general Stilicho.</summary>

--- Page 141 ---
<meta>This page continues the summary of Olympiodorus’s history, detailing the fallout of Stilicho’s execution and Alaric’s subsequent siege and sack of Rome in 410 AD.</meta>

<page-num>135</page-num>
<header>OLYMPIODORUS 135</header>

Stilicho had achieved great power through many successful foreign wars. However, he met his death at the hands of the cruel and inhuman Olympius, a man whom Stilicho himself had recommended to the emperor.

Alaric, the leader of the Goths, had previously been summoned by Stilicho to secure the territory of Illyricum <note>A Roman province in the Balkans.</note> for Emperor Honorius (the region had been assigned to him by his father, Theodosius). Following the murder of Stilicho, and because the promises made to Alaric had been broken, he besieged and sacked Rome. He carried away a massive amount of plunder along with Placidia, the sister of Honorius, who was in the city at the time. Before the city fell, Alaric declared a prominent citizen named Attalus, who was then the city prefect, to be the new emperor. Another reason for Alaric’s hostility was that the Romans had formed an alliance with Sarus. Sarus was also a Goth and a brave, undefeated warrior who led a small band of only 200 or 300 men. Because Sarus was an enemy of Alaric, this Roman alliance made Alaric their bitter and final foe.

During the siege of Rome, the famine was so severe that the inhabitants were forced to resort to cannibalism. While Stilicho was still alive, Alaric had received 4,000 pounds of gold to cover the costs of his military expedition. After Stilicho’s death, his widow, Serena, was strangled to death; it was believed she might have been responsible for Alaric’s attack on the city. Their son, Eucherius, had already been executed.

During the reign of Honorius, the name <term>Bucellarii</term> <note>original: Bucellarii; literally "biscuit-eaters." These were private soldiers or bodyguards who served individuals in exchange for food and protection. One of the military administrative districts of the empire was also named after them.</note> was given not only to Roman soldiers but also to foreign ones. Similarly, the name <term>Foederati</term> <note>original: Foederati; "federated" allies. These were irregular troops, often Goths, who were technically subjects but often had their taxes reduced or canceled. Eventually, they became a paid frontier defense force.</note> referred to a mixed and irregular body of troops.

Olympius, the man who had plotted against Stilicho, was appointed <term>Master of the Offices</term> <note>original: Magister Officiorum; the highest-ranking official in the imperial civil service, acting as a sort of chief of staff and private secretary to the emperor.</note>, but he was later removed from his post. He eventually regained the position, only to be deprived of it once more. Finally, he was beaten to death by order of Constantius, the husband of Placidia, after...

---

¹ The name is said to have originally meant a body of soldiers who accepted anyone's "bread" (specifically *buccellatum*, a type of military hardtack) in return for serving him. They functioned as a type of bodyguard. One of the military divisions of the empire was also called the Bucellarian.

² These were primarily the Goths. They were nominally subjects who paid tribute, but that tribute was often reduced or entirely forgiven. Over time, they were viewed as a frontier defense force and received regular pay.

³ This was the most important officer in the civil administration. He combined the roles of chief secretary for several departments with the duties of a private secretary to the emperor.

<meta>This page continues the summary of Olympiodorus’s history, detailing the rise of the usurper Constantine in Britain and Gaul, the death of Alaric the Goth, and the political maneuvers of Emperor Honorius.</meta>

<page-num>136</page-num>

# OLYMPIODORUS

...his ears were first cut off. Thus the ungodly wretch met with his rightful punishment at last.

The leaders of the Goths under Radagaisus, numbering about 12,000 and known as the *Optimati* <note>original: Optimati; meaning "the Elite" or "the Best Men"</note>, were defeated by Stilicho, who then entered into an alliance with Radagaisus.

Following an illness, Alaric died and was succeeded by his brother-in-law, Ataulf.

The author states that dry bread was called *bucellatum* <note>original: bucellatum; a type of hard military biscuit</note>, and jokingly suggests that the soldiers were called *bucellarii* <note>original: bucellarii; literally "biscuit-eaters"</note> for this reason. <note>Another suggested origin is the word *bucula* (original: bucula), which refers to the part of a helmet that covers the mouth and cheeks.</note>

Constantine, having seized power as a usurper in Gaul, sent ambassadors to Emperor Honorius. He excused his actions by claiming he had been forced to take the imperial power <note>original: "assume the purple," as purple was the color reserved for emperors</note> by his soldiers, and he asked for Honorius's forgiveness and recognition as a co-emperor. Because Honorius was in a very difficult position, he agreed to the request. 

This Constantine had been declared emperor during a military revolt in Britain. Before Honorius had reached his seventh year as consul, the soldiers there had first proclaimed a man named Marcus as emperor. Marcus was soon removed by them, and Gratian was appointed in his place. After four months, the soldiers grew tired of Gratian as well and put him to death, promoting Constantine to the rank and title of *Augustus* <note>The highest imperial title</note>. Having appointed Justin and Neobigastes to command his forces, Constantine left Britain and crossed over to Bononia <note>modern-day Boulogne</note>, a coastal town and the first settlement in Gallic territory. He stayed there for some time, winning over all the soldiers of Gaul and Aquitaine, and occupied the whole of Gaul as far as the Alps, which separate Italy and Gaul. He had two sons, Constans and Julian; he raised Constans to the rank of *Caesar* <note>a junior emperor</note> and gave the title of "Most Noble" <note>original: Nobilissimus; a title bestowed on the emperor’s close relatives, who ranked just below the highest nobility</note> to Julian.

Attalus, who was established as a rival emperor to Honorius, marched toward Ravenna. There Jovian <note>Also known as Jovius</note>—the Praetorian Prefect <note>The most powerful officials in the empire after the emperor himself</note> and a Patrician <note>Under the later Empire, this was a high personal honor granted by the emperor rather than a hereditary rank</note>—

--- Page 143 ---
<meta>This page continues the summary of Olympiodorus’s history, detailing the power struggles between the legitimate Emperor Honorius and the usurper Attalus, as well as the fate of various high-ranking Roman officials and a legendary statue in southern Italy.</meta>

<page-num>137</page-num>

# OLYMPIODORUS

Valens, the <term>magister utriusque militiae</term> <note>original Latin: "commander of both services," meaning he led both the infantry and cavalry</note>, Potamius the <term>quaestor</term> <note>a high-ranking legal and financial official</note>, and Julian the <term>primicerius notariorum</term> <note>original Latin: "chief of the secretaries," the head of the imperial civil service</note> are sent as envoys to Attalus by Emperor Honorius. They inform Attalus that Honorius has sent them to discuss the possibility of sharing the empire as partners. 

Attalus refuses this offer. Instead, he offers to allow Honorius to retire unharmed to some island or any other location he might choose. Jovian <note>Likely referring to Jovius, a high-ranking official who defected from Honorius to Attalus</note> gladly accepts this proposal and goes further, suggesting that Honorius should be physically mutilated. Attalus rebukes Jovian, stating there is no reason to mutilate Honorius if he agrees to abdicate the throne voluntarily. After several failed diplomatic missions, Jovian remains with Attalus as his <term>patricius</term> <note>Patrician, a high honorary title in the late Roman Empire</note>. 

Meanwhile, the command of the city of Ravenna falls to the <term>praepositus</term> <note>Specifically the *praepositus cubiculi sacri*, or the Grand Chamberlain of the imperial bedchamber</note> Eusebius. Shortly afterward, due to the cruelty of Allobich and a public decree, Eusebius is flogged to death in the presence of the emperor. After a significant amount of time, Attalus—who does not remain loyal to Alaric the Goth, largely because of Jovian, who had betrayed Honorius's ambassadors—is stripped of his title. He then joins Alaric’s entourage as a private citizen. He is later restored to power but once again forced to abdicate. Finally, while traveling to Ravenna, he is captured. After having the thumb and forefinger of his right hand cut off <note>Mutilation was often used to disqualify a person from ruling or participating in military service without killing them</note>, he is sent into exile.

Soon after these events, Allobich is executed for the murder of the chamberlain Eusebius, being put to death in the emperor’s presence. When the usurper Constantine is informed of Allobich’s death, he hurriedly leaves for Ravenna to negotiate a treaty with Honorius; however, becoming frightened, he turns back.

Rhegium <note>Modern-day Reggio Calabria</note> was the chief city of the Bruttii <note>A region in modern Calabria, the "toe" of Italy</note>. The historian records that Alaric intended to cross from there into Sicily but was prevented by a sacred statue. This statue is said to have been consecrated by the ancients as a protective charm against the volcanic fires of Mount Etna and the arrival of barbarians from across the sea. In one of its feet, it contained a fire that was never extinguished, and in the other, a supply of water that never ran dry. When this statue was later destroyed by Asclepius—the manager of the Sicilian estates belonging to Constantius and Placidia <note>Galla Placidia, the sister of Emperor Honorius</note>—the inhabitants suffered terribly from both the eruptions of Etna and barbarian invasions.

<meta>This page continues the historical summary of Olympiodorus, shifting focus from the conflict between Honorius and Attalus in Italy to the collapse of the usurper Constantine’s regime in Gaul and the subsequent rise of new challengers like Jovinus and Ataulf.</meta>

<page-num>138</page-num>

# OLYMPIODORUS

The usurper Constantine and his son Constans—who was first a Caesar and later an Augustus—had been defeated and forced to flee. His general, Gerontius, eagerly made peace with the barbarians and declared Maximus to be the new emperor. Maximus was one of the <term>domestics</term> <note>original Latin: *domestici*, the household troops or imperial bodyguard</note> and was also Gerontius’s own son <note>While the author uses the Greek word <term>pais</term> (child), other historical sources identify Maximus only as a dependent or servant of Gerontius</note>. Gerontius then pursued Constans, killed him, and went after Constantine. 

While these events were unfolding, Emperor Honorius sent his generals Constantius and Ulphilas against Constantine. They reached <term>Arelate</term> <note>modern-day Arles, France</note>, where Constantine lived with his son Julian, and laid siege to the city. Constantine sought sanctuary in a church and was ordained as a priest after receiving a solemn promise that his life would be spared. The city gates were opened to the attackers, and Constantine and his son were taken to Honorius. However, the emperor—who held a grudge because Constantine had murdered his cousins—violated his oath and ordered them to be executed thirty miles from Ravenna. 

When Gerontius heard that Constantius and Ulphilas had arrived, he fled, only to be captured by his own rebelling troops who hated his strict discipline. The house where he hid was set on fire. Alongside one of his servants—an Alan by birth—he fought bravely against the mutineers. Finally, at their own desperate request, he killed the Alan and then his wife before stabbing himself to death. His son Maximus, hearing of this, fled to take refuge with friendly barbarians <note>According to some historical accounts, he fled to Spain</note>.

In the meantime, Jovinus was proclaimed emperor at <term>Moguntiacum</term> <note>modern-day Mainz, Germany</note> in Upper Germany, aided by Goar the Alan and Guntiar <note>also spelled Gundicar</note>, a Burgundian chieftain. On the advice of Attalus, the Gothic leader Ataulf joined Jovinus with his army. However, Jovinus was offended by Ataulf’s presence and, using cryptic language, blamed Attalus for advising the union. 

A man named Sarus was also on his way to join Jovinus. When Ataulf heard this, he gathered 10,000 men to ambush Sarus, who had only twenty-eight followers. Sarus fought with incredible heroism and was only captured alive with great difficulty after a soldier threw a sack over his head; he was killed shortly after. Sarus had previously rebelled against Honorius because the emperor had failed to punish the murderer of Sarus’s servant...

<meta>This page continues the summary of Olympiodorus’s history, moving from the death of the usurper Constantine to the Emperor Honorius’s dealings with the Huns and the Visigothic king Ataulf in Gaul and Spain.</meta>

<page-num>139</page-num>

# OLYMPIODORUS

...[Honorius treated the death of] Bellerides as a matter of indifference and had refused to find out and punish his murderer.

He discusses Donatus and the Huns, and the skill of their kings in shooting with the bow. The author relates that he himself was sent on a diplomatic mission to Donatus, and he gives a tragic account of his wanderings and the dangers he faced at sea. He describes how Donatus, having been deceived by a solemn oath, was unlawfully put to death. He also tells how Charaton, the first of the Hunnish kings, was incensed at the murder but was eventually appeased by gifts from the Emperor. Such are the events of the first decade <note>original: "decade," referring to the first ten books of his historical work</note> of the history.

The second decade begins as follows. Jovinus, acting against the advice of the Visigothic king Ataulf, proclaimed his own brother Sebastian as "Augustus" <note>the title for a co-emperor</note>. Ataulf, deeply offended by this, sent envoys to Emperor Honorius, promising to send him the heads of these usurpers <note>original: "tyrants"</note> and offering to make peace. After oaths were exchanged, the envoys returned, and the head of Sebastian was indeed sent to the Emperor. Jovinus was then besieged by Ataulf and surrendered; he was sent to the Emperor and executed by the praetorian prefect Dardanus with his own hand. Both heads were displayed outside Carthage,¹ where the heads of Constantine and Julian, and of Maximus and Eugenius—who had all aspired to the throne during the reign of the Great Theodosius and met the same fate—had already been displayed.

The restoration of Galla Placidia to her brother Honorius was urgently demanded from Ataulf by Constantius, who later became her husband. But because the promises made to Ataulf remained unfulfilled—especially regarding the supply of grain <note>original: "corn"</note>—he refused to give her back and prepared for war instead of peace.

When requested again to restore Placidia, Ataulf asked for the grain promised to him. Although those who had promised it were unable to supply it, they agreed to do so only if Placidia was first restored; the barbarian leader made a similar pretense of complying. In the meantime, he set out for Massilia,² hoping to capture it by treachery. However, after being severely, almost mortally, wounded by the noble Boniface, he retreated to his own quarters, abandoning the city, which joyfully acclaimed and praised Boniface.³

¹ According to the historian Bury, this refers to New Carthage in Spain.
² Modern-day Marseilles, France.
³ A distinguished Roman general, Count of Africa, rival of the general Aëtius, and friend of Saint Augustine.

<meta>This page continues the summary of the historian Olympiodorus’s work, focusing on the Roman general Constantius’s rise to the consulship, his physical appearance, and the royal wedding between the Visigothic King Ataulf and the Roman princess Galla Placidia.</meta>

<page-num>140</page-num>

# OLYMPIODORUS

Ataulf, determined to marry Placidia despite the request of Constantius for her return, increased his demands. He did this so that if his terms were not met, he would have a plausible excuse for keeping her in his custody.

Constantius, who had previously been elected as consul, was officially inaugurated as consul at Ravenna. At the same time, Constans was made consul at Constantinople. Enough gold was discovered among the assets of Heraclian—who had been executed <note>In the year 413</note> for attempting to seize the throne—to pay for the expenses of the consulship, even though the total amount was less than anticipated. The amount of gold found was approximately 4,600 pounds, and the value of his landed estates was 2,000 *litrae* <note>original: "litrae"; a unit of weight roughly equal to a pound. The value cited here is approximately £92,000 in the currency of the translator's era</note>. Emperor Honorius granted all of this to Constantius "at a single request."

When Constantius appeared in public processions <note>original: "προόδοις" (proodois); some interpret this as "as he walked"</note>, he had a gloomy and scowling look. He had large eyes, a thick neck, and a broad head. His entire body would lean forward over his horse as he rode, and he would glance sideways from one side to the other, behaving in a way that—as an old saying puts it—made him "appear worthy of imperial power" <note>This is a reference to a fragment from the play "Aeolus" by the Greek playwright Euripides</note>. At parties and banquets, however, he was pleasant and social; he would even engage in friendly competition with the mimes <note>term: "mimes"; professional entertainers who performed comedic skits or gestures</note> who entertained at the table.

On the advice and with the help of Candidian, the marriage of Ataulf and Placidia was celebrated at the start of January in the city of Narbo (modern-day Narbonne, France). The ceremony took place in the home of Ingenius, one of the city's most prominent citizens. Placidia sat in the inner chamber, dressed in the Roman fashion and wearing royal robes. Ataulf sat beside her, wearing a Roman-style woolen tunic and traditional Roman clothing.

Among the many wedding gifts, Ataulf presented his bride with fifty handsome young men dressed in silk. Each youth carried two very large bowls in his hands: one filled with gold and the other with precious—or rather, priceless—gemstones. These were part of the spoils taken from Rome during the Goths' sack of the city. Wedding songs were then performed, with the former puppet-emperor Attalus leading the choir, accompanied by Rusticius and Phoebadius. The celebration concluded with great displays of joy and public games, which were attended by Romans and barbarians alike.

Following the capture of Rome by the Goths, Albinus, the city prefect, reported to the Emperor that once order had been restored, the amount of grain distributed to the citizens was no longer enough. This was because the population of the city was increasing...

<meta>This page continues the summary of the historian Olympiodorus’s work, focusing on the Roman general Constantius’s rise to the consulship, his physical appearance, and the royal wedding between the Visigothic King Ataulf and the Roman princess Galla Placidia.</meta>

<page-num>141</page-num>

# OLYMPIODORUS

...as many as 14,000 strangers passing through in one day. <note>original: "Reading τετάχθαι. Τετέχθαι would mean that 14,000 children were born in one day in Rome, which is absurd." The Greek word *tetachthai* refers to people being stationed or arranged, while *tetechthai* refers to being born.</note>

After Placidia gave birth to a son whom Ataulf named Theodosius, the king sought the friendship of the Romans even more earnestly. However, the opposition of Constantius and his supporters made the efforts of Ataulf and Placidia useless. The infant son died soon after, and his grieving parents buried him in a silver coffin <note>original: "coffer"</note> in a church near Barcino <note>Modern-day Barcelona, Spain</note>. 

Shortly afterward, Ataulf himself was murdered while tending to his horses in the stable—a task he was in the habit of performing. He was killed by a Goth in his service named Dubius, who had been waiting for a long time for an opportunity to satisfy a deep-seated hatred. Dubius’s former master <note>This refers to Sarus, a Gothic chieftain mentioned on page 138</note>, who was the leader of a Gothic tribe, had been killed by Ataulf. Ataulf had later taken Dubius into his own household. To avenge his first master, Dubius killed his second. 

Before Ataulf died, he instructed his brother to return Placidia to the Romans and, if possible, to seek a friendly alliance with Rome. He was succeeded by Singeric, the brother of Sarus, who seized the throne through violence and plotting rather than through legal right or family connection. Singeric murdered Ataulf’s children from a previous marriage, dragging them from the arms of Bishop Sigesarus. As a further insult, he forced Placidia to walk in a procession in front of his horse alongside other captives for twelve miles outside the city. After reigning for only seven days, Singeric was killed and was succeeded by the Gothic chieftain Walia.

The historian records that he heard a story from a distinguished man named Valerius regarding certain silver statues that were dedicated <note>original: "consecrated"</note> to ward off barbarian invasions. During the reign of Constantius, when Valerius was the governor of Thrace, he received information about the location of a hidden treasure. He went to the spot and learned from the local inhabitants that it was considered sacred, and that certain statues had been dedicated there according to ancient rituals. 

Valerius reported this to the Emperor, who gave him written permission to remove them. When the spot was excavated, three statues of solid silver were found. They were buried in a "barbaric" style <note>The Romans often used the term "barbaric" to describe the fashion and customs of non-Roman tribes like the Goths or Huns</note>—with their arms positioned on their hips, dressed in multi-colored foreign clothing, and depicted with long hair. They were facing toward the north, the direction of the land of the barbarians.

<summary>This page describes the tragic death of Ataulf and Placidia’s infant son, the assassination of King Ataulf, the brief and cruel reign of Singeric, and a curious account of magical silver statues intended to protect Thrace from northern invaders.</summary>

<keywords>Ataulf, Galla Placidia, Theodosius (son of Ataulf), Singeric, Walia, Valerius, Thrace, silver statues, Visigoths, Roman-Gothic relations</keywords>

--- Page 148 ---
<meta>This page continues the summary of the historian Olympiodorus’s work, focusing on the supernatural defenses of the Roman Empire and the academic traditions of Athens during the 5th century.</meta>

<page-num>142</page-num>

<header>OLYMPIODORUS</header>

A few days after these statues were removed, the Goths first overran and plundered Thrace. A little later, the Huns and Sarmatians invaded Illyricum <note>A Roman province in the western Balkans</note> and Thrace itself. These consecrated areas were located between Thrace and Illyricum; based on the number of statues dedicated there, they seem to have been intended as a supernatural protection against these barbarian nations.

The historian describes the perils and hardships of his sea voyage. He also mentions that he landed at Athens, and that through his support and influence, Leontius was appointed to the chair of rhetoric <note>original: "sophistic"</note>, even though Leontius did not desire the position. Regarding the philosopher’s cloak <note>A specific garment, the *tribon* or *pallium*, worn by scholars</note>, he says that no one in Athens—especially a stranger—was allowed to wear it unless permitted by a general vote of the sophists <term>Sophists: In this era, these were highly respected professional teachers of rhetoric and philosophy.</term>, and unless his right to it had been confirmed by their formal regulations. 

The following rites were performed on such occasions: All newcomers (novices), whether young or old, were taken to the public baths. Those who were of an appropriate age to wear the cloak were brought forward by the "scholastics" <note>In this context, the term refers to the teachers or senior students who acted as escorts.</note> who accompanied them. Then, while some people ran in front to push them back, others ran behind to push them forward and resist them, all amid shouts of "Stop, stop, he must not wash!" Those who pushed back the people trying to hinder the novice’s progress were considered the winners of the contest. After a significant amount of time, and after a long traditional debate had taken place, the person being escorted was finally taken into a warm room and washed. After dressing himself, he received permission to wear the cloak as he left the bath, accompanied by a large and distinguished crowd. Large sums of money are voted for the presidents of the schools, who are called *Acromitae* <note>original: "Acromitae." A rare term likely meaning "those at the top" or senior members of the academy.</note>.

The Vandals call the Goths *Truli*, because during a time when the Goths were suffering from a severe famine, they bought a *trula* <note>A small vessel or scoop</note> of wheat from the Vandals for a gold coin <note>original: "aureus," a Roman gold coin.</note>. A *trula* holds no more than a third of a pint.

When the Vandals were ravaging Spain, the Romans who

<page-num>143</page-num>

# OLYMPIODORUS

Those who took refuge in the fortified cities were so destitute of food that they were driven to cannibalism. A woman who was the mother of four children ate them all; in each case, she pretended that she was doing so to provide food for the rest and save their lives. But when she had eaten them all, she was stoned to death by the people.

Euplutius the chamberlain was sent to Walia, King of the Goths, to negotiate a peace treaty and to recover Placidia <note>Galla Placidia, the sister of Emperor Honorius, who had been captured by the Goths after the sack of Rome.</note>. Walia received him kindly, and upon the delivery of 600,000 measures of grain <note>original: "corn"</note>, Placidia was released and handed over to Euplutius to be escorted back to her brother, Honorius.

When a discussion arose in Athens about how books could be bound <note>original: "fastened together"</note> and people wanted to know how much glue should be used, Philtatius—the author's companion and an expert in all matters related to literature—showed them what to do. A statue was erected in his honor by the grateful citizens.

Regarding the oasis <note>Specifically the Great Oasis in the Western Desert of Egypt.</note>, the author relates much that appears incredible. First, the climate is so healthy that not only do none of the inhabitants suffer from epilepsy, but those who come from other parts are cured of it. Next, he speaks of the vast tracts of sand and the wells—dug 200, 300, and sometimes even 500 cubits <note>A cubit is approximately 18 inches, meaning these wells could reach 750 feet deep.</note> deep—which spurt up a stream of water. The farmers who took part in the work take turns drawing water to irrigate their fields. The trees bear fruit perpetually, and the grain that grows there is finer than any other and whiter than snow. There are sometimes two crops of barley in a year and three of millet. The inhabitants water their little plots of land every third day in summer and every sixth day in winter, which makes the soil very fertile. Clouds are rarely, if ever, seen. He also mentions the clocks made there. The author says that the oasis was formerly an island which had been detached from the mainland, and that it is called the "Islands of the Blest" by Herodotus, but that Herodorus (who wrote the lives of Orpheus and Musaeus) calls it "Phaeacis." He argues that it was an island, first, from the fact that sea shells are found stuck to stones upon the mountain which leads to the oasis from the Thebaid <note>The region around the city of Thebes in Upper Egypt.</note>, and secondly, because of the vast quantity of sand which fills three oases. For he tells us that the oases are three in number—two large, an outer and an inner, opposite each

...separated from each other by a hundred miles, while the third is small and located a great distance from the other two. Another argument that this region was once an island is that fish are often found there, having been carried by birds, as well as the remains of fish that have been eaten; this leads one to speculate that the sea was once not far off. The author also mentions that the family of the poet Homer belonged to the Thebaid <note>A region in Upper Egypt surrounding the ancient city of Thebes</note>.

During the eleventh consulship of Honorius and the second of Constantius <note>roughly 417 AD</note>, the marriage of Placidia <note>Galla Placidia, sister of Emperor Honorius</note> was arranged. She was personally very opposed to the union, which made Constantius angry with her household servants. Nevertheless, on the first day of his consulship, her brother, Emperor Honorius, took her by the hand and—despite her protests—handed her over to Constantius. The wedding was celebrated with great splendor. They had two children: a daughter, Honoria, and a son, Valentinian. At the urgent request of Placidia, Valentinian received the title *Nobilissimus* <note>original Latin: "Most Noble," a high-ranking imperial title</note> during the lifetime of Honorius. After the death of Honorius and the defeat of the usurper John <note>John was a high-ranking civil servant, specifically the *primicerius notariorum* or "chief of the secretaries"</note>, Valentinian became emperor. 

Honorius reluctantly agreed to accept Constantius as his partner in the empire, and Placidia received the title of *Augusta* <note>Empress</note> from both her brother and her husband. However, Theodosius—the cousin of Honorius and Emperor of the East—refused to receive the diplomatic mission sent to inform him of Constantius's promotion. Constantius soon grew tired of the throne because he could no longer come and go as he pleased, and his imperial dignity prevented him from enjoying his usual amusements. This restriction seriously affected his health. After he had been on the throne for six months, a vision appeared to him and said, "Six are gone, the seventh begins." He died of pleurisy <note>An inflammation of the lungs</note>, and with him died the anger caused by the East's refusal to acknowledge his rule. The planned attack on the Eastern Empire was abandoned. Walia, King of the Goths, died and was succeeded by Theodoric <note>This refers to Theodoric I, King of the Visigoths (West Goths) from 418–451 AD. He should not be confused with the later, more famous Theodoric the Great of the Ostrogoths. This Theodoric was eventually killed fighting alongside the Romans against Attila the Hun at the Battle of Châlons.</note>.

The author also describes various dangers at sea from which he barely escaped with his life. While discussing a miraculous star...

He also tells of a parrot, with which he himself lived for twenty years, that mimicked nearly all the acts of a human being. He also relates that, when he was staying at Thebes and Syene <note>original: "Soëne"; modern-day Aswan, Egypt</note> for the sake of gathering information, the chiefs and prophets of the barbarians at Talmis <note>A town on the west bank of the Nile</note>, called Blemmyes <note>An Ethiopian people of the eastern desert</note>, were eager to meet him owing to his reputation. "They took me as far as Talmis," he says, "that I might examine the country, which is a five-day journey from Philae <note>An island in the Nile and site of a famous temple complex</note> as far as the city called Prima." This was the nearest city of the Thebaid <note>The region surrounding the ancient city of Thebes in Upper Egypt</note> to barbarian soil, and was therefore called by the Romans *Prima* <note>original Latin: "First"</note>, the name being still preserved although it has long been in the possession of the barbarians along with four other cities: Phoenicon, Chiris, Thapis, and Talmis. In this district, he heard that there were emerald mines which furnished an abundant supply of those precious stones for the Egyptian kings. The prophets of the barbarians invited him to inspect them, but this was impossible without the king's permission.

He tells a wonderful story about a certain Libanius, an Asian, who appeared at Ravenna during the reign of Honorius and Constantius; he was a most highly skilled magician. He declared that he could work wonders and promised to perform them against the barbarians without the aid of soldiers. After his promise had been put to the test, the report reached the ears of Placidia <note>Galla Placidia, sister of Emperor Honorius</note>, who threatened to demand a divorce from Constantius unless the magician and infidel was removed. Libanius was accordingly put to death. Constantius was an Illyrian from Naissus <note>original: "Naisus"; modern-day Niš, Serbia</note> in Dacia, who, having served in numerous campaigns from the time of Theodosius the Great, was afterwards raised to the throne. In many respects he was worthy of praise and of a generous disposition until his marriage with Placidia, when he became greedy and possessive. After his

<header>OLYMPIODORUS</header>

After his death, numerous petitions against him <note>Likely referring to Constantius III, whose death was mentioned on the previous page</note> from those who had been financially injured by him were presented at Ravenna. But the indifference of Honorius and Placidia’s intimacy with him made these petitions useless and thwarted the power of justice.

After the death of Constantius, Honorius lavished the greatest affection upon his sister, which, however, soon turned to mistrust and hatred. This was aggravated by the intrigues of Spadusa and Elpidia (Placidia’s nurse), in whom she had the greatest confidence, and Leontius, her steward. There were frequent riots in Ravenna, where a large number of barbarians—who sided with her because of her marriages to Ataulf and later to Constantius—frequently came to blows with the imperial guards. At length the quarrel became so bitter that, as the result of the hatred her brother now felt for her instead of love, Placidia found herself unable to resist. She retired with her children to Constantinople. Boniface alone remained loyal to her; he sent her money whenever he was able from Africa, where he was governor, and rendered her every service in his power. He also subsequently assisted her in regaining the throne.

Honorius died of dropsy <note>original: "dropsy"; an archaic medical term for edema, or the swelling of soft tissues due to the accumulation of excess water</note> on the 27th of August, and an announcement of the news was sent to the East. In the meantime, a man named John <note>Known to history as Joannes, a high-ranking civil servant who seized the throne in 423 AD</note> seized the throne. While his inauguration was taking place, a voice was heard, as if proceeding from some oracle, uttering the words, “He falls, he does not stand.” Upon hearing this, the people, as if to break the spell, shouted, “He stands, he does not fall.”

Boniface was a heroic soldier who often distinguished himself against the barbarians, sometimes with large forces, sometimes with small, and sometimes even in single combat. In short, he entirely freed Africa from many barbarian nations. He was a lover of justice and despised wealth.

The author says that each of the large houses in Rome contained all the conveniences of a well-arranged city—a hippodrome, forums <note>original: "fora"; the public squares and marketplaces that served as the centers of Roman civic life</note>, temples, fountains, and baths. This leads him to exclaim: “One house is a town; a city has ten thousand towns.” There were also public baths of great size. Those called the Antonine Baths <note>original: "Antoninianae"; the massive Baths of Caracalla, named for Emperor Marcus Aurelius Antoninus</note> had 1,600 seats made of polished marble for the convenience of bathers; those called the Diocletian Baths <note>original: "Diocletianae"; the Baths of Diocletian, the largest of the imperial baths</note> had twice as many. The wall of Rome, according to the measurement of Ammon the geometer <note>original: "geometrician"</note>, at the time when...

<meta>This page continues the description of Rome’s immense size and wealth, and details the military campaign led by Galla Placidia to reclaim the Western Empire from the usurper John.</meta>

<page-num>147</page-num>
<header>OLYMPIODORUS 147</header>

[The city of Rome,] when it was first overrun by the Goths, was twenty-one miles in circumference.

Many Roman families received annual incomes from their properties amounting to about forty *centenaria* <note>original: *centenarii*; a Roman weight unit equivalent to 100 pounds of gold</note> of gold (£160,000), not including the grain, wine, and other produce which, if sold, would equal a third of that amount. Families of the next rank enjoyed an income of fifteen or ten *centenaria* (£60,000–£40,000). Probus, the son of Olympius—who was Prefect of the City during the reign of the usurper John—spent twelve *centenaria* of gold (£48,000). Before the sack of Rome, the orator Symmachus,¹ a senator of moderate rank, and a wealthy citizen named Maximus spent twenty (£80,000) and forty (£160,000) *centenaria* respectively on their sons' praetorships. The games and shows hosted by these praetors lasted for a week.

The author [Olympiodorus] claims that the setting for Odysseus's wanderings was not the coast of Sicily, but rather the farthest shores of Italy. He asserts that after crossing the ocean, Odysseus descended into the Underworld and undertook many dangerous voyages across that sea—a theory he attempts to prove with various arguments. I have read many other writers who agree with him.

Empress Placidia was sent back from Constantinople with her children by the Eastern Emperor Theodosius II to oppose the usurper John. She was confirmed in her title of *Augusta* <note>original: *Augusta*; the title for an Empress</note>, and Valentinian in that of *Nobilissimus* <note>original: *Nobilissimus*; "Most Noble," a title for an imperial heir</note>. They set out accompanied by an army of both cavalry and infantry under the command of Ardaburius, his son Aspar,² and Candidian.³ At Thessalonica, Helion—the Master of Offices who had been sent by Theodosius—invested the five-year-old Valentinian with the imperial robes. On the journey, Ardaburius was captured by John's soldiers and taken to the usurper, with whom he established friendly relations.⁴ Meanwhile, his son Aspar and Placidia were overwhelmed by grief and anxiety. However, Candidian dispelled their sorrow and boosted their spirits by capturing many towns and winning great fame.

¹ Quintus Aurelius Symmachus (flourished around 400 AD). He served as Prefect of Rome and consul; he was the author of letters and speeches, some of which survive today.
² It was through the help of this Aspar that Leo I became Emperor of the East (457 AD), though Leo later ungratefully murdered him. Both Aspar’s father and son were named Ardaburius.
³ One of the generals of Emperor Honorius.
⁴ He only pretended to be disloyal to Placidia.

<header>THEODORE OF ANTIOCH</header>

...spirits. The tyrant John was put to death, and Empress Placidia, along with her son the Caesar [Valentinian III], entered the city of Ravenna. Helion, the Master of Offices and a member of the noble class <note>original: *patrician*</note>, took possession of Rome. In the presence of a massive crowd, he dressed the seven-year-old Valentinian in the imperial robes. At this point, the history [by Olympiodorus] ends.

### LXXXI

I read three short treatises by Theodore¹ regarding *Persian Magic and How it Differs from Christianity*,² dedicated to Mastubius, an Armenian assistant bishop <note>original: *suffragan bishop*</note>. In the first book, the author explains the wicked doctrine of the Persians, introduced by Zoroaster³ <note>original: *Zarades*</note>, concerning Zervan⁴ <note>original: *Zuruam*</note>. Zoroaster claims Zervan is the origin of all things and calls him Fortune. The text describes how, after Zervan performed a ritual offering <note>original: *libation*</note> to father Ormuzd⁵ <note>original: *Hormisdas*</note>, he actually fathered both Ormuzd and Satan. It also mentions *the ritual of mixing blood.*⁶ After laying out this unholy and shameful doctrine in plain words, Theodore disproves it in the first book. In the remaining two books, he discusses the Christian faith, starting from the creation of the world and moving rapidly through history to the Era of Grace⁷ <note>original: *law of grace*; referring to the New Testament era</note>.

This Theodore is believed to be Theodore of Mopsuestia, because he speaks favorably of the Nestorian heresy, especially in the third book. He also foolishly discusses the universal restoration of sinners to their former state <note>original: *restoration of sinners*; the doctrine of "Apokatastasis," the belief that all souls will eventually be saved</note>.

<header>DEXIPPUS</header>

...during the reign of Emperor Claudius II <note>reigned 268–270 AD</note>. I also read his *History of the Scythian Wars* <note>original: *Scythica*</note>, describing the wars between the Scythians <note>original: *Scythians*; referring here to the Goths</note> and the Romans, along with other noteworthy events. His style is free from unnecessary wordiness; it is weighty <note>original: *massive* or *grave* (the Greek term *onkos* refers to a majestic, sublime style)</note> and dignified. He might be called a second Thucydides, although he writes more clearly. His best characteristics are chiefly shown in this last-mentioned work.

In his record of the events following the death of Alexander the Great, he relates how the throne fell to Alexander's brother Arrhidaeus (the son of Philip of Macedon and Philinna of Larissa). The yet unborn child of Roxana by Alexander—should it be a son—was to be co-ruler with him. Perdiccas was chosen by the Macedonians to manage the affairs of the empire. 

### The division of Alexander’s empire 

In Asia, Ptolemy Lagus obtained the government of Egypt, Libya, and the adjacent lands beyond Egypt. Cleomenes, who had been appointed by Alexander as the <term>satrap</term> <note>a provincial governor in the ancient Persian and Macedonian empires</note> of this district, was made subordinate to him. 

*   **Laomedon of Mytilene** obtained Syria.
*   **Philotas** obtained Cilicia.
*   **Pithon** obtained Media.
*   **Eumenes** obtained Cappadocia, Paphlagonia, and the shores of the Euxine <note>The Black Sea</note> as far as Trapezus (Trebizond).
*   **Antigonus** obtained Pamphylia and Cilicia <note>likely a transcription error for Lycia</note> as far as Phrygia.
*   **Asander** obtained Caria.
*   **Menander** obtained Lydia.
*   **Leonnatus** obtained the Phrygian Hellespont. 

In Europe, **Lysimachus** obtained Thrace and the Chersonese <note>The Gallipoli Peninsula</note>. **Antipater** obtained the whole of Macedonia, Greece, Illyria, the country of the Triballi and the Agrianes, and all the mainland over which he had been appointed commander-in-chief since the time of Alexander. The general management of affairs and the defense of the kingdom was entrusted to **Craterus**. **Perdiccas** obtained the <term>chiliarchy</term> <note>original: *chiliarchy*; the command of the elite "Companions" (*Hetairoi*) cavalry, formerly held by Hephaestion</note>, which was the highest rank among the Macedonians.

Porus and Taxilus were the rulers of India: **Porus** was assigned the country between the Indus and the Hydaspes rivers, and **Taxilus** received the rest. **Pithon** received the territory of the neighboring peoples, except for the Paropamisadae <note>original: *Paramisades*</note>. The districts near the Caucasian mountains, bordering India, were given to the Bactrian **Oxyartes** (the father of Roxana). Roxana's son, born after his father’s death, was also called Alexander. **Siburtius** ruled the Arachosians and Gedrosians; **Stasanor** of Soli ruled the...

### LXXXIII

I read the twenty books of the *Histories* by Dionysius of Halicarnassus. <note>Dionysius was a Greek rhetorician and historian who moved to Rome in 30 BC and remained there until his death in 7 BC. He wrote many works on rhetoric, including a significant study of ancient Greek orators. Of the twenty books of his *History*—also titled *Roman Antiquities*—only the first eleven have been preserved, covering the period down to 441 BC. It is a rhetorical work intended to show Roman superiority while also suggesting that the Romans were originally of Greek descent to soothe Greek pride.</note> He begins with the arrival of Aeneas in Italy after the capture of Troy and describes in detail the foundation of Rome, the births of Romulus and Remus, and other events down to the war between the Romans and Pyrrhus, the King of Epirus. The work ends at the third year of the 128th Olympiad <note>approximately 266–265 BC</note>, which the author says is the point where the history by Polybius of Megalopolis begins. 

Dionysius flourished during the age of Augustus, as he tells us that he sailed to Italy after the end of the civil war between Mark Antony and Augustus and lived there for twenty-two years. During this time, he acquired an accurate knowledge of the Latin language and of Roman antiquities; having thoroughly equipped himself with research materials, he began to write his history. 

His style and choice of words are marked by innovation, which drives the narrative out of the beaten track, but his fondness for detail produces a certain simplicity of thought, so that the language does not seem to be carried away into harshness or unpleasantness. He is fond of digressions <note>departures from the main subject</note> which relieve the reader, preventing them from becoming tired of the history, and instead refreshing and reviving them. In a word, the elegance of his style, the...

<header>HERACLIAN OF CHALCEDON</header>

The inclusion of minor details and side-stories <note>original: "digressions"</note> helps to soften the writing style, which otherwise has a tendency toward harshness.

### LXXXIV

I also read the same author's *Synopsis* <note>A condensed summary or abridgment</note> of the twenty books mentioned above, which is compiled in five volumes. In this summary, his style is more refined but not nearly as pleasant to read. At the same time, the work is more practical, as nothing is included except what is absolutely necessary. Because his style is brief and avoids unnecessary repetition <note>original: "redundancies"</note>, he sounds like a king issuing a decree. His sentence structure and choice of words hit the listener’s ear with a somewhat harsher sound. While this manner of writing is suitable for a summary, it is by no means appropriate for a full and complete history. It is evident that the author lived before Dio Cassius <note>original: "Dion Coccaeus"; a Roman historian and senator of Greek origin</note> and Appian of Alexandria, both of whom also wrote on Roman history.

### LXXXV

I read the twenty books by Heraclian, Bishop of Chalcedon, titled *Against the Manichaeans.* <note>The Manichaeans followed a dualistic religious system that recognized two fundamental principles: good and evil. They believed the first was the source of man's spiritual nature and the second was the source of his physical body. They taught that the souls of men, animals, and plants were eternal; that baptism was useless; that humans lacked free will; and that Christ was actually the physical sun enlightening the world.</note> His style is brief, avoids repetition, and is elevated without sacrificing clarity; it is also balanced with a sense of dignity. He mixes Attic Greek <note>The prestigious, "pure" dialect of classical Athens</note> with everyday language, much like a schoolmaster competing to show off an even higher level of literary sophistication. 

In this work, he refutes the *Gospel*, the *Book of the Giants*, and the *Treasures*—all central texts of the Manichaeans. He also provides a list of those who wrote against the Manichaean "heresy" <note>original: "impiety"</note> before him:
* **Hegemonius**, who recorded the debate of Archelaus against Mani <note>original: "Manes"; the founder of Manichaeism</note>.
* **Titus** <note>Bishop of Bostra in Arabia (362–371 AD)</note>, who was thought to be an opponent of the Manichaeans, though he actually focused his attacks on the writings of Addas <note>One of the first three disciples of Mani</note>.
* **George of Laodicea** <note>Bishop from 335–347 AD</note>, who used nearly the same...
