<meta>This page continues the analysis of Heraclian of Chalcedon’s "Against the Manichaeans" (Section 85) and introduces the letters of John Chrysostom (Section 86).</meta>

...arguments like those of Titus against the unholy <note>original: "impious"</note> heresy; Serapion, the bishop of Thmuis <note>a city in Egypt; this work still exists</note>; and finally Diodorus <note>a priest of Antioch and later the bishop of Tarsus; he was a leader of the school that interpreted the Bible literally and logically</note>. Diodorus wrote twenty-five books against the Manichaeans. In the first seven volumes, he thinks he is refuting the *Living Gospel* written by Mani <note>original: "Manes," the founder of the Manichaean religion</note>, but he is actually refuting a work by Addas called *The Bushel* <note>original: "Modion," a Greek unit of measure; see Mark 4:21</note>. In the remaining books, he explains and clarifies the meaning of certain passages in the Scriptures which the Manichaeans were known for twisting to support their own views. This is Heraclian's account of Diodorus. Whenever the statements in the works of these Church Fathers (as the pious Heraclian calls them) do not seem forceful enough, he briefly strengthens them. He carefully fills in what is missing and quotes effective passages in their entirety, adding further reflections of his own.

Heraclian is a man of great philosophical energy and is very well-educated in other fields of study. Because of this, he energetically fights and destroys the silly fables of Mani, and by considering the reality of what exists, he disproves their nonsensical myths about "Being" (existence).

This treatise against the Manichaeans was written at the request of a man named Achillius, whom the author calls his faithful and beloved son. This Achillius, seeing that the Manichaean heresy was spreading, begged that it might be publicly disproven. The resulting work is a perfect victory over this unholy belief. The most devout Heraclian lived during the time of...

The most helpful of these are the seventeen letters addressed to the devout deaconess Olympias <note>Olympias the Younger (c. 368–420 AD). After Chrysostom was forced out of the city, she faced much harassment for her loyalty to him</note>, and those written to...

<meta>This page concludes the analysis of John Chrysostom’s letters and begins reviews of the romance novelist Achilles Tatius and a history of the Council of Nicaea.</meta>

[These were written to] Innocent I <note>Bishop of Rome from 402–417 AD</note>, the pope of Rome; in them, Chrysostom relates everything that had happened to him as far as he was able to do so in the form of letters. These letters are typical of the man. His style is brilliant, clear, persuasive, somewhat flowery <note>original: "florid"</note>, and pleasant. The letters to Olympias <note>a wealthy widow and deaconess who was a close friend and supporter of Chrysostom</note>, however, seem to have been written with even greater care. The importance of the matters discussed—for which the letter-writing style <note>original: "epistolary style"</note> is usually not intended—requires a correspondingly dignified composition.

### LXXXVII

I read the *Adventures of Clitophon and Leucippe* by Achilles Tatius of Alexandria <note>an author likely from the late 2nd century AD; his complete novel still exists today</note>, which consists of eight books. It is a dramatic work that includes some inappropriate <note>original: "unseemly"</note> romantic episodes. The word choice and structure are excellent, the style is clear, and the figures of speech are well-suited to their purpose whenever they are used. As a rule, the sentences are pithy <note>original: "aphoristic"</note>, clear, pleasant, and smooth to the ear. However, the vulgarity <note>original: "obscenity"</note> and immoral tone cloud his judgment, undermine the seriousness of the work, and make the story unpleasant to read—or something to be avoided altogether. Except for the names of the characters and his terrible indecency, the story's structure and imagination are very similar to the *Aethiopica* <note>The Ethiopian Story</note> by Heliodorus.

Also present was Eustathius, the patriarch of Antioch <note>a fierce opponent of Arianism who was later exiled on false charges</note>,

# GELASIUS OF CYZICUS

...while the priest Alexander represented Metrophanes of Constantinople. Silvester <note>Bishop of Rome, or Pope, from 314–335 AD</note>, who was over a hundred years old, was unable to attend due to his advanced age. Alexander, the bishop of Alexandria <margin>Patriarch of Alexandria (312–326 AD). He excommunicated the Arians and caused their doctrines to be condemned at the council.</margin>, also attended, along with Athanasius (who later succeeded him as bishop), Macarius <margin>Bishop of Jerusalem from about 311 to 335 AD. Helena, the mother of Constantine the Great, visited Jerusalem during his time as bishop.</margin>, the bishop of Jerusalem, and many other bishops and priests. The council <note>original: "synod"</note> was called in the sixteenth year of the reign of Constantine, and its proceedings lasted six years, until he had reigned for twenty-one years and six months.

The author reports that Arius was condemned and excommunicated <note>original: "anathematized"; a formal curse by the church excommunicating a person and condemning their doctrines</note>, but he tried again to gain readmission to the Church. He was supported in this by Eusebius <margin>Successively the bishop of Beirut, Nicomedia, and Constantinople. Though exiled by Constantine, he was recalled through Arian influence and became a bitter enemy of Athanasius. He became bishop of Constantinople in 341 AD through the patronage of Constantia, the emperor’s sister.</margin>, the bishop of Nicomedia, and Eutocius the Arian, an ordained priest whom the emperor’s sister, Constantia, had recommended to her brother on her deathbed. Although they tried to bring Arius back into the Church, divine justice did not allow its enemy to insult its temple and shrine. He was condemned to die in the public toilets <note>original: "latrines"</note> on the very day when he and his supporters had planned to desecrate the Church of God and its holy rites by his entry. His death occurred in a public area, as the toilets were near the forum <note>the central public square or marketplace of the city</note>. The author states that Constantine the Great rejoiced that God, the incorruptible judge, had resolved the matter through His sentence. The emperor wrote several letters recording his opinion on the justice of the end that had come upon Arius. In this regard, the author’s account agrees with those of Athanasius the Great, Theodoret, and many others. However, some believe that Arius met his disgraceful end not during the reign of Constantine, but during that of his son, Constantius.

This is the content of this book. In another copy of the same account, the title names the author as Gelasius, the bishop of Caesarea in Palestine <note>The identity of the various authors named Gelasius is very unclear</note>. The style

<meta>This page continues the discussion of church historians, moving from the events of the Council of Nicaea to the identity and writings of the author Gelasius of Cyzicus.</meta>

# GELASIUS OF CYZICUS

The writing style is average and common. I have not been able to determine exactly who this Gelasius was, as I have encountered three different bishops of Caesarea named Gelasius and have read works attributed to at least two of them. One of these works is a polemical treatise <note>a written attack on a specific doctrine or opinion</note> titled *Against the Anomoeans*<gloss>1</gloss>. The other two works—one of which I have just mentioned—deal with church history and affairs. The title, as we found it, is *Three Books of Church History by Gelasius, Bishop of Caesarea in Palestine*.

The work begins as follows: "The proceedings of the holy, great, and universal council <note>original: "synod"</note> of bishops, gathered from nearly every province of the Roman Empire and Persia," and so on. It concludes with the death of Constantine the Great <note>The first Roman Emperor to convert to Christianity (reigned 306–337 AD)</note>, at the moment he received the forgiveness of his sins through divine baptism, by which the stains of guilt that all people accumulate during life were washed away. The author claims that Constantine was baptized and initiated into the holy sacraments <note>original: "mysteries"</note> by an orthodox priest, and not, as some others claim, by a heretic. His baptism had been delayed because he had strongly desired to be baptized in the waters of the Jordan River. 

The writer states that he lived during the reign of Basiliscus<gloss>2</gloss>, who seized the throne after Emperor Zeno had been driven out. He explains that while living in his father's house, he discovered and read the record of the council's proceedings written on an old parchment. He compiled his history from his memories of that document and from other writings that provided useful information. He also mentions and quotes some passages from a certain Gelasius, whom he also refers to as Rufinus <note>likely Tyrannius Rufinus (c. 345–410 AD), a monk and historian known for translating Greek church works into Latin</note>. The author states that he was a native of Cyzicus and that his father served as a priest in that same city. This is the account given by the author regarding the origins and contents of his work.

## LXXXIX

The other book, which I referred to earlier, is titled *The Preface by the Bishop of Caesarea in Palestine to his Continuation of the History by Eusebius, the friend of Pamphilus* <note>original: "Eusebius Pamphili"; referring to Eusebius of Caesarea, the "Father of Church History"</note>. It begins as follows: "Others who have dedicated themselves to writing and have decided to leave a record of historical events for future generations..."

<gloss>2</gloss> A Byzantine Emperor who usurped the throne and reigned from 475 to 477 AD.

<meta>This page concludes the analysis of Gelasius of Cyzicus and begins a review of the works of the famous rhetorician Libanius.</meta>

events, and so on. The author states that he was encouraged to write the work by his uncle Cyril,¹ bishop of Jerusalem. I have read elsewhere that this Cyril and Gelasius translated the history written by Rufinus² the Roman into Greek, but did not compose any original history of their own. It is evident that this Gelasius was older than the other one, provided he was active during the time of Cyril of Jerusalem. He also certainly differs from him in the greater eloquence of his language, although both are inferior to the author of the treatise *Against the Anomoeans* <note>original: "Against the Anomoeans" — a work opposing a radical sect of Arianism that argued God the Son was "unlike" God the Father</note>, who is also called the bishop of Palestine. For this latter Gelasius—through his vocabulary, learning, and logical methods (though his use of logic is somewhat clumsy)—leaves the other two far behind, as their style appears much inferior. I have not yet been able to learn whether any of these men is the true author of the work mentioned, or who compiled it and supplemented it with his own additions.

### XC <note>90</note>

I read two volumes by Libanius.³ The author's imaginary speeches, written as practice exercises for public speaking <term>oratory</term>, are more useful than the rest of his works. The excessive complexity and over-refinement of his other writings spoil their natural and, so to speak, spontaneous grace and charm, and destroy their clarity. Much confusion is also caused by his use of parentheses <note>asides or interrupting sentences</note>, and sometimes by his habit of leaving out indispensable information. In other respects, he is a standard <term>canon</term> and model of the Attic style <note>a prestigious, classical style of Greek prose associated with ancient Athens</note>. His letters also have a considerable reputation. Several other works of different kinds are also attributed to him.

---

¹ Cyril of Jerusalem (315–386, became bishop in 348). He was exiled for several years because of Arian intrigues <note>plots by followers of Arius, who denied the full divinity of Christ</note>, but was recalled by the Emperor Julian the Apostate. His surviving *Catecheses* <note>original: "Catecheses" — lectures given to those preparing for baptism</note> contain the oldest and most concise summary of Christian doctrine.

² Rufinus Tyrannius or Toranus (345–410), born at Concordia, a small town in Italy. He is called "of Aquileia," where he was baptized and entered a monastery. He is chiefly known for his quarrel with Jerome regarding his translation of various works by Origen <note>an early Christian scholar whose views became controversial</note>. He was condemned by Pope Anastasius and died in Sicily. He was the author of a large number of translations and other works.

³ A famous sophist <note>a professional teacher of rhetoric</note> (314–393). Educated at Athens, he was a professor of rhetoric at Constantinople, where he taught students such as Saint Basil and Saint John Chrysostom. Much of his work still exists, consisting of purely rhetorical exercises and instructions, actual speeches on various subjects, a biography of Demosthenes <note>a famous Greek orator</note> along with summaries of his speeches, and an extensive collection of letters. He remained a pagan throughout his life.

# ARRIAN

### XCI <note>91</note>

I read the *History of the Reign of Alexander* by Arrian <note>Codex 58. Arrian was a 2nd-century Roman historian of Greek descent whose "Anabasis of Alexander" is considered the most reliable account of the conqueror's life.</note> in seven books. It recounts how Alexander made a treaty with the Athenians and the rest of the Greeks, with the exception of the Lacedaemonians <note>original: "Lacedaemonians" — the Spartans</note>. It tells how he crossed over into Asia and defeated the Persians in three major battles. At the Granicus <note>334 BCE</note>, he routed the satraps <term>provincial governors</term> of Darius, who commanded an army of 20,000 cavalry and nearly as many infantry. At Issus <note>333 BCE</note>, he put Darius himself and his army to flight, capturing the Persian king’s wife and children. At Arbela—also known as Gaugamela <note>331 BCE</note>—Darius was finally defeated; while attempting to escape, he was seized and put to death by his own soldiers. 

Bessus, who took Darius’s place as leader, was later mutilated and executed by Alexander as punishment for his treason toward Darius. The history also describes how Alexander was wounded seven times in battle and how he seized the royal treasure at Pasargadae <note>The earliest capital of Persia, where Cyrus the Great was buried.</note>. Becoming convinced that Philotas was conspiring against him, Alexander put him to death along with his father, Parmenio. 

The text covers Alexander's conquest of Sogdiana <note>Modern-day Uzbekistan and Tajikistan.</note> and his victory over the Asiatic Scythians. It relates how he murdered Clitus during a drunken argument and the deep remorse he felt once he became sober. It also details the conspiracy of the royal pages against Alexander and their subsequent punishment. The narrative includes the capture of the Sogdian Rock and the taking of the wife of Oxyartes, the local chieftain, along with his daughter Roxana, who later became Alexander’s lawful wife. 

Alexander then set out from Bactria against the Indians, defeating them in several battles and besieging and capturing many of their cities. This included the storming of the rock of Aornus <note>The name means "Birdless," implying a summit so high that even birds could not fly to it.</note> and the invasion of the country of the Ascanians. Having built a bridge over the Indus River, Alexander crossed over and defeated Porus, a king of India, in a single battle and took him prisoner. Alexander treated him with great generosity, not only allowing him to keep his kingdom but actually expanding its borders. The history also notes how the rivers of India, much like the Nile, flood during the summer but recede in the winter. There was also another Porus, an Indian ruler of bad character, in pursuit of whom Alexander crossed the Hydaspes <note>Modern-day Jhelum River.</note>.

<summary>This page summarizes Arrian's account of Alexander the Great's military campaigns, including his major victories over Persia, his push into India, and personal incidents like the murder of Clitus.</summary>
<keywords>Arrian, Alexander the Great, Darius, Battle of Issus, Battle of Gaugamela, Porus, Roxana, Persian Empire, Indian campaign, military history</keywords>

<page-num>158</page-num>
<header>ARRIAN</header>

He subdued the neighboring Indian tribes, took their large and populous towns by siege, and advanced as far as the Hyphasis <note>original: "Hyphasis" — the modern Sutlej River.</note>. While he was preparing to cross this river, his soldiers began to show signs of discontent, complaining of their hardships and endless marches. As a result, Alexander decided to leave India. Here the fifth book ends.

In the sixth book, the numerous battles and brilliant victories of Alexander on his journey home are recounted. During these engagements, he received two more wounds in addition to those already mentioned; although the seventh wound seemed likely to be fatal, he eventually recovered. He returned from India by land, while Nearchus <note>Nearchus was Alexander’s admiral and one of the most famous navigators of ancient times.</note> was sent back by sea with part of the army. Both divisions met in Carmania <note>Carmania: the Persian coast-land on the Persian Gulf and Indian Ocean, known today as Kerman.</note>, from which Alexander marched into Persia. Nearchus was ordered to sail to Susiana <note>Susiana: a large Persian province, identical to ancient Elam; its only city of major importance was Susa.</note> and the mouth of the Tigris. Arrian describes the voyage of Nearchus in his work *Indica*, which was written in the Ionic dialect of Greek.

Alexander repaired the neglected tomb of Cyrus the Great and allowed the gymnosophist <term>an ancient Indian philosopher-ascetic, from the Greek for "naked wise man"</term> named Calanus—who had been attacked by illness—to put himself to death on a funeral pyre. This was done according to ancestral custom <note>original: "κατὰ τὰ πάτρια" — according to ancestral custom; this was their regular practice when ill.</note>. The text also describes his splendid marriages and those of his generals. His wives were Roxana, Arsinoë (the eldest daughter of Darius), and Parysatis (the youngest daughter of Ochus). Drypetis, another daughter of Darius, was given in marriage to Hephaestion; Amastrinē to Craterus; Artacana and Artonē, daughters of Artabazus, to Ptolemy and Eumenes; the daughter of Barsinē and Mentor to Nearchus; and the daughter of Spitamenes to Seleucus. The rest of his friends, totaling eighty, received in marriage the daughters of the most prominent Medes and Persians. The discharged Macedonian soldiers were sent home, and Antipater was ordered to bring back new recruits to take their place. Meanwhile, Harpalus <note>Harpalus was Alexander’s early friend and untrustworthy treasurer. He fled from Babylon with 5,000 talents before Alexander’s return from India and went to Athens. By giving gifts of grain and money, he gained the favor of the inhabitants and protection against Antipater, who demanded he be handed over. He finally took refuge in Crete, where he was killed.</note> fled with a massive sum of money from the treasury. The death...

<meta>This page concludes the summary of Arrian’s "Anabasis of Alexander" (The Campaigns of Alexander) and begins a summary of his lost work, "Events After Alexander," which details the power struggles among his generals.</meta>

<page-num>159</page-num>
<header>ARRIAN</header>

The text describes the death of Hephaestion <note>Hephaestion was Alexander’s closest friend and most trusted general; his death in 324 BC devastated the king.</note> and the immense grief of Alexander, followed by his lavish funeral rites <note>original: "obsequies"</note>. At the same time, ambassadors arrived from Libya and Carthage, and even from Italy, to meet Alexander. When he saw the Italians, he predicted the future greatness of their country. As he prepared to enter Babylon, his seers <note>Prophets or diviners who interpreted omens.</note> predicted his death. When a stranger unexpectedly sat down on his royal throne, his approaching end was seen as even more certain. Nevertheless, he began equipping a fleet to campaign against the numerous Arabian tribes, who were said to believe in only two gods: Uranus <note>The personification of the Sky.</note> and Dionysus <note>The god of wine and festivity.</note>. While these preparations were underway, he fell ill and died. Many conflicting stories are told about his death. He lived for thirty-two years and eight months, and reigned for twelve years and eight months. Arrian praises him as having possessed almost every virtue. The seventh book of the *History of Alexander’s Campaigns* ends here, followed by the *Indica* <note>original: "Indica" — Arrian’s "History of India," which serves as a geographic and cultural supplement to his main work.</note> in one book.

## XCII

The same author also wrote an account of the events that took place after Alexander’s death, spanning ten books. <note>original: "XCII" — This refers to entry 92 in Photius’s *Bibliotheca*, a 9th-century summary of ancient books, many of which are now lost.</note> ^1 He describes the rebellion in the army and the proclamation of Arrhidaeus as king. Arrhidaeus was the son of Alexander’s father, Philip, by a Thracian woman named Philinna. He was chosen on the condition that if Roxana’s unborn child were a son, the child would share the throne with him. Arrhidaeus was then formally proclaimed under the name Philip III. A conflict broke out between the infantry and the cavalry. The most prominent and influential leaders of the cavalry were Perdiccas the son of Orontes, Leonnatus the son of Anthes, Ptolemy the son of Lagus, Lysimachus the son of Agathocles, Aristonus the son of Pisaeus, Pithon the son of Crateuas, Seleucus the son of Antiochus, and Eumenes of Cardia. Meleager was in command of the infantry. After negotiations, the infantry (who had already chosen a king) and the cavalry reached an agreement: Antipater would be the general of the forces in Europe; Craterus would look after the affairs of King Arrhidaeus's realm; and Perdiccas would be the chiliarch <term>originally a "commander of a thousand," this title designated the king's deputy or regent</term> ^2 of the troops previously commanded by Hephaestion. This appointment effectively entrusted Perdiccas with the administration of the entire

---

^1 For more information, see J. P. Mahaffy, *Alexander's Empire* (1887) in the "Story of the Nations" series, and E. R. Bevan, *The House of Seleucus* (1902).
^2 See page 149, note 4.

<meta>This page continues the summary of Arrian’s lost work "Events After Alexander" (from Photius's *Bibliotheca*), detailing the "Partition of Babylon" where Alexander the Great's generals divided his empire following his death in 323 BC.</meta>

<page-num>160</page-num>
<header>ARRIAN</header>

empire, and that Meleager should be his second-in-command <note>original: "lieutenant"</note>. Under the pretense of reviewing the army, Perdiccas seized the ringleaders of the rebellion and executed them in the presence of Arrhidaeus <note>Philip III Arrhidaeus, Alexander's half-brother, who was mentally disabled and served as a figurehead king.</note>, making it appear as if the king himself had ordered it. This struck terror into the rest of the troops, and Meleager was murdered soon afterward. 

Following these events, Perdiccas became an object of general suspicion and, in turn, suspected everyone else. Nevertheless, he began making appointments for the governorships <note>original: "satrapies"</note> of the various provinces, acting as if Arrhidaeus had commanded him. Ptolemy, the son of Lagus, was appointed governor of Egypt, Libya, and the part of Arabia bordering Egypt, with Cleomenes (the former governor of Egypt under Alexander) serving as his deputy. 

The adjacent region of Syria was given to Laomedon; Cilicia was given to Philotas; Media to Pithon; and Cappadocia, Paphlagonia, and the coastal lands of the Euxine <note>The Black Sea.</note> as far as Trapezus <note>The modern city of Trabzon, Turkey.</note> (a Greek colony from Sinope) were given to Eumenes of Cardia. Pamphylia, Lycia, and Greater Phrygia were granted to Antigonus; Caria to Cassander; and Lydia to Menander. Phrygia on the Hellespont was given to Leonnatus; this territory had previously been given by Alexander to a man named Calas and was later handed over to Demarchus. Such was the distribution of the empire in Asia.

In Europe, Thrace and the Chersonese <note>The Gallipoli Peninsula.</note>, along with the lands bordering Thrace as far as Salmydessus on the Black Sea, were given to Lysimachus. The lands beyond Thrace—extending to the territories of the Illyrians, Triballians, and Agrianians—as well as Macedonia itself and Epirus as far as the Ceraunian Mountains, along with the whole of Greece, were given to Craterus and Antipater. This was the division of Europe. At the same time, several provinces remained under their native rulers according to the arrangements Alexander had originally made, and these were not affected by the new distribution.

Meanwhile, Roxana <note>Alexander’s widow.</note> gave birth to a son, who was immediately proclaimed king by the soldiers. Following Alexander's death, many disturbances broke out. Antipater waged war against the Athenians and the rest of the Greeks, who were commanded by Leosthenes <note>This conflict is known as the Lamian War (323–322 BC), an attempt by Greek cities to free themselves from Macedonian rule.</note>. Antipater was initially defeated and found himself in great difficulty, but he was eventually victorious. However, Leonnatus, who had come to his aid, fell in battle. Lysimachus also suffered a defeat while recklessly fighting against Seuthes the Thracian with an inferior force, though his troops fought with great distinction. Perdiccas also went to war against Ariarathes, the king...

<note>The text begins by discussing Ariarathes I</note> of Cappadocia, because he refused to give up his kingdom to Eumenes, upon whom it had been bestowed <note>Eumenes of Cardia was one of Alexander's secretaries who became a powerful general.</note>. Having defeated him in two battles and taken him prisoner, Perdiccas hanged him and reinstated Eumenes. Craterus, by the assistance he rendered to Antipater against the Greeks <note>The Lamian War (323–322 BC), a Greek rebellion against Macedonian rule.</note>, chiefly contributed to their defeat, after which they unhesitatingly obeyed Craterus and Antipater. This is the contents of the first five books.

The sixth book relates how Demosthenes and Hyperides, Aristonicus of Marathon and Himeraeus (the brother of Demetrius of Phalerum), fled to Aegina. While there, they were condemned to death by the Athenians on the motion of Demades <note>Demades was a pro-Macedonian orator.</note>, and Antipater carried out the sentence. The book also details how Archias the Thurian, who put them to death, died in the utmost poverty and disgrace. It tells how Demades was soon afterward sent to Macedonia, where he was put to death by Cassander after his son had been murdered in his arms. Cassander alleged in excuse that Demades had once insulted his father, Antipater, in a letter which he wrote to Perdiccas, begging him to rescue the Greeks, whom he abusively described as being held together only by an "old and rotten thread" (referring to Antipater). Dinarchus of Corinth was the accuser of Demades, who paid the just penalty for his venality <note>original: "venality"; meaning bribery and corruption</note>, treachery, and unfaithfulness.

The author also relates how Harpalus, who during the lifetime of Alexander had stolen money belonging to him and fled to Athens, was slain by Thibron the Spartan <note>original: "Lacedaemonian"</note>. Thibron seized all the money that remained and set out for Cydonia in Crete, from where he crossed over to Cyrene <note>In modern-day Libya.</note> with a body of 6,000 men at the request of some exiles from Cyrene and Barca. After many battles and mutual plots, in which he was sometimes successful and sometimes unsuccessful, he was finally captured during his flight by some Libyan drivers. He was taken to Epicydes the Olynthian at Teuchira, which had been entrusted to him by Ophellas, a Macedonian whom Ptolemy (the son of Lagus) had sent to help the Cyrenians. The inhabitants, by permission of Ophellas, first tortured Thibron and then sent him to the port of Cyrene to be hanged. But since the Cyrenaeans still persisted in their revolt, Ptolemy in person visited the place, and after having restored order, sailed home again.

Perdiccas, plotting against Antigonus, summoned him to stand trial <note>original: "called him to judgment"</note>, but Antigonus, aware of the plot, refused to appear.

# ARRIAN

This led to deep hostility <note>original: "enmity"</note> between them. At the same time, Iollas and Archias came from Macedonia to see Perdiccas, accompanied by Nicaea, the daughter of Antipater, with a proposal of marriage. Olympias, the mother of Alexander the Great, also sent messengers to Perdiccas, offering him the hand of her daughter Cleopatra <note>Cleopatra was Alexander the Great's full sister; marrying her would have given Perdiccas a direct claim to the Macedonian throne.</note>. Eumenes of Cardia favored the match with Cleopatra, but Perdiccas’s brother Alcetas persuaded him to accept Nicaea instead. 

Soon afterward, Cynane was put to death by Perdiccas and his brother Alcetas. This Cynane was the daughter of Philip <note>Philip II, Alexander’s father</note> and her mother was Eurydice, the wife of Amyntas. Alexander had put this Amyntas to death just before he set out for Asia. Amyntas was the son of Perdiccas (Philip’s brother), making him Alexander’s cousin. 

Cynane brought her daughter Adea (later called Eurydice) to Asia and offered her hand in marriage to Arrhidaeus <note>Philip III Arrhidaeus, Alexander's half-brother and successor king.</note>. The marriage eventually took place with the approval of Perdiccas. He allowed it to soothe the increasing anger of the soldiers, which had been sparked by the execution of Cynane. 

In the meantime, Antigonus <note>Antigonus "the One-Eyed," a powerful general and future king.</note> took refuge with Antipater and Craterus in Macedonia. He informed them of Perdiccas's plots against him, declaring that these schemes were directed against all of them. He also described the death of Cynane in such dramatic <note>original: "exaggerated"</note> terms that he persuaded them to go to war against Perdiccas. 

Arrhidaeus <note>This Arrhidaeus was the general in charge of the funeral procession, not the king.</note>, who had custody of Alexander’s body, ignored the wishes of Perdiccas. He took the body from Babylon and traveled through Damascus to Ptolemy, the son of Lagus, in Egypt. Although he was often hindered on his journey by Polemon, a friend of Perdiccas, he nevertheless succeeded in his plan.

Meanwhile, Eumenes carried gifts from Perdiccas to Cleopatra at Sardis, since Perdiccas had decided to reject <note>original: "repudiate"</note> Nicaea and marry Cleopatra instead. When Antigonus learned of this through Menander, the governor of Lydia, he informed Antipater and Craterus. This made them more determined than ever to make war on Perdiccas. 

Antipater and Craterus set out from the Chersonese <note>The Gallipoli Peninsula.</note> and crossed the Hellespont <note>The narrow strait between Europe and Asia, now known as the Dardanelles.</note>, having first sent messengers to deceive those guarding the passage. They also sent ambassadors to Eumenes and Neoptolemus, who were supporters of Perdiccas. Neoptolemus defected to their side, but Eumenes refused.

Because Eumenes suspected Neoptolemus of treachery, war broke out between them, and Eumenes was victorious. Neoptolemus...

Antigonus fled with a few men to Antipater and Craterus and succeeded in persuading Craterus to join him; so they both waged war against Eumenes. Eumenes did his best to prevent his own men from knowing that Craterus was fighting against them. He was afraid that, influenced by Craterus's great reputation, his soldiers might either desert to the enemy or, if they remained faithful, lose their courage. Eumenes was as successful in his schemes as he was in battle. Neoptolemus was killed by the hand of Eumenes "the Secretary" <note>original: "the secretary"; Eumenes had served as the lead administrator and record-keeper for Philip II and Alexander the Great before becoming a general.</note> himself, after Neoptolemus had proven himself a brave soldier and commander. Craterus, who fought boldly against everyone who opposed him and showed himself openly so he would be recognized, was killed by some Paphlagonians <note>A group from the northern coast of Anatolia (modern-day Turkey).</note> before they realized who he was, even though he had thrown off his hat. <note>original: *kausia*, a light, broad-brimmed felt hat worn by the Macedonians to keep off the burning heat (*kausis*) of the sun.</note> However, the infantry escaped and returned to Antipater, which considerably reassured him.

Perdiccas, setting out from Damascus to make war upon Ptolemy the son of Lagus, reached Egypt with the kings <note>The kings were Arrhidaeus (Philip III) and the infant Alexander IV, the son of Roxana.</note> and a large force. He made many accusations against Ptolemy, who publicly cleared himself, so that the charges appeared to be baseless. Despite the opposition of his troops, Perdiccas decided to carry on the war. He was twice defeated. Because he had treated those who were inclined to defect to Ptolemy with great severity and had otherwise behaved in camp with more arrogance than was appropriate for a general, he was killed by his own cavalry during an engagement. 

After Perdiccas's death, Ptolemy crossed the Nile to visit the kings, upon whom he bestowed gifts. He treated them, as well as the other high-ranking Macedonians, with the utmost kindness and attention. At the same time, he openly showed sympathy for the friends of Perdiccas and did all he could to calm the fears of those Macedonians who imagined they were in danger. Because of this, he was held in great esteem immediately and ever afterward.

At a full council of war, Pithon and Arrhidaeus <note>This Arrhidaeus was a general, not to be confused with King Philip III Arrhidaeus.</note> were appointed commanders-in-chief of all the forces for the time being. About fifty of the supporters of Eumenes and Alcetas were condemned, chiefly because Craterus had died during this civil strife. Antigonus was summoned from Cyprus, and Antipater was ordered to go with all speed to the kings. Before they arrived, Eurydice <note>The wife of King Philip III Arrhidaeus; she was a politically ambitious woman who attempted to seize control of the regency.</note> refused to allow Pithon and Arrhidaeus to manage affairs...

When they arrived, Antigonus was placed in chief command. When the army demanded the pay that had been promised them for the campaign, Antipater replied straightforwardly that he had no money; however, to avoid being blamed by them, he said he would thoroughly search the treasury and other places where money might be hidden. 

These words angered the army. When Eurydice <note>The wife of the mentally disabled King Philip III Arrhidaeus; she sought to exercise political power in her husband's name.</note> joined in the accusations against Antipater, the people became indignant and a riot broke out. Eurydice then delivered a speech against him, in which she was assisted by Asclepiodorus the scribe and supported by Attalus. Antipater barely escaped with his life after Antigonus and Seleucus, at his urgent request, addressed the people on his behalf and nearly lost their own lives as a result. Antipater, having thus escaped death, withdrew to his own army, where he summoned the cavalry commanders. After the disturbance was put down with difficulty, he was once again reinstated in his command.

He then made a division of Asia, partly confirming the earlier distribution of provinces and partly altering it as circumstances required:

It was no easy matter to displace these men, since they had been confirmed in their positions of government by Alexander himself, and their strength had greatly increased. Of the countries to the north of Mount Taurus, Cappadocia was assigned to **Nicanor**; Greater Phrygia, Lycaonia, Pamphylia, and Lycia remained with **Antigonus** as before; Caria was given to **Asander**; Lydia to **Clitus**; and Phrygia on the Hellespont to **Arrhidaeus**. **Antigenes** was appointed to collect the revenues in the district of Susa, and 3,000 of the Macedonians who were inclined toward mutiny were sent with him. 

As the kings’ bodyguards <note>The "kings" refers to the joint reign of Alexander’s disabled half-brother, Philip III Arrhidaeus, and his infant son, Alexander IV.</note>, Antipater appointed Autolycus (the son of Agathocles), Amyntas (the son of Alexander and brother of Peucestes), Ptolemy (the son of Ptolemy), and Alexander (the son of Polysperchon <note>original: "Polyperchon"</note>). He made his own son, **Cassander**, the **chiliarch** <term>original: "chiliarch of the cavalry"; a high-ranking commander of 1,000 men, often serving as a deputy to the regent</term> of the cavalry. Meanwhile, Antigonus received command of the forces which had formerly been under Perdiccas, along with the care and custody of the kings’ persons and, at his own request, the task of finishing the war against **Eumenes**. Antipater, having secured the general approval for everything he had done, returned home. With this, the ninth book concludes.

The tenth book relates how Eumenes, having heard what had happened to Perdiccas and that he himself had been declared an enemy of the state by the Macedonians, made all preparations for war. It tells how Alcetas, the brother of Perdiccas, took refuge with him for that reason; and how **Attalus**, who had been one of the ringleaders in the insurrection against Antipater, also joined the exiles with a force of 10,000 infantry and 800 horsemen. It describes how Attalus and his troops attacked Cnidus, Caunus, and Rhodes, but the Rhodians, under their admiral Demaratus, completely repulsed them. 

The book also recounts how Eumenes nearly came to blows with Antipater upon his arrival at Sardes, but **Cleopatra** <note>The sister of Alexander the Great and a powerful political figure in her own right.</note>, wanting to prevent the Macedonian people from accusing her of starting a war, persuaded Eumenes to leave Sardes. Nevertheless, Antipater reviled her for her friendship with Eumenes and Perdiccas. She defended herself more vigorously than a woman was expected to do, brought counter-charges against him, and in the end, they parted amicably. Eumenes, having unexpectedly attacked those who did not acknowledge his authority, collected much booty and money, which he distributed among his soldiers. He also sent messages to Alcetas and his friends, begging them to assemble...

Meanwhile, Cassander was at odds with Antigonus, but by the command of his father, Antipater, he abandoned his opposition. Nevertheless, when Cassander met his father in Phrygia, he advised him not to let himself get too far away from the kings and to keep a close watch on Antigonus. Antigonus, however, through his quiet behavior, courtesy, and good qualities, did everything he could to remove any suspicion. 

Antipater, being satisfied, appointed Antigonus to the command of the forces which had crossed over with him to Asia—8,500 Macedonian infantry and the same number of foreign cavalry, together with half the elephants (seventy in total)—to assist him in ending the war against Eumenes. Thus, Antigonus began the war. 

Antipater, accompanied by the kings <note>The "kings" are the infant Alexander IV and the intellectually disabled Philip III Arrhidaeus.</note> and the rest of his forces, made it appear as though he were about to cross over into Macedonia. However, the army mutinied again and demanded their pay. Antipater promised that he would pay them when he reached Abydos, or at least give them the greater part of it if not the whole amount. Having encouraged their hopes in this way, he reached Abydos without further disturbance. But having deceived the soldiers, he crossed the Hellespont <note>The narrow strait now known as the Dardanelles, connecting the Aegean Sea to the Sea of Marmara.</note> by night with the kings to join Lysimachus. On the following day, the soldiers also crossed, and for the moment, they made no further demand for their pay. With this, the tenth book ends.

### An Appraisal of Arrian’s Style

This author is second to none among the best historical writers. He is very strong in concise narrative and never disrupts the flow of the story with ill-timed digressions or long asides. He is original in his arrangement rather than his vocabulary, which he uses in such a way that it would be impossible for the story to be told more clearly or plainly. 

His style is distinct, pleasing to the ear, and concise,¹ characterized by a combination of smoothness and grandeur. His innovations in language are not merely far-fetched or strange; they are clear and impactful—true figures of speech rather than just a simple changing of ordinary words. The result is that clarity is achieved not only in his language but also in the structure, order, and nature of the narrative itself, which is the hallmark of his artistic skill.

---

¹ Or "well-rounded."

<vocab>Arrian, Antipater, Eumenes, Antigonus, Cassander, Asander, Attalus, Alcetas, Phrygia, Abydos, Hellespont, Lysimachus</vocab>

<summary>This page concludes the narrative of the successor wars following Alexander the Great's death, detailing Antipater's maneuvers against Eumenes and his strategic retreat to Macedonia. It concludes with a scholarly praise of Arrian's clear, concise, and sophisticated historical writing style.</summary>

--- Page 173 ---
<meta>This page continues the stylistic evaluation of Arrian's writing before transitioning into a summary of his work "Bithynica," which details the history of his homeland.</meta>

<page-num>167</page-num>

<header>ARRIAN</header>

the very essence of clarity <note>original: "perspicuity"</note>. For straightforward sentences <note>original: "periods"; in classical rhetoric, a "period" is a long, well-structured sentence often containing multiple clauses.</note> are used even by those who are not specialists. However, if this is done without anything to provide variety, the style sinks into dullness and ordinariness <note>original: "meanness"; referring here to a lack of literary dignity or being too common.</note>. In spite of his clarity, there are no traces of such flaws in our author. He makes use of omissions <note>original: "ellipsis"</note>—not of entire sentences, but of specific words—so skillfully that the omission is not even noticed. Any attempt to provide the missing words would seem like adding unnecessary fluff and would not actually improve the text. The variety of his rhetorical figures is admirable; they do not suddenly depart from simple forms and common usage, but are gradually woven in from the beginning, so that they neither cause boredom through excess nor create confusion by sudden changes. In short, anyone who compares him with other historians will find that many classical writers are inferior to him in their composition.

### XCIII

I have read the same author’s *Bithynica* <note>A history of Bithynia, an ancient region in northwest Asia Minor (modern-day Turkey).</note> in eight books, containing a detailed account of the mythical and general history of Bithynia. It is a history of his own country, dedicated to it as a patriotic offering. He tells us specifically in this work that he was born in Nicomedia, was raised and educated there, and held the office of priest to Demeter and her daughter <note>The daughter is Persephone; both were primary deities in the ancient Greek religion.</note>, to whom the city was sacred.

He mentions various other works he has written, such as the life of the Corinthian Timoleon in Sicily, and the notable deeds of Dion of Syracuse. Dion was the man who freed Syracuse and the whole of Sicily from the second Dionysius (the son of the first tyrant) and from the barbarians whom Dionysius had brought in to support his rule. It appears that the history of his own country was the fourth work he wrote, following his histories of Alexander the Great, Timoleon, and Dion. 

It is clear that from the time he first began his literary career, he had intended to write on this subject, but the work took some time to complete due to a lack of source material; at least, this is the reason he gives for the delay in its production. He begins, as mentioned, with the mythical history of the region and continues down to the death of the last Nicomedes. <margin>The first independent king of Bithynia was Nicomedes I (278 B.C.); the Nicomedes referred to here is Nicomedes IV, who left his kingdom to Rome in his will in 74 B.C.</margin>

### XCIV

I have read the *Dramaticon* of Iamblichus,¹ a narrative of love adventures. *The author displays fewer indecencies than Achilles Tatius, but he is more immoral than the Phoenician Heliodorus.* Of these three writers, who have all adopted the same subject and have chosen love intrigues as the material for their stories, Heliodorus is more serious and restrained, Iamblichus less so, while Achilles Tatius pushes his obscenity to the point of shamelessness <note>original: "impudence"</note>. The style of Iamblichus is soft and flowing; if there is anything vigorous and resonant <note>original: "sonorous"</note> in it, it is characterized less by intensity than by what may be called a stimulating but weak quality <note>original: "titillation and nervelessness"; suggesting the prose is more concerned with sensory pleasure than structural strength.</note>. Iamblichus is so distinguished by his excellent style, arrangement, and narrative order that it is a pity he did not devote his skill and energies to serious subjects instead of to childish <note>original: "puerile"</note> fictions.

The characters of the story are a handsome couple named Rhodanes and Sinonis, joined by the bonds of mutual love and marriage. Garmus, king of Babylon, having lost his wife, falls in love with Sinonis and is eager to marry her. Sinonis refuses and is bound with chains of gold, while Rhodanes is placed upon a cross by Damas and Sacas, the king's eunuchs. He is taken down through the efforts of Sinonis, and the lovers flee, one thus escaping death and the other a hated marriage. Sacas and Damas have their ears and noses cut off and are sent to hunt for the fugitives. They take different routes to carry out the search. Rhodanes and Sinonis are nearly caught by Damas in a meadow. A fisherman had told Damas about some shepherds who, after being tortured, finally showed him the meadow where Rhodanes had discovered a treasure. This treasure had been revealed to Rhodanes by an inscription engraved on a <term>cippus</term>² topped with a lion.

A ghost <note>original: "spectre"</note> in the form of a goat falls in love with Sinonis, which forces the lovers to leave the meadow. Damas finds a garland of flowers dropped by Sinonis and sends it to—

¹ A Syrian romance writer who probably lived around the middle of the second century AD. The complete work no longer survives <note>original: "is no longer extant"; see Code 73 of Photius's *Bibliotheca*.</note>.
² <term>cippus</term>: A small, low pillar used by the ancients as a landmark or a tombstone.

<keywords>Iamblichus, Dramaticon, Greek Romance, Rhodanes, Sinonis, Garmus, Achilles Tatius, Heliodorus, Photius</keywords>

--- Page 175 ---
<meta>This page continues the plot summary of the "Dramaticon" (also known as the *Babyloniaca*) by Iamblichus, picking up during the flight of the lovers Rhodanes and Sinonis from King Garmus of Babylon.</meta>

# IAMBLICHUS

...[marriage to] Garmus as a consolation. <note>This continues the sentence from the previous page: Sinonis fled to avoid a forced marriage intended to console the king after his first wife's death.</note> In their flight, the lovers come across an old woman at the door of a hut; they hide themselves in a cave, thirty stades <term>stade: an ancient Greek unit of distance; thirty stades is roughly 3.4 miles or 5.5 kilometers</term> long and open at both ends, the mouth of which is concealed by thick bushes. Damas comes up with his companions and questions the old woman, who is terrified by the sight of an unsheathed sword. The horses on which Rhodanes and Sinonis had ridden are captured. 

The soldiers surround their hiding place; the bronze <note>original: "brazen"</note> shield of one of those who were keeping watch strikes against the cave; the hollow echo reveals the whereabouts of the fugitives. The soldiers begin to dig, and Damas's shouts reach the ears of those within. They retreat further into the cave and make their way to the second opening. Here, a swarm of wild bees attacks the diggers, and drops of honey also fall upon the fugitives. The bees, as well as the honey, are tainted with poison because they have eaten certain venomous reptiles, so that the diggers who are stung either lose a limb or die.

Rhodanes and his companion, overcome by hunger, lick up some drops of the honey; they are seized with abdominal pain <note>original: "colic"</note> and collapse on the road as if dead. The soldiers, exhausted by the attack of the bees, take flight but later renew the pursuit of the lovers. Seeing Rhodanes and Sinonis lying prostrate on the road, they pass them by, taking them for two dead strangers. While in the cave, Sinonis had cut her hair and made a rope with it to draw water; Damas finds it and sends it to Garmus as a pledge <note>original: "earnest"</note> of the speedy capture of the fugitives. 

The soldiers who passed by where Rhodanes and Sinonis were lying on the road pay respect to them as if they were really dead, according to the custom of the country; some cover them with their tunics, while others throw over them anything they have at hand—even pieces of bread and meat—and then go their way. The lovers recover from the stupor caused by the honey; Rhodanes had been awakened by some crows quarreling over the pieces of meat and then woke Sinonis. Getting up, they go in the opposite direction of the soldiers so as to be less easily recognized. They meet two donkeys <note>original: "asses"</note> and mount them, having first loaded them with some of the supplies the soldiers had thrown over them when thinking they were dead. They stop at an inn but soon leave it for another in the neighborhood of a crowded marketplace. Two brothers have died, and the lovers are accused of their murder but are eventually acquitted.

<summary>This page details the harrowing escape of Rhodanes and Sinonis, including their concealment in a long cave and a death-like stupor caused by poisonous honey that leads soldiers to mistake them for corpses.</summary>
<keywords>Iamblichus, Babyloniaca, Rhodanes, Sinonis, Garmus, Damas, poisonous honey, ancient Greek romance, Photius</keywords>

--- Page 176 ---
<meta>This page continues the summary of Iamblichus's *Babyloniaca*, detailing the ongoing flight of the lovers Rhodanes and Sinonis as they evade the soldiers of King Garmus.</meta>

# IAMBLICHUS

The elder of the two brothers, who had poisoned the younger and accused the lovers, poisons himself, thereby proving their innocence. Rhodanes takes possession of the remaining poison without being seen. They stay at the house of a brigand <term>brigand: a member of a gang of outlaws or robbers</term> who robbed travelers and ate them. Soldiers sent by Damas capture the brigand and set fire to his house; Rhodanes and Sinonis, surrounded by the flames, barely escape with their lives after they kill their donkeys and throw the carcasses onto the fire to create a bridge across the blaze. The soldiers who set the house on fire meet them during the night and ask who they are. "We are the ghosts of those murdered by the brigands," they reply. Their thin, pale faces and the weakness of their voices convince the soldiers they are telling the truth, leaving the men greatly alarmed. 

The lovers resume their flight and encounter a young girl being carried to her grave, so they join the crowd of spectators. An old Chaldean <note>original: "Chaldaean"; in this context, an ancient Babylonian priest or astrologer known for wisdom and prophecy</note> comes forward and stops the funeral, claiming that the girl is still alive, and his claim proves true. He predicts to Rhodanes and Sinonis that they will one day reach royal rank. The girl’s grave is left empty, and a large portion of the robes intended to be burned, along with the funeral food and drink, is left behind. Rhodanes and Sinonis eat a good meal, take some of the clothes, and sleep inside the grave. 

In the morning, the soldiers who had burned the house realize they were deceived and set out to pursue Rhodanes and Sinonis, imagining they are accomplices of the brigand. Having traced them as far as the grave and seeing them lying there motionless—overcome by wine and sleep—the soldiers imagine they are looking at corpses and leave them, though they were hesitant because the tracks had led them directly there. <note>The original text notes an alternative reading: "or, 'being uncertain whether their footsteps led thither.'"</note> 

Rhodanes and Sinonis leave the grave and cross the river, the waters of which are sweet and clear and reserved for the King of Babylon alone to drink. When Sinonis tries to sell the clothes she took from the grave, she is arrested for sacrilege <term>sacrilege: the violation or misuse of what is regarded as sacred</term> and brought before Soraechus, the son of Soraechus the tax collector, who is known as "the Just." Because of her beauty, he intends to send her to King Garmus. At this, Rhodanes and Sinonis prepare a dose of poison, preferring death to the sight of the King. Their plan is revealed to Soraechus by a female slave; he secretly empties the cup containing the deadly potion and refills it with a sleeping draught.

<summary>Rhodanes and Sinonis survive a cannibalistic robber's house fire and a funeral-turned-prophecy before being captured; they attempt a suicide pact to avoid King Garmus, unaware that their poison has been replaced with a sleeping potion.</summary>
<keywords>Rhodanes, Sinonis, Soraechus the Just, Damas, Chaldean, Babylon, prophecy, escape, suicide pact</keywords>

--- Page 177 ---
<meta>This page continues the summary of Iamblichus's "Babyloniaca," picking up as the soldiers discover the lovers, Rhodanes and Sinonis, in a tomb where they have fallen into a deep, drug-induced sleep.</meta>

# IAMBLICHUS

After they have drunk the potion and are in a deep sleep, they are placed in a carriage to be taken to the king. A little way from Babylon, Rhodanes is frightened by a dream and cries out in his sleep; this wakes Sinonis, who, in a fit of despair, takes up a sword and wounds herself in the breast. Soraechus <note>a local official or magistrate in the story</note> wants to know their history, and after the lovers receive a solemn promise of safety from him, they tell him everything. He sets them free and points them toward a temple of Aphrodite <note>the Greek goddess of love</note> on a small island, where Sinonis can be healed of her wound.

By way of a digression <note>original: "digression"; a temporary departure from the main narrative</note>, the author relates the history of the temple and the little island, which is formed by the surrounding waters of the Euphrates and Tigris rivers. The priestess of Aphrodite had three children: Euphrates, Tigris, and Mesopotamia. The last, Mesopotamia, was born ugly but was eventually changed into a woman so beautiful that three suitors quarreled for her hand. Bochorus, the most famous judge of the time, was chosen to decide their claims, and the three rivals pleaded their cause. Now, Mesopotamia had given one of them the cup from which she drank, had crowned the second with a garland of flowers from her own head, and had kissed the third. Bochorus decided that she belonged to the one whom she had kissed, but this decision only embittered the quarrel, which ended in the rivals killing one another. 

In another digression, the author gives details of the temple of Aphrodite. The women who visit it are obliged to reveal in public the dreams they have had while staying in the temple; this leads to minute details about Pharnuchus, Pharsiris, and Tanaïs, after whom the river is named. Pharsiris and Tanaïs initiated those who lived on the banks of the river into the mysteries <term>mysteries: secret religious rites or cults</term> of Aphrodite. Tigris died on the little island mentioned earlier after eating some roses; in the buds, which were not yet fully in bloom, lurked a poisonous little beetle. His mother believed she had made him a demi-god through her enchantments.

Iamblichus then describes different kinds of enchantments—using locusts, lions, and mice. According to him, the last is the oldest, with the "mysteries" being named after these animals. <note>The author is playing on the Greek words "mysterion" (mystery) and "mys" (mouse).</note> There are also enchantments involving hail, snakes, necromancy <term>necromancy: the practice of communicating with the spirits of the dead</term>, and ventriloquism. The ventriloquist is called Eurycles by the Greeks and Sacchuras by the Babylonians. The author calls himself a Babylonian and says that, after learning the art of magic, he devoted himself to the study of the...

--- Page 178 ---
<meta>This page continues the summary of Iamblichus’s "Babyloniaca," a Greek novel about the adventures of the lovers Rhodanes and Sinonis. On the previous page, the author provided a digression on the history of a temple of Aphrodite located on an island between the Euphrates and Tigris rivers.</meta>

-># IAMBLICHUS<-

Iamblichus was a master of Greek arts and sciences. He flourished <note>original: "flourished"; reached his prime and was most active</note> during the reign of Soaemus, son of Achaemenides the Arsacid <note>a member of the royal dynasty of the Parthian Empire</note>, who held his ancestors' throne and later served as a Roman senator, consul, and king of Greater Armenia <note>this occurred around 164 AD</note>. During this era, Marcus Aurelius was the Roman emperor. When Aurelius sent Verus—his adopted brother, son-in-law, and fellow emperor—to wage war against the Parthian king Vologaesus <note>also known as Vologases III, who reigned from 148–190 AD</note>, Iamblichus predicted the start, progression, and conclusion of the conflict. He also describes how Vologaesus fled across the Euphrates and Tigris rivers, and how the kingdom of Parthia was transformed into a Roman province.

Tigris and Euphrates, the children of the priestess mentioned earlier, were very similar in appearance, and the hero Rhodanes resembled them both. As previously mentioned, the boy Tigris had died after being poisoned by eating roses. When Rhodanes crosses over to the island with Sinonis, Tigris’s mother sees Rhodanes and declares that her son has returned from the dead, accompanied by Kore <note>original: "Korē"; meaning "The Maiden," a title for Persephone, the queen of the underworld. If the word is not capitalized, it could simply mean "and bids her daughter follow him."</note>. Rhodanes plays along with this deception, highly amused by how easily the islanders are fooled. 

Meanwhile, Damas <note>an official pursuing the lovers</note> is informed of Rhodanes and Sinonis's whereabouts and learns how Soraechus <note>a local official who previously helped them</note> has aided them. His informant is the physician whom Soraechus had secretly sent to treat Sinonis's wound. Consequently, Soraechus is arrested and taken to Garmus <note>the King of Babylon</note>. At the same time, the informant is sent with a letter to the priest of Aphrodite, ordering him to capture Rhodanes and Sinonis. To cross the river, the physician clings to a camel’s neck in the customary way, having first tucked the letter into the animal’s right ear. The physician drowns in the river, but the camel reaches the island alone. Rhodanes and Sinonis find Damas's letter in the camel's ear and realize the danger they are in.

They immediately flee and encounter Soraechus while he is being taken as a prisoner to Garmus; they all stay at the same inn. During the night, Rhodanes bribes certain men to kill Soraechus's guards. Soraechus then flees with the lovers, which serves as a reward for his earlier kindness to them. Meanwhile, Damas arrests the priest of Aphrodite and questions him about...

--- Page 179 ---
<meta>This page continues the summary of the plot of Iamblichus’s "Babyloniaca," picking up as the characters Sinonis and Rhodanes face further complications in their flight from King Garmus.</meta>

-># IAMBLICHUS<-

Sinonis; the old man is condemned to change his religious service <note>original: "ministry"</note> for the office of executioner; the manners and customs relating to this office are described. Euphrates—whom his father the priest mistakes for Rhodanes and calls by that name—is arrested, and his sister Mesopotamia takes to flight. Euphrates is taken before Sacas <note>an officer of King Garmus</note> and questioned about Sinonis, being mistaken for Rhodanes and examined as such. Sacas sends a messenger to Garmus to inform him that Rhodanes is captured and that Sinonis soon will be. This occurs because Euphrates, when questioned under the name of Rhodanes, was obliged to call his sister Mesopotamia by the name of Sinonis; he declares that "Sinonis" fled when she saw him arrested.

The fugitives Rhodanes, Sinonis, and Soraechus stay at the house of a farm laborer. He has a beautiful daughter who has just lost her husband and has cut her hair out of her affection for him <note>cutting one's hair was a common sign of mourning in the ancient world</note>. She is sent to a goldsmith to sell the gold chain which Sinonis had brought from her former prison. The goldsmith, seeing the beauty of the young woman and recognizing part of the chain (which he happened to have made himself), and noticing that she has her hair cut, suspects that she is Sinonis <note>King Garmus had previously ordered Sinonis's hair to be cut as a humiliation</note>. He accordingly informs Damas <note>an official pursuing the lovers</note> and has the laborer's daughter secretly watched. Suspecting what is happening, she takes refuge in an empty house. 

The text then recounts the story of the young girl named Trophime: of the slave who was both her lover and murderer; of the golden ornaments; of the lawless conduct of the slave; of his suicide; of the blood that splashed <note>original: "spirted"</note> over the laborer's daughter when the murderer was committing suicide; of the fear and flight of the young woman; of the terror and flight of those who were keeping watch on her; of the young woman's return to her father; of the story of her adventures; of the departure of Rhodanes; and of the letter sent by the goldsmith to inform Damas that Sinonis has been found. To confirm his letter, he sends the chain which he has bought and mentions the other suspicious circumstances connected with the laborer's daughter.

At the moment of leaving, Rhodanes kisses the laborer's daughter. Sinonis is furiously jealous; at first, she had only suspected this kiss, but her suspicions were confirmed when she wiped off the marks of blood with which his lips were stained <note>blood from the suicide in the empty house had splashed onto the girl, and then onto Rhodanes when he kissed her</note>. Sinonis makes up her mind to kill the young woman and hastens back like a madwoman, followed by Soraechus, who is unable to calm her passionate fury.

<meta>This page continues the summary of the plot of Iamblichus’s "Babyloniaca," following the flight of the lovers Rhodanes and Sinonis and the various misunderstandings regarding their identities.</meta>

-># IAMBLICHUS<-

They stayed at the house of a wealthy man of immoral <note>original: "dissolute"</note> habits named Setapus, who fell in love with Sinonis and tried to seduce her. She pretended to return his love, but at night, while Setapus was intoxicated, she stabbed him with a sword. She then ordered the servants to open the door and left Soraechus—who was unaware of what had happened—setting out in haste to find the laborer’s daughter. When Soraechus heard of her departure, he started in pursuit, having hired some of Setapus's slaves to accompany him so he could prevent the murder of the laborer's daughter <note>Sinonis likely intended to kill the girl to prevent her from being mistaken for her or out of jealousy</note>. He overtook Sinonis, made her get into a carriage which had been prepared beforehand, and turned back with her. On their return, the servants of Setapus, who had found their dead master, rushed upon them in a rage. They seized Sinonis, bound her, and took her to Garmus to be punished as a murderess. Soraechus, having sprinkled his head with dust and torn his cloak <note>traditional signs of mourning</note>, announced the sad news to Rhodanes, who would have killed himself had he not been prevented by Soraechus.

Garmus, having received the letters from Sacas and the goldsmith informing him of the capture of Rhodanes and Sinonis <note>referring to the false Rhodanes and Sinonis mentioned on the previous page</note>, rejoiced greatly. He offered sacrifices to the gods, ordered preparations to be made for the marriage, and issued a decree that all prisoners should be unbound and set free. Consequently, Sinonis was released from her bonds by the servants of Setapus <note>ironically, the king's decree to celebrate his "marriage" to the fake Sinonis led to the release of the real Sinonis</note>. Garmus ordered Damas to be put to death, and he was handed over to the priest whom he himself had previously deprived of his priesthood and turned into an executioner. Garmus was angry with Damas because he had allowed others to have the honor of arresting the supposed Rhodanes and Sinonis. Damas was succeeded in his office by his brother Monasus.

The text then relates the story of Berenice, daughter of the king of Egypt: of her disgraceful affairs <note>original: "amours"</note> and of her intimacy with Mesopotamia, who was afterwards seized by Sacas and sent to Garmus as "Sinonis," along with her brother Euphrates. Garmus, hearing from the goldsmith that the real Sinonis had escaped, ordered him to be put to death. He also ordered the guards, who had been assigned to watch the pretended Sinonis and bring her to him, to be buried alive along with their wives and children.

An Hyrcanian dog <note>a breed of dog from the region of Hyrcania, near the Caspian Sea, famous in antiquity for its ferocity</note> belonging to Rhodanes found the bodies of the unhappy girl and the slave (her infatuated lover and murderer) in the ill-fated <note>original: "ill-omened"</note> inn. It had already devoured the body of the slave and half-eaten that of the...

<summary>Sinonis kills the lecherous Setapus and is captured, but she is inadvertently freed by King Garmus's celebratory decree. Meanwhile, Garmus punishes his officials for their failures while the truth of the mixed-up identities begins to surface.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the summary of the plot of Iamblichus’s "Babyloniaca," following the escape of Rhodanes and Sinonis and the confusion caused by their many "doubles" or look-alikes.</meta>

-># IAMBLICHUS<-

...the young girl, when the father of Sinonis <note>In this convoluted plot, this likely refers to a man who mistakenly believes the dead girl is his daughter, or the summary is referring to a character acting as her father.</note> comes on the scene. Recognizing the dog as belonging to Rhodanes and seeing the half-eaten body of the girl, he first kills the dog as a sacrifice to Sinonis and then hangs himself, having first buried the remains of the girl and written on her tomb with the blood of the dog: “Here lies the beautiful Sinonis.” Meanwhile, Rhodanes and Soraechus arrive and see the dog lying dead by the tomb, the father of Sinonis hanging by a rope, and the epitaph written on the tomb. Rhodanes stabs himself and adds to the epitaph for Sinonis the words: “and the handsome Rhodanes,” written in his own blood. Soraechus puts his head in a noose, and Rhodanes is preparing to give himself a fatal blow when the laborer’s <note>original: "labourer’s"</note> daughter rushes in, shouting loudly, “Rhodanes, the one who lies here is not Sinonis!” She runs and cuts the rope by which Soraechus is hanging and snatches the dagger from Rhodanes's hand. At last, she manages to convince them by telling the story of the unhappy girl and of the buried treasure she had come to carry off.

Meanwhile Sinonis, released from her bonds, hurries to the laborer’s house, still furious with his daughter. Unable to find her, she asks the father where she is; when he tells her the way she has taken, she immediately sets out in pursuit with a drawn sword. At the sight of Rhodanes lying on the ground and her rival sitting alone by his side, trying to stop the bleeding <note>original: "staunch"</note> of the wound in his chest (Soraechus having gone to fetch a physician), her rage and jealousy know no bounds, and she rushes upon the young woman. But Rhodanes, forgetting his wound at the sight of her violence, finds the strength to throw himself in front of Sinonis and hold her back, while simultaneously snatching the sword from her hands. Sinonis, consumed with rage, rushes out of the inn and, running like a madwoman, shouts to Rhodanes: “I invite you today to Garmus’s wedding!” <note>King Garmus is the antagonist who wants to marry Sinonis by force.</note> Soraechus, upon his return, hears what has taken place and consoles Rhodanes. After his wound has been dressed, the laborer’s daughter is sent back to her father with money.

Euphrates and Mesopotamia—the two people mistaken for <note>original: "the supposed"</note> Rhodanes and Sinonis—along with Soraechus and the real Rhodanes, are taken before Garmus. Garmus, seeing that Mesopotamia is not Sinonis, hands her over to Zobaras with orders to cut off her head on the banks of the Euphrates River, to prevent anyone else...

<summary>This page describes a series of tragic misunderstandings in which Rhodanes and Soraechus nearly commit suicide over a girl they mistake for Sinonis. After the real Sinonis appears and flees in a jealous rage, the protagonists and their doubles are captured and brought before King Garmus.</summary>
<keywords>Iamblichus, Babyloniaca, Rhodanes, Sinonis, Soraechus, King Garmus, mistaken identity, suicide attempt, ancient Greek romance</keywords>

--- Page 182 ---
<meta>This page continues the summary of the plot of Iamblichus’s "Babyloniaca," moving from the scene at the tomb to the subsequent fates of Euphrates, Soraechus, and Rhodanes.</meta>

-># IAMBLICHUS<-

In the future, she adopts the name Sinonis. Meanwhile, Zobaras, who has already fallen in love <note>original: "drunk at the fountain of love"</note>, is captivated by Mesopotamia. He spares her life and sends her back to Berenice, who became Queen of Egypt after her father’s death and from whom Mesopotamia had been abducted.¹ Berenice is reunited with Mesopotamia, whose situation causes King Garmus to threaten war.

Euphrates <note>In this story, Euphrates is a character, likely named after the river.</note> is delivered to his father, who is now an executioner. The father recognizes him and spares his life. Euphrates then takes his father's place—preventing his father's hands from being stained <note>original: "soiled"</note> with human blood—and later, disguised as the executioner's daughter, he escapes from prison and regains his freedom.

This was the state of affairs when Soraechus was condemned to be crucified. The designated place of execution was the meadow with the fountain where Rhodanes and Sinonis had first rested during their escape. It was the same spot where Rhodanes had discovered hidden treasure; he tells Soraechus about it as the latter is being led to his death. A group of Alans <note>The Alans were a nomadic Iranian people of antiquity.</note>, outraged because they had not been paid by Garmus, drive away the guards and free Soraechus. King Garmus had stopped at the place where the execution was to happen, but the Alans intervened. Having found the treasure Rhodanes described and cleverly removed it from its hiding place, Soraechus convinces the Alans that he has learned of this and other secrets from the gods. After gradually gaining their trust, he persuades them to elect him as their king. He eventually goes to war against Garmus and defeats him, though these events happened later in the story.

While Soraechus is on his way to his intended execution, Garmus—wearing flower garlands and dancing—orders Rhodanes to be taken back to his previous execution site and placed upon a cross. While Garmus, drunk and dancing around the cross with flute players, gives himself over to celebration, he receives a letter from Sacas. The letter informs him that Sinonis has just married the young King of Syria. Rhodanes is overjoyed by the news. Garmus initially wants to kill himself, but he changes his mind and forces the reluctant Rhodanes—who would have preferred death—to come down from the cross. Garmus then appoints Rhodanes commander of an army to be sent against the King of Syria, intending to pit the lover against his rival.

<meta>This page concludes the summary of Iamblichus's "Babyloniaca" and reviews two theological works: one by John Scythopolita against Monophysite heresies, and a biography of St. John Chrysostom by George of Alexandria.</meta>

->JOHN SCYTHOPOLITA<-
->VOL. I. M<-

Rhodanes is received by the army in a friendly but treacherous manner. Garmus had privately instructed the generals under Rhodanes that if their army was victorious and Sinonis was captured, they were to put Rhodanes to death. Rhodanes wins the victory, recovers Sinonis, and becomes the King of Babylon, just as a swallow had foretold. For when Garmus himself came to see Rhodanes set out on the expedition, an eagle and a hawk <note>original: "kite"</note> pursued this swallow; it escaped the eagle but became the prey of the hawk. This concludes the contents of the sixteen books.

### XCV

Read the work of John Scythopolita the Scholar <note>original: "Scholasticus"</note> titled *Against Separatists from the Church*, or *Against Eutyches*¹ and *Dioscorus*¹ *and those who held the same ideas*, and denied that there were two natures in Christ <note>These groups were known as Monophysites, who believed Christ had only one divine nature rather than being both fully human and fully divine.</note>. The work, consisting of twelve books, was written at the request of a certain patriarch named Julian.² The writer’s style is clear and pure, and he uses vocabulary suited to a historical style. He vigorously combats heresy and makes full use of evidence from Scripture, while also using logical methods when they are appropriate to the subject. The author of the separatist treatise attacked by Scythopolita concealed his name, but craftily titled it *Against Nestorius* in order to trick simpler-minded people into reading it. It is perhaps the work of Basil of Cilicia,³ who afterwards wrote a theatrical <note>original: "dramatic"</note> dialogue against John that was worthy of the religion he professed.

### XCVI

Read the work by George, Bishop of Alexandria,⁴ titled *The Life of St. Chrysostom*. I cannot state with certainty who the author is. The style is simple, at times sinking into crude and vulgar language; even the proper construction of nouns and verbs, usually followed even by elementary students,⁵ is—
