<meta>This page continues the review of George of Alexandria’s biography of St. John Chrysostom, moving from the author's sources to the details of John's birth, early education in Antioch, and his influential travels to Athens.</meta>

<page-num>178</page-num>

# GEORGE OF ALEXANDRIA

...neglected. The author states that he has compiled his history using material taken from Bishop Palladius,¹ who wrote an admirable and careful life of Chrysostom in the form of a dialogue, as well as from Socrates Scholasticus² and other writers.

According to the author, the great John [Chrysostom] was born in Antioch to noble parents named Secundus and Anthusa. Meletius the Armenian, who was then the head of the Church at Antioch,³ initiated the parents into the rites of Christianity and prepared them to receive the saving grace of baptism, after having first initiated and baptized their son. John began school at an early age. From his boyhood, he was known for his modesty; he showed none of the pampered behavior <note>original: "effeminacy"</note> common to children of wealthy and high-born families,⁴ and he would not even ride on horseback. In Antioch, he attended the lectures of Libanius on grammar and rhetoric, and those of Andragathius on philosophy. After his father’s death, he became the primary comfort of his mother; abstaining from all worldly pleasures and amusements, he devoted himself entirely to his studies.

He traveled to Athens to further his knowledge, and in a short time, he proved himself so superior to all other students that Anthemius—the priest of the Temple of Athena and reputed to be the wisest man in Athens—became jealous of him. Demosthenes, the prefect <term>prefect: a high-ranking administrative official or governor</term> of the city, sent a very complimentary summons to him, in response to which John presented himself with great humility. In a discussion that took place between them, John showed himself superior in learning, intelligence, and piety. A marvelous result of this was that Anthemius, finally convinced by John’s divine eloquence and prayers, was baptized along with his entire household by the bishop of the city. The prefect, who had already been baptized, received further instruction in the doctrines of Christianity alongside a large number of former pagans <note>original: "heathen"</note>. The bishop of the city wanted to ordain John and leave him as the bishop in his place, but when John became aware of this, he secretly and hastily set sail for his own country.

His friends and acquaintances wanted him to enter the legal profession, but he was himself inclined toward a monastic life, even though he was only eighteen years of age. Two of his fellow students, Theodore—who later became the Bishop of Mopsuestia—and Maximus...

***

¹ *circa* 364–431, Bishop of Helenopolis in Bithynia and author of the *Lausiaca* <note>original: "Historia Lausiaca," a collection of biographies of early Christian desert hermits</note>.
² See Code XXVIII.
³ About 360.
⁴ Add: “he would not allow such people to accompany him.”

<meta>This page continues the biography of John Chrysostom by George of Alexandria. It follows John's transition from his studies in Athens and Antioch to his early clerical career and his eventual withdrawal into a monastery following his mother's death.</meta>

<page-num>179</page-num>

<header>GEORGE OF ALEXANDRIA</header>

...who later became the Bishop of Seleucia, gave up a career in public and for-profit service to choose a private and simple life. He was a very close friend of Basil the Great (not the other Basil, as some claim), who was ordained as a deacon by Meletius and whom John valued more than any of his other friends. Basil said goodbye to John and tried to convince him to adopt the same lifestyle, but for a time, John's mother stood in the way. Bishop Zeno arrived from Jerusalem <sup>1</sup> and appointed John as a reader <term>reader: a minor clerical rank in the early church responsible for reading scripture during public worship</term> in the church at Antioch. Soon after, his mother died. John gave away his father's inheritance to the poor, left the city, and entered a nearby monastery, where he became a model and example for the other monks.

Hesychius, a Syrian monk who was known for his gift of prophecy <note>original: "knowledge of the future"</note>, had a vision of two men in white clothing. One held a book and the other held keys, both of which they gave to John. The one with keys identified himself as the Apostle Peter, and the one with the book as John the Theologian <note>John the Theologian: a title used for John the Apostle, the traditional author of the Fourth Gospel and Revelation</note>. Hesychius told the other monks about this, but took care that it did not reach John's ears, fearing that John's great humility might cause him to flee the monastery. During this time, John also practiced intense religious devotions and wrote several books on monastic life.

He also performed miracles while in the monastery. One citizen suffered from such severe pain on one side of his head that his right eye had been pushed out; however, when he consulted John, he was healed instantly. A wealthy and prominent man named Archelaus, who suffered from leprosy on his face, was told to wash in the pool used by the monks for drinking water. He was cured; afterward, he gave away his wealth, renounced the world, and entered the monastery, followed by many others. Another man named Eucleus, who had lost his right eye due to the influence of a demon <note>original: "evil spirit"</note>, asked to join the monastery. His head was shaved while the man of God prayed for him, and his sight was restored. A woman who had suffered from chronic bleeding <note>original: "issue of blood"</note> for seven years was also healed. Finally, a lion that was reported to have killed several travelers was itself killed after John made the sign of the cross over others; the lion was destroyed by the power of that sign.

After four years, owing to the number of people who applied...

<meta>This page continues the biography of John Chrysostom by George of Alexandria. Following the visions of Peter and John the Apostle, the narrative describes John's ascetic life, his return to Antioch for his health, and his rise through the church hierarchy through divine signs and miracles.</meta>

<page-num>180</page-num>

<header>GEORGE OF ALEXANDRIA</header>

To him, he left the monastery and spent two years in a cave, rarely sleeping and not lying down during the entire time. Having developed a chill in his stomach and kidneys <note>original: "contracted a chill," likely referring to chronic health issues caused by his extreme asceticism</note>, he was forced to return to Antioch. There, he was ordained as a deacon by Meletius and served at the altar. At that time, he wrote the three treatises to Stagirius¹ and the books *On the Priesthood* and *On the Incomprehensible*. After the death of Meletius at Constantinople, the holy John returned to the monastery. Flavian, who had succeeded Meletius, brought him back to the city from the monastery and ordained him a priest because of a divine revelation. A command had been given to Flavian in a vision that John should be ordained and that Flavian himself should perform the ceremony. A dove that hovered over John's head during the ceremony was seen as clear proof of the divine grace with which he was to be filled. He spent twelve years serving in the sanctuary. From his early years, because of his passion for virtue, he was harsh and strict, and more prone to anger than to showing consideration for others. He wrote several commentaries while at Antioch and, at the bishop’s urgent request, spoke to the people spontaneously from the pulpit.

The son of a woman named Euclaea, who was suffering from a violent fever and whose life had been despaired of, was healed by being sprinkled with water that John had blessed. A certain woman belonging to the sect of the Marcionists,² whose husband held an office in the city, was in a desperate condition from dysentery. However, after being healed by John, she, her husband, and their entire household, along with several other Marcionists, returned to the true faith.

On the death of Nectarius, the Archbishop of Constantinople,³

---

¹ A young friend of Chrysostom who, after leading an undisciplined <note>original: "dissipated"</note> life, entered a monastery. He was at first unable to endure the restrictions and suffered from convulsions (which were then regarded as demonic attacks), but he gradually reached a more religious and gentler state of mind. The treatises contain Chrysostom's advice to his friend.

² Named after Marcion of Sinope, a leader of a heretical sect <term>heresiarch: the founder or leader of a group that contradicts established church doctrine</term> in the second century A.D. His followers believed in two Christs: one sent by an unknown God to save the whole world, and another whom the Creator would one day send to save the Jews. They denied the resurrection of the physical body and argued that the body of Christ only looked like a human body but was not actually real.

³ He succeeded Gregory of Nazianzus and served as archbishop from 381 to 397 (or 398).

It concludes with the death of Archbishop Nectarius, setting the stage for John's move to Constantinople.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the biography of John Chrysostom by George of Alexandria, detailing his move from Antioch to Constantinople to serve as Patriarch and his early reforms and missionary efforts.</meta>

<page-num>181</page-num>

<header>GEORGE OF ALEXANDRIA</header>

John was summoned from Antioch, despite the protests of the residents who claimed him as their own special blessing. But the Emperor’s command prevailed; John was consecrated by Theophilus, the Patriarch of Alexandria <note>Theophilus served as Patriarch from 385–412 AD</note>, who was reluctant to perform the ceremony. Theophilus was forced to do so because certain papers containing charges against him were held over him as a threat if he did not comply. While John was being consecrated amid general approval, he performed a miracle by freeing a possessed man from an evil spirit.

The great Chrysostom <note>meaning "Golden-Mouthed," a title given to John for his legendary eloquence</note> then immediately abolished the custom of clergy receiving "spiritual sisters" <term>spiritual sisters: women (subintroductae) who lived with celibate clergy, a practice Chrysostom found scandalous and prone to abuse</term> and delivered long sermons against the unjust, the gluttonous, and the pleasure-loving. He was so charitable that many called him "the Almsgiver." In short, he taught every virtue and discouraged every vice. He also sent monks to Phoenicia to convert those who were dedicated to idolatry; these monks, armed with imperial authority, overthrew the pagan temples, with the expenses paid for by certain pious women. A band of Celts <note>likely referring to Goths or other Germanic tribes serving in the Roman military</note>, who had been infected by the Arian heresy <term>Arianism: the belief that Jesus Christ was a created being and not co-eternal with God the Father</term>, was brought back to the true faith by missionaries who spoke their language. He also voluntarily brought the nomadic Scythians back to Christianity. He completely rooted out the Marcionite heresy <note>an early sect that rejected the Old Testament and believed the God of the New Testament was different from the God of the Jews</note>, which was rising again in the East. He increased the number of nightly services that included chanting. 

He took his meals alone for three reasons: he abstained entirely from wine because it affected his head (except when he drank it flavored with roses in the summer); he suffered from a weak stomach so that he often could not eat the food served to him and had to ask for something else; and when he was at leisure, he often refrained from food all day. The clergy considered him very harsh and austere. His deacon, Serapion, was also a source of great hatred toward him. John expelled several members of the clergy from the Church for various reasons. Serapion also quarreled with Severian, the Bishop of Gabala, who developed a deep and lasting hatred for John. John was greatly loved by the people because of his sermons. He himself was very fond of commenting upon the letters of St. Paul; according to John’s friend Proclus, St. Paul visited him for three nights and inspired his interpretation of the epistles.

John also offended the Empress in the case of Theodoric the

...the patrician woman, whom he had successfully freed from the Empress Eudoxia's unfair financial demands <term>exactions: the act of demanding or levying money or taxes through authority</term>. Theodoric gave the greater part of his property to the church poorhouse as a thank-offering to God, which inflamed Eudoxia with anger and malice. 

Eutropius <note>a powerful official and advisor to Emperor Arcadius</note> introduced a law stating that criminals who fled for refuge to the churches should not enjoy the privilege of asylum. But when, soon afterwards, he himself was forced to take refuge there, he reaped the bitter fruits of his own legislation. While he lay face down at the altar, the great John delivered a speech full of harsh criticism, which turned many people against John; they thought he rebuked the miserable man too cruelly. 

John deprived the Arians <term>Arianism: the belief that Jesus Christ was a created being and not co-eternal with God the Father</term> of their churches and, with the Emperor's consent, drove them out of the city. Since the Arians had composed responsive hymns <term>antiphons: a short sentence or hymn sung or recited responsively</term> to deceive simple-minded people, John outdid them—with the assistance of the Empress—by displaying silver crosses while his own hymns were being sung. It is said that the God-inspired Ignatius <note>Saint Ignatius of Antioch (c. 35–107 AD)</note> first introduced these responsive hymns in imitation of the angels, who sing the praises of God in this manner. 

The influential Arian general Gaïnas <margin>Gaïnas (died 401 AD) was a Roman general of Gothic birth. He later rebelled, was declared an enemy of the state, and was eventually killed by the Huns.</margin> demanded a church from the Emperor, but John, who was present, expressed his disapproval with great boldness and persuaded the Emperor to refuse. Soon after, when Gaïnas rebelled, John immediately went on a diplomatic mission to the barbarian general at the public's request and successfully put down the revolt.

Eusebius, who succeeded Celbianus as bishop of Valentinopolis <margin>Modern-day Valenza in Liguria, Italy.</margin>, presented a document containing seven charges against Antoninus, the bishop of Ephesus. The first three charges accused him of sacrilege; the fourth was that he had kept a youth who had committed murder in his service without punishing him; the fifth was that he had seized and sold land that had been left to the Church by Basilina, the mother of the Emperor Julian <margin>Julian the Apostate, who reigned 361–363 AD.</margin>; the sixth was that he had returned to a sexual relationship with his wife after he had supposedly retired from worldly life, and that he had fathered a child with her; the seventh was that he accepted fees for ordaining priests <term>consecration: the act of ordaining someone into a holy office, in this context, the sin of Simony (selling church positions)</term>. This last charge, being regarded as the most serious, was investigated. The trial was dragged out to a great length because the accuser himself intentionally neglected his duties, and Antoninus died before the trial could end. John therefore...

<meta>This page continues the biography of John Chrysostom, focusing on his ecclesiastical reforms in Ephesus and the growing political and religious conspiracies against him involving Empress Eudoxia and various rival bishops.</meta>

<page-num>183</page-num>

<header>GEORGE OF ALEXANDRIA</header>

John went to Ephesus and removed six bishops from their sees <term>sees: the official seats or jurisdictions of bishops</term> because they had paid bribes to secure their ordinations and had confessed their guilt. He also deposed six others in Asia for the same offense. In place of Antoninus, he ordained Heraclides, his own deacon, an act which caused a disturbance. In place of the six removed bishops, others were appointed who were known for their greater piety and virtue. However, when Chrysostom was later banished, all these men were stripped of their positions, while those who had been removed were restored.

Severian, the bishop of Gabala, heard that Antiochus was in Constantinople and had earned a great deal of money through his sermons, so he went there himself. When John set out for Ephesus, he recommended Severian as his substitute in the pulpit; in this way, Severian became well known to the Emperor and all the people.

Callitrope, the widow of a ship captain, had been unfairly taxed, and Paulacius, the Prefect <term>prefect: a high-ranking administrative official or governor</term> of Alexandria, harshly pressured the poor woman for the money (500 gold pieces). She appealed to the Empress, who fined Paulacius 100 pounds of gold; however, the suffering woman only received thirty-six gold pieces from that amount. Consequently, she sought refuge with the "universal port in a storm," the great John, who filed a lawsuit against Paulacius to force the payment of the 500 pieces to the widow. This provoked the hostility of Eudoxia, who was anxious for Paulacius to be excused. John did not listen to her, however, and the righteous man claimed and restored to the mistreated woman the money of which she had been unfairly cheated. Then a miraculous thing happened: when Eudoxia sent men to rescue Paulacius in defiance of John, an angel appeared carrying a spear and terrified her messengers, so that their mission failed.

Because of these and similar actions by John, several men—including Acacius of Beroea, Theophilus, Antiochus, and Severian, as well as many others whom he had offended with his criticisms—began to plot against him with the help and encouragement of Eudoxia. Theophilus accused Peter, the chief priest of Alexandria, of having administered the sacrament to a woman who was a Manichaean <term>Manichaean: a follower of a dualistic religious movement that the early Church viewed as a major heresy</term>. Peter's defense was that she had been converted and that he had granted her permission to be admitted to communion. To prove this, he called upon Isidore—the priest<sup>1</sup> and hospital-manager of—to serve as a witness.

---

<sup>1</sup> Isidore was a priest and monk of the fifth century AD. He was a great friend and supporter of Athanasius <note>Saint Athanasius (c. 296–373 AD), the Bishop of Alexandria and a chief defender of Trinitarianism</note>.

<meta>This page continues the narrative of the political and religious tensions surrounding John Chrysostom, moving from his reforms in Ephesus to the rising conflict involving Theophilus of Alexandria and the persecuted Egyptian monks known as the "Tall Brethren."</meta>

<page-num>184</page-num>

<header>GEORGE OF ALEXANDRIA</header>

Alexandria. This Isidore, because of his blameless character, had previously been sent to Damasus¹ by Theophilus. He had brought an offer of friendship and alliance from Rome to Flavian, ending a twenty-year disagreement between the two churches. However, Isidore’s testimony later provoked the anger of Theophilus, who expelled a man named Peter from the Church and falsely accused Isidore of gross immorality. 

When this lie was discovered, Theophilus was driven to even greater wickedness, fueled by the following incident: a wealthy woman named Theodota had given Isidore 1,000 pieces of money to distribute among the poor without consulting Theophilus. Isidore had carried out her request. To escape Theophilus’s rage, Isidore fled to the mountain of Nitria,² where he had previously lived in a monastic cell. 

The leaders of the Egyptian monks were Dioscorus, Ammonius, Euthymius, and Eusebius—four brothers known as "the Tall Brethren" <note>original: "the long"</note> because of their great height. At that time, a dispute had broken out involving the Anthropomorphite <term>Anthropomorphite: a group of Christians who believed that God possessed a physical human body, interpreted literally from biblical descriptions</term> heretics. When some uneducated and crude monks started a riot in Egypt, Theophilus—who was apparently frightened when they insulted him—tried to manipulate them with flattery, saying, "I have seen your faces as if they were the face of God." 

However, when these monks further demanded that the works of Origen <note>Origen (c. 184–253) was a prolific early Christian theologian whose more metaphorical interpretations of scripture were later deemed controversial</note> be condemned <term>anathematized: formally cursed or excommunicated by the Church</term> because he argued that God has no human form, Theophilus agreed to their demands just to save his own life. Using this as an excuse against the "Tall Brethren" (since they had stopped associating with him and also denied that God had a human form), he denounced them to the other monks. He incited the uneducated mob against them and also against Isidore, toward whom he was especially hostile. 

Finally, after suffering from plots and abuse, and having their monastic cells set on fire, the "Tall Brethren" fled to Constantinople. John <note>John Chrysostom, the Bishop of Constantinople</note> received them with kindness and sympathy, but he did not allow them to take Holy Communion for fear of offending Theophilus. John wrote a letter to Theophilus suggesting they reconcile, but Theophilus ignored him. In the meantime, the "Tall Brethren" submitted formal charges against Theophilus, and in return, they were accused by others at Theophilus's prompting. When these accusers were unable to prove anything, they were thrown into prison and whipped; some of them died, and the rest were condemned

...into exile on the island of Proconnesus. <note>Proconnesus: Modern-day Marmara Island (named for its marble) in the Sea of Marmara.</note> John informed Theophilus of the charges brought against him, to which Theophilus replied angrily: "I believe you are familiar with the canons <term>canons: laws or decrees ordained by a church council</term> of the Council of Nicaea, which dictate that no bishop shall exercise authority outside his own province. If you are not, then familiarize yourself with them and do not interfere with the charges against me." Nevertheless, the very same Theophilus who wrote these words later condemned John, even though John belonged to a different diocese. As the monks did not stop their accusations against Theophilus, the emperor ordered him to appear for trial. However, the hostility against John grew to such an extent that, upon his arrival, Theophilus was instead appointed as the judge for John’s own trial.

The wife of the senator Theognostus—who had been outlawed and died in exile—had been stripped by the empress of a field that had been left to her. She turned to John, the champion of widows, for help. Although he spoke with great courage on her behalf, his efforts were unsuccessful and only provoked the hatred of the empress. Consequently, John ordered that on the day of the Exaltation of the Cross (September 14th), when the unjust empress was about to enter the church, the gates should be shut against her. His order was carried out, and the empress retreated in shame and anger; from that time on, she began to plot John’s removal, banishment, and every other humiliation her rage could suggest. When she had approached and found the church gates being closed, one of her attendants drew his sword against those shutting them; suddenly, his hand withered, though it was later restored after he showed respect <note>original: "doing homage"</note> to John.

The great Epiphanius, whom Theophilus had deceived and stirred up against John, caused a disturbance upon his arrival in Constantinople. He ordained a deacon at Hebdomon <note>Hebdomon: A suburb of Constantinople, located seven miles from the city center.</note> in St. John’s Church contrary to church law, performed the service without Chrysostom’s permission, and demanded that John condemn the writings of Origen. Our author records (as is also stated in the *Life of Epiphanius*) that Epiphanius did not actually approve of the removal of Chrysostom, despite what others believed or the empress’s constant pressure. <meta>This page continues the narrative of the conflict between John Chrysostom, Bishop of Constantinople, and Theophilus, Bishop of Alexandria. It details the legal maneuvers and political backlash following John's initial deposition and brief return to power.</meta>

Before his condemnation, John—having heard that Eudoxia was angry with him—delivered a lengthy sermon against women in general, which the people interpreted as a direct attack on the empress. Upon the arrival of Theophilus, the plots against the great champion, John, began. When John did not appear at the synod <term>synod: an assembly of church leaders to decide on matters of doctrine or administration</term>, Theophilus and his faction pronounced a sentence against him, even though John loudly protested that he was ready to appear and defend himself if his known enemies were removed from the council.

Forty bishops were prepared to support John against Theophilus and his party. However, when they protested loudly, John comforted them and begged them not to cause division within the Church. Following his removal from office, he was banished to Hieron,¹ but due to a severe earthquake—which was interpreted as a sign of divine anger—he was brought back to the city. Against his own will, he was placed back on his bishop's throne. He declared that he did not wish to resume his duties as a pastor until the unjust sentence against him had been properly investigated. 

Soon afterward, Eudoxia became enraged again because John had criticized her regarding a statue erected in her honor near his church; its presence was causing disturbances inside the sanctuary. Consequently, new plots were set in motion against him. Theophilus, although this was an unexpected stroke of good fortune for him, did not appear in person because he feared the hatred of the citizens. The reason for their hatred was that, after John’s removal, Theophilus had reconciled with the "Tall Brothers" <note>original: "long brethren"; a group of four monks whom Theophilus had previously persecuted</note>—by whose help he had plotted against John—and because he did not stop reading the writings of Origen, even though he had accused John of doing that very thing. 

Therefore, he did not attend in person but sent others to declare that John should not even be allowed a trial. They argued that because John had dared to perform church services after his removal, the Synod of Antioch left no room for his defense, as it forbade anyone from defending themselves once they had been stripped of their office. Those who supported John argued that both the law <term>canon: a church law or rule</term> and the synod itself were tainted with Arianism <note>Arianism: a theological belief that Jesus was created by God the Father and was not co-eternal with Him; it was considered a heresy by the mainstream Church</note>, and that the law had been specifically designed to target Athanasius. They further argued that the Council of Sardica² had rejected the validity of that synod and not only allowed...

Athanasius sought not only to defend himself but also to perform the duties of the priesthood alongside Marcellus.

Consequently, Chrysostom was prohibited from officiating in the church and even from entering it. The festival of the Birth of Christ was approaching, and until the feast of Pentecost and for five days afterward, he took no part in the services. He was then finally expelled from the city and the church and was banished to Cucusus. <note>A village on the borders of Armenia and Cilicia.</note> At that time, a fire broke out in the pulpit and spread to the rest of the building. Many of John's enemies were made an example of, being carried off by disgusting diseases or disasters sent from heaven. After John was deported to Cucusus, he both lectured in public and consecrated several bishops, priests, and deacons. He also performed many miracles during his banishment and after his death, which Basiliscus—the bishop of Comana and a martyr <note>Martyred in 312 AD.</note>—foretold after appearing to him in a vision. John was buried in the same grave as the martyr.

Theophilus and his party condemned Heraclides, the bishop of Ephesus, in his absence. Furthermore, after suffering cruel insults, Serapion was deprived of the bishopric of Heraclea, a position to which he had been consecrated by John after his first return from exile. A eunuch belonging to the tribune Victor, a man of disgraceful character, was elected in his place. About twenty other bishops were driven from their sees <term>sees: the jurisdiction or office of a bishop</term>, together with a large number of priests, deacons, and laypeople who were accused of favoring John. These included some pious women, the most distinguished of whom were Olympias, <note>A wealthy widow and deaconess who was a close friend of Chrysostom.</note> Pentadia, Procle, and Silvanē.

Innocent, the Bishop of Rome, strongly supported John’s cause, although without success. He sent messengers who were dismissed with scorn <note>original: "contumely"</note> and wrote letters, but his efforts were unavailing. Subsequently, Arsacius was with difficulty induced to enter John's name on the diptychs. <note>Literally, "a double-folded tablet" of wood, ivory, or metal. In the early Church, these were tablets on which the names of those specially commemorated during the Eucharist were written.</note> Some time afterward, Proclus transported John's remains to Constantinople.

This writer appears to relate much that is contrary to historical truth, but there is nothing to prevent the reader from picking out what is useful and passing over the rest.

<summary>This page describes the final exile and death of John Chrysostom, the divine retribution allegedly faced by his enemies, and the eventual rehabilitation of his memory within the Church.</summary>

<keywords>John Chrysostom, Cucusus, Theophilus, Serapion, Olympias, Bishop Innocent of Rome, diptychs, church history, exile, miracles</keywords>

-># PHLEGON OF TRALLES<-

### XCVII

I have read the *Collection of Chronicles and List of Olympian Victors* by Phlegon of Tralles <note>original: Phlegon. He was a writer from Tralles in Lydia (modern-day Turkey) who lived until the reign of Emperor Antoninus Pius. His other surviving works include *On Marvels* and *On Long-lived Persons*.</note>, a freedman <note>A former slave who has been granted his freedom.</note> of the emperor Hadrian. The work is dedicated to a man named Alcibiades, who was one of the emperor's bodyguards. It begins with the 1st Olympiad <note>Beginning in 776 BC.</note> because, as nearly all other writers agree, there are no careful or accurate accounts of the periods before then. Different writers make conflicting statements, and even those who were eager to claim the credit for writing about those times ended up contradicting themselves. For this reason, as mentioned, the author begins with the 1st Olympiad and continues, as he himself says, down to the time of Hadrian.

I have read as far as the 177th Olympiad <note>72–69 BC.</note>, in which Hecatomnus of Miletus was the victor in the *stadium* <term>stadium: a sprint covering one length of the track (about 192 meters)</term> and the *diaulos* <term>diaulos: a double-lap race where runners ran to the end of the track and back</term>. He also won the race for men in armor three times. Other victors included Hypsicles the Sicyonian and Gaius of Rome in the long-distance race; Aristonymidas of Cos in the *pentathlon* <term>pentathlon: a "five-exercise" competition consisting of running, leaping, wrestling, boxing, and throwing the discus</term>; Isidore of Alexandria in wrestling; and Aptotus in the *periodos* <term>periodos: literally "the circuit"; a title for an athlete who won at all four major Greek games (Olympic, Pythian, Isthmian, and Nemean)</term>. Also victorious were Atyanas of Adramyttium (son of Hippocrates) in boxing, and Sphodrias the Sicyonian in the *pancratium* <term>pancratium: an intense combat sport combining wrestling and boxing</term>.

Among the youths, the winners were Sosigenes of Asia in the sprint; Apollonius of Cyparissus in the wrestling match; Sotericus of Elis in the boxing match; and Calas of Elis in the *pancratium*. Hecatomnus of Miletus also won the race in armor; he was crowned three times in a single day—for the *stadium*, the *diaulos*, and the race in armor. In the equestrian events, the winners were Aristolochus of Elis in the four-horse chariot race; Hagemon of Elis with his racehorse; Hellanicus of Elis with his pair of horses and his four-horse chariot; Cletias of Elis with his pair; and Callippus of Pelion with his racehorse.

During this time, the Roman general Lucullus <note>Lucius Licinius Lucullus (c. 110–57 BC) was a Roman general famous for his victories in the East and for his legendary, extravagant banquets.</note> was besieging the city of Amisus <note>modern-day Samsun, on the coast of the Black Sea</note>, but having...

<meta>This page continues the summary of Phlegon of Tralles' historical chronicles, moving from the 177th Olympiad into Roman military campaigns in the East and a critical review of the author’s writing style.</meta>

<page-num>189</page-num>

<header>PHLEGON OF TRALLES</header>

[Lucullus] left Murena <gloss>1</gloss> with two legions to carry on the siege, while he himself set out with three others against the territory of the Cabiri, where he went into winter quarters. He also ordered Hadrian <note>Marcus Aurelius Hadrianus, a military officer serving under Lucullus, not to be confused with the later emperor.</note> to make war against Mithridates, who was defeated. An earthquake in Rome did much damage, and many other events happened during this <term>Olympiad</term> <note>A four-year period used by the Greeks to track time</note>. In its third year, the population according to the census was 910,000. Sinatruces, king of the Parthians, was succeeded by Phraates <gloss>2</gloss> *Theos* <note>original: Theos; meaning "the Divine"</note>, and Phaedrus the Epicurean <gloss>3</gloss> was succeeded by Patron. Virgil <note>original: Virgilius Maro</note> was born on the 15th of October in this year. In the fourth year of this <term>Olympiad</term>, Tigranes and Mithridates—having collected an army of 40,000 infantry and 30,000 cavalry who were drawn up in the Roman order of battle—engaged Lucullus and were defeated; Tigranes lost 5,000 killed and a large number of prisoners, besides a disorganized mob of followers. Catulus <gloss>4</gloss> dedicated the Capitol at Rome. Metellus <gloss>5</gloss> set out against Crete with three legions and occupied the island; having defeated Laosthenes and shut up the inhabitants within their walls, he was rewarded with the title of <term>imperator</term> <note>A title of honor given to a victorious general by his army</note>. The pirate Athenodorus enslaved the people of Delos and insulted the statues of the so-called gods; but Gaius Triarius, having repaired the damaged parts of the city, fortified the island.

We have read five books as far as this <term>Olympiad</term>. The author's style, though not too base or ordinary, does not always preserve a classical <note>original: "Attic"</note> character. But his tedious—if hardworking—diligence in calculating the <term>Olympiads</term>, his lists of names of the victors and their achievements, and his accounts of the oracles, not only weary the reader (since they do not allow a glimpse of anything else to appear) but also make the language disagreeable and rob it of all charm. He also attaches undue importance to oracles of all kinds.

---

1 Lucius Licinius Murena, <term>consul</term> <note>One of the two annual chief magistrates of the Roman Republic</note> in 63 B.C. He was accused of bribery, defended by Cicero in a speech still in existence, and acquitted.
2 Phraates III.
3 Head of the Epicurean school at Athens, who died around 70 B.C. Cicero draws freely from his work *On the Gods* (a fragment of which was discovered at Herculaneum) in his own book, *On the Nature of the Gods*.
4 Quintus Lutatius Catulus. The Capitol had been destroyed during the civil wars of Sulla's time.
5 Quintus Caecilius Metellus, <term>consul</term> in 69 B.C. and conqueror of Crete after a three-year campaign. He received the title of *Creticus* for his services.

<page-num>190</page-num>

<header>ZOSIMUS</header>

### XCVIII

I read the *History* of Count Zosimus, a former legal official of the treasury <note>original: "ex-advocate of the fisc"</note>, written in six books. Since he was a devout pagan <note>original: "impious heathen"</note>, he frequently attacks <note>original: "yelps at"</note> those of the Christian faith. His writing style is concise, clear, and elegant, and it is not without charm. He begins his history nearly from the time of Augustus and briefly reviews the emperors down to Diocletian, mentioning only their proclamations and the order of their succession. Starting from Diocletian, he treats his successors at much greater length across five books. The first book covers the emperors from Augustus to Diocletian, and the sixth book ends at the time when Alaric—who was besieging Rome for the second time while the citizens were in desperate straits—lifted the siege and proclaimed Attalus as emperor. Shortly afterward, he removed Attalus from power due to his incompetence and sent messengers to Honorius, who was then in Ravenna, with peace proposals. However, Sarus—a Goth himself and an enemy of Alaric—joined Honorius with about 300 men. By promising to do everything possible to help him against Alaric, Sarus succeeded in causing the negotiations to fail. This is where the sixth book ends.

One might say that Zosimus did not write the history himself, but rather copied the work of Eunapius <note>A 4th-century Greek rhetorician and historian whose work is now mostly lost</note>. Zosimus’s version differs only in being shorter and less insulting toward Stilicho <note>A high-ranking Roman general of Vandal origin who served under Honorius</note>. In other respects, his account is much the same, especially in his attacks upon the Christian emperors. I believe both of these authors published revised editions. Although I have not seen the first edition, I can infer from the title of the "New Edition" which I read that, like Eunapius, Zosimus published a second version. As I have mentioned, Zosimus is clearer and more concise than Eunapius, and he rarely uses elaborate figures of speech.

---

¹ His history, probably written between 450–501 AD, actually begins with the death of Commodus (192 AD) and ends in 410 AD, just before Alaric’s final sack of Rome. He is an extremely biased pagan, fond of old mythological legends, oracles, omens, and everything supernatural; he is also often confused about geography and pays little attention to exact dates. However, the work is valuable because it was mainly compiled from reliable eyewitness accounts <note>see Codex LXXX (80)</note>.
² Codex LXXVII (77).

<page-num>191</page-num>

<header>HERODIAN</header>

### XCIX

I read the eight books of the *History* written by Herodian <note>original: "Herodianus." He flourished around A.D. 238; his history covers the years 180 to 238 AD, from the death of Marcus Aurelius to the rise of Gordian III</note>. Starting from the death of Marcus Aurelius, he describes how the emperor's son and successor, Commodus, proved to be completely degenerate and was controlled entirely by flatterers. Ultimately, he was killed by his concubine Marcia as part of a conspiracy involving the officials Laetus and Eclectus. 

He was succeeded by Pertinax, an elderly man of excellent character, but the Praetorian Guard <note>the elite unit of the Roman Army that served as personal bodyguards to the emperors</note>, who hated his virtue, murdered him in the palace. Didius Julianus, who secured the throne by bribing the Praetorians, was soon afterward executed by them. 

Pescennius Niger <note>original: "Niger"</note>, who seems to have been a worthy man, was proclaimed emperor while Julian was still alive. However, Septimius Severus <note>original: "Severus"</note>—who was sharp-witted, clever, and courageous in the face of danger—defeated and killed his rival. After taking the throne, he eliminated all who resisted him through either direct violence or cunning traps. He behaved with extreme arrogance toward his subjects. He eventually died of an illness while leading a military campaign against the Britons <note>Severus died in A.D. 211 at Eboracum, modern-day York, England</note>. 

Antoninus <note>more commonly known by his nickname, Caracalla</note>, the older of his two sons, made a treaty with the Britons and returned to Italy. He reluctantly accepted his brother Geta as a co-emperor, but soon afterward he murdered him while Geta was in their mother's arms. Striving to exceed everyone else in wickedness and cruelty, Antoninus was eventually killed in Syria. He fell victim to a conspiracy organized by Macrinus, who acted out of fear for his own life after being threatened with death by the emperor. 

Macrinus—an elderly man who was slow to act and lacked self-discipline, though otherwise a decent person—became emperor after the death of Antoninus. Maesa, the sister of Empress Julia, had two daughters, Soaemis and Mamaea. Soaemis had a son named Bassianus <note>later known as the emperor Heliogabalus or Elagabalus</note>, and Mamaea had a son named Alexinus; both were claimed to be the secret sons of Antoninus. Using a minor excuse, the army proclaimed Bassianus as emperor in their camp and gave him the name Antoninus. After being defeated in battle, Macrinus fled from the borders of Phoenicia and Syria to Chalcedon, intending to make his...

<page-num>192</page-num>

<header>HERODIAN</header>

...way from there to Rome; but he was intercepted by the agents <note>original: "emissaries"</note> of Antoninus <note>referring here to the emperor Elagabalus, who took the name Marcus Aurelius Antoninus</note>, who cut off his head and carried it back with them.

As long as Antoninus followed the advice of his mother, he ruled with moderation. He adopted Alexinus—whose name he changed to Alexander <note>original: "Alexander 1"; he is known historically as Alexander Severus</note>—as his son and appointed him Caesar <note>a title for the junior emperor or designated successor</note>. However, after he fell under the influence of sycophants <note>original: "flatterers"</note>, there was no limit to the vice and lack of self-control <note>original: "intemperance"</note> he displayed. His attempt to plot against Alexander was blocked by the soldiers; when he decided to punish them for their resistance, they killed him instead. Alexander, the son of Mamaea, reigned for fourteen years to the best of his ability with goodness, mercy <note>original: "clemency"</note>, and without bloodshed. But it is said that due to the greed and stinginess <note>original: "avarice and meanness"</note> of his mother, they were both put to death, and Maximin was proclaimed emperor.

Maximin, a brutal and oppressive tyrant, was a man of massive physical size <note>historical accounts claim he was over eight feet tall</note> and extreme cruelty; he reigned for nearly three years. The soldiers in Africa revolted and killed the governor—a man who was just as cruel as the one who appointed him—and elected the proconsul Gordian (a man eighty years old) as emperor against his will. Rome joyfully accepted his election, stripped Maximin of all his honors, and at the same time declared the proconsul’s son, Gordian <note>Gordian II</note>, joint-emperor with his father. While Maximin was preparing for war, the elder Gordian, who had occupied Carthage with his son, saw that his position was hopeless and hanged himself. His son was defeated by Maximin's forces and died on the field of battle. 

The Romans were deeply saddened by their deaths. Because they both hated and feared Maximin, they proclaimed Balbinus and Maximus <note>also known as Pupienus</note> as emperors at Rome. The soldiers caused a disturbance and demanded that Gordian <note>Gordian III</note>—the grandson of the elder Gordian and his daughter’s son, who was still just a boy—should be made a partner in the empire. While Maximus was advancing to fight Maximin, the latter was murdered by his own soldiers. Maximin's head was taken to Maximus and then sent on to Rome. Soon afterward, the soldiers revolted again; they dragged Maximus and Balbinus from the palace and, after subjecting them to every kind of insult, put them to death. They then gave the throne to Gordian alone, who was then about thirteen years old. Here the eighth book ends.

The author’s style is clear, vibrant <note>original: "brilliant"</note>, and pleasant; his...

<meta>This page concludes the analysis of the historian Herodian’s writing style and moves into shorter reviews of the Emperor Hadrian, the orator Victorinus, and the theological works of Gelasius of Caesarea and Diodorus of Tarsus.</meta>

<page-num>193</page-num>

<header>GELASIUS OF CYZICUS</header>

His style <note>original: "diction"</note> avoids extremes. He is neither overly devoted to Atticism <term>Atticism: a style of writing that mimics the refined, classical Greek used in ancient Athens</term>—which can destroy the natural appeal of everyday speech—nor is he so careless that his writing becomes crude <note>original: "degenerate into meanness"</note> or ignores the rules of literary art. He does not take pride in unnecessary details, nor does he leave out anything essential. In short, he is equaled by few in the qualities that make a great historian.

### 100 <note>original: "C"</note>

I read several of the *Declamations* <term>Declamations: formal rhetorical speeches or practice orations</term> of the Emperor Hadrian <note>Reigned 117–138 AD; a patron of the arts known for his "Philhellenism" or love of Greek culture</note>. These are distinguished by a balanced style and are quite pleasant to read.

### 101 <note>original: "CI"</note>

I read the political and imperial speeches of Victorinus, the son of Lampadius of Antioch. These were written in honor of the Emperor Zeno, during whose reign Victorinus lived to a very old age. His style is characterized by clarity, a lack of repetitive filler <note>original: "redundancy"</note>, and the use of standard, everyday language.

### 102 <note>original: "CII"</note>

I read the treatise by Gelasius <note>Gelasius of Caesarea, a 4th-century bishop and nephew of the historian Eusebius</note>, the Bishop of Caesarea in Palestine, titled *Against the Anomoeans* <term>Anomoeans: a radical sect of Arianism that argued Jesus Christ was "unlike" God the Father in nature</term>, which is contained in one volume. His style is vigorous and direct. He frequently uses sophisticated Athenian vocabulary, his arguments are carefully constructed, and he is very skilled at logical reasoning. In every respect, he is a talented writer, except for one point: he uses the rules and technical terms of logic in a childish and exhausting way. It is as if he had only recently glanced at logic textbooks and then used the terminology incorrectly. While he does include an apology for using this poorly timed technical jargon, it would have been better if he had simply avoided using it at all.

Additionally, the organization of the work is somewhat disorganized. This same small volume also contained various arguments by Diodorus of Tarsus regarding the Holy Spirit, in which Diodorus reveals that he was already affected by the flaws of the Nestorian heresy <term>Nestorianism: the controversial belief that there were two separate persons in Christ, one human and one divine, rather than one person with two natures</term>.

---

<note>^1 Emperor 117-138 AD. He was a great patron of literature and wrote several works himself, in both prose and verse. His address to the soul is famous (see *Life*, by F. Gregorovius, English translation by M. E. Robinson, 1898).</note>
<note>^2 See Entry 88 <note>original: "Cod. LXXXVIII"</note>.</note>

<meta>This page continues the review of historical and philosophical authors, transitioning from the works of Gelasius of Caesarea to the significant Jewish-Hellenistic philosopher Philo Judaeus.</meta>

<page-num>194</page-num>

<header>PHILO JUDAEUS</header>

### 103 <note>original: "CIII"</note>

I read the *Allegories of the Sacred Laws* and *On the Civil Life* by Philo Judaeus <note>c. 20 B.C.—A.D. 40. Philo was the most important Jewish Hellenist, often called the "Jewish Plato." Born into a priestly family in Alexandria, he spent his life reconciling Jewish scripture with Greek philosophy.</note>.

### 104 <note>original: "CIV"</note>

I also read his description of the lives of those among the Jews who pursued either a contemplative or an active philosophy: the Essenes <term>Essenes: a Jewish sect known for communal living and strict observance of religious law</term> and the Therapeutae. The latter not only established monasteries and sanctuaries <note>original Greek: "semneia"</note>, but they also established the rules of monastic life that are followed by monks today.

### 105 <note>original: "CV"</note>

Additionally, I read his two short works, the *Censure of Gaius* <note>original: "Embassy to Gaius"</note> and the *Censure of Flaccus*. In these books, more than in his other writings, he displays a vigorous style and beautiful language. However, he frequently errs by shifting his interpretations and by describing certain matters in a way that conflicts with traditional Jewish philosophy. He lived during the reign of the Emperor Gaius <note>Roman Emperor A.D. 37–41, more commonly known as Caligula</note>, to whom he says he led a diplomatic mission <note>original: "deputation"</note> on behalf of his people while Agrippa was king of Judea. Philo was the author of many other treatises.

***

¹ Philo (c. 20 B.C.—A.D. 40) was the most important Jewish Hellenist and was born in Alexandria to a priestly family. Few details of his life are known, but in the year 40, he led a delegation of his fellow Jews to the Emperor Caligula in Rome to protest the persecution of Jews in Alexandria. He wrote many philosophical, historical, and apologetic works, including *On the Indestructibility of the World*, *That Every Good Man is Free*, and his commentaries on the Pentateuch <term>Pentateuch: the first five books of the Hebrew Bible</term>. His philosophy is a form of Neo-Platonism that attempts to reconcile Plato’s teachings with the Bible. He proposed two worlds: an intelligible world and a physical <note>original: "sensible"</note> world. The latter was formed by God based on a model of eternal ideas, personified as the **Logos** (Reason or the Word) <note>original: "Logos"</note>, which emanates from God. The **Logos** acts as an intermediary between God and the world, existing with God as His wisdom.

² The Essenes were divided into the "active" <note>original: "practici"</note>, who lived in communities, and the "contemplative" <note>original: "theoretici"</note>, who lived in solitude. In Egypt and Greece, these contemplative groups were called **Therapeutae**.

³ Gaius was the Roman emperor from A.D. 37–41, better known by his nickname, Caligula.

⁴ Avillius Flaccus was the governor of Egypt and a known persecutor of the Jewish community.

<vocab>Philo Judaeus, Essenes, Therapeutae, Gaius, Flaccus, Caligula, Jewish Hellenist, Neo-Platonism, Logos, Alexandria, Pentateuch, Agrippa</vocab>

<summary>This page reviews the works of Philo Judaeus, highlighting his philosophical treatises on Jewish law, his descriptions of the Essenes and Therapeutae, and his historical accounts of the persecutions under Emperor Caligula and Governor Flaccus.</summary>
<keywords>Philo Judaeus, Alexandria, monasticism, Essenes, Therapeutae, Caligula, Logos, Neo-Platonism, Jewish philosophy, religious history</keywords>

--- Page 201 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of Philo Judaeus, specifically his writing style, his rumored relationship with early Christianity, and a famous proverb about his philosophy. It then transitions to a review of Theognostus of Alexandria.</meta>

<page-num>195</page-num>

<header>THEOGNOSTUS OF ALEXANDRIA</header>

Philo wrote on various subjects, including ethical discussions and commentaries on the Old Testament, which mostly consist of forced allegorical <term>allegorical: a method of interpretation that finds hidden spiritual or moral meanings behind the literal words of a text</term> explanations. I believe that it was from him that all allegorical interpretation of Scripture in the Church originated. It is said that he was converted to Christianity but later abandoned it in a fit of anger and resentment. 

Before this, during the reign of the Emperor Claudius, he visited Rome where he met St. Peter—the chief of the apostles—and became close with him. This explains why he praised the disciples of St. Mark the Evangelist (who was himself a disciple of St. Peter). Philo says these disciples led a contemplative life among the Jews. He refers to their dwellings as monasteries and declares that they always led an ascetic <term>ascetic: a lifestyle of rigorous self-denial and spiritual discipline</term> life, practicing fasting, prayer, and poverty.

Philo came from a priestly family in Alexandria. The Greeks admired his eloquence so much that they had a common proverb: "Either Plato writes like Philo, or Philo writes like Plato" <note>original: "Either Plato philonizes or Philo platonizes." This suggests their styles and philosophies were so similar they were nearly indistinguishable.</note>.

### 106 <note>original: "CVI"</note>

I read the work by Theognostus of Alexandria <gloss>He flourished around the middle of the third century A.D.</gloss> titled *The Outlines of the Blessed Theognostus of Alexandria, Interpreter of the Scriptures*, which is divided into seven books. 

In the first book, he discusses the Father and attempts to show that He is the creator of the universe, opposing those who believe that matter has existed as long as God has <note>original: "coeternal"</note>. In the second book, he uses arguments to prove that it is necessary for the Father to have a Son. However, when he speaks of the "Son," he describes Him as a created being <note>This was a controversial point; later "Orthodox" Christian theology insisted the Son was "begotten, not made." Describing the Son as a creation was a hallmark of what became known as the Arian heresy.</note> who has charge over rational beings. 

Like Origen <note>a highly influential but controversial early Christian scholar (c. 185–254 A.D.)</note>, he says other similar things about the Son. He was either led astray by the same error <note>original: "impiety"</note>, or perhaps he was eager to defend Origen’s views by putting forward these arguments as a rhetorical exercise rather than as an expression of his true opinion. Or finally, he may have allowed himself to stray slightly from the truth because his listeners were in a "feeble condition"—perhaps being entirely ignorant of the mysteries of the Christian faith and unable to grasp the true doctrine. He might have thought that providing any knowledge of the Son would be more profitable to the listener than complete ignorance of Him. In oral discussion, it would not appear absurd or...

<gloss>1. Flourished about the middle of the third century A.D.</gloss>

<meta>This page continues the review of Theognostus of Alexandria's work, *The Outlines*, before transitioning to a new entry (107) regarding Basil of Cilicia and his polemical writings against John Scythopolita.</meta>

<page-num>196</page-num>

<header>BASIL OF CILICIA</header>

It might be considered blameworthy to use incorrect language, for such discussions are generally carried on according to the judgment, opinion, and energy of the person debating. However, in written discourse—which is intended to serve as a law for everyone—if anyone puts forward such a defense for blasphemy to excuse himself, his justification is a weak one. As in the second book, so in the third, while treating the subject of the Holy Spirit, the author introduces arguments by which he tries to prove the existence of the Holy Spirit; but in other respects, he talks as much nonsense as Origen in his *First Principles* <term>original: "Principles"; referring to "De Principiis," a foundational but controversial work by the early theologian Origen</term>. In the fourth book, he speaks similar nonsense about angels and demons, attributing "refined" or subtle bodies to them <term>refined bodies: the belief that spiritual beings possess a thin, ethereal physical substance rather than being purely spirit</term>. In the fifth and sixth books, he relates how the Saviour became incarnate and attempts, in his usual manner, to show that the incarnation of the Son was possible. Here, also, he speaks very foolishly, especially when he dares to say that we only imagine the Son to be confined now to this place, and now to that, but that in His power <note>original: "energy"</note> alone He is not restricted. In the seventh book, entitled *On God's Creation*, he discusses other matters with a greater spirit of devotion—especially at the end of the work concerning the Son.

His writing style is vigorous and free from unnecessary words. He uses beautiful language, similar to the classical Attic style <term>Attic: the dialect of ancient Athens, considered the gold standard for refined Greek prose</term>, in such a way that he does not depart from the standard style of composition. He does not sacrifice dignity for the sake of clarity and accuracy. He lived during the time of...

### 107 <note>original: "CVII"</note>

I read the work of the priest <term>presbyter: an elder or priest in the early church</term> Basil of Cilicia,¹ written against John Scythopolita, whom he calls a "pettifogger" <term>pettifogger: a legalistic trickster or someone who uses underhanded methods to win an argument</term> and several other names, subjecting him to various abuses. For instance, he claims that John was suspected of being a Manichaean <term>Manichaean: a follower of a dualistic religion that the early Church viewed as a major heresy</term>; that he shortened the sacred forty days of Lent to only three weeks, and during those weeks did not even abstain from eating poultry; that he took part in pagan <note>original: "heathen"</note> rites; that he was prone to gluttony; and that he never received Communion while the sacred liturgy was being performed, but only after the reading of the Gospel. He allegedly took part in the holy mysteries with the altar boys and then immediately hurried to the dining table. Such insulting and brutal remarks are scattered throughout the work. The work is written like a play, in the form of a dialogue, and is dedicated to a certain Leontius, who had requested the work.

<note>1 See Code 42 <note>original: "XLII"</note>.</note>

<summary>This page concludes Photius's critique of Theognostus of Alexandria, specifically his controversial Origenist views on the Holy Spirit and angels, before starting a review of Basil of Cilicia's aggressive polemic against John Scythopolita.</summary>
<keywords>Theognostus, Origen, Basil of Cilicia, John Scythopolita, Manichaeism, Holy Spirit, Incarnation, Church polemics, Attic Greek style</keywords>

--- Page 203 ---
<meta>This page continues the summary of Basil of Cilicia's polemical work against John of Scythopolis, detailing the contents and characters of his sixteen-book dialogue.</meta>

<page-num>197</page-num>

<header>BASIL OF CILICIA</header>

The author was compelled to write it. The characters are Lampadius, who defends Basil, and Marinus, who defends John. Marinus, during the course of the discussion, is portrayed as condemning his own client <term>original: "client"; here meaning the person whose theological views he is defending</term> and switching to Lampadius’s side, at which point a man named Tarasius is introduced as a new speaker <term>original: "interlocutor"</term>. The participants are shown asking and answering questions intended to bolster their own positions while criticizing and confusing the opposing advocate. The entire work is divided into sixteen books: the first thirteen are written as a dialogue in which the author has spent great energy and effort attacking only John's first book, while the remaining three books are directed against the statements in John's second and third books.

In the first book, after the preface is finished, he takes a stand against two main points: first, that “The Word suffered in the flesh,”¹ and second, that “To say Christ is the same as saying God.” In the second book, he attempts to show that he has been unfairly blamed and that John has misunderstood the words, “There shall come forth a rod out of the stem of Jesse.”² <note>stem of Jesse: a traditional messianic reference to the lineage of King David (Isaiah 11:1)</note> In the third book, he asks how the words, “Now the Son of Man was glorified and God was glorified in him,”³ and the rest of that passage are to be understood. 

In the fourth book, he inquires how the body is said to be unique to God, discusses His unity, and examines the words, “God, your God has anointed you,”⁴ and “I sanctify myself.”⁵ In this book Marinus, abandoning the role of opponent, defects to Lampadius's side. In the fifth book, where Tarasius is introduced to take over Marinus's role, the author makes even more bitter accusations against John, which take up nearly the entire book. In the sixth book, he attacks more severely the union of Christ our Savior. He also discusses the words “God, your God, has anointed you,” and the attitude of the Church toward the expression “He was made flesh.”⁶ In the eighth book, he falls headlong into numerous absurdities while discussing “The Word was made flesh” and also “No man has ascended up to heaven, but he that came down from heaven, even the Son of Man who is in heaven.”⁷ In the ninth book, he speaks of the phrase “That Rock was Christ.”⁸

<meta>This page continues the summary of Basil of Cilicia's sixteen-book work against John of Scythopolis. It concludes the description of the individual books (9 through 16) and provides a biographical and stylistic critique of the author, Basil.</meta>

<page-num>198</page-num>

<header>BASIL OF CILICIA</header>

"Your life shall hang in doubt before you,"<sup>1</sup> and "This gate shall be shut,"<sup>2</sup> to all of which he gives a sacrilegious <note>original: "impious"</note> interpretation. The tenth book deals with the passage, "To you the message of this salvation has been sent,"<sup>3</sup> and "He who did not spare his own Son,"<sup>4</sup> and "Concerning the Word of life," which your hands have touched, and "God so loved the world that he gave his only-begotten Son,"<sup>5</sup> and so on. In the eleventh book, he discusses, "This is our God; no other shall be considered in comparison to him,"<sup>6</sup> and "Afterward he appeared on earth and lived among men,"<sup>6</sup> and "Arise, O God, and judge the earth,"<sup>7</sup> and "He who has seen me has seen the Father."<sup>8</sup> He also argues against those who claimed that the apostles could not teach the full truth because their listeners were too weak to understand it. 

In the twelfth book, he teaches that one person of the Trinity suffered, and discusses the verse, "Had they known it, they would not have crucified the Lord of glory."<sup>9</sup> He offers a weak and misguided <note>original: "sinful"</note> argument for denying that there are two Christs—a defense driven more by his own wishes than by fact. In the thirteenth book, he investigates why there are not "two Sons," though his defense actually makes it seem as if there must be. Tarasius, appearing unable to counter the arguments against him, remains silent; Basil (or Lampadius) then ends his enthusiastic session of questions and answers. The last three books are more detailed and attack the writings of John in his second and third books.

This Basil, as he tells us himself, was a priest <term>original: "presbyter"</term> of the Church at Antioch when Flavian was bishop there and Arcadius was the Roman Emperor. His writing style is poor, and in the dialogues especially, it differs little from the common speech of the people. He is not precise in his composition, frequently making mistakes and grammatical errors <term>original: "solecisms"</term>; nevertheless, he does try to be clear. His arguments against the orthodox <term>the established, correct Christian beliefs</term> are sharp and show he is a skilled logician. In fact, he seems to have wasted his entire life on these useless attacks against the true faith. Although he is influenced by Nestorianism <term>a heresy suggesting Christ had two separate persons, one human and one divine</term>, he does not explicitly defend Nestorius, but he praises the Church fathers Theodore <note>likely Theodore of Mopsuestia, a mentor to Nestorius</note> and—

<meta>This page continues the critique of Basil of Cilicia’s work against John of Scythopolis. It concludes the review of Basil’s final books before moving on to new entries regarding Theodore the monk and Clement of Alexandria.</meta>

<page-num>199</page-num>

<header>THEODORE OF ALEXANDRIA</header>

Diodorus. He <note>This refers to Basil of Cilicia, the author discussed on the previous page.</note> does not openly use as much blasphemous language against the divine Cyril as Diodorus did. He declares that John, the target of his attack, relies for support on nothing but the twelve "chapters" <note>original: "capitula"; these are the Twelve Anathemas written by Cyril of Alexandria against Nestorius.</note> of Cyril, especially the twelfth, in which he introduces the idea of God suffering. With this, he concludes his useless efforts. As mentioned above, the work is dedicated to a certain Leontius, whom he pompously addresses as "most holy," "most beloved of God," and "Father."

### CVIII

Read the work of Theodore the monk *Against Themistius*, entitled: "A brief refutation of the old, reckless, and absurd attacks of Themistius against the Fathers, now developed by us in response to the questions and propositions he put forward against the truth; and a clear and accurate arrangement of the subject under discussion." This title is more like a whole book than a title. Both Theodore and Themistius are heretics, followers of Severus <note>Severus of Antioch, a major leader of the Miaphysite movement.</note>, and belong to the *Theopaschites* <note>original: *Theopaschitae*; a group who added the phrase "who was crucified for us" to the Trisagion hymn, implying that the divine nature suffered on the cross.</note>. Themistius (who also calls himself Calonymus) was the leader of the sect called the *Agnoetae* <note>original: "Ignorants"; they taught that Christ's human soul was limited in knowledge and ignorant of certain things, such as the timing of the Day of Judgment.</note>, about whom he wrote a work called, *An Apology for the Holy Theophobius by Calonymus or Themistius*. In that work, he also attacks Severus, despite being his devoted follower. Such is the nature of falsehood and those who love it. Theodore challenges Themistius to a debate and shows that his four arguments—intended to prove ignorance in Christ—lead to countless absurdities when examined one by one. Later, Themistius, trying to recover from his defeat, wrote a single book against Theodore. Theodore refuted those objections again in three volumes, setting forth his own true opinion on the subject. Both are skilled writers who aim for clarity, combined with an earnest and serious style.

### CIX

Read three volumes of the works of Clement <note>Titus Flavius Clemens, known as Clement of Alexandria (c. 150–213 AD). A Christian theologian and philosopher who sought to bridge Greek philosophy and Christian faith.</note>, a priest of Alexandria, entitled *Outlines*, *The Miscellanies*, and *The Tutor*.

***

<note>1. Theopaschites: Those who added the words “One of the Trinity was crucified” to the Trisagion hymn. According to them, Christ had only one nature—a divine one—and therefore the divine nature itself suffered during the Crucifixion.</note>
<note>2. Agnoetae: They taught that the human soul of Christ was like our own, even in its imperfect knowledge and ignorance.</note>
<note>3. Titus Flavius Clemens (c. 150–213). A Platonic philosopher who...</note>

--- Page 206 ---
<meta>This page continues the review of authors within Photius's "Bibliotheca," moving from the refutation of heretics to a critique of the works of Clement of Alexandria, specifically his "Outlines" and "The Tutor."</meta>

<page-num>200</page-num>
<header>CLEMENT OF ALEXANDRIA</header>

The *Outlines* <note>original: "Hypotyposes"</note> contain a brief explanation and interpretation of certain passages in the Old and New Testaments. Although in some cases what he says appears orthodox, in others he indulges in unholy and legendary fables. For he is of the opinion that matter is eternal and that "ideas" <note>Platonic forms or blueprints for creation</note> are introduced through certain fixed conditions; he also reduces the Son to the status of a created being. He speaks massive nonsense about the transmigration of souls <note>reincarnation</note> and the existence of a number of worlds before Adam. He endeavors to show that Eve came from Adam, not as Holy Scripture tells us, but in an unholy and shameful manner. He foolishly imagines that angels have sexual relations with women and father children, and that the Word <note>original: "Logos"; referring to Christ</note> was not truly incarnate but only appeared to be so <note>This belief is known as Docetism</note>. He is further convicted of making shocking statements about two "Words" of the Father, the lesser of which appeared to mortals—or rather, not even that one, for he writes:

> "The Son is called the Word, sharing the same name as the Word of the Father, but this is not the Word that became flesh, nor even the Word of the Father itself. Rather, it is a certain power of God, a sort of outflow <note>original: "efflux"</note> from the Word itself, which, having become mind, has spread through the hearts of men."

He attempts to support all of this using passages from Scripture. He speaks much other blasphemous nonsense, whether it was actually him or someone else writing under his name. These shocking blasphemies are contained in eight books, in which he frequently discusses the same points and quotes passages from Scripture randomly and confusedly, like someone possessed. The entire work includes notes on Genesis, Exodus, the Psalms, Saint Paul’s epistles, the Catholic epistles <note>The "General Epistles" of the New Testament (James, Peter, John, and Jude)</note>, and Ecclesiasticus. Clement was a pupil of Pantaenus, as he himself says. Let this suffice regarding the *Outlines*.

->CX<- <note>Codex 110</note>

The *Tutor* <note>original: "Paedagogus"</note> is an elaborate work in three books, containing rules for behavior and conduct. It was preceded by and—

---

—embraced Christianity, and succeeded Pantaenus as head of the catechetical school of Alexandria. Among his pupils were Origen and Alexander, the bishop of Jerusalem. His chief works are those mentioned here by Photius. To these may be added his *Hortatory Address to the Greeks* <note>original: "Protrepticus"</note>, which shows the superiority of Christianity over pagan and other religions. As a result of the persecution under Emperor Septimius Severus, he fled to Palestine, where he died.

<meta>This page continues the review of Clement of Alexandria's literary output, specifically contrasting his more orthodox works with the controversial "Outlines" discussed on the previous page.</meta>

<page-num>201</page-num>
<header>CLEMENT OF ALEXANDRIA</header>

This is combined with another work in which he refutes the impiety of the pagans <note>original: "heathen"</note>. These discourses have nothing in common with the *Outlines* <note>original: "Hypotyposes"</note>, since they are entirely free from idle and blasphemous opinions. The style is flowery <note>original: "florid"</note>, rising at times to a pleasant and moderate loftiness, while the display of learning is appropriate. In the final book, he discusses the subject of images.

### CXI

The *Miscellanies* <note>original: "Stromateis," meaning "tapestries" or "patchwork"</note>, written in eight books, contain an attack upon heresy and the pagans. The material is arranged randomly <note>original: "promiscuously"</note> and the chapters are not in order. He provides the reason for this himself at the end of the seventh book in the following words: "Since these points have been thoroughly discussed, and our ethical formula has been sketched out briefly and in fragments, as we promised, we have scattered teachings here and there that are designed to kindle the flame of true knowledge. This ensures that the discovery of the sacred mysteries may not be easy for anyone who is uninitiated <note>those not yet instructed in the deeper truths of the faith</note>," and so on.

According to Clement, this is the reason why the subject matter is so unsystematically arranged. In an old manuscript <note>original: "copy"</note>, I found the title of this work given not only as *Miscellanies*, but in full as follows: *Miscellany of Gnostic Notes in accordance with the True Philosophy, books 1–8*. The first seven books have the same title and are identical in all the copies. The title of the eighth book, however, varies, as does its content. In some copies, it is titled *Who is the Rich Man that is saved?* and begins, "Those who . . . laudatory speeches," etc.; in others, it is called *The Miscellanies, the eighth book*, just like the previous seven, and begins, "But not even the oldest of the philosophers," etc. In some parts, the work is doctrinally unsound, but it is not like the *Outlines*, and it even refutes some of the statements made in that earlier work.

Clement is said to have written several other works, which are mentioned by other writers: *On Easter*; *On Fasting*; *On Slander* <note>original: "Evil-speaking"</note>; and *On the Ecclesiastical Canons, and against those who follow the Erroneous Doctrine of the Jews*, which was dedicated to Alexander, the bishop of Jerusalem. He flourished during the reign of the Emperors Severus and his son Antoninus in Rome.

<sup>1</sup> *Stromateis* <note>original Greek: Στρωματεῖς</note>, meaning bed-coverlets or "patchwork quilts."

<meta>This page transitions from the review of Clement of Alexandria to Clement of Rome, two distinct early Christian figures often discussed together in historical catalogs of church fathers.</meta>

<page-num>202</page-num>
<header>CLEMENT OF ROME</header>

### CXII, CXIII

I have read two volumes containing the works of Clement, Bishop of Rome. $^1$ One is titled *The Apostolic Constitutions by Clement*, which contains the church laws <note>original: "synodical canons"</note> attributed to the gathered Apostles. The other, written in the form of a letter, is dedicated to James, the brother of the Lord; it contains what are called *The Acts of the Apostle Peter*, *His Conversations with Simon Magus*, and *The Recognition of Clement, his Father, and his two Brothers*. Consequently, in some manuscripts, it is titled *The Recognition of Clement of Rome*. As we have mentioned, a letter is attached as a preface addressed to James, the Lord’s brother, but it is not always the same letter nor from the same author. According to some copies, it was sent by the Apostle Peter; according to others, it was sent by Clement to James. In the first case, Peter would seem to have compiled an account of his own deeds and sent it to James at his request; in the second, Clement compiled it by Peter’s command and sent it to James after Peter had passed into eternal life. It may be inferred, then, that there were two editions of the *Acts of Peter*, and that when one was lost over time, only the version by Clement survived. In all the copies I have seen—which are quite many—despite these different letters and titles, I have invariably found the same text beginning with the words, “I, Clement,” etc. 

The work is full of countless absurdities and blasphemy against the Son of God, in keeping with the Arian heresy <note>A belief that Jesus Christ was a created being and not co-eternal or equal with God the Father</note>. The *Constitutions* appear to be open to criticism on three counts: clumsy fiction, which is easy to identify and remove; abusive charges against Deuteronomy <note>the fifth book of the Bible</note>, which can easily be answered; and its Arianism, which can be refuted by a strong defense. However, the book of the *Acts of Peter* is so superior to the *Constitutions* in its clarity, earnestness, purity, and passion, as well as its general linguistic quality and great learning, that no comparison between the two works is possible regarding their style.

It is this Clement of whom Saint Paul speaks in the Epistle...

---

$^1$ Clement lived during the first century AD <note>Latin: *Anno Domini*, "In the year of our Lord"</note>. According to tradition, he was one of the first successors of St. Peter as Bishop of Rome. Numerous works attributed to him, such as the *Homilies*, *Recognitions*, and *Apostolic Constitutions*, are considered fake <note>original: "spurious"</note>. The only writing bearing his name that is widely accepted as genuine is the *First Epistle to the Church of Corinth* (the second is considered fake). For a full discussion of this topic, see the 1890 edition by Bishop Lightfoot.
