<page-num>203</page-num>
<header>LUCIUS CHARINUS</header>

In the letter to the Philippians, it is written, “With Clement also, and my other fellow-laborers, whose names are written in the book of life.” <note>original: Philippians 4:3. While the name Clement was common, the author here identifies this person as the future Bishop of Rome.</note> He also wrote an important letter to the Corinthians, which was held in such high regard that it was read aloud in public assemblies. A second letter attributed to him is rejected as a forgery, as is the lengthy discussion written as a dialogue between Peter and Apion. <note>Apion was an Alexandrian scholar and commentator on Homer who lived in the mid-first century AD. He was famous for his hostility toward the Jews.</note> Some say that Clement succeeded Peter as bishop of Rome; others claim that he was the fourth bishop—with Linus and Anacletus serving in between—and that he died in the third year of the Emperor Trajan’s reign.

### CXIV

I have read a book titled *The Travels of the Apostles* <note>original: "Circuits"</note>, which includes the Acts of Peter, John, Andrew, Thomas, and Paul. The author is identified as one Lucius Charinus <note>Also known as Leucius Charinus; his exact dates are unknown, though he likely lived in the 4th or 5th century AD</note>, as the work itself indicates. The writing style is completely inconsistent and peculiar; while the vocabulary and sentence structures are occasionally clear, they are for the most part ordinary and unoriginal. There is no trace of the fluent and natural expression that characterizes the language of the Gospels and the Apostles, nor is there any of the natural grace that follows from it. 

The content is also very foolish and contradicts itself. The author claims that the God of the Jews—whom he calls evil and whose servant Simon Magus was—is one god, and that Christ—whom he calls good—is another. Confusing everything together, he identifies the same person as both the Father and the Son. He claims that Christ was never actually made human, but only appeared to be so <note>This is the heresy of Docetism, from the Greek word *dokein*, meaning "to seem."</note>. He writes that Christ appeared at different times in different forms to his disciples: sometimes as a young man, sometimes as an old man, and other times as a boy; sometimes taller, sometimes shorter, and once so tall that his head almost reached the heavens. He also invents much idle and absurd nonsense about the Cross, claiming that Christ was not the one crucified, but that someone else was killed in his place, and that therefore Christ could laugh at those who imagined they had crucified him. He declares...

<keywords>Clement of Rome, Lucius Charinus, Circuits of the Apostles, Apion, Gnosticism, Docetism, Church History, Apocrypha</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the critique of the apocryphal "Acts of the Apostles" attributed to Lucius Charinus, focusing on its heretical views on marriage and its bizarre stories.</meta>

<page-num>204</page-num>
<header>METRODORUS</header>

…that he claims legal marriages are illegal and that all fathering of children is evil and the work of the devil. He talks foolishly about a creator of demons. He tells monstrous tales of ridiculous and childish resurrections of dead men, oxen, and cattle. In the *Acts of Saint John*, he even seems to support the iconoclasts <note>original: "opponents of images," those who opposed the use of religious icons in worship</note> by attacking the use of images. In short, the book contains a massive amount of childish, unbelievable, poorly devised, lying, silly, self-contradictory, and godless statements. One would not be far wrong in calling this work the source and mother of all heresy.

### CXV

I have read an anonymous work titled *A Disputation against the Jews and those who hold the same Heretical Views, and those called Quartodecimans* <note>The Quartodecimans were a group who celebrated Easter on the 14th day of the Jewish month of Nisan—the same day as the Jewish Passover—regardless of which day of the week it fell on.</note>. These people do not celebrate the Holy Easter Feast in the first month as the Hebrews do. The author’s style is concise and avoids unnecessary words, though it is somewhat grand and boastful. 

The author asserts that our Lord Jesus Christ did not partake in the regular Passover feast <note>original: "Paschal feast"</note> on Holy Thursday, because that specific day had not been established yet. Instead, he claims Christ celebrated it on the following day. He even argues that Jesus did not strictly follow the law in what He ate, for He ate neither lamb nor unleavened bread and followed none of the traditional practices observed by those who keep Passover according to Jewish Law. The author claims that Jesus participated in a private, mystical feast, from which He gave bread and wine to His disciples.

Another treatise is also included by a certain Metrodorus. It consists of a list of twenty-eight cycles of nineteen years each, used for calculating the date of the holy Easter feast. This Metrodorus—whoever he was, for I have not been able to learn anything about him—begins his calculations with the reign of the Emperor Diocletian. He has collected the dates for Easter over a period of 533 years <note>This covers a full cycle of 532 years (28 multiplied by 19) plus one year to start the next cycle. Metrodorus likely lived in the 6th or 7th century.</note>, following the accepted and accurate methods of computation.

<summary>Photius concludes his scathing review of Lucius Charinus’s heretical "Acts" and examines an anonymous work on the timing of Easter and a chronological study by Metrodorus.</summary>
<keywords>Lucius Charinus, heresy, Iconoclasm, Quartodecimans, Passover, Easter computation, Metrodorus, Diocletian, church history</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the discussion of the "Quartodeciman" controversy—a dispute in the early Church over the correct date to celebrate Easter—and transitions into reviews of technical works on the calendar and a defense of the controversial theologian Origen.</meta>

<page-num>205</page-num>
<header>ORIGEN</header>

…of the fourteenth day. For neither the Church nor ancient tradition appears to have observed those days.

### CXVI

I read another anonymous work titled *A Third Volume on the Holy Easter Feast*, consisting of eight books. The style is simple and very clear, and it contains many sensible ideas. In the fourth book, the author frequently refutes Metrodorus, and while supporting his arguments with evidence from the Scriptures, he incorporates some useful remarks on the Creation into his discussion of Easter as a form of interpretation. 

The work is dedicated to a certain Theodore, whom the author calls his "beloved brother." This author wrote other treatises on the same subject, but this is the most comprehensive and useful, as he thoroughly discusses everything related to the topic. He provides a clear and detailed account of leap years and the intercalary month <note>original: "intercalary month," an extra month added to the calendar periodically to keep the lunar and solar years in sync</note>, the solar and lunar epacts <note>the difference in days between the solar year and the lunar year</note>, the nineteen-year cycle <note>also known as the Metonic cycle, used to determine the date of Easter</note> and the method of calculating it, the months, the new moon, the week and its days, and which years are considered cyclical or intercalary. 

He also discusses the twenty-eight-year solar cycle, the nineteen-year lunar cycle and its fourteenth day, the lunar and solar months, the timing of the new moon for both lunar and solar months, the distinction between the lunar month and the exact month, and the calculation of the "years of the world" <note>the number of years since the biblical creation</note>. He states that according to the other years of His arrival on Earth, our Lord and God Christ participated in the prescribed Easter feast <note>the Jewish Passover</note>, but not on the day that was commonly reported. This point deserves careful consideration, since John Chrysostom and the Church teach that He participated in the regular feast <note>the Jewish Passover</note> before the Last Supper <note>original: "mystic supper"</note>.

### CXVII

I read an anonymous work in five volumes defending Origen and his abominable writings. The style is neither clear nor refined, and it contains nothing worth mentioning. The author cites Dionysius of Alexandria <note>Bishop of Alexandria from 247–264, known as "the Great" and a student of Origen</note>, Demetrius <note>Bishop of Alexandria from 189–232; he was initially friendly with Origen but later fell out with him and condemned him for being ordained without permission</note>, Clement, and several others to support Origen and his doctrines.

<summary>This page reviews an anonymous, detailed technical guide on calculating the date of Easter and the calendar cycles, as well as a multi-volume defense of the controversial Church Father Origen.</summary>
<keywords>Easter, Metrodorus, Theodore, solar cycle, lunar cycle, calendar, John Chrysostom, Origen, Dionysius of Alexandria, Demetrius, Clement of Alexandria, Passover</keywords>

--- Page 212 ---
<meta>This page continues the review of a defense (apology) of the theologian Origen, likely the one written by Pamphilus and Eusebius mentioned at the end of the previous entry.</meta>

<page-num>206</page-num>
<header>ORIGEN</header>

...others, but chiefly relies upon Pamphilus the martyr and Eusebius, the bishop of Caesarea in Palestine. This defense <note>original: "apology"</note> is not for the most part a refutation of the charges against Origen; instead, it actually supports the accusations, since the author is not altogether free from Origen’s blasphemous opinions. For instance, he asserts that souls existed before bodies, supporting this nonsense with passages from the Scriptures and the Church Fathers, and he imagines the soul taking on other bodies. 

Regarding the Holy Trinity, however, he is orthodox. He asserts that Origen was not guilty of error in his opinions on the subject, but rather that he was opposing the Sabellian<sup>1</sup> heresy, which had spread extensively at that time. He explains that in Origen's endeavor to show that the Trinity of Persons was distinct and differed in many ways, he allowed himself to be carried away beyond what was right in the opposite direction. 

However, regarding Origen’s other dogmas—to which the author does not even venture to give a plausible <note>original: "specious"</note> assent and for which he does not think it possible to adapt a defense—he takes great trouble to prove that they were only intended as a rhetorical exercise, or that they were foisted into his writings by certain heretical <note>original: "heterodox"</note> persons. As proof of this, he quotes Origen himself protesting loudly; for Origen says that even while he was alive, he discovered that such reckless statements were being made against him. 

The counts on which he asserts that Origen was falsely accused are fifteen in number. He declares these to be mere slanders, proving it by quotations from Origen's own writings in his fourth book, and refuting them by the evidence of others on his behalf in the fifth. The counts are as follows: 

1. He is charged with teaching that prayer should not be offered to the Son.
2. That the Son is not absolutely good.
3. That the Son does not know the Father as He knows Himself.
4. That rational souls <note>original: "natures"</note> enter into the bodies of animals <note>original: "irrational beings"</note>.
5. That there are migrations from one body into another.
6. That the soul of the Savior was the same as the soul of Adam.
7. That there is neither eternal punishment nor resurrection of the body <note>original: "flesh"</note>.
8. That magic is not an evil.
9. That astrology <note>original: "astronomy"</note> is the cause of events.
10. That the Only Begotten <note>referring to Christ</note> has no share in the...

---

1 A sect named after Sabellius (second–third century AD). While they did not believe the Son was lower than the Father, they denied His distinct personality. They viewed the Trinity (Father, Son, and Holy Spirit) not as three real and eternal persons, but as three different ways the Divine Nature manifested itself over time.

While defending his views on the Trinity as an overreaction to Sabellianism, the authors struggle to reconcile his more controversial ideas about the soul and the resurrection.</summary>

<page-num>207</page-num>
<header>PAMPHILUS</header>

Kingdom; that the holy angels came into the world by falling down from heaven, rather than to serve others; that the Father is invisible to the Son; that the Cherubim are merely the "ideas" of the Son; and that the image of God—in relation to the one whose image it is—is false when considered simply as <note>original: "qua"</note> an image. He rejects these charges, as already stated, as slanders against Origen, and does his utmost to prove that Origen is an orthodox member of the Church. But, my dear sir, even if someone is shown to be not entirely godless, this is no reason why he should escape punishment for such obvious blasphemies.

### CXVIII

Read the *Apology for Origen* <gloss>1</gloss> by Pamphilus the martyr and Eusebius.<gloss>1</gloss> It consists of six books, five of which were written by Pamphilus while in prison in the company of Eusebius. The sixth is the work of Eusebius alone, written after the martyr was executed by the sword and returned to the God his soul desired. Many other distinguished people at that time also wrote in defense of Origen. It is said that during the persecutions in the reign of Severus, Origen wrote to his father Leonides, encouraging him toward martyrdom, and that Leonides ran the race nobly and received the crown. It is added that Origen himself prepared with great zeal to enter into the same struggle, but that his mother restrained his fervor despite his protests, as he himself testifies in a letter.

Pamphilus the martyr and many others who have written an accurate account of Origen—based on reports from those who knew him—assert that he departed from this life by a glorious martyrdom at Caesarea during the cruel persecution of the Christians by the emperor Decius.<gloss>2</gloss> Others say that he lived until the reigns of Gallus <gloss>3</gloss> and Volusianus, and that he died at Tyre in the sixty-ninth year of his age and was buried there. This is likely the more accurate account, unless the letters supposed to have been written by him after the Decian persecution are forgeries. They say that he studied and taught every branch of knowledge. He is also said to have been called "Adamantius" <note>meaning "man of steel" or "unbreakable"</note> because his arguments...

---

<gloss>1</gloss> Book I exists in a Latin version by Rufinus.
<gloss>2</gloss> Emperor from 249–251 AD. He was notorious as a relentless enemy and persecutor of the Christians.
<gloss>3</gloss> Emperor from 251–253 AD. His son Volusianus reigned alongside him in the empire.

<summary>This page concludes a list of alleged heresies by Origen and reviews the six-book defense written by Pamphilus and Eusebius, while also debating whether Origen died as a martyr or of old age.</summary>
<keywords>Pamphilus, Origen, Eusebius, Apology for Origen, Leonides, martyrdom, Emperor Decius, Adamantius, church history</keywords>

--- Page 214 ---
<meta>This page continues the biography of Origen and his defenders, picking up from the description of the persecutions and those who suffered for their faith.</meta>

...were linked together like unbreakable chains <note>original: "chains of adamant"</note>. He attended the lectures of Clement, the author of the *Miscellanies* <note>original: "Stromateis"</note>, and succeeded him as head of the school for religious instruction <term>catechetical school</term> at Alexandria. It is said that Clement was the pupil of Pantaenus and his successor as head of his school, and that Pantaenus heard teachers who had seen the apostles, and had even heard them himself.

It is said that the movement against Origen originated as follows. Demetrius, the bishop of Alexandria, had a high opinion of Origen and admitted him to his intimate friendship. But when Origen was about to leave for Athens without the permission of the bishop, he was ordained by Theotecnus, the bishop of Caesarea in Palestine, contrary to the law of the Church, with the approval of Alexander, the bishop of Jerusalem. This incident changed the love of Demetrius to hate and his praise to blame. A council <term>synod</term> of bishops and some elders <term>presbyters</term> was summoned to condemn Origen. According to Pamphilus, it was decided that he must not remain in Alexandria or teach there, but that he should be allowed to retain his priesthood. But Demetrius and some Egyptian bishops, with the agreement of those who had formerly supported him, also deprived him of his sacred office. After he had been banished from Alexandria, Theotecnus, the bishop of Caesarea in Palestine, welcomed him, allowed him to live at Caesarea, and gave him permission to preach. Such are the reasons which Pamphilus gives for the attack upon Origen.

The *Apology for Origen* was composed, as we have said, by Pamphilus while he was imprisoned together with Eusebius, and it was addressed to those who were condemned to the mines <note>During Roman persecutions, Christians were often sentenced to forced labor in mines under brutal conditions.</note> for the sake of Christ; the chief of these was Patermythius, who shortly after the death of Pamphilus ended his life at the stake with others. Pierius was the teacher of Pamphilus and the head of the school for religious instruction at Alexandria. It is said that he suffered martyrdom together with his brother Isidore, and that a church and houses of prayer were built in his honor at Alexandria. The holy Pamphilus was a priest <term>presbyter</term>, and is said to have copied most of Origen's commentaries on Scripture with his own hand.

Read a work by Pierius the priest <term>presbyter</term>, who is said to have

# PIERIUS

He is said to have suffered martyrdom ¹ with his brother Isidore, and to have been the teacher of the martyr Pamphilus in theology and head of the school for religious instruction <term>catechetical school</term> at Alexandria. The volume contains twelve books. The style is clear and brilliant, and, so to speak, spontaneous; there is nothing elaborate about it, but it flows along evenly, smoothly, and gently, as if it were unpremeditated. The work is distinguished by a wealth of reasoning <note>original: "argumentation"</note>. It contains much that is different from <note>original: "foreign to"</note> the current practices <note>original: "institutions"</note> of the Church, but is possibly in accordance with older regulations. 

In regard to the Father and the Son, his statements are orthodox, except that he asserts there are two substances and two natures. He uses these terms—as is clear from the context of the passage—in the sense of individual personhood <term>hypostasis</term>, rather than in the sense used by the followers of Arius <note>The Arians used similar language to argue that the Son was a separate, created being, rather than of the same essence as the Father.</note>. However, in regard to the Holy Spirit <note>original: "Holy Ghost"</note>, his views are dangerous and disrespectful <note>original: "impious"</note>; for he declares that the Spirit's glory was less than that of the Father and the Son. 

There is a passage in the treatise titled *On St. Luke’s Gospel*, from which it can be shown that the honor or dishonor shown to an image is actually honor or dishonor shown to the original subject <term>prototype</term> <note>This principle later became a central argument in the defense of using icons in worship.</note>. It is hinted, in agreement with Origen’s absurd idea, that souls exist before they are born into bodies <term>pre-existence</term>. In his work on Easter and the sermon <note>original: "homily"</note> upon the prophet Hosea, the author discusses the Cherubim made by Moses and Jacob’s pillar. He admits that they were physically made, but speaks foolishly <note>original: "talks nonsense"</note> about them being provided by divine guidance <note>original: "providentially granted"</note>, as if they were insignificant or meant to represent something else entirely. He claims they did not have any real shape, but absurdly asserts that they only had a sort of wings.

This Pierius was a priest <term>presbyter</term> of the Church of Alexandria when Theonas was bishop, and Carus and Diocletian were emperors of Rome. It is said that he was so distinguished for his love of work and learning, and that his lectures to the people were so pleasant and instructive, that he was called “the young Origen,” since Origen enjoyed a very high reputation at that time. They say that he was well-versed in the art of debate <term>dialectic</term> and public speaking <term>rhetoric</term>, and that he voluntarily practiced self-control <note>original: "temperance"</note> and lived in poverty. According to some, he suffered martyrdom; according to others, he spent the rest of his life at Rome after the time of the persecution.

---

¹ During the persecution of the Emperor Diocletian (303 AD).

<language>English</language>
<page-type>text</page-type>
<page-num>210</page-num>
<header>IRENAEUS</header>

### CXX

I have read the work of Irenaeus, Bishop of Lyons,<sup>1</sup> entitled *The Refutation and Subversion of Knowledge Falsely So Called*—also known as *Against Heresies*—in five books. 

The first book, which discusses Valentinus and his unholy heresy, traces the history of these ideas as far back as Simon Magus<sup>2</sup> and continues down to Tatian.<sup>3</sup> Tatian was initially a disciple of Justin Martyr<sup>4</sup> but later plunged into heresy. This book also deals with those specifically called Gnostics and the Cainites,<sup>5</sup> exposing their detestable doctrines. Such is the content of the first book. 

In the second book, the unholy dogmas of the heretics are disproved. The third book quotes all kinds of evidence from the Scriptures against them. The fourth book answers certain difficulties and objections raised by the heretics. The fifth book demonstrates that everything said and done by the Lord in the form of parables—drawn both from His saving doctrine and from the apostolic letters—is perfectly suited to refute the nonsense <note>original: "claptrap"</note> of the heretics.

Saint Irenaeus is said to have been the author of many other works of various kinds, including letters. In some of these, it should be noted that the precise truth of Church doctrines appears to be distorted by questionable <note>original: "spurious"</note> arguments.

It is said that he was a student of the holy martyr Polycarp,<sup>6</sup> Bishop of Smyrna, and served as a priest <term>presbyter</term> under Pothinus, whom he succeeded as Bishop.

---

<sup>1</sup> Lived approximately 120–140 to 202 AD; became Bishop of Lyons in 177. He was born in Asia Minor, near Smyrna, and moved to Rome around 155. He is said to have been martyred under Emperor Severus, though this is not certain. He was a passionate opponent of the Valentinian Gnostics and worked hard to prevent a split between the Eastern and Western Churches regarding the date of Easter. His major work survives only in a crude Latin <note>original: "barbarous"</note> version, though parts of the original Greek can be reconstructed from quotes in later writers.

<sup>2</sup> Simon the Sorcerer, who was active around 37 AD, gained great influence in Samaria through his magic (see Acts 8:9–24).

<sup>3</sup> An Assyrian student of Greek philosophy who converted to Christianity in Rome around 150 after reading the Bible. Initially a Christian defender <term>apologist</term>, he later adopted Gnostic views. He moved to Mesopotamia and is thought to have died at Edessa around 180. Besides an *Apology for Christianity*, he wrote the *Diatessaron* <note>original: Greek for "through four"</note>, a combined harmony of the four Gospels.

<sup>4</sup> Lived approximately 103–164 AD. Born in Palestine, he was a Greek philosopher who converted to Christianity. He was reportedly whipped and beheaded for refusing to offer sacrifices to pagan gods.

<sup>5</sup> A Gnostic sect of the second century, who were followers of Carpocrates of Alexandria.

<sup>6</sup> A martyr who died around 155 AD during the reign of Marcus Aurelius.

<header>HIPPOLYTUS OF Rome</header>

[Irenaeus] succeeded to the bishopric <term>the office of a bishop</term> of Lyons. At that time, Victor was the Pope of Rome. Irenaeus frequently urged <note>original: "exhorted"</note> Victor by letter not to excommunicate any members of the Church because of a disagreement over the date of Easter.

### CXXI

I have read the treatise <note>original: "tractate"</note> by Hippolytus,¹ the pupil of Irenaeus, entitled *Against the Thirty-two Heresies*. It begins with the Dositheans² and goes down to the heresies of Noëtus³ and his followers. Hippolytus says these were refuted by Irenaeus in his lectures, and that this current work is a summary <note>original: "synopsis"</note> of those talks. The style is clear, somewhat serious, and free from unnecessary words, although it shows no attempt to mimic the classic Attic Greek style <note>original: "atticism"</note>. 

Some of his statements are inaccurate—for instance, his claim that the Letter to the Hebrews was not written by the Apostle Paul. Hippolytus is said to have preached to the people in the style of Origen, with whom he was very close. He admired Origen’s writings so much that he urged him to write a commentary on the Bible. To help with this, Hippolytus paid for seven shorthand writers and seven expert copyists <note>original: "calligraphists"</note> at his own expense. Having provided this service, he constantly demanded that the work be finished; because of this, Origen calls him a "taskmaster" <note>original: "hustler"; Greek: ergodioktes</note> in one of his letters. Hippolytus is said to have written many other works as well.

### CXXII

I have read the *Medicine Chest* <note>original: "Panaria"</note> written by the most holy Bishop Epiphanius⁴ against eighty heresies. It consists of three volumes containing seven books. It begins with the era of "Barbarism" and goes down to the—

---

¹ Hippolytus flourished during the third century. He was a student of Irenaeus and a fierce opponent of the Gnostics. He was a priest <term>presbyter</term> in Rome and eventually became an "anti-pope" in opposition to Calixtus (Callistus), who followed a doctrine that denied the distinct persons of the Trinity <term>Monarchianism</term>. This treatise *Against Heresies* was lost for centuries until it was discovered in a monastery on Mount Athos in 1842.

² A Jewish sect named after Dositheus of Samaria (first century AD), who reportedly killed himself by fasting. He insisted on a very strict observance of the Sabbath.

³ Noëtus died around 200 AD and was born in Smyrna or Ephesus. According to his teaching, "Christ was the Father himself, and therefore the Father was born, suffered, and died."

⁴ Epiphanius (c. 316–403 AD) was born near Eleutheropolis in Palestine and served as the Bishop of Constantia (Salamis) in Cyprus. He was a strong opponent of Origen and John Chrysostom. The title *Panaria* is the Latin equivalent of the Greek word for *Bread-baskets* <note>original: Ἀρτοφόρια (Artophoria)</note>, though it is usually understood as a "medicine chest" containing remedies against the "poison" of heresy.

<header>JUSTIN MARTYR</header>

The Messalians.<note>original: See Codex 52 (LII) for more on this sect.</note> The author [Epiphanius] writes more extensively and effectively against heretics than any of those who came before him. He has included every useful argument used by his predecessors and added any others he could discover himself. However, his writing style is plain, lacking the refined elegance of classical Attic Greek <term>the prestigious dialect used by ancient Athenian writers</term>. He is at his weakest when debating wicked <note>original: "impious"</note> heresies; yet, at other times, he is excellent in his attacks, even though his choice of language and quality of composition do not improve at the same time.

### CXXIII

Read the same author's *The Anchored One* <note>original: "Ancoratus"</note>,² which is a type of summary <note>original: "synopsis"</note> of his larger work, the *Panarion* <note>The "Medicine Chest" of remedies against heresies</note>.

### CXXIV

Read the same author’s treatise *On Weights and Measures*.

### CXXV

Read Justin Martyr’s *Apology for the Christians*, which he wrote to address both Jews and non-Christians <note>original: "gentiles"</note>. Also, read his treatise *Against the First and Second Books of the Physics*, which argues against the Aristotelian concepts of form, matter, and "privation" <term>the absence or lack of a quality</term>. This is a collection of logical <note>original: "dialectical"</note>, forceful, and practical arguments. I also read his work *Against the Fifth Essence and Eternal Motion*, targeting the concepts Aristotle created through his clever reasoning, and finally, his *Short Explanations for Doubts Raised Against Christianity*.

Justin is thoroughly knowledgeable in our own Christian teachings and especially in pagan <note>original: "heathen"</note> philosophy. He is overflowing with all kinds of learning and a vast wealth of historical knowledge. However, he did not try to decorate the natural beauty of his philosophy with flashy public speaking techniques <note>original: "rhetorical arts"</note>. Therefore, while his choice of words is forceful and maintains a formal, scientific tone, it is not "seasoned" with the typical ornaments of rhetoric. He does not try to win over a crowd of listeners with manipulative or overly charming language.

He wrote four speeches <note>original: "discourses"</note> against the pagans: the first was dedicated to the Emperor Antoninus Pius, his sons, and the Roman Senate; the second was dedicated to the Emperor's successors. The third speech discusses the nature of demons. The fourth book, also written against the pagans, is called a *Refutation* <note>original: "Elenchos"</note>. He also wrote *On the Sole Sovereignty of God*, a work called *The Harper* <note>original: "Psaltes"</note>, several works *Against Marcion* which are worth reading, and a useful treatise entitled *Against All Heresies*.

---

1 <margin>See Code 52.</margin>
2 <note>original: Greek "Ankyrotos"</note> This title means "secured by an anchor," representing the anchor of faith that holds steady amidst the storms of heresy.

<language>English</language>
<page-type>text</page-type>
<page-num>213</page-num>
<header>CLEMENT OF ROME</header>

He <note>Referring to Justin Martyr, whose entry began on the previous page</note> was the son of Priscus and grandson of Bacchius, and was a native of Neapolis <note>The modern city of Nablus</note> in the province of Palestine. He lived for some time in Rome, where his speeches, lifestyle, and clothing identified him as a true philosopher. Because he was a deeply devoted man of faith, his life and religion provoked the anger of a man named Crescens from the Cynic sect. Being falsely accused by him, Justin patiently endured his persecution in a way that was worthy of his entire life's work. Using this persecution as an occasion for martyrdom, he died for Christ with nobility and joy.

### CXXVI

I read the small volume containing Clement’s two *Epistles to the Corinthians*. The first letter accuses the Corinthians of having disturbed the peace and harmony proper to community life through unrest, disorder, and division <note>original: "sedition, disturbance, and schism"</note>, and it urges them to turn away from such evil ways. The style is simple and clear, lacking elaborate phrasing and thus resembling the style of other church writers. 

The author, however, deserves criticism for a few things: for suggesting there are other worlds beyond the ocean, for using the myth of the phoenix as an undeniable argument <note>Clement used the legend of the phoenix bird regenerating from its own ashes as a proof for the resurrection of the body</note>, and for calling our Lord Jesus Christ a "high priest and leader" <note>original: "chief priest and president"</note> without using the more exalted titles that befit God—though he never openly speaks sacrilegiously of Him. 

The second letter, which contains advice and encouragement for a better life, proclaims Christ as God at the very beginning. However, it introduces certain unusual expressions as if they were from Holy Scripture, and even the first letter is not entirely free from these. Some passages are interpreted in a strange manner. The ideas are somewhat weak and, at times, inconsistent.

In the same small volume, I also read Polycarp’s *Epistle to the Philippians*. It is full of good advice, written with clarity and simplicity in the style of church interpretation. The author also mentions that he sent the letters of the divinely-inspired Ignatius to the Philippians, asking to be informed if they had heard any news of him.

### CXXVII

I read the *Life of Constantine the Great*, the Emperor, by Eusebius Pamphili <note>Eusebius of Caesarea, the famous church historian</note>, which is a praise-filled biography <note>original: "eulogy"</note> in four books. It describes the man's entire way of life and all of his actions relating to church history, from his earliest years until the day he passed away at the age of...

<header>EUSEBIUS OF CAESAREA</header>

sixty-four. Even here, the author maintains his characteristic style, except that his language is necessarily more elaborate <note>original: "brilliant"</note>, and he inserts flowery words more frequently than usual. However, he does not show much charm or grace in his explanations, which is a common flaw in his other works. Many passages from all ten books of his *Ecclesiastical History* are spread throughout this work, which consists of four books. 

He notes that Constantine the Great was baptized in Nicomedia, having delayed his baptism until that time because he wished to be baptized in the waters of the Jordan River. He does not clearly state who performed the baptism. 

Regarding the Arian heresy <note>A 4th-century belief that Jesus was a created being, subordinate to God the Father, which was declared heretical at the Council of Nicaea</note>, he does not make it clear whether he still followed that doctrine or if he had changed his mind. He also fails to state whether Arius’s views were right or wrong, though he should have mentioned this, given that a significant part of Constantine's reign involved the council <note>original: "synod"</note>, which requires a detailed explanation. 

Instead, he mentions that a "dispute"—a term he uses to hide the heresy's true nature—arose between Arius and Alexander. He claims the pious emperor was deeply saddened by this "dispute" and tried to resolve it. Through letters and through Hosius, the Bishop of Cordova, Constantine attempted to persuade the two sides to end their conflict and restore friendship and harmony. When he failed to persuade them, he called a council from all regions and ended the conflict, establishing peace. 

However, his account is neither accurate nor clear. It is as if he were ashamed or unwilling to reveal the facts about Arius, the council's decree against him, or the just punishment of his ungodly companions who were exiled with him; he says nothing of these matters. He does not even mention the divine punishment Arius suffered <note>According to church tradition, Arius died suddenly and violently in a public place, which his opponents saw as a sign of God's judgment</note>, which was witnessed by everyone. He brings none of these facts to light and provides very little detail about the council and its proceedings. 

Because of this, when he speaks of the holy Eustathius <note>Eustathius of Antioch, a staunch opponent of Arianism who was deposed and exiled through the intrigues of the pro-Arian faction</note>, he does not even mention his name, nor the bold and successful plots against him. Attributing these events to mere "rebellion and chaos," he instead highlights the calm behavior of the bishops at the Council of Antioch, credited to the emperor’s dedication and cooperation, which supposedly turned the unrest into peace. He speaks in a similar way...

regarding the intrigues against the much-persecuted <note>original: "much-tried"</note> Athanasius <note>Athanasius of Alexandria (c. 296–373) was a chief defender of Trinitarianism against Arianism</note>. In his desire to include these events in his history, he says that Alexandria was again filled with civil unrest <note>original: "sedition"</note> and disturbance, which were calmed by the presence of the bishops, supported by the emperor. However, he does not make it clear who started the unrest, its nature, or how it was suppressed. He maintains nearly the same pattern of concealment in his narrative concerning the quarrels of the bishops about doctrine <note>original: "dogma"</note> or their disagreements on other matters.

### CXXVIII

Read Lucian’s¹ declamation *On Phalaris* <note>Phalaris was a notorious 6th-century BC tyrant of Sicily, famous for the "brazen bull" in which he roasted his enemies</note> and his various *Dialogues of the Dead* and *Dialogues of the Courtesans*, and other works on various subjects. In nearly all of these, he ridicules the beliefs of the pagans <note>original: "heathen"</note>. Thus, he attacks their foolish errors in the invention of gods; their brutal and uncontrollable passions and lack of restraint; the monstrous fantasies and fictions of their poets; the resulting errors in their governance; the erratic course and "ups and downs" <note>original: "changes and chances"</note> of their lives; and the arrogant behavior of philosophers who are full of nothing but pretension and empty opinions. In short, his goal—as we have said—is to use prose to hold the pagans up to ridicule.

He seems to be the type of person who takes nothing seriously. While ridiculing and mocking the opinions of others, he does not state what opinions he himself holds—unless we say his view is that nothing can be known for certain. His style is excellent, his language clear, appropriate, and expressive. He shows a particular fondness for clarity and purity, combined with brilliance and a suitable dignity. His writing is so well-constructed that the reader does not seem to be reading prose; instead, a pleasant song, whose structure is not too obvious, seems to flow into the listener’s ears. In a word, as previously mentioned, his style is charming, but it is not consistent with the subjects he has chosen to ridicule. That he was one of those who believed nothing could be known for certain is shown by the following inscription in his work:

¹ Lucian (c. 125–180 AD) was a well-known rhetorician <note>original: "sophist"</note> and humorous writer. He was a native of Samosata in Syria, and after traveling extensively, settled in Athens before moving to Egypt, where he died. His *True History* was the inspiration for works like Jonathan Swift’s *Gulliver’s Travels*; he shares much in common with both Swift and the French philosopher Voltaire.

### CXXIX

Read the various stories of *Metamorphoses*<gloss>1</gloss> by Lucius of Patrae. The style is clear, pure, and pleasant; avoiding new or unusual words, the author goes to extremes in his tales of wonders, so that he might be called a second Lucian. The first two books are almost word-for-word translations from Lucian's *Lucius or The Ass*, unless Lucian was the one who borrowed from Lucius—which, if I may make a guess <note>original: "hazard the conjecture"</note>, is what happened, though I have not been able to determine for certain which wrote first. 

It seems that Lucian, having shortened <note>original: "cut down"</note> the more extensive <note>original: "copious"</note> work of Lucius and removed everything that seemed unsuitable for his goal, combined what remained into a single work. In this, he kept the wording and arrangement of the original and gave the title *Lucius or The Ass* to what he had borrowed. Both works are full of mythical fantasies and shameful <note>original: "disgraceful"</note> indecency. The only difference is that Lucian—as in all his other writings—mocks and ridicules pagan <note>original: "heathenish"</note> superstitions, whereas Lucius took the transformations of men into other men or beasts <note>original: "brutes"</note>, and beasts into men, quite seriously. Believing all the idle talk and nonsense of ancient myths <note>original: "fables"</note>, he wrote them down and turned them into a story.

### CXXX

Read a work by Damascius<gloss>2</gloss> in four books. The first, containing 352 chapters, is titled *On Unbelievable Events* <note>original: "Incredible Events"</note>; the second, in 52 chapters, *On Unbelievable Stories of Spirits* <note>original: "Demons"</note>; the third, in 63 chapters, *On Unbelievable Stories of Souls That Have Appeared After Death*; and the fourth, in 105 chapters, *On Unbelievable Natures*. They all contain impossible, unbelievable, and clumsily invented tales of marvelous things—foolish and worthy of...

<gloss>2</gloss> Damascius (c. 458–after 538 AD) was a Neoplatonist philosopher from Damascus. He was the last head of the School of Athens before it was closed by Emperor Justinian. His treatise *Problems and Solutions Concerning First Principles* still survives.

<header>AMYNTIANUS</header>

### CXXXI

Read a work *On Alexander* by Amyntianus, dedicated to the emperor Marcus.<sup>1</sup> The author claims to describe the deeds of Alexander in a befitting <note>appropriate or suitable</note> manner, but the course of his narrative proves him to be too ready and audacious in his promises, but backward and timid in performance. For he is far inferior to those who have previously written of Alexander, and his style, although clear, is characterized by a lack of vigor and energy, and much that is of the greatest importance is omitted.

Among other works, Amyntianus also wrote parallel lives of Dionysius and Domitian, and of Philip of Macedon and Augustus, both in two books. A life of Olympias, the mother of Alexander, is also attributed to him.

### CXXXII–CXXXV

Read various *Declamations* <note>formal rhetorical speeches or practice orations</note> by Palladius<sup>2</sup> the sophist <note>term: "sophist" — here, a professional teacher of rhetoric</note>, Aphthonius,<sup>3</sup> Eusebius<sup>4</sup> the sophist, and Maximus the sophist of Alexandria. Palladius is far superior to the others in excellence of style.

### CXXXVI

Read the *Thesauri* <note>original: "Treasuries"</note> of Cyril.<sup>5</sup> The work is dialectical <note>term: "dialectical" — using logical discussion and reasoning to find the truth</note>, and attacks the madness of Arius and Eunomius <note>leaders of the Arian heresy, which argued that Jesus Christ was not equal to God the Father</note> with vigorous and manifold arguments. By logical methods he skillfully refutes their folly and by combining and introducing evidence from Holy Writ <note>the Bible</note> he completely exposes the unsoundness of their doctrine. He quotes the simple evidence from Scripture,

<header>EUNOMIUS</header>

...thus everywhere closing their insolent mouths so that they are unable to answer. This work is the clearest of all Cyril's works, especially to those who are able to grasp the significance of his logical methods.

### CXXXVII

Read a short work by Eunomius <note>original: "Eunomius of Cappadocia." He was the leader of an extreme Arian sect and Bishop of Cyzicus. He argued that the Son was not of the same substance as the Father, but was only "God" in name and virtue.</note>, titled *[Title Missing]* <note>The title was likely "Apology"</note>, which is clear proof of his impiety. It was much admired by his followers, and they made the greatest efforts to keep it secret and prevent it from becoming known to others. However, the great Basil <note>St. Basil the Great (c. 330–379), Bishop of Caesarea and a defender of Nicene orthodoxy.</note> managed to obtain a copy. With his brilliance, vigor, and the excellence of his arguments, he overthrew and refuted it. It was as if Basil had taken a Babylonian child and dashed it against the granite rock of truth <note>A reference to Psalm 137:9, metaphorically describing the destruction of an "evil" idea in its infancy.</note>, showing that what was once so admired was a mere corpse, deserving only of ridicule.

### CXXXVIII

Read a work by the same impious man in three books. This work serves as a rebuttal to the absurdities that St. Basil showed were contained in Eunomius's blasphemous writings. It is said that while he was laboring over this work, he spent several Olympiads <term>Olympiads</term> <note>periods of four years; thus, roughly twelve years</note> shut up in his chamber. Only after an interval of many years did he bring forth the "abortion" and "evil monstrosity" with which he had become pregnant through secret dealings. 

With great difficulty, he raised and exhibited this wretched offspring to his fellow-initiates. He was especially afraid that it might somehow fall into the hands of Basil and be torn to pieces before it had gained any consistency, or that it might prematurely wither and perish before it reached maturity. Therefore, like Kronos <note>In Greek mythology, Kronos (Saturn) swallowed his children to prevent them from overthrowing him.</note> in the old fable who swallowed his own children, he carefully hid and concealed the work for as long as he could.

<summary>This page discusses the theological conflict between the Arian heretic Eunomius and St. Basil the Great, describing Basil's decisive refutation of Eunomius's secretive and long-developed writings.</summary>
<keywords>Eunomius, St. Basil, Cyril of Alexandria, Arianism, heresy, Cyzicus, Kronos, Saturn, Olympiads, theology, polemics</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the critique of the Arian leader Eunomius, specifically his response to St. Basil the Great, and concludes with an introduction to the writings of St. Athanasius.</meta>

<page-num>219</page-num>
<header>ATHANASIUS</header>

...as long as Basil's mortal life lasted and inspired him with dread. But after that saint had left his earthly dwelling and had ascended to his own inheritance in heaven, Eunomius was relieved of this great fear. Although it was late in the day, he finally dared to release the work—not for the eyes of the general public, but only for his own friends. Theodore, Gregory of Nyssa, and Sophronius (whom I have already mentioned) discovered the work. They savaged it unmercifully, just as they had his earlier writing, and threw it back in its author’s face—now a mere corpse smelling of total impurity. In this way, he paid the penalty for his <note>original: "impiety"</note> irreverence. 

His writing style lacks so much grace and charm that the author seems unaware that such qualities even exist. He displays immense vanity and creates clashing sounds by piling up consonants. He uses words that are difficult to pronounce and contain too many consonants, written in a poetical or, more accurately, a <term>dithyrambic style</term> <note>original: "dithyrambic." This refers to a style of writing that is wild, irregular, and overly emotional, similar to ancient Greek hymns.</note>. The composition is forced, cramped, and harsh; a reader is obliged to struggle intensely with their lips if they wish to clearly speak the words which the author—through excessive coarseness, compression, distortion, and mutilation—has clumsily put together. His sentences are sometimes stretched to an unreasonable length, and the entire work is filled with obscurity and a lack of clarity. His goal was to use the weight of his words to persuade the majority of people that his intellect was beyond their reach. He also used this very obscurity and confusion to hide the significant weakness of his thoughts and to mask his lack of actual ideas. He seems to have great faith in logical arguments, attacking others on this point and showing great eagerness to use them himself. However, because he began his studies late in life and never fully mastered the subject, he is often caught in errors of reasoning.

Also read his *Letters* to various people, forty in number. While he tries for the same cleverness of form in these, he is ignorant of the rules of the <term>epistolary style</term> <note>the traditional rules and etiquette governing the writing of formal letters</note> and has had no practice in them; consequently, his failures have been publicly exposed and shamed.

### CXXXIX

Read the *Commentary on Ecclesiastes* and the *Song of Songs* by the great Athanasius <note>St. Athanasius of Alexandria (c. 296–373), a chief defender of Trinitarianism against Arianism.</note>. The style is clear, like that of all [his works...]

<page-num>220</page-num>
<header>BASIL THE GREAT</header>

...his writings. But neither this nor any other of his works with which I am acquainted approaches the grace and beauty of the letters containing an apology for his flight and an account of his exile. <note>This likely refers to St. Athanasius’s *Apology for his Flight*, a famous defense written after he fled from Roman soldiers in 356 AD.</note>

### CXL

Read the same holy man’s *Against Arius and his Doctrines*, in five books. The style, as in all his works, is clear, simple, and free from unnecessary repetition, yet it is also powerful and profound. The arguments, which he possesses in great abundance, are extremely forceful. He uses logical arguments, but not with the exact technical phrasing used by beginners or those with only recent knowledge of the subject who are eager to make a childish display. Instead, he writes in the imposing and dignified manner of a philosopher, using simple ideas that are clearly presented. He also strongly supports his position with evidence and proofs from Holy Scripture <note>original: "Holy Writ"</note>. In short, this work alone is a complete refutation of Arianism <term>Arianism</term> <note>A 4th-century Christian belief that held that Jesus Christ was a created being, subordinate to God the Father, which was declared heretical at the Council of Nicaea.</note>. If anyone were to say that Gregory the Theologian <note>St. Gregory of Nazianzus</note> and the holy Basil drew from this work as if from a fountain—using its clear and beautiful stream for their own works against the same heresy—they would not be far wrong.

### CXLI

Read also the work of St. Basil titled *The Six Days' Work* <note>original Greek: *Hexameron*</note>. He is admirable in all his writings. More than anyone else, he knows how to use a style that is pure, distinct, and appropriate—generally suited for both civic <note>original: "political"</note> and celebratory <note>original: "panegyrical"</note> oratory. In terms of structure and the purity of his thought, he is second to none. He favors persuasiveness, sweetness, and brilliance; his words flow like a stream gushing naturally from a spring. He uses persuasive reasoning <note>original: "probability"</note> so effectively that if anyone were to use these discourses as a model for public speaking and practice with them—provided they already had some knowledge of the rules of rhetoric—I do not think they would need to consult any other author. They would not even need Plato or Demosthenes, whom the ancient writers recommend to those who wish to master the styles of civic and celebratory oratory.

### CXLII

Read also his *Moral Discourses*, which are especially notable for the excellent language mentioned above.

<summary>This page evaluates the literary and theological works of St. Basil the Great, praising his clear yet powerful refutation of Arianism and his supreme mastery of rhetorical style in "The Six Days' Work."</summary>

<meta>This page continues the review of St. Basil the Great’s writings, specifically his correspondence and monastic rules, before transitioning to a series of reviews of ancient lexicons (dictionaries), starting with the extensive work of Helladius of Alexandria.</meta>

<page-num>221</page-num>
<header>HELLADIUS</header>

### CXLIII

Read also his *Letters*, both the canonical ones and those written to various individuals. They clearly show the admirable nature of the man’s character, and anyone might take them as a model for letter-writing if they have no other example.

### CXLIV

Read also his *Ascetica* <note>original Greek: *Asketika*; referring to spiritual exercises and rules for monks</note> or *Regulations for a Monastic Life*. He who follows these rules will inherit a heavenly kingdom. In this work, contrary to his usual custom, he makes frequent use of headings throughout many chapters.

### CXLV

Read the *Lexicon* of Helladius <note>Helladius of Alexandria flourished under the reign of Emperor Theodosius II (408–450 AD)</note>, arranged in alphabetical order. It is the most comprehensive of the lexicons that I know; the collection consists not only of single words but also of some very pleasant short phrases <note>original: "clauses"</note>, which frequently form complete grammatical units <term>members</term> <note>In ancient rhetoric, a "member" or *colon* is a distinct part of a sentence that expresses a complete thought.</note>. 

The words are mostly taken from prose writers rather than from poets, unlike the compilation by Diogenianus <note>A grammarian during the time of the Emperor Hadrian</note>. The alphabetical order is not maintained through every syllable, but only for the first letter. The collection is very large, so much so that the entire work could not be contained in five fair-sized volumes; our copy is spread across seven. It is a useful work for those engaged in literary studies who desire to acquire a wide variety of knowledge, for it contains quotations from famous orators and poets.

### CXLVI

Read a *Lexicon* of the "pure style," arranged in alphabetical order. The work is large and extensive, feeling more like a series of books. It is extremely useful to those who study this subject.

### CXLVII

Read a *Lexicon* of the "serious style." It is of great length, and this labor of love would be more convenient for readers if it were bound in two or three volumes. It is arranged in alphabetical order and is useful for those who wish to elevate their writing style to be more sublime and dignified.

<keywords>St. Basil the Great, Letters, Ascetica, Monasticism, Helladius of Alexandria, Lexicons, Diogenianus, Rhetoric, Greek Literature, Linguistics</keywords>

--- Page 228 ---
<meta>This page continues Photius’s survey of ancient Greek reference works, shifting from the general lexicon of Helladius to specialized dictionaries focused on political rhetoric, the Attic orators, and the philosophy of Plato.</meta>

<page-num>222</page-num>
<header>JULIAN</header>

### CXLVIII

Read a specialized <term>lexicon</term> <note>original: "Lexicon"; a dictionary or vocabulary book</note> of political style, comprised of three large volumes. This very extensive work will greatly assist a person in achieving success in public speaking and provides a ready supply of counter-arguments. The reader will not have to learn the material from scratch, but merely note the specific words they require, provided they already possess some knowledge of the ancient writers. This work is also organized in alphabetical order.

### CXLIX

Read the *Lexicon* of Valerius Pollio <note>Valerius Pollio of Alexandria, a grammarian who lived during the reign of Hadrian</note> in alphabetical order. It contains many poetic words, though not as many as the lexicon by Diogenianus, whose work is twice as large.

### CL

Read the *Lexicon* of Julian <note>Nothing is known of this specific Julian, though he is clearly distinct from the Emperor Julian.</note>, which contains the vocabulary used by the ten orators <note>The "Ten Attic Orators" were considered the greatest public speakers of classical Athens, including figures like Demosthenes and Lysias.</note>, arranged in alphabetical order. This book is also of very great size and contains an explanation of all legal terms used by the Athenians, as well as words used by the orators regarding private affairs or terms adopted according to local customs. It is clear that this work will be extremely useful for studying these speeches collectively.

I also encountered a work by Philostratus of Tyre <note>Not to be confused with the more famous Philostratus who wrote the *Life of Apollonius of Tyana*.</note> on the same subject; it is an impressive work, although Julian’s *Lexicon* is superior. A similar work by Diodorus <note>Valerius Diodorus, the son of Pollio mentioned in section CXLIX.</note> is in no way inferior to Julian’s, except that Julian makes more use of illustrative quotations. I cannot say which of them was written earlier or which author borrowed from the other, for it is evident that they are not independent compilations.

### CLI

Read the *Lexicon to Plato* by Timaeus <note>Timaeus the Sophist, likely writing in the early 4th century AD. This work is still extant today.</note>, dedicated to a man named Gentianus. It is a small work in one book, arranged in alphabetical order.

---

¹ Valerius Pollio of Alexandria.     ² Nothing is known of him.
³ Not to be confused with the author of the *Life of Apollonius of Tyana* (Codex XLIV).
⁴ Valerius Diodorus, son of Pollio (Codex CXLIX).
⁵ Written about the beginning of the fourth century AD. This lexicon still exists.

<page-num>223</page-num>
<header>PAUSANIAS</header>

### CLII

The same volume contains the five books of the first edition of the *Lexicon of Attic Words* by Aelius Dionysius of Halicarnassus <note>original: "Aelius Dionysius of Halicarnassus"; not to be confused with the famous historian Dionysius of Halicarnassus who wrote the *Roman Antiquities*</note>. In this work, the words are arranged alphabetically from the first to the last letter. It is dedicated to a man named Scymnus and is highly beneficial for those who wish to write in correct <term>Attic</term> <note>The prestigious dialect of ancient Athens used by famous playwrights, philosophers, and orators</note>, as well as for those who intend to study the works of Athenian writers. 

It contains all the words used by the Athenians at festivals and in the law courts. It is easy to find specialized and idiomatic expressions, especially if one consults not only the first edition but also the second (which also consists of five books). This second edition contains words omitted from the first, or provides more examples for words that were previously unsupported. Because the second edition is more comprehensive and the quotations are more numerous, it is a superior resource. If someone were to combine these two editions—which would not require much effort—the usefulness of the work would be greatly increased.

### CLIII

In the same volume, I read the *Lexicon of Pausanias* <note>A grammarian who flourished during the reign of the Emperor Hadrian (r. 117–138 AD)</note>, which is also organized in alphabetical order. It is just as useful as the previous work, if not more so, for the study of Athenian authors. Although it does not include as many illustrative quotations, it contains a larger number of words under certain letters. Consequently, on its own, it is as large as both editions of Aelius Dionysius combined, even though, as mentioned, it provides fewer examples. If someone were to merge the two editions by Dionysius with this work by Pausanias—which could be done quite easily—the result would be a magnificent and highly practical resource for anyone reading ancient Greek literature.

### CLIV

In the same volume, I read the *List of Platonic Words* by Boëthus <note>A grammarian whose specific identity is debated among scholars</note>, arranged alphabetically. It is dedicated to a man named Melanthas and is far more useful than the similar collection compiled by Timaeus <note>Timaeus the Sophist, author of a surviving lexicon of Plato's vocabulary</note>.

### CLV

Boëthus was also the author of another short work addressed to Athenagoras, entitled *On the Words of Doubtful Meaning in Plato*. If someone were to combine the vocabulary in these two small treatises with the words included in the work by Timaeus, it would provide a great benefit to everyone who wishes to study Plato.

### CLVI

In the same volume, I read a list of *New and Foreign Words in Plato* by Dorotheus <note>original: "Dorotheus"; likely Dorotheus of Ascalon, a Greek grammarian frequently cited by the author Athenaeus</note>, arranged in alphabetical order. It will clearly be useful to the student.

### CLVII

I also read the *Atticist* by Moeris <note>Moeris was a 2nd-century AD grammarian. His lexicon, titled *Attic Words* (original: <term>Λέξεις Ἀττικαὶ</term>), still exists today</note>. This short work is also organized in alphabetical order.

### CLVIII

I read the *Rhetorical Preparation* <note>original: "Rhetorical Equipment"</note> by Phrynichus the Arabian <note>Phrynichus of Bithynia flourished under the emperors Marcus Aurelius and Commodus. He was a strict proponent of stylistic purity. While only excerpts and this summary of his 36-book *Preparation* survive, his shorter handbook, the *Atticist*, remains extant</note> in thirty-six books. It contains a collection of words and phrases, some of which are elegantly expressed and arranged in a novel way, and are expanded into complete "members" <term>members</term> <note>In ancient rhetoric, a "member" (original: <term>κῶλα</term>, *cola*) is a rhythmic segment of a sentence, typically 7 to 10 syllables long, making it longer than a "clause" (original: <term>κόμματα</term>, *commata*), which is 4 to 6 syllables</note>. 

Many of these expressions can be found in the collection of Helladius, but there they are scattered throughout a massive work, whereas here they are all gathered together. Phrynichus’s goal was to create a list of specialized and refined words, whereas Helladius, being simply a...

<page-num>225</page-num>
<header>PHRYNICHUS THE ARABIAN</header>
<sig>P</sig>
<meta>VOL. I.</meta>

lexicographer, added them to the common stock and included them among the rest.

The writer flourished during the reign of Marcus Aurelius and his son Commodus, to whom the work is dedicated and inscribed: "Phrynichus to Commodus Caesar, greetings." In the preface, he encourages Commodus to pursue learning while also praising his own work. He claims that he had already composed thirty-seven books and dedicated them to the emperor, promising to write as many more if his life were spared. As we have said, we have only read thirty-six books, containing words from the first to the last letter of the alphabet.

Despite his claim that he dedicated the work to the emperor, he seems to have inscribed the individual books to different people. For instance, the first, second, and third books are addressed to a certain Aristocles,¹ in the hope that they might serve as a source of amusement and recreation for him on his birthday. The fourth book is addressed to a certain Julian, a fellow citizen and friend. The author adds that he had initially intended to dedicate the entire work to Aristocles; however, after he was appointed to the great council at Rome by imperial decree, he decided to choose Julian as his friend and partner in his labors, making use of his services as a judge and critic of his writings. 

Despite this promise, he dedicates the fifth book to a learned friend named Menodorus, who had previously criticized him for not adequately investigating the inflection of words. The author says the current book was written at Menodorus's request, and he excuses the delay in finishing it because he is suffering from strangury <term>strangury</term> <note>a condition characterized by slow, painful, and interrupted urination</note>—a common ailment of old age—as well as prolonged and serious mental distress, hemorrhage of the stomach, and several other illnesses. If, however, he should regain his health, he promises to complete the current assignment and any other work his friend might suggest regarding learning and good taste that has not yet been attempted. Nevertheless, the sixth book is again dedicated to a different person—Tiberinus. The seventh is dedicated to another man, Menophilus, whom he describes as a highly accomplished scholar who provided entire passages to illustrate the subject matter of the sixth book; Phrynichus encourages him to collect as many of these as possible.

---

¹ Of Pergamum; a Greek philosopher and sophist, consul under Marcus Aurelius, and author of declamations and technical writings on rhetoric.

--- Page 232 ---
<meta>This page continues the catalog of the books within Phrynichus the Arabian’s "Rhetorical Preparation." The previous page detailed the first five books and their various dedications to Aristocles, Julian, and Menodorus, noting Phrynichus's recurring health issues.</meta>

<page-num>226</page-num>
<header>PHRYNICHUS THE ARABIAN</header>

The author aims to include as many Attic words as possible in his writings. The eighth book is again dedicated to Julian, whom the author asks to be kind enough to correct any of his statements that are unsatisfactory, for which illness must be his excuse. Inscribing the ninth book to Rufinus, he says that Aristocles first prompted him to begin the book, but that Rufinus will be responsible for its completion; having seen the rest of it, Rufinus recognized its usefulness and approved of his labors. 

Turning back again, he dedicates the tenth book to Aristocles and the next to Menodorus. In this eleventh book, he states that he had just come across the orations of Aristides,¹ who flourished at that time, and speaks very highly of him. He also says that Marcianus the critic—neglecting Plato and Demosthenes—expresses his preference for the letters of the Roman Brutus ² above all others and declares him to be the model of stylistic excellence. The author says that he quotes this remark not because he approves of this judgment, but to prevent anyone from being surprised if some people think this man’s reputation is inferior to that of Aristides, despite his splendid oratory. For Aristides also, like other men distinguished for learning, has been assailed by some who were jealous of his achievements. 

The eleventh book is also dedicated to Menodorus. As for the rest—so that we do not incur the charge of excessive wordiness <note>original: "garrulity"</note>—one is dedicated to Rheginus, another to Aristocles, and another to Basilides of Miletus, the sophist <note>a professional teacher of rhetoric and philosophy</note>. In this book, he says that as soon as he found a respite from his disease, he wrote this volume for him, and he begs him to correct any mistakes that may be due to his illness. All the other books down to the end, which we have read, are dedicated to Menophilus.

The work will no doubt be useful to aspiring authors and orators. The compiler says that he has divided the words collected by him into categories: those adapted for oratory, written composition, and conversation, as well as those suited for a derisive, contemptuous, or romantic <note>original: "amatory"</note> style. The author considers the best models, norms, and standards of undiluted and pure Attic speech <note>the prestigious dialect of ancient Athens</note> to be Plato, Demosthenes and the other nine Attic orators, Thucydides, Xenophon, and Aeschines ³ the son of Lysanias.

---

¹ Publius Aelius Aristides (around 129–189 AD) was a famous rhetorician born at Hadrianutherai in Mysia and a friend of Marcus Aurelius. He was a priest of Asclepius <note>original Latin: "Aesculapius"</note> at Smyrna. More than fifty of his orations and practice speeches <note>original: "declamations"</note> are still in existence.
² Marcus Junius Brutus (85–42 BC), the conspirator who assassinated Julius Caesar.
³ He lived in Syracuse at the court of Dionysius the Younger (356 BC).

<page-num>227</page-num>
<header>ISOCRATES</header>

The Socratic philosopher Critias, $^1$ the son of Callaeschrus, and Antisthenes $^2$ with his two orations in praise of Cyrus and Odysseus; among the writers of comedy, Aristophanes and his circle, where they use the Attic dialect; among the tragedians, Aeschylus the mighty-voiced, Sophocles the sweet, and the all-wise Euripides. While the author prefers these to all other authors, orators, and poets, he again puts first those whom (as he says) Momus $^3$ <note>Momus was the personification of mockery and criticism in Greek mythology.</note> himself would not find fault with, and at whom even the legendary Corycian god $^4$ would not rejoice, even if he made faces at them; these are Plato, Demosthenes, and Aeschines, the son of Lysanias, because of the excellence of his seven dialogues—though some people believe these were not written by Aeschines but instead attribute them to Socrates. So much for these matters.

The writer displays great learning, but he is wordy and excessively long-winded. The work, without omitting anything of importance, could have been reduced to a fifth of its size. Instead, the author has spun it out to an unmanageable length by a poorly timed use of words. While he collects material to promote elegance and beauty of style, he fails to put his own rules into practice as an example.

## CLIX

Read the twenty-one *Orations* and nine *Letters* of Isocrates.$^5$

<margin>and afterwards wrote speeches for the law courts at Athens. He is, of course, not the same person as the rival of Demosthenes (see p. 62, note $^1$).</margin>

$^3$ The personification of blame or censure.
$^4$ According to the scholar Suidas, the inhabitants of Corycus (a coastal area in Pamphylia), in order to avoid being robbed by pirates, used to listen in other harbors to find out where ships were going and then told the pirates. <meta>This page continues the summary of Code 159, detailing the specific orations of the Greek orator Isocrates, following the introductory list of authors and the start of the entry on Isocrates from the previous page.</meta>

<page-num>228</page-num>
<header>ISOCRATES</header>

His "deliberative" <note>Speeches intended to advise on public policy or future actions.</note> speeches include those titled *To Demonicus* and *To Nicocles*, which contain useful advice; a second speech addressed to Nicocles; and one titled *On the Peace*. The purpose of the *Panegyricus* is to consider how to ensure harmony among the Greeks themselves and the best methods for waging war against the barbarians <note>In this context, "barbarians" refers to the Persians and other non-Greek speakers.</note>, though by far the largest portion of the work is dedicated to glorifying the Athenians. The *Areopagiticus* is also one of his deliberative speeches; it encourages the Athenians to act virtuously by praising their ancestors and criticizing the failings of their descendants. The *Plataicus* and the *Archidamus* are also deliberative; in the latter, he urges the Spartans to go to war against the Thebans over the issue of the Messenians.

In the oration *Against the Sophists*, he attacks his political rivals. The next speech is a *Praise of Busiris* <note>original: "Encomium of Busiris"</note>,² as the title suggests. The eleventh is a *Praise of Helen* <note>original: "Encomium of Helen"</note>. The twelfth, *Evagoras*,³ is a eulogy for that king, dedicated to his son Nicocles. The *Philippus* is a deliberative speech, advising Philip [of Macedon] to try to promote harmony among the Greeks and focus his attention on a united invasion against the barbarians in Asia.

The *Panathenaicus* is a eulogy of Athens and the ancestors of the Athenians. Isocrates mentions that he began writing it when he was ninety-four years old, but a severe three-year illness prevented him from completing it until he was ninety-seven. The speech called *Antidosis* (meaning an "exchange of property") <note>In ancient Athens, a wealthy citizen could be challenged to either take on a public expense or swap estates with the challenger.</note> appears to belong to the category of "forensic" <note>Legal or courtroom speeches.</note> orations; it contains a defense against the slanders made against him by a man named Lysimachus. This speech, the longest of its kind, was composed when Isocrates was eighty-two years old. Its subject matter is more varied than his other works, as he includes excerpts from his other speeches to prove that he is not corrupting the youth, but rather promoting the public good. The speech *Against Callimachus*, as well as the *Aegineticus* (which deals with...

---

<margin>The news of the victory of Philip of Macedon [at Chaeronea] is said to have killed the elderly orator—as Milton wrote, he was "killed by the report of that eloquent old man." Isocrates was too timid to speak in public himself, so he opened a school of rhetoric which was very popular.</margin>

¹ Speeches were traditionally divided into three types: deliberative (political), forensic (legal), and epideictic (ceremonial or "show" speeches).
² Busiris was a legendary Egyptian prince who executed foreigners who arrived in his country; he was eventually killed by Hercules.
³ Evagoras was the King of Salamis in Cyprus (see page 109).
