<meta>This page continues the catalog of the works of the Athenian orator Isocrates, moving from his political and advisory speeches to his legal orations and personal letters, before concluding with a stylistic critique and a new entry on the orator Choricius of Gaza.</meta>

<page-num>229</page-num>
<header>CHORICIUS</header>

...with a claim to inheritance), the speech *Against Euthynus* on behalf of Nicias, the *Banker’s Speech* <note>original: *Trapeziticus*; an action against a banker</note>, and *Against Lochites* (a claim for damages for outrage and assault) are also "legal" <note>original: "forensic"</note>. These are the twenty-one orations of Isocrates that we have read. Of his nine *Letters*, one is addressed to Dionysius the tyrant of Sicily, another to Archidamus, two to Philip, one to Alexander, one to Antipater, one to Timotheus, and the ninth to the Archons <note>high-ranking magistrates</note> of Mytilene.<sup>1</sup>

This writer preferred to teach rhetoric <note>the art of persuasive speaking</note> rather than take part in managing public affairs, unlike the other nine famous orators, which included Demosthenes. Even before the time of those orators, he had a great reputation as a teacher, and later, he was never considered inferior to them. His chief characteristics, as the reader immediately notices, are clarity and precision, along with an excessive care in the craftsmanship of his speeches. However, this often descends into a distracting neatness and over-elaboration. His infinite habit of over-working his text results in a lack of artistic taste rather than a lack of clever arguments. He lacks forcefulness,<sup>2</sup> genuine emotion, and liveliness,<sup>3</sup> but in his political speeches, he balances dignity and clarity remarkably well. His style is somewhat weak; his constant use of perfectly balanced phrases—repeated to a sickening degree <note>original: *ad nauseam*</note>—is the primary evidence of his excessive focus on trivial details. Nevertheless, we acknowledge the general excellence of his language and only offer these criticisms to show where he failed to do justice to his own talent. Indeed, compared to some other speechwriters, even his faults appear to be virtues.

### CLX

Read the *Practice Speeches* <note>original: "Declamations"</note> and *Orations* of various kinds by the professional teacher <note>original: "sophist"</note> Choricius of Gaza.<sup>4</sup> He is a lover of clarity and purity of style. Even when he speaks at length for a useful purpose, the clarity of his thoughts is never lost, since the...

---

<sup>1</sup> The letter to the children of Jason of Pherae completes the nine. Scholars are divided on whether these letters are genuine.
<sup>2</sup> Or, "the ability to portray character" (according to the scholar Van Hook).
<sup>3</sup> "Fiery earnestness" (according to the scholar Jebb).
<sup>4</sup> Choricius lived at the beginning of the sixth century AD and was a pupil of Procopius of Gaza. He was officially a Christian, but his style and way of expressing himself are heavily influenced by classical Greek (pagan) traditions. Several of his speeches of praise, funeral orations, wedding speeches, and school exercises still exist.

<summary>This page concludes the review of Isocrates' works, criticizing his overly polished and sometimes "weak" style, then begins a review of Choricius of Gaza, a 6th-century Christian orator known for his clear, classical style.</summary>
<keywords>Isocrates, rhetoric, forensic oratory, Greek letters, Choricius of Gaza, Procopius of Gaza, declamations, classical style</keywords>

--- Page 236 ---
<meta>This page continues the stylistic analysis of the orator Choricius of Gaza, emphasizing his Christian faith and his relationship with his teacher, Procopius of Gaza, before transitioning to a new entry regarding the historical extracts compiled by the sophist Sopater.</meta>

<page-num>230</page-num>
<header>SOPATER</header>

His expansion is not poorly timed and never reaches the length of a complete rhetorical cycle <term>period; a long, complex sentence with a balanced structure</term>. In his writings, character and sincerity are combined, while at the same time he does not neglect the teaching of moral lessons. As a rule, he uses carefully selected words, although not always in their literal sense; for sometimes, owing to his unrestricted use of figurative language, he falls into stiffness <note>original: "frigidity"; a rhetorical term for a style that is overly formal or artificial</note>, and sometimes is carried away into a poetic style. 

But he is at his best in descriptions and speeches of praise <term>eulogies</term>. He is an upholder of the true religion and respects the rites and holy places of the Christians, although for some reason or another—scornfully and without any excuse—he wrongly introduces Greek myths and pagan stories in his writings, sometimes even when discussing sacred matters. Many of his writings of various kinds are widely read; one finds fictional, celebratory, and argumentative speeches, funeral laments <term>monodies</term>, wedding songs, and many others. 

He flourished during the time of the Emperor Justinian and was a pupil of the rhetorician Procopius—not the famous Procopius of Caesarea, a most distinguished man who, at that time, left behind an eternal fame among all lovers of learning by writing his useful and valuable historical works. In his own country, Choricius was connected with the other Procopius as his teacher in rhetoric; when that teacher reached old age, he had the pleasure of seeing his pupil take his place as the head of his school. Many of his speeches of all kinds are available, all deserving of careful study and imitation. Indeed, the entire book called *Translations of Homeric Verses*, in which the form of Homer's poetry is completely changed into various prose styles, is sufficient to show his vigorous rhetorical powers, which Choricius has imitated as far as a pupil can. Both men were Christians, and in their speeches they frequently, and not casually, discuss the creation of sacred images. The death of his master provided Choricius with the subject for a funeral speech.

### CLXI

Read the *Various Extracts* of the sophist <term>a teacher of rhetoric or philosophy</term> Sopater, in twelve books, compiled from the works of different historians and—

---

¹ Procopius of Gaza (c. 465–528 AD), a Christian rhetorician.
² Perhaps Sopater of Apamea, who was initially a close friend of Constantine the Great but was later executed by him, possibly due to his promotion of paganism.

<summary>This page concludes a critique of the Christian rhetorician Choricius of Gaza, noting his style and his relationship with his teacher Procopius, before beginning an entry on the "Various Extracts" by the sophist Sopater.</summary>
<keywords>Choricius of Gaza, Procopius of Gaza, Procopius of Caesarea, Emperor Justinian, Sopater, rhetoric, Greek myths, Christian literature, Homeric Verses</keywords>

--- Page 237 ---
<meta>This page continues the catalog of works compiled by the scholar Sopater, moving from a discussion of the orator Choricius of Gaza to a detailed breakdown of the sources Sopater used for his multi-volume collection of historical and mythological extracts.</meta>

<page-num>231</page-num>
<header>SOPATER</header>

The first book gives an account of the stories of the gods from the third book of Apollodorus,¹ an Athenian who taught literary scholarship, in his work *On the Gods*. The selections are not taken from the third book alone, but also from the first, fourth, fifth, ninth, twelfth, fifteenth, and sixteenth, all the way down to the twenty-fourth. This collection includes the mythical tales and legends concerning the gods and whatever else has historical value, such as the stories of the heroes, the <term>Dioscuri</term> <note>The twin brothers Castor and Pollux</note>, the inhabitants of Hades <note>the underworld</note>, and similar subjects. The compiler has also drawn from the second book of Juba's³ *On Painting* and from the *Learned Banqueters* <note>original: "Deipnosophists"</note> by Athenaeus⁴ of Naucratis. These are the sources and contents of the first book.

The second book is compiled from the first book through the tenth book of the *Summaries* <note>original: "Epitomes"</note> of Pamphila,⁵ the daughter of Soteridas. It also includes material from Artemon⁶ of Magnesia’s *On the Remarkable Deeds of Women*, the *Wise Sayings* <note>original: "Apophthegms"</note> of Diogenes⁷ the Cynic, the eighth book of Sappho,⁸ and various other writers. This concludes the second book of the extracts.

The third book is drawn from the *Various History* of Favorinus,⁹ beginning with the volumes labeled Nu <note>original: "N"</note> and Xi <note>original: "Ξ"</note> and continuing through the rest of the alphabet in order, with the exception of the volume labeled Tau <note>original: "T"</note>, up to Omega <note>original: "Ω"</note>. These contain various historical accounts, the reasons and origins of traditions, the meanings of words and names, and similar topics. This ends the third book.

...the eighth book of the *Dramatic History* by Rufus.¹ This section contains many improbable and unbelievable accounts, various tragic and comic incidents, dialogues and speeches, descriptions of manners and customs, and similar topics. This concludes the fourth book of Sopater's collection.

The fifth book consists of extracts from the first, second, and third books of Rufus’s *Musical History*. It contains accounts of various comic and tragic actors, writers of <term>dithyrambs</term> <note>ancient Greek choral hymns sung in honor of the god Dionysus</note>, and performers on the flute and <term>cithara</term> <note>an ancient Greek musical instrument similar to a lyre</note>. It also includes material on wedding songs, songs accompanied by dance, and the dancers and others who participated in theatrical competitions. The text explores the origins and family backgrounds of famous performers, both men and women, identifying who pioneered certain artistic practices and who were the close friends and associates of emperors and kings. It also explains what the various contests were, where they originated, and how each artist displayed their skill. The general festivals celebrated by the entire Athenian public are also described. The reader will find all these subjects and more in the fifth book.

The sixth book is drawn from the fourth and fifth books of the *Musical History* by the same author, Rufus. It provides an account of flute players and the songs performed to the flute by both men and women. It discusses the poets Homer, Hesiod, and Antimachus,² along with most of the other poets belonging to their school. It also provides information about female prophetesses—who the so-called <term>Sibyls</term> <note>women in the ancient world believed to possess the gift of prophecy</note> were and where they came from—all of which is borrowed from Rufus. This book also draws upon the second book of the *Halieutica* <note>original: "On Fishing"</note> by Damostratus,³ as well as the first, fifth, ninth, and tenth books of the *Lives of the Philosophers* by Diogenes Laërtius.⁴ We are given an account of the philosophers and the origin of that divine study, philosophy; the text describes how it flourished, who the leaders and champions of the different schools were, and who their followers were...

The seventh book is a kind of anthology taken from the *History* of Herodotus.

The eighth book consists of extracts from an old and anonymous work. It provides a list of women who rose to fame and earned a distinguished reputation, the deeds of certain men and their meetings for the study of rhetoric, the excellence of friendship, and the <term>maxims</term> <note>short statements expressing a general truth or rule of conduct</note> of worthy men. Among these extracts is much that is worth remembering—both words and deeds—which will be found useful in many respects. Such is the content of the eighth book.

This last section deals with the systems of the Thessalians, Achaeans, Parians, Lycians, Chians, and all the peoples he mentioned in his political writings, the usefulness of which is evident. Such are the contents of the twelfth book.

<header>EUSEBIUS OF THESSALONICA 235</header>

The work is very useful to the reader. For although it is not free from mythical <note>original: "fabulous"</note>, monstrous, false, and improbable statements—as I have already mentioned more than once—one may still gather much from the parts that serve as a storehouse of learning to promote virtue and integrity <note>original: "honesty"</note>. For the student of rhetoric and the art of the sophists <note>the "Second Sophistic" was a rhetorical movement focusing on style and performance</note>, these sections are of considerable importance and will be very serviceable, as the author himself says to his friends in the introduction. The writing style is varied and not confined to a single approach, though clarity is the defining characteristic of the entire work.

### 162 <note>CLXII</note>

I read a work by a certain Eusebius,¹ a bishop of the orthodox faith, in ten books. It was written against a monk named Andrew and was prompted by Andrew's behavior. Andrew had written a letter to Eusebius, which he called a "pastoral letter," imploring the bishop to read it. Having done so, Eusebius first reprimands Andrew for his ignorance and audacity <note>original: "temerity"</note>, showing that he had made many mistakes in spelling and could not write a single line without making a blunder. He notes that, despite this, Andrew had the arrogance to write, forgetting his religious calling <note>original: "profession"</note> and the quiet of a monastic life. 

Eusebius then refutes Andrew's heretical opinions at length, for Andrew belonged to the sect called the *Aphthartodocetae*² <note>literally "Incorruptible-Seemists"</note>, named for the unholy doctrine they held. Eusebius first points out that Andrew must explain why he used the word **corruption** <note>original: "φθορά" (phthora)</note> in only one sense, thinking that it referred to sin only, whereas our Holy Fathers, in their usage of words, have traditionally applied it to various different things. Secondly, he reproves him because he ventured to declare—as Julian did—that the body of the Lord was immortal, incapable of suffering <note>original: "impassible"</note>, and incorruptible from the moment of the union of His two natures. He notes this even though Andrew asserts in this very letter that he has undertaken to oppose Severus and Julian.

---

¹ Bishop of Thessalonica, around the year 600.
² A Monophysite sect which held that the one nature of Christ was not subject to corruption (**incorruptible** <note>original: "ἄφθαρτος" (aphthartos)</note>). They understood "corruption" (**phthora**) to mean not only the decay following death and moral depravity, but all physical needs, sufferings, and weaknesses, which were considered blameless <note>original: "ἀδιάβλητα" (adiablēta)</note>. The "docetae" part of the name seems to imply that Christ's body was not what it appeared to be; however, it does not seem that they regarded it as a phantom or a body in appearance only. They admitted it was real and substantial.

<meta>This page continues Eusebius of Thessalonica's detailed refutation of the monk Andrew, specifically focusing on Andrew's rejection of the two natures of Christ and his unorthodox views on the original state of humanity and the world.</meta>

<page-num>236</page-num>
<header>EUSEBIUS OF THESSALONICA</header>

...since they deny that there are two <term>natures</term> or two <term>substances</term>, two <term>properties</term> or two <term>energies</term> <note>original: "energies" refers to the "operations" or "actions" of Christ</note>, in Christ. Third, because he insisted that the body of Adam before the Fall was not created as naturally mortal or subject to decay <note>original: "corruptible"</note>. From this, according to Andrew's logic, it would follow that Christ had assumed an <term>incorruptible</term> and <term>impassible</term> (incapable of suffering) body from the moment of the union. In reality, Eusebius argues that Andrew should have believed and stated that Adam's body was naturally mortal and <term>passible</term> (subject to suffering), but was kept immortal and free from suffering by divine grace until his sin <note>original: "transgression"</note> stripped him of that protection. This is the unanimous opinion of the Holy Fathers. Fourth, because Andrew called the current world incorruptible and indestructible, whereas he should believe that it is subject to decay and change. In his first reply, the bishop urged Andrew to retract his opinions on many other points, while also proving that Andrew's language was both unclear and blasphemous.

After Andrew had received this encouragement to change his ways, he only grew worse. He wrote another book in which he expanded on his previous views and, in his own mind, proved them to be true. As we have mentioned, the pious Eusebius wrote ten books to counter these arguments. In them, he shows that Andrew was not satisfied with the definition of faith established by the holy <term>synods</term> (church councils) and instead boldly created his own <term>exposition of faith</term>. He shows that Andrew distorted <note>original: "wrenched"</note> many passages from the Church Fathers, even falsifying and forcing them to support his views. Eusebius argues that Andrew contradicts both the Old and New Testaments and the Holy Fathers by claiming the world is incorruptible and indestructible. Andrew claims that concepts like change, transformation, and <term>flux</term> (constant change) are easily misrepresented, much like the suffering that originates from sin <note>original: "vice"</note>. He asserts that our Lord Jesus Christ took on a body that was unchangeable, incapable of suffering, incapable of decay, and without flux.

Again, Eusebius criticizes him for claiming the world is eternal, incorruptible, and <term>ungenerated</term> (uncreated). Andrew does not even allow for the transformation of the elements which contributes to the world's endurance, since he teaches that such change is merely a type of suffering that can be easily misrepresented. Furthermore, Andrew claims that the body of Adam was created naturally incorruptible, immortal, and incapable of suffering—and even claims that the very clay it was made from is incorruptible. Eusebius also criticizes him for defining the word "corruption" in only one sense, through which he...

<summary>Eusebius of Thessalonica refutes the monk Andrew for his heretical claims that the physical world and Adam's original body were naturally incorruptible, and for denying the dual nature of Christ.</summary>
<keywords>Eusebius of Thessalonica, Andrew, Christology, Two Natures, Adam, Fall of Man, Corruptibility, Church Fathers, Synods, Eternity of the World</keywords>

--- Page 243 ---
<meta>This page continues the refutation of Andrew the monk by Eusebius of Thessalonica, specifically addressing the nature of decay (corruption) and the use of patristic authorities to define Christ's physical nature.</meta>

<page-num>237</page-num>
<header>EUSEBIUS OF THESSALONICA</header>

Eusebius demonstrates that God is not the creator of decay, death, or any sinful passion—not even of sinful thoughts. While God is the creator of perishable and mortal substances, decay and death themselves are not essential properties of being and do not exist independently. He also rebukes Andrew for claiming that the Lord’s body, from the moment of its union <note>the union of Christ's divine and human natures</note>, was <term>impassible</term> (incapable of suffering), <term>incorruptible</term> (immune to decay), and unchangeable. To prove his point, Eusebius demonstrates that Andrew’s argument depends on his other nonsensical ideas about the world and Adam.

In the same work, Eusebius explains the many different meanings of the Greek words *phthora*, *kataphthora*, and *diaphthora* <note>original: φθορά, καταφθορά, and διαφθορά; these terms all relate to "corruption," "destruction," or "decay"</note> as they are used in the Scriptures:
- Natural physical changes that are not shameful, such as those resulting from labor, exhaustion, and aging (since old age is the "decay" of youth, just as labor and exhaustion wear down the body's strength).
- The physical toll taken during ascetic and spiritual struggles; as the Apostle says, "Even though our outward self is wasting away <note>original: "corrupted"</note>, our inner self is being renewed."^1
- The suffering and wasting away of the body caused by beatings and punishment.
- Damage, whether partial or total, to animals, seeds, and plants.
- Death itself.
- The decomposition and breakdown of bodies that occurs in the grave.
- Finally, sinful passions or vices.

Since the words for "corruption" can be used in so many different ways, Eusebius argues that Andrew is wrong to insist on only one meaning for these terms.

He supports his arguments with passages from the Old and New Testaments and from several selected Church Fathers: Athanasius, the three Gregories (Thaumaturgus, Theologus, and of Nyssa), Basil of Caesarea, John Chrysostom, Cyril of Alexandria, Proclus of Constantinople,^2 the holy martyr Methodius,^3 and Quadratus.^4 Andrew had distorted and falsified passages from some of these writers. Whether through deliberate malice or simple ignorance, Andrew interpreted them to support his own views, imagining that he was validating his mistaken opinions. However, Eusebius holds him accountable for all these errors—for tampering with the text, for his wickedness and folly, and for misrepresenting these passages...

---

<note>^1 2 Corinthians 4:16.</note>
<note>^2 Patriarch of Constantinople, died 447. He was a friend and student of Chrysostom; some of his sermons and letters still exist.</note>
<note>^3 Bishop of Olympus and Patara in Lycia in the fourth century. It is not certain that he was actually martyred. *See* Volumes 234–237.</note>
<note>^4 A Christian apologist (defender of the faith) during the reign of Emperor Hadrian.</note>

<summary>Eusebius refutes the monk Andrew by arguing that God is not the author of death or sin and by clarifying that "corruption" has many meanings in Scripture. He accuses Andrew of distorting the writings of the Church Fathers to support his unorthodox views on Christ's body.</summary>
<keywords>Eusebius of Thessalonica, Andrew the Monk, corruption, phthora, nature of Christ, Church Fathers, Athanasius, John Chrysostom, biblical interpretation, asceticism</keywords>

--- Page 244 ---
<meta>This page continues the refutation of the monk Andrew by Eusebius of Thessalonica. Eusebius is arguing against Andrew's claim that Christ's body was immune to suffering and decay from the moment of his birth, rather than becoming so after the Resurrection.</meta>

<page-num>238</page-num>
<header>EUSEBIUS OF THESSALONICA</header>

...from heretical writings and venturing to attribute them to our Holy Fathers.

From Andrew’s foolish statements, Eusebius also shows that Andrew believes our Lord Jesus Christ was not changed by the Resurrection from a state of decay <note>original: "corruption"</note> to a state of being immune to decay <note>original: "incorruption"</note>. Instead, Andrew believes Christ was equally <term>impassible</term> <note>incapable of suffering or feeling pain</note> in both His manhood and His divinity. Andrew even dared to call those people "blasphemers" who believed and taught that our Lord Jesus Christ lived on earth with a mortal and <term>passible</term> <note>capable of suffering</note> body. 

Meanwhile, Andrew himself is not ashamed to make the monstrous claim that the body of the Lord is still capable of suffering—even after Christ’s victory over pain and the total removal of death and decay. While Andrew insults the orthodox Christians by calling them *Phthartolatrae*^1 <note>original: Φθαρτολάτραι; "worshippers of the corruptible"</note>, he himself is clearly proven to be a *Pathetolatrae*^2 <note>original: Παθητολάτραι; "worshippers of a suffering being"</note>. 

Eusebius then adds several more passages from the Church Fathers. He shows that the Lord’s body was capable of suffering, mortal, and consequently subject to decay until His glorious Resurrection. Only at that point did the body, by its own nature, become immortal and immune to suffering. He also demonstrates that Andrew is talking nonsense when he calls orthodox Christians "worshippers of the corruptible." That name is fitting and suitable for heretics like Arius, Aëtius, Eunomius, Apollinarius, and Nestorius, but not for orthodox Christians. 

Eusebius also establishes the doctrine that our Lord and God was the creator of nature. Because of this, He took on our natural and blameless "passions" <note>In this context, "passions" refers to natural physical needs like hunger, thirst, or fatigue, rather than sinful emotions.</note>. These are not truly "passions" in the negative sense, but might more accurately be called "the workings of nature." Because of His divine nature, He was incapable of taking on "passions" in the strict sense—those originating from sin or vice. 

Furthermore, Eusebius explains that when Christ ate and drank with His disciples after the Resurrection, He did not do so in the same way He had before the Resurrection. 
- In the latter case (before the Resurrection), He acted according to the laws of nature, refreshing and maintaining His mortal flesh with food and drink. 
- In the former case (after the Resurrection), He performed the act supernaturally as a divine sign <note>original: "dispensation"</note>. 

This was intended to inspire the disciples—and through them, all the faithful—to believe that the body that suffered and was crucified rose again from the dead. It was the same body, not a different one, even though it had been transformed and had become immune to decay and suffering. 

Having presented these doctrines in a manner acceptable to God, Eusebius finishes his tenth book. His style is clear, simple, and pure, and it is marked by great precision whenever the subject requires it.

---

^1 From the Greek *phthartos* (corruptible) and *latreia* (worship). This was a derogatory term for those who held that the human body of Christ was subject to ordinary natural decay before the Resurrection.
^2 Those who believe in a Christ who is still capable of suffering.

<summary>Eusebius concludes his tenth book by arguing that Christ's body was truly mortal and capable of suffering until the Resurrection, at which point it became immortal and incorruptible. He refutes the monk Andrew's accusations of heresy, labeling Andrew's own views as inconsistent and "monstrous."</summary>

<meta>This page marks a shift in the collection from theological disputes to reviews of scientific and practical literature, specifically works on agriculture and medicine.</meta>

<page-num>239</page-num>
<header>GALEN</header>

## CLXIII

Read the work of Vindanius Anatolius of Berytus <note>modern-day Beirut</note>, entitled *A Collection of Agricultural Precepts*. This work was compiled by him from the writings of Democritus Africanus, Tarantinus, Apuleius, Florentius, Valens, Leo, and Pamphilus, as well as the *Paradoxa* <note>original: "Paradoxes"</note> of Diophanes. It consists of twelve volumes and, as our own experience has demonstrated in many instances, it is highly useful for the cultivation of land and agricultural labor. It is perhaps the most useful of all treatises on this subject. However, it does contain some wonderful and unbelievable tales, filled with Greek myths <note>original: "fables"</note>. A pious farmer <note>original: "husbandman"</note> should skip over these sections while gathering the practical and useful information found in the rest of the work. All other writers on agriculture, so far as I am aware, express nearly the same opinions on these matters and differ very little from one another. Where they do disagree, the practical experience of Leo is to be preferred above all the others.

## CLXIV

Read the work of Galen *On Medical Schools*. The author discusses the various schools of thought that have formed within the medical profession. He declares that there are three primary schools:
1.  **The Logical**, which he also calls the **Dogmatic** or **Analogistic**.
2.  **The Empirical**, also referred to as the **Observant** or **Memorial**.
3.  **The Methodical**.

These schools differ in their methods of discovery <note>original: "invention"</note> and in several other respects. The dogmatic physician bases his practice upon the use of logical reasoning to discover remedies. The empirical physician relies entirely upon experiment and observation. The methodical physician, while claiming to use both reasoning and experiment, fails to make careful use of either and is rightly distinguished from the other two groups.

This work is divided into three sections. The first contains a description of the empirical and dogmatic schools.

---

¹ Fourth or fifth century <term>A.D.</term> <note>or CE</note>. About the middle of the tenth century, a selection was made from this and similar works by order of the emperor Constantine Porphyrogenitus <note>the "Purple-born"</note>.
² Of Nicaea; he lived during the time of Cicero. The statement that he wrote a volume on *Paradoxa* is likely a mistake.
³ Claudius Galenus (129–199 <term>A.D.</term>), the most celebrated physician of the ancient world. Born in Pergamum, he studied in Alexandria and was later summoned to Rome by the emperor Marcus Aurelius, who placed great confidence in him. He wrote numerous treatises on medicine and other scientific subjects, as well as grammar and literary criticism.

<summary>This page reviews two practical texts: a comprehensive twelve-volume collection on agricultural techniques by Vindanius Anatolius and Galen's foundational treatise on the three major schools of medical philosophy.</summary>
<keywords>Vindanius Anatolius, agriculture, farming manuals, Galen, medical schools, dogmatic medicine, empirical medicine, methodical medicine, history of science</keywords>

--- Page 246 ---
<meta>This page continues the review of Galen’s work "On Medical Schools" and begins a review of the "Declamations" and "Various Discourses" by the 4th-century rhetorician Himerius.</meta>

<page-num>240</page-num>
<header>HIMERIUS</header>

He sets out the nature of each school. The second book describes the first two schools fiercely debating which one is superior. In the third book, he introduces the Methodical school, which argues with the other two as they each put forward their own claims and try to disprove their rivals. This concludes the third book.

It is clear that this work is superior to all other medical writings for anyone wanting to learn which school is best to join. However, it should not strictly be considered a medical text, but rather a philosophical introduction to the field of medicine. The wording and sentence structure are clear and refined. Galen always pays close attention to these qualities, even though in many other works he confuses and clouds his meaning. He often overloads his writing with poorly timed speeches, side-topics, and long-winded sentences. These seem to break up the flow of the text, and his tedious rambling can make a reader lose interest. This specific treatise, however, is free from those flaws.

### CLXV

Read the *Declamations* <gloss>Practice Speeches <note>original: "Meletai," which were elaborate fictitious speeches written for practice in the schools of rhetoric.</note></gloss> and *Various Discourses* by the sophist <note>A professional teacher of rhetoric and public speaking.</note> Himerius. <gloss>Himerius was born in Prusa around 315 AD and died around 386. He lived at the court of the Emperor Julian and later in Athens. He was the teacher of Gregory of Nazianzus and Basil. Of his twenty-four surviving Declamations, some are school exercises, and others are formal opening speeches.</gloss> 

Two of the speeches are "deliberative" (designed to persuade an audience to take action), and three are "forensic" (legal arguments for a trial); all of them include introductions. Of the deliberative speeches, the first is imagined as being delivered by Hyperides in support of Demosthenes. The second is supposed to be by Demosthenes, speaking on behalf of Aeschines. 

Of the other three speeches, the first is an attack on the philosopher Epicurus, who is imagined to be on trial for lack of religious respect <note>original: "impiety"</note>. The second is written against a wealthy man who had ruined a poor man through his wicked actions; it features the poor man pleading his own case. In the third speech, the Athenian leader Themistocles speaks out against the King of Persia, who had made many promises in hopes of ending the war. <gloss>Themistocles convinced the Athenians to reject the King's offers.</gloss>

More than the others, these speeches are written in a way that demonstrates the power of a superior writing style and—

<summary>Photius concludes his review of Galen's medical philosophy, praising its clarity, and begins an analysis of the fictitious "practice speeches" written by the 4th-century teacher Himerius.</summary>

<keywords>Galen, Medical Schools, Himerius, Sophistry, Declamations, Rhetoric, Demosthenes, Epicurus, Themistocles, Photius</keywords>

--- Page 247 ---
<meta>This page continues the review of the stylistic qualities of Himerius's declamations and provides a list of his specific speeches, including funeral orations, marriage speeches, and academic addresses.</meta>

<page-num>241</page-num>
<header>HIMERIUS</header>

...brilliance and vigor of ideas. The author often builds up complex sentences <note>original: "periods"</note> in a variety of forms, imitating Demosthenes. Although he particularly favors a high-status, elevated style, he skillfully varies his language by using different structures. His choice of words—at least regarding his phrasing and the power of his vocabulary—is certainly not lacking in clarity. The frequent use of broken phrasing <note>original: "breaking up of words"</note> sounds unusual to the ear but is not unpleasant, even though it makes the meaning harder for the average reader to understand. 

Another reason for the author’s general clarity of style is that he uses expressions that create distinctness. As I have mentioned, he uses layered sentences in his reasoning <note>original: "argumentation"</note> and at other times, but by brightening them with energetic figures of speech, he removes any confusion that might arise from them. He frequently and skillfully uses hyperbaton <term>an inversion of the normal order of words for emphasis</term> and other figures of speech, though not as much as he uses complex sentences. He is intense and genuinely energetic whenever necessary. This is how he appears in the speeches previously mentioned.

Following these speeches is the *Polemarchicus*, a speech of praise for those who died in battle fighting for freedom against the Persians, as well as a tribute to war itself. Next is the *Areopagiticus*, in which he claims citizenship for his son Rufinus (this is a real legal request, not a made-up exercise). Then comes a funeral song <note>original: "monody"</note> on the death of that same son; a speech for the marriage of his friend Severus (also clearly a real occasion), which includes an introduction; the *Diogenes* or *Propempticus* <note>a send-off speech</note>, which also has an introduction and is written as a dialogue; the *Syntacterius*, a farewell speech to his friends before leaving for Corinth; a *Propempterius* <note>send-off speech</note> for Flavian regarding his promotion to the governorship <note>original: "consulship"</note> of Asia; a speech to a new student named Piso; another *Diogenes* or *Propempticus*; a short improvised speech <note>original: "impromptu"</note> about a disagreement that broke out in the school; and a speech on the arrival of some...

---

¹ The word used, *peribole* <note>original: "περιβολή"</note>, describes combining various styles, feelings, and figures of speech into a single long sentence. The Latin equivalent is "rounded or encircling speech" <note>original: "*circumducta* or *circumjecta oratio*"</note>: see Quintilian, book 9, chapter 4, section 124: "when a single thought is drawn out by a lengthy period" <note>original Latin: "*cum sensus unus longiore ambitu circumducitur*"</note>.
² *Aetiologia* <note>original: "Αἰτιολογία"</note>, the practice of explaining the causes or reasons for things.
³ *Gorgos* <note>original: "Γοργός"</note>, meaning "vigorous" or "fiery," the opposite of "sluggish" or "spiritless."
⁴ The inversion of the normal order of words or clauses in a sentence, mainly used for emphasis.
⁵ A send-off speech (similar to a *Propempterius*).
⁶ *Schedion* <note>original: "Σχέδιον"</note>, a short, improvised speech.

...the Cyprians; a speech for his first student from Cappadocia; a show-speech <note>original: "Ἐπίδειξις" (Epideixis), a speech delivered in a theater or public place to exhibit the composer’s rhetorical powers</note>, which he at first refused to deliver when asked, in which the thesis "Beautiful things are rare" is discussed; a speech addressed to Musonius, the proconsul of Greece; one for the new pupil Severus, who arrived on the scene during a scuffle <note>this likely refers to a student brawl or a heated academic debate in the school</note>; a short address <note>original: "Λαλιά" (Lalia), a short complimentary address to a real personage</note>; a speech for Count Ursacius; one for another Severus; one for Scylacius, the proconsul of Greece; one for several new pupils who were Ephesians, Mysians, and citizens of Leon <note>The text for "Leon" (τοῦ Λέοντος) may be corrupted; another reading suggests "of the speaker" (τοῦ λέγοντος), referring to Himerius himself</note>; a speech for companions from his own homeland; one for Count Athenaeus; one for the Roman Privatus, the tutor of the son of Ampelius the proconsul; a speech upon his return from Corinth; one for Phoebus, the son of Alexander the proconsul; and one for Count Arcadius, a physician. 

He also wrote an encouraging <note>original: "Hortatory"</note> speech to his newly arrived pupils and a farewell speech <term>a "propemptic," or a speech given to someone about to depart on a journey</term> to Flavian; a speech for the marriage of Panathenaeus; two short addresses; a lecture <note>original: "Διάλεξις" (Dialexis), a private lecture or dissertation for the instruction of pupils</note> given when leaving Philippi in obedience to a summons from the Emperor Julian; an oration delivered in Constantinople concerning the city itself, the Emperor Julian, and the religious rites of Mithras <note>an ancient mystery religion centered on the god Mithras, popular in the Roman military</note>; a discourse on the prefect Sallust, including an introductory summary <note>original: "argument"</note>; a speech for the proconsul Flavian; one for his friend’s birthday; a short address on his friend’s recovery from illness; a speech against those who plot or intrigue; two speeches for Basil the proconsul; others for Hermogenes, Plocianus, Ampelius, and Praetextatus (all proconsuls of Greece) and their companions; a speech on his departure to join the Emperor Julian; a speech delivered in Nicomedia at the request of Pompeianus the prefect; a speech regarding new students; one for a specific new student; one for his companion Zeno; one for Aphobinus, a new student; one for a student who entered the school because of an oracle from the god Poseidon; a speech for those who had arrived from Ionia; one for the Ionian strangers; an unrehearsed <note>original: "extempore"</note> oration to his listeners; a speech in honor of a friend in Constantinople; a discussion with his pupils after returning from his home country; an unrehearsed oration regarding his (modest) lecture room; a rebuke of those who listened to speeches with indifference; an unrehearsed speech concerning some students who attended his lectures but were inclined to be unruly; and a speech for Cytianus and his companions who...

--- Page 249 ---
<meta>This page continues the list of orations by the 4th-century rhetorician Himerius and provides a critical evaluation of his style and religious views by the narrator, Photius.</meta>

<page-num>243</page-num>
<header>HIMERIUS</header>

...behaved in a disorderly manner when he was speaking extemporaneously; an exhortation on the necessity of striving to ensure variety in discourse; a discussion held after his wound had healed <note>This wound was received during a "clash" or riot in the school</note>; a speech after his return from Corinth; a piece on the stylus <note>original: "pencil"</note> and his pupils; on Amyclae, a city of the Lacedaemonians <note>the Spartans</note>, which he visited to offer prayer to God in obedience to a dream; a speech arguing that lectures should not be delivered in public; a speech on the necessity of taking exercise; and another speech delivered at Corinth.

I think that these are all the orations of Himerius, nearly seventy in number, which you patiently and laboriously read while I was present. In all of them, while preserving the same type of vocabulary and the same kind of style, he uses complex, layered sentences <term>original: "heaped-up periods," a rhetorical technique involving long, multi-clause sentences</term> and figures of speech in such a manner that his cleverness and the way he adapts them prevent the reader from becoming bored. As far as I know, I believe that no one has ever used figures of speech so admirably or pleasantly. His writings are full of all kinds of historical and mythical examples, used either for demonstration, for drawing parallels, for providing pleasure, or for the embellishment of the subjects discussed. Through these, he guides and diversifies his language, and constructs his introductions <term>original: "exordia"</term>, conclusions <term>original: "epilogues"</term>, and arguments. He also often gives a preliminary outline of the subject matter and the manner of the discussion. 

But while such is the character of his orations, it is obvious that he held impious views on religion <note>The author, Photius, was a Christian Patriarch and viewed Himerius’s paganism as "impious"</note> and imitates the "dogs" who bark against us in secret. He flourished in the time of Constantius and the most impious Julian <note>Julian the Apostate, the Roman Emperor who attempted to revive paganism</note>, and was the head of the rhetorical school at Athens.

^1 Received during the clash <note>original Greek: "συμπληγάς" (symplegas), literally "clashing rocks," here referring to a violent disturbance or riot</note> in the school.
^2 See also the *Excerpts* <note>original: "Eclogae"</note> by Photius (Codex 243) and the *Orations*, edited by F. Dübner in the Didot series (Paris, 1849).

The text identifies Himerius as a contemporary of the Emperor Julian and a leader of the Athenian school of rhetoric.</summary>

# BOOKS FOR STUDENTS

## *Translations of Early Documents*

->A series of texts important for the study of Christian origins. Under the joint editorship of the Reverend <term>W. O. E. Oesterley, D.D.</term> <note>William Oscar Emil Oesterley (1866–1950) was a prominent Church of England theologian and scholar of the Old Testament and Apocrypha.</note>, and the Reverend Canon <term>G. H. Box, M.A.</term> <note>George Herbert Box (1869–1933) was an English Old Testament scholar and professor of Hebrew at King's College London.</note><-

*The object of this series is to provide short, inexpensive, and convenient textbooks for students, whether working by themselves or in classes. The aim is to furnish translations of important texts without the burden of commentary or elaborate notes, which can be found in larger works.*

### EXCERPTS FROM PRESS REVIEWS.

The *Times Literary Supplement* says: “These <term>Jewish Apocalypses</term> <note>Ancient Jewish writings, such as the Book of Enoch, that reveal hidden information about the end of the world or the heavenly realm.</note> have a direct relation to the thought and religious ideals which confronted primitive Christianity in Palestine. Not only for their own sakes, but for their influence on the New Testament and Apostolic Christianity, they deserve careful attention. Handbooks that are at once so scholarly and so readable will be welcomed by all interested in Christian origins.”

The *Church Quarterly Review* says: “To the theological student who is anxious to know something of the circumstances and thought of the time during which Christianity grew up—and of the Jewish environment of the teaching of our Lord and the Apostles—there is no class of books more valuable than the later <term>Jewish Apocrypha</term> <note>A group of religious texts written by Jewish authors during the Second Temple period that are not included in the standard Hebrew Bible but are often found in the Greek version (the Septuagint).</note>.”

The *Church Times* says: “The names of the editors are a guarantee of trustworthy and expert scholarship, and their work has been admirably performed.”

*The Tablet* says: “A valuable series . . . well produced and should prove useful to students.”

*Catholic Book Notes* says: “The <term>S.P.C.K.</term> <note>Society for Promoting Christian Knowledge.</note> is to be congratulated on its various series of cheap and useful books for students.”

The *Journal of the Society of Oriental Research* (U.S.A.) says: “The S.P.C.K. have again made the whole body of students interested in things Jewish and Early Christian their debtors . . . through their splendid work in this series.”

*The Living Church* (U.S.A.) says: “To praise this project too highly is an impossibility. Everyone has felt the need for such a series of convenient and inexpensive translations of these documents, and . . . we are assured of excellent results.”

# ***Translations of Christian Literature***

->General Editors:<-
->W. J. Sparrow Simpson, Doctor of Divinity; W. K. Lowther Clarke, Bachelor of Divinity <note>original Latin: "B.D." / Baccalaureus Divinitatis</note>.<-

A number of translations from the Church Fathers <note>Early Christian theologians and leaders whose writings established the foundations of the Church.</note> have already been published by the S.P.C.K. <term>S.P.C.K.: The Society for Promoting Christian Knowledge, one of the oldest missionary and publishing societies in the Church of England.</term> under the title “Early Church Classics.” It is now proposed to enlarge this series to include texts which are neither “early” nor necessarily “classics.” The divisions currently proposed are given below. Volumes belonging to the original series are marked with an asterisk.

> *The Month* says: “The cheap and useful series.”
> *The Church Times* says: “The splendid series.”
> *Studies* says: “For the intelligent student of Church history who cannot afford to be a specialist . . . such books are full of information and suggestions.”

->**SERIES I.—GREEK TEXTS.**<-

Dionysius the Areopagite: *The Divine Names* and *The Mystical Theology*. By C. E. Rolt. 7 shillings and 6 pence net.

The Library of Photius. <note>A massive 9th-century Greek work by the Patriarch of Constantinople, summarizing nearly 300 books he had read.</note> By J. H. Freese, Master of Arts. In 6 Volumes. Volume 1. 10 shillings net.

* **The Epistle to Diognetus.** Translated by the Right Reverend L. B. Radford, Doctor of Divinity. 1 shilling and 6 pence net.

* **The Epistle of the Gallican Churches: Lyon and Vienne.** <note>original Latin: "Lugdunum and Vienna"; the Roman names for these cities in modern-day France, which were sites of significant Christian persecution in the second century.</note> With an Appendix containing Tertullian's Address to Martyrs and the Passion of Saint Perpetua. Translated by T. H. Bindley, Doctor of Divinity. 1 shilling and 6 pence net.

* **Saint Gregory of Nyssa: The Catechetical Oration.** <note>A foundational work providing a systematic explanation of Christian doctrine for teachers and new converts.</note> Translated by the Venerable J. H. Srawley, Doctor of Divinity. 2 shillings net.

* **Saint Gregory of Nyssa: The Life of Saint Macrina.** Translated by W. K. Lowther Clarke, Bachelor of Divinity. 1 shilling and 6 pence net.

**Gregory Thaumaturgus (Origen the Teacher): the Address of Gregory to Origen, with Origen's Letter to Gregory.** <note>"Thaumaturgus" is a title meaning 'Wonder-worker.'</note> Translated by W. Metcalfe, Bachelor of Divinity. [*Re-issue*.]

* **The Shepherd of Hermas.** <note>An early Christian literary work from the second century, popular for its allegorical visions and moral instructions.</note> Translated by C. Taylor, Doctor of Divinity. 2 volumes. 2 shillings each net.

**Eusebius: The Proof of the Gospel.** Translated by W. J. Ferrar, 2 volumes.

**Hippolytus: *Refutation of All Heresies*.** <note>original Greek title: "Philosophumena," which literally means 'Philosophical Teachings.'</note> Translated by F. Legge. 2 volumes.

**The Epistles of Saint Ignatius.** Translated by the Venerable J. H. Srawley, Doctor of Divinity. 4 shillings net.

## SERIES 1: GREEK TEXTS (*continued*)

* **Saint Irenaeus: *Against the Heresies*.** <note>original: "Adversus Haereses." This foundational work by the Bishop of Lyon refuted Gnosticism.</note> Translated by F. R. M. Hitchcock, Doctor of Divinity <note>original abbreviation: "D.D." / Divinitatis Doctor</note>. 2 volumes. 2 shillings each net.

## SERIES 2: LATIN TEXTS

**Tertullian’s Treatises *Concerning Prayer* and *Concerning Baptism*.** <note>original Latin titles: "De Oratione" and "De Baptismo."</note> Translated by A. Souter, Doctor of Letters <note>original abbreviation: "D.Litt."</note>. 3 shillings net.

**Tertullian: *Against Praxeas*.** <note>original: "Adversus Praxean." This work is famous for its early and influential formulation of the doctrine of the Trinity.</note> Translated by A. Souter, Doctor of Letters. 5 shillings net.

**Novatian: *On the Trinity*.** <note>original: "De Trinitate." Novatian was a 3rd-century Roman scholar and priest.</note> Translated by H. Moore. 6 shillings net.

* **Saint Augustine: *The City of God*.** <note>original: "De Civitate Dei." One of the most influential works of Western philosophy and theology.</note> Translated by F. R. M. Hitchcock, Doctor of Divinity. 1 shilling and 6 pence net.

* **Saint Cyprian: *The Lord’s Prayer*.** <note>original: "De Dominica Oratione."</note> Translated by T. H. Bindley, Doctor of Divinity. 1 shilling and 6 pence net.

**Minucius Felix: *The Octavius*.** <note>An early Christian defense of the faith written in the form of a dialogue set on the beach at Ostia.</note> Translated by J. H. Freese. 3 shillings and 6 pence net.

* **Tertullian: *On the Testimony of the Soul* and *On the Prescription of Heretics*.** <note>original: "De Testimonio Animae" and "De Praescriptione Haereticorum."</note> Translated by T. H. Bindley, Doctor of Divinity. 2 shillings net.

* **Saint Vincent of Lerins: *The Commonitory*.** <note>original: "Commonitorium." This work established the famous "Vincentian Canon" for determining true Christian doctrine: that which has been believed everywhere, always, and by all.</note> Translated by T. H. Bindley, Doctor of Divinity. 2 shillings net.

**Saint Bernard: *On Grace and Free Will*.** <note>original: "De Gratia et Libero Arbitrio." Written by Bernard of Clairvaux in the 12th century.</note> Translated by W. Watkin Williams.

## SERIES 3: LITURGICAL TEXTS

->EDITED BY C. L. FELTOE, DOCTOR OF DIVINITY<-

**Saint Ambrose: *On the Mysteries* and *On the Sacraments*.** <note>original: "De Mysteriis" and "De Sacramentis." These works explain the rites of baptism, confirmation, and the Eucharist to the newly baptized.</note> Translated by T. Thompson, Bachelor of Divinity, and J. H. Srawley, Doctor of Divinity. 4 shillings and 6 pence net.

### *Texts for Students (continued).*

8. **Select Extracts from Chronicles and Records relating to English Towns in the Middle Ages.** Edited, with Introduction, Notes, and Glossary, by F. J. C. Hearnshaw, <note>Master of Arts (M.A.), Doctor of Laws (LL.D.)</note>. Paper cover, 9 pence net. <note>"Net" indicates a fixed retail price not subject to discount.</note>

9. **The Inscription on the Stele of Mesha.** <note>A stone slab inscribed in the 9th century BCE by King Mesha of Moab, providing a rare non-biblical account of events mentioned in the Book of Kings.</note> Commonly called the **Moabite Stone**. The text is provided in Moabite and Hebrew, with a translation by the Reverend H. F. B. Compston, <note>Master of Arts (M.A.)</note>. Paper cover, 6 pence net.

10. **The Epistles of St. Ignatius.** <note>Letters written by the early Christian bishop Ignatius of Antioch while traveling to Rome for his execution in the early 2nd century.</note> Edited by T. W. Crafer, <note>Doctor of Divinity (D.D.)</note>. 1 shilling net.

11. **Christian Inscriptions.** By H. P. V. Nunn, <note>Master of Arts (M.A.)</note>. With two Illustrations. 1 shilling net.

12. **Selections from the *History of English Affairs*** <note>original: "Historia Rerum Anglicarum"</note> **of William of Newburgh.** <note>A 12th-century chronicle noted for its critical approach to historical legends.</note> 1 shilling 3 pence net.

13. **The Teaching of the Twelve Apostles.** <note>Also known as the *Didache*, an early Christian treatise from the 1st or 2nd century regarding church ethics and rituals.</note> By T. W. Crafer, <note>Doctor of Divinity (D.D.)</note>. 4 pence net.

14. **The Epistle of Barnabas.** <note>A Greek letter likely written in the early 2nd century, discussing the relationship between Christianity and Judaism.</note> Edited by T. W. Crafer, <note>Doctor of Divinity (D.D.)</note>. 6 pence net.

15. **The Code of Hammurabi.** <note>One of the oldest deciphered writings of significant length in the world, containing a collection of 282 laws from ancient Babylon (c. 1750 BCE).</note> By Percy Handcock, <note>Master of Arts (M.A.)</note>. 1 shilling net.

16. **Selections from the Tell El-Amarna Letters.** <note>An archive of clay tablets consisting of diplomatic correspondence between the Egyptian administration and its representatives in Canaan and Amurru during the New Kingdom.</note> By Percy Handcock, <note>Master of Arts (M.A.)</note>. 4 pence net.

17. **Select Passages Illustrating Commercial and Diplomatic Relations between England and Russia.** By A. Weiner, <note>Master of Arts (M.A.), Fellow of the Royal Historical Society (F.R.Hist.S.)</note>. 1 shilling 6 pence net.

***The Journey of King Richard.*** <note>original: "Itinerarium Regis Ricardi"; a Latin prose narrative of Richard I's participation in the Third Crusade (1189–1192).</note> By M. T. Stead.

**Select Extracts Illustrating Florentine Life in the Thirteenth and Fourteenth Centuries.** By E. G. Roper.

**Select Extracts Illustrating Florentine Life in the Fifteenth Century.** By Esther G. Roper.

**Slavonic Settlements in Dalmatia, Croatia, and Serbia.** By J. B. Bury, <note>Fellow of the British Academy (F.B.A.)</note>.

**Babylonian Flood Stories.** By Percy Handcock, <note>Master of Arts (M.A.)</note>.

**Babylonian Penitential Psalms.** By Percy Handcock, <note>Master of Arts (M.A.)</note>.

**Documents Illustrating Irish History in the Sixteenth Century.** *4 Volumes.* By Constance Maxwell.

O. Bower, <note>Doctor of Science (Sc.D.), Fellow of the Royal Society (F.R.S.)</note>.
